Potential Hessian Ascent III: Sampling the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick Model at β < 1 / 2 \beta<1/2
Ewan Davies, Holden Lee, Juspreet Singh Sandhu, Jonathan Shi

Abstract
. We give a polynomial-time algorithm to sample from the Gibbs measure of the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model with negligible total-variation distance (TVD) error up to inverse temperature β < 1 / 2 \beta<1/2 . Prior work obtained TVD error guarantees only up to β ≈ 0.295 \beta\approx 0.295 , while results covering the entire replica-symmetric regime β < 1 \beta<1 gave guarantees only in Wasserstein distance. Our approach demonstrates that the same potential Hessian ascent previously developed for optimization also functions as a sampling algorithm by implementing algorithmic stochastic localization at high temperature. By estimating the covariance of the tilted Gibbs distribution via Gaussian integration by parts, overlap concentration, and precise cavity estimates, we show that a Hessian-ascent process achieves an O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) Wasserstein error guarantee for finite-time localization, improving on the previous o ⁡ ( n ) o(n) . A careful comparison of stochastic localization with the Hessian ascent process and a free probability argument controlling the diagonal sub-algebra of the Hessian improves this to O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) in KL divergence. We then use Jarzynski’s equality with rejection sampling, along with entropy contraction on the time- T T localized distribution, to refine the error to o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) in TVD up to a constant time T T and to complete the sampling with the polarized walk.



1.
Introduction

We give a polynomial-time algorithm to sample from the Gibbs measure of the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick (SK) model at inverse temperatures
\beta<1/2
with total variation error
o_{n}(1)
.
The (random) Gibbs measure for the
n
-dimensional SK model is a probability measure on
\{-1,1\}^{n}
given by

\mu_{\beta A}(x)=\frac{1}{Z(\beta A)}e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle x,\beta Ax\rangle},

where
A
is a random
n\times n
matrix from the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble and the normalizing constant

Z(\beta A)=\sum_{x\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle x,\beta Ax\rangle}

is the partition function.
This means that
A
is a symmetric matrix whose diagonal entries
A_{i,i}
are independently distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,2/n)
and whose off-diagonal entries
A_{i,j}=A_{j,i}
for
i\neq j
are independently distributed according to
\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
, where
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
is the Gaussian distribution with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
.
The parameter
\beta>0
represents inverse temperature, and with this parametrization, the
replica-symmetric
(or high-temperature) regime corresponds to
\beta<1
.
It is widely expected that a polynomial-time sampling algorithm exists throughout the replica-symmetric regime.
Our argument establishes this up to
\beta<1/2
.

Theorem 1.1
(Informal version)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
0<\beta<1/2
.
Given any fixed
\delta>0
, for all
n\geqslant n_{0}(\beta,\delta)
, with probability
1-\delta
over
A
,
Algorithm
2
gives a sample from a distribution that is
o_{n}(1)
in TVD from
\mu_{\beta A}
in polynomial time.

1.1.
Spin glass models and sampling

The SK model is the canonical example of a spin glass model: first introduced in statistical physics to study systems with dense, disordered interactions,
it has since become a central object in probability, with a Gibbs measure that exhibits intricate and rich structure, including
replica symmetry breaking
at low temperatures
[
104
,
100
,
99
,
63
,
62
,
64
,
108
,
109
,
110
,
96
,
97
,
20
,
45
,
107
]
.
Interest in spin glass models in computer science is motivated by their utility as a model of noise in unsupervised learning, signal recovery, or hypothesis testing problems
[
92
,
89
,
69
,
18
,
17
]
, their deep relationship to average-case constraint satisfaction problems
[
51
,
98
,
61
,
48
,
72
,
79
]
, and their challenge as a testbed for algorithms working in high-dimensional and highly non-convex settings
[
106
,
93
,
74
,
103
,
78
,
77
]
, including noisy quantum algorithms
[
60
,
27
,
35
]
.

As random Gibbs measures on the hypercube, spin glass models are also a natural testing ground for sampling algorithms: the target measure is the stationary distribution of natural local Markov chain dynamics, such as Glauber dynamics, and a central question is whether these dynamics mix rapidly.
Average-case guarantees in these settings are well-motivated, with Bayesian posterior sampling as an example application where the target distribution is itself random, and with worst-case analyses often failing in regimes where algorithms actually succeed.
The SK model presents a prototypical challenge for sampling from these random measures induced by dense interactions
[
23
, Open Problem 15]
.

One line of work proves functional inequalities for the Gibbs measure or transformations of it
[
55
,
13
]
, as this immediately implies fast mixing of the associated Markov chain.
For the SK model, this currently yields high-accuracy total-variation guarantees up to
\beta<0.295

[
14
]
.
These results rely on simple spectral properties of
A
, suggesting that further progress requires more fine-grained understanding of the Gibbs measure and the underlying random matrix.

A complementary line of work is
Algorithmic Stochastic Localization
(ASL)
[
9
,
71
]
.
Stochastic localization (SL)
[
56
]
(equivalently, a diffusion model
[
94
]
) is a measure-valued stochastic process that randomly evolves the original distribution
\mu_{0}
to progressively more localized distributions
\mu_{t}
while maintaining the Bayesian posterior property that sampling a random
\mu_{t}
and then sampling
\sigma\sim\mu_{t}
is equivalent to sampling from the original distribution
\sigma\sim\mu_{0}
.
ASL is the algorithmization of SL by replacing its computationally intractable component—namely the magnetization, or equivalently, the score—with efficiently computable surrogates.
Using Approximate Message Passing (AMP), prior work achieves sampling throughout the replica-symmetric regime
\beta<1
with
o(n)
Wasserstein error
[
9
,
42
]
.
We improve the error guarantee to
o_{n}(1)
total variation error when
\beta<1/2
with a Hessian-based approach.

1.2.
Sampling strategy

In SL, the
tilt
or
linear field

y_{t}
defines
\mu_{t}
by the reweighting

\mu_{t}(\sigma)\propto\exp\left(\langle\sigma,y_{t}\rangle\right)\mu_{0}(\sigma)\,,

and the
magnetization

m_{t}
is the average

m_{t}:=\sum_{\sigma}\sigma\,\mu_{t}(\sigma)

of the reweighted/conditional distribution.
The evolution of SL can be described in two equivalent ways that involve
m_{t}
: the usual stochastic differential equation (SDE) definition is

(SL)

dy_{t}=m_{t}\,dt+dB_{t}\,,

where the
m_{t}\,dt
term gradually dominates as
t
gets large.
A change of variables yields an equivalent SDE that we call Hessian Dynamics
1
1

1

The name reflects the general equivalence between the covariance of the Gibbs measure and the Hessian of the free energy with respect to the linear field
[
122
, Proposition 3.1(a)]
. It also reflects that HD belongs to the same Hessian-based formalism as Potential Hessian Ascent
[
78
,
77
]
.
(HD):

(HD)

dm_{t}=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{t})\,dB_{t}\,.

Neither of these two SDEs are algorithmic due to the difficulty of estimating
m_{t}
from
y_{t}
or
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\sigma\sim\mu_{t}}(\sigma)
from
m_{t}
.
But mathematical spin glass theory gives us a third relationship between
m_{t}
and
y_{t}
by positing that at all times, they should jointly extremize an explicit function
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}(m,y)
called the TAP free energy
[
112
,
45
]
.
This
TAP stationarity principle
is only approximately true, but the relationship it specifies is efficiently computable.

We obtain algorithmic versions of SL and HD by using the TAP stationarity principle to compute surrogates for the non-algorithmic
m_{t}
in
(
SL
)
and the non-algorithmic covariance
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{t})
in
(
HD
)
. The former yields ASL-TAP and the latter Potential Hessian Dynamics (PHD). Although these arise from the SL and HD viewpoints respectively, both end up with the same diffusion coefficient for
dm_{t}
, namely the inverse TAP Hessian
(\nabla_{m}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}(m_{t},y_{t}))^{-1}
; they differ only in drift (see
§
2
).

The first major step of the analysis shows that the surrogate covariance
(\nabla_{m}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}(m_{t},y_{t}))^{-1}
is within
O(1)
of the true covariance
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{t})
in Frobenius norm distance in expectation.
The argument is involved, using cavity interpolation estimates, overlap concentration, and various delicate symmetry arguments.
The result implies that
m_{t}
in PHD stays within
O(1)
Wasserstein distance of HD for constant time, improving on the
o(n)
Wasserstein guarantee in
[
9
]
.

The second step compares ASL-TAP to PHD-TAP to show that
y_{t}
in ASL-TAP stays within
O(1)
KL divergence error of SL for constant time.
ASL-TAP and PHD-TAP have the same diffusion terms, and their drift difference is determined by the diagonal entries of the square of the surrogate covariance, which we control by a free probability analysis.

Once this
O(1)
KL divergence error is achieved, we boost it to
o(1)
using rejection sampling.
To accomplish this, Jarzynski’s equality
[
75
]
provides an explicit algorithmic density function by comparing ASL-TAP to a TAP-based scaffold of explicit density functions.

If we could run ASL to
T=\infty
, the localized measure would be a point mass. Since our analysis only permits
T=O(1)
, we stop at a large constant time
T=T(\beta)
and sample from
\mu_{T}
using an appropriate Markov chain on the hypercube—the polarized walk—after showing entropy contraction holds for
\mu_{T}
at large enough
T
.

1.3.
Technical challenges and contributions

The analysis of our algorithm requires several major innovations or extensions of techniques beyond what is established in the literature, which we describe here.

Rejection sampling with Jarzynski’s equality (
§
3
)

Jarzynski’s equality (JE) is a nonequilibrium identity from statistical mechanics that yields importance weights for SDE-based sampling
[
75
,
11
]
.
We use it to reweight ASL-TAP toward a scaffold of explicit densities, enabling rejection sampling.
To our knowledge, this is the first use of Jarzynski’s equality in a theoretical sampling problem, showing that ASL can be corrected by reweighting probabilities instead of displacing mass,
and it may have broader applicability in algorithmic contexts.

Covariance estimate from overlap moments (
§
4
)

We show that the covariance of the Gibbs distribution of the planted SK model with plant-dependent linear field on the Nishimori line, and consequently also the SL-tilted SK model
[
9
]
, is well-approximated by the inverse of the Hessian of the TAP free energy.
The main difficulty here is that, unlike the well-studied case of the SK model with independent external field, the tilt
y_{t}
is itself a random function of the disorder
A
.

El Alaoui and Gaitonde established this approximation for the standard SK model without plant, tilt, or field by decomposing the error into functions of overlaps
[
6
]
, then invoking known cavity interpolation

[
109
, §1.6]
and overlap concentration
[
110
, §11]
estimates.
We generalize and extend their work by performing the decomposition for the planted model with plant-dependent linear field. Since no existing cavity estimates sufficiently covered the planted model, we also
de novo
establish various analytic estimates made available by the cavity interpolation for the planted model (
§
5
).

Regularity of ASL-TAP and PHD (
§
6
)

On a high-probability event in
A
, we show that the coefficients of ASL-TAP and PHD are uniformly bounded and Lipschitz. These regularity estimates control the SDE comparison error that goes into the
O(1)
KL divergence bound, and they also imply concentration of Lipschitz functionals of the algorithmic trajectory, which is used to control the sample complexity of the Jarzynski reweighting.

Control of the diagonal through free probability (
§
B.    Resolvent analysis: Regularity and control of the diagonal
)

We use a free probability argument to control the drift mismatch between ASL-TAP and PHD-TAP, since this mismatch is determined by the diagonal entries of a squared resolvent matrix.
The analysis is similar to a previous characterization for Potential Hessian Ascent
[
78
]
, though here the degree of control required is tighter, and singularities may arise because we cannot afford to regularize with an imaginary component in the resolvent.
The new ingredients are a chaining argument in place of an epsilon net, a cutoff to avoid real-axis singularities, and sharper edge and derivative estimates for the squared resolvent.

Entropy contraction for localized distributions on wedges (
§
7
)

The last technical challenge comes in sampling from the time-
T
localized distribution
\mu_{T}
for large enough
T
.
For this, we run the polarized walk restricted to a wedge
of the hypercube
2
2

2

For technical reasons, the polarized walk is more convenient to analyze than Glauber dynamics.
. We show that this mixes rapidly by proving that the polarized walk satisfies entropy contraction with respect to such measures.
This is accomplished
through the framework of localization schemes
[
46
]

by extending and combining Eldan, Kohler and Zeitouni’s
[
55
]

needle decomposition
technique into a 2-stage decomposition, with a bound on the covariance of the decomposed measures, itself established via a local limit theorem for Bernoulli random variables.

Hessian Ascent for sampling

Potential Hessian Ascent
[
78
]
was introduced as an optimization algorithm for the SK model Hamiltonian at
\beta>1
, with the covariance of the algorithmic increments chosen based on a resolvent of the Hessian of the generalized TAP free energy
[
45
]
. Here we show that at
\beta<1/2
, the same Hessian-resolvent formalism yields a sampling algorithm: up to a small rank-one correction, PHD is the limit of PHA as the imaginary component in the resolvent and the step size both go to 0.
This demonstrates that Hessian Ascent is both an optimization algorithm and a sampling algorithm, and in fact implements a denoising diffusion model
[
94
]
.

TVD sampling without a functional inequality

Our method achieves
o_{n}(1)
total-variation sampling under weaker hypotheses than current functional-inequality approaches appear to require. In particular, we do not assume a high-probability operator-norm bound on the covariance along the localization path
[
46
,
70
]
—an estimate that is not presently known throughout the replica-symmetric regime—and instead combine
O(1)
covariance control with Jarzynski correction and a final localized mixing argument.

1.4.
Prior work

There are two main approaches to sampling from spin glass models. One is to prove functional inequalities (e.g. Poincaré or modified log-Sobolev inequalities), typically via stochastic localization, and deduce rapid mixing of Glauber or Langevin dynamics.
It can be sufficient to show
weak
functional inequalities, which essentially establish the efficacy of these dynamics given a warm start or suitable annealing.
The other is to algorithmize stochastic localization itself. Our method combines these perspectives: we use ASL up to a large finite time, and then prove a functional inequality for the localized distribution.

Functional inequalities and fast mixing

Eldan, Koehler and Zeitouni
[
55
]
, taking inspiration from Bauerschmidt and Bodineau
[
28
]
,
were the first to give nontrivial sampling guarantees (i.e. to within small total variation distance) for the SK model up to
\beta<1/4
by proving a Poincaré inequality (PI).
This is a corollary of their result which holds for any Ising model with a PSD interaction matrix
J
satisfying
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}<1
, and they introduce stochastic localization as a tool to show functional inequalities.
A limitation of their work is that it is a “worst-case” analysis over
\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
.
The result is tight for the Curie–Weiss model, whereas for the SK model we expect the randomness inherent to the interactions to allow sampling at higher temperatures.

Anari et al.
[
13
]
improved the result to a modified log-Sobolev inequality.
Chen and Eldan
[
46
]
established the general framework of localization schemes and streamlined the methods so that they rely only on bounding the covariance along the stochastic localization process.
In later work, Anari, Koehler, and Vuong
[
14
]
proved a log-Sobolev inequality that applies for the SK model up to
\beta\approx 0.295
through a more carefully designed scheme taking advantage of the symmetry of the spectrum of
A
. This is the best result to date for high-accuracy sampling for the SK model.
Mikulincer and Sohn
[
90
]
show a modified log-Sobolev inequality for Ising models with a negative spectral outlier. All aforementioned works, when applied to the SK model, are limited by their use of simple properties of the spectrum of
A
as a black box.

In another direction, Huang et al.
[
70
]
showed a
weak
Poincaré inequality for
spherical

p
-spin models by bounding the covariance along stochastic localization with high probability; this shows efficient sampling for
annealed
Langevin dynamics (up to a so-called SL threshold in the inverse temperature that lies below the shattering threshold). An earlier work of Adhikari et al.
[
3
]
proved a Poincaré inequality for
p
-spin models on the hypercube with a different approach based on induction, giving the first results for
p>2
, though the inverse temperature threshold is weaker

Sampling with negligible TV error up to the conjectured threshold is known for the simpler Continuous Random Energy Model (CREM) on the hypercube
[
84
]
.

Algorithmic stochastic localization

El Alaoui, Montanari and Sellke
[
9
]
introduced algorithmic stochastic localization (ASL) to sample from the SK measure. By using AMP and natural gradient descent on (a custom version of) the TAP free energy to estimate the magnetization, they were the first to achieve nontrivial sampling guarantees throughout the high-temperature regime (initially for
\beta<1/2
and extended to
\beta<1
by Celentano
[
42
]
). The error guarantees are much weaker than TV distance, and they achieve a squared 2-Wasserstein distance of
o(n)
.

Huang, Montanari and Pham
[
71
]
showed that ASL obtains
o(1)
TV error on spherical
p
-spin models by using a higher-order TAP correction to reduce aspects of the
p
-spin problem to the exactly solvable spherical 2-spin model. In contrast, for the Ising case, the SK model is itself already a 2-spin model.
Wang et al.
[
123
]
also studied ASL for models supported on the hypercube but their analysis via Dobrushin uniqueness requires prohibitively small
\beta
in the context of the SK model.

Table 1.1.
Selected sampling guarantees for spin-glass models. All guarantees are polynomial-time. On the hypercube, functional-inequality–based results sample in
\varepsilon
-TVD in
O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
time (noting just the dependence on
\varepsilon
). We obtain
\varepsilon
-TVD in
e^{O(1/\varepsilon)}
time.

The (A)SL process is a reparameterization of a standard denoising diffusion model
[
94
]
, and these models have been independently discovered by the machine learning community for use in generative modeling.
A long line of work has established efficient sampling under access to an approximate score function
[
44
,
43
,
30
]
.

Jarzynski’s equality

Jarzynski’s equality
[
75
]
is an identity in statistical mechanics based on the Feynman-Kac formula
[
80
]
(see also
[
124
, §8.2]
). It gives an SDE-ODE system to calculate changes in the partition function
Z_{t}=\int e^{-U_{t}(x)}dx
as a potential
U_{t}
evolves. It has recently seen wide application in sampling and in machine learning
[
116
,
11
]
, with theoretical guarantees recently given in
[
67
]
.

Interpolations, overlaps and covariances

The analysis of our algorithm requires understanding the overlap array of independent samples from the Gibbs measure of the planted SK model with random linear fields given by SL.
Historically, the “quadratic” Guerra interpolation
[
63
,
64
]
and the cavity interpolation
[
4
,
97
]
have both played significant roles in reasoning about such overlap arrays.
El-Alaoui and Gaitonde
[
6
]
used such tools to show a bound on the operator norm of the covariance matrix for the Gibbs measure of the standard SK model (resolving a conjecture of Talagrand).

Related Bayes-optimal/Nishimori work includes adaptive interpolation formulas of Barbier and Macris, the Franz–Parisi viewpoint of El Alaoui and Krzakala, general overlap-matrix concentration results of Barbier, and fluctuation refinements for overlap arrays on the Nishimori line due to Camilli, Contucci, and Mingione
[
25
,
8
,
26
,
40
]
. These works provide together provide overlap concentration in planted models, but fall short of directly supplying the precise moment estimates needed for various terms that show up in the covariance estimate using the planted-SK model with the SL random external field.

Potential Hessian ascent, free probability, and control of the diagonal

Subag pioneered the Hessian Ascent algorithm—following the top eigenvector of the Hessian of the objective function—to optimize spherical spin glass models
[
106
]
, which inspired both Montanari’s AMP-based optimization work
[
93
]
and the later Potential Hessian Ascent framework for the SK model
[
78
,
77
]
. In the Ising/hypercube setting, PHA adds a coordinate-wise
potential function
to the objective function (recovering the generalized TAP free energy
[
45
]
in the case of the SK model), showing that this adapts the algorithm to the geometry of the hypercube.
Resolvent bounds and free-probability methods then obtain control of the diagonal entries of the Hessian, which determines how individual coordinates evolve.
We use the same basic mechanism here in a sampling setting.

1.5.
Organization of paper

§
2
introduces stochastic localization, Hessian dynamics, the algorithmic ASL-TAP/PHD-TAP framework, Jarzynski reweighting, and the reduction to sampling from the time-
T
localized distribution.

§
2.6
in particular summarizes the main ideas and the structure of the remaining sections as they fit into the main argument.

§
3
develops the quantitative SDE analysis and the comparison between the ideal and algorithmic processes.
§
4
and
§
5
prove the main covariance estimate for tilted and planted SK measures using Gaussian integration by parts, overlap concentration, and cavity arguments.
§
6
establishes the regularity, Lipschitz, and diagonal-control estimates for the algorithmic coefficients, relying on the resolvent and free-probability analysis developed in
§
B.    Resolvent analysis: Regularity and control of the diagonal
. Then
§
7
proves concentration of the time-
T
localized measure on wedges and the functional inequalities needed for the final sampling step. The final section
§
8
combines these ingredients to prove the main theorem.

1.6.
Notation

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
refers to the set of all probability measures on the measure space
\left(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\right)
.

\dist(x)
is the law of
x
when
x
is a random variable.

d_{W,p}(X,Y)
denotes the Wasserstein-
p
distance between two distributions
X,Y\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
. We denote the empirical measure of a vector by
\emp:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
, so that
\emp(v)
is given by
x\mapsto\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}\delta(x-v_{i})
, where
\delta
is the Dirac delta measure.
When
\Omega
is an event in a probability space,
\mathds{1}_{\Omega}
is the indicator function for
\Omega
.
\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\Omega
is the probability of
\Omega
.

M_{n}(\mathbb{D})
refers to the set of
n\times n
matrices whose entries are in
\mathbb{D}
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
denotes the
n\times n
diagonal matrices whose entries are in
\mathbb{D}
.
The map
\diag:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{D}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
takes a vector to the diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are given by that vector, and
\diag^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
takes a matrix to the vector of its diagonal entries.

E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[X\right]
is the diagonal matrix that has the same diagonal entries as
X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{D})
, so that
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[X\right]=\diag(\diag^{*}(X))
.
3
3

3

In operator theory,
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[X\right]
is known as the non-commutative conditional expectation onto the diagonal subalgebra when
M_{n}(\mathbb{D})
is regarded as the base set of a non-commutative probability space, see
§
B.4
for details.

The all-ones matrix will be denoted as
J:=\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\mathsf{T}}
, where
\mathbf{1}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the all-ones vector.
The notation
\circ
denotes the Hadamard (entrywise) product, and the algebra of
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is closed under this operation.
When
X
is a function of other variables,
dX
is its Frechét derivative given in
Definition
B.2
.

Given
X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, we define
\Spec(X):=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:X-\lambda I_{n}\text{ is not invertible}\}
to be the spectrum of
X
(later we will use the same notation for more general operators).
By
\operatorname{tr}_{n}
we denote the normalized trace so that
\operatorname{tr}_{n}[X]:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{i,i}
when
X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.
\mathsf{Tr}
denotes the un-normalized trace.
The norms
\left\lVert{X}\right\rVert_{L^{p}}
are normalized Schatten norms
\left\lVert{X}\right\rVert_{L^{p}}:=(\operatorname{tr}_{n}[|X|^{p}])^{1/p}
, where
|X|:=(XX^{\mathsf{T}})^{1/2}
.
However, the vector
\ell^{p}
norms
\left\lVert{v}\right\rVert_{p}:=(\sum_{i\in[n]}|v_{i}|^{p})^{1/p}
are not normalized for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We also use
\left\lVert{X}\right\rVert_{F}
to refer to the non-normalized Frobenius norm
\left\lVert{X}\right\rVert_{F}:=(\sum_{i,j\in[n]}X_{i,j}{}^{2}){}^{1/2}
, and
\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
is the spectral/operator norm.

\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}
is the Lipschitz norm of a function
f
.

\left\lVert{dX}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}
is given in
Definition
B.2
.

In
§
4
, given a vector
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
m(\sigma):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}
denotes its magnetization. The normalized inner-product between two (independent) samples form the Gibbs measure is denoted as
R_{1,2}:=\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2}\right\rangle
. The notation
\left\langle f(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\right\rangle_{s}
denotes an expectation with respect to the Gibbs measure
G_{n,\beta,s}^{\otimes m}
at time
s
where the
s
-dependent Hamiltonian may be deduced from context. When the Gibbs itself is random, then
\nu_{s}(f):=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle f(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\right\rangle_{s}\right]
. For an ideal process
\bullet_{t}
, we denote the continuous algorithmic process as
\widehat{\bullet}_{t}
and the discretized algorithmic process as
\widetilde{\bullet}_{t}
. In
§
4
and
§
5
, we use the notation
\mathsf{span}\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\}
to denote a finite linear combination of terms made from
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
with real coefficients that depend on
\beta
and fixed integers
k
(but not
n
). The terms
a_{i}
will (often) be of the form
\nu(\cdot)
. The difference between the
i
-th spin of two configurations
\sigma^{a}
and
\sigma^{b}
is denoted
\Delta^{ab}_{i}
.

Let
d(x,y)
denote the Hamming distance between two vectors. Define
B_{k}(x_{0}),\,\partial B_{k}(x_{0})\subseteq\{\pm 1\}^{n}
and
\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k},\,\binom{[n]}{k}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
as follows:

\displaystyle B_{k}(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\left\{{x\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}:{d(x,x_{0})\leqslant k}\right\}

\displaystyle\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}

\displaystyle=\left\{{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}:{\left\|{x}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k}\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\partial B_{k}(x_{0})

\displaystyle=\left\{{x\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}:{d(x,x_{0})=k}\right\}

\displaystyle\binom{[n]}{k}

\displaystyle=\left\{{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}:{\left\|{x}\right\|_{1}=k}\right\}.

The objects
B_{k}
and
\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}
are termed “wedges”, while the objects
\partial B_{k}
and
\binom{[n]}{k}
are termed “slices” of the hypercube. We will identify
\{0,1\}^{n}
with
\mathcal{P}([n])
(the power set of
[n]
) through
x\leftrightarrow\left\{{i\in[n]}:{x_{i}=1}\right\}
.

Lastly, standard notation (and results) from probability theory, spin-glass theory, random matrix theory and Itô calculus are used in multiple places. See
[
16
,
109
,
110
,
81
,
53
]
for reference.

2.
Localization, Hessians and sampling

One of our main tools is the stochastic localization framework of Eldan
[
56
,
57
]
and Chen and Eldan
[
46
]
.
Given a sample space
\Omega
, a
localization process
is a stochastic process
(\nu_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
where
\nu_{t}
is a probability measure on
\Omega
and for all events
A\subseteq\Omega
the process
t\to\nu_{t}(A)
is a martingale such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\nu_{t}(A)\in\{0,1\}
.
A
localization scheme
is then a mapping which assigns to each probability measure
\nu
on
\Omega
a localization process
(\nu_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
with
\nu_{0}=\nu
.
An important consequence of the localization property is that
\nu_{t}
must approach a Dirac measure as time progresses. Hence, a localization scheme is a method for interpolating from the given
\nu
to a random Dirac measure via a measure-valued martingale.
We call the measure
\nu_{T}
obtained from
\nu
by running a localization scheme for time
T
a
T
-localized measure
.

Algorithmic stochastic localization
hinges on the observation that
if one can accurately and efficiently simulate the localization process, then one can obtain an approximate sampling algorithm for the measure
[
9
,
10
,
71
]
, even without proving a functional inequality.

The localization scheme that we implement in our algorithm is the “linear tilt localization” that is commonly referred to as “stochastic localization”.
For convenience, in the following we assume the measure
\nu
is supported on a subset of the hypercube.
A concrete way to understand the measure-valued process is via a process
(y_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
that generates a tilt.
We can think of
\sigma^{*}\sim\nu
and define the tilt
y_{t}=t\sigma^{*}+B_{t}
where
B_{t}
is a standard Brownian motion.
Then the
linear-tilt localization
of
\nu
is given by
\nu_{t}(\sigma)\propto\nu(\sigma)e^{\left\langle y_{t},\sigma\right\rangle}
.
Since
y_{t}/t\to\sigma^{*}\sim\nu
as
t\to\infty
almost surely, this description makes it clear that the process localizes in the sense that
\nu_{t}\Rightarrow\delta_{\sigma^{*}}
as
t\to\infty
. It is also straightforward to check the required martingale property.

Let
m_{t}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{t}}[\sigma]
be the magnetization of the measure
\nu_{t}
, and note that
m_{t}\in[-1,1]^{n}
.
Another perspective on the linear-tilt localization is the fact that the tilt defined above is the solution to the following SDE,

(SL)

\displaystyle dy_{t}

\displaystyle=m_{t}dt+dB_{t},

\displaystyle y_{0}

\displaystyle=0.

We remark that this localization scheme is equivalent to the diffusion process

\displaystyle dx_{t}

\displaystyle=dB_{t},

\displaystyle x_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\nu,

under the substitution
y_{t}=tx_{1/t}
.
It follows conceptually that to sample from the measure
\nu
it suffices to simulate (
SL
).
It is not at all clear, however, how to efficiently compute or approximate the drift term
m_{t}
corresponding to the magnetization.
For more details on these assertions, see e.g. the paper of Chen and Eldan
[
46
]
introducing localization schemes, the survey of Montanari
[
94
]
on the connection between linear-tilt localization and diffusion, and the citations therein.

We introduce Hessian Dynamics (HD) by means of a change of coordinates using Itô’s lemma (
Lemma
3.1
).
From the definition of
m_{t}
and the SDE (
SL
) we obtain (see
§
3
for details)

(HD)

\displaystyle dm_{t}

\displaystyle=Q_{t}dB_{t},

\displaystyle m_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{x_{0}\sim\nu}[\sigma].

where
Q_{t}=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\nu_{t})
is the covariance matrix.
By the localization property of (
SL
), as
t\to\infty
we also have
m_{t}\to\sigma^{*}\sim\nu
, and hence to sample from
\nu
it is also sufficient to simulate (
HD
).
The name “Hessian dynamics” comes from the general equivalence between the covariance of the Gibbs distribution and the Hessian of the free energy
[
122
, Proposition 3.1(a)]
.

In principle, one could approximate (
SL
) or (
HD
) for constant time
T
, round the resulting magnetization
m_{T}
to the cube to obtain a sample
\sigma_{0}
(or equivalently take
\sigma_{0}=\sign(y_{T})
), and hope this is good enough.
We improve the accuracy of the sampler by running the polarized walk restricted to a ball around the point
\sigma_{0}
for for
T^{\prime}
steps, establishing the effectiveness of this step via entropy contraction.
As explained above, this is insufficient for us to prove
o_{n}(1)
TV distance error guarantees. The final idea we implement in the algorithm is to correct the distribution of
y_{T}
with a round of rejection sampling using importance weights computed during the simulation of (
SL
).
Using
\widehat{\,\cdot\,}
to denote an algorithmic version of some quantity and
\widetilde{\,\cdot\,}
to denote a discretized algorithmic version, this results in the following outline for our algorithm.
We give the full details in the following subsections.

Algorithm 1

Informal description of our algorithm

1:

repeat

2:

Initialize
y_{0}=0
.

3:

Approximate (
SL
) for time
T=O(1)
to obtain a tilt
\widetilde{y}_{T}
, while computing importance weights
\widetilde{w}_{T}
.

4:

Accept
\widetilde{y}_{T}
with some probability computed from
\widetilde{w}_{T}
.

5:

until
accept
\triangleright
Now
\widetilde{y}_{T}
is approximately from the time-
T
distribution of (
SL
).

6:

Initialize
\sigma_{0}=\sign(\widetilde{y}_{T})
.

7:

Run the polarized walk (
Definition
7.9
) restricted to a wedge
\{\sigma:d_{H}(\sigma_{0},\sigma)\leqslant k\}
for
T^{\prime}
steps and output the final point.

2.1.
Algorithmic processes

Fixing
\beta>0
, we augment the SK model with an external field
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and define

\mu_{\beta A,y}(\sigma)=\frac{1}{Z(\beta A,y)}e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle\sigma,\beta A\sigma\rangle+\langle y,\sigma\rangle}.

Note that we generalize the partition function
Z
to the case of an external field as necessary.
We denote expectations with respect to the measure
\mu
with
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle
, so that a function
g:\{\pm 1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
has expectation

\left\langle g(\sigma)\right\rangle_{\beta A,y}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\beta A,y}}[g(\sigma)].

Where
\beta A
is clear from context, we omit it from subscripts and the arguments of the function
Z
. For the rest of this section we suppose an arbitrary fixed
\beta A
.

Algorithmic versions of (
SL
) and (
HD
) arise if one replaces the quantities involved in the SDEs with approximations that are carefully chosen to be efficiently computable.
For the SK model we can use the so-called TAP free energy of Thouless, Anderson and Palmer
[
112
]
for this purpose.
Note that we retain the continuous-time perspective for now and separately consider discretization at the end.

For a partition function
Z
, let the free energy be
\mathcal{F}=\log Z
(note that this definition may differ by multiplicative terms from standard definitions).
For the SK model with fixed
\beta A
, and varying external field
y
, we consider
\mathcal{F}
a function of
y
.
By a direct computation, that the gradient
\partial_{y}\mathcal{F}
is the magnetization
\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{y}
, and the Hessian
\nabla^{2}_{y}\mathcal{F}
is the covariance matrix of the Gibbs measure
\mu_{\beta A,y}
.

Writing
\mathcal{F}^{*}(m)
for the the Legendre transform of the free energy, which is valid as
\mathcal{F}(y)
is convex, we have

\mathcal{F}^{*}(m):=\sup_{y}\Big\{\langle m,y\rangle-\mathcal{F}(y)\Big\}.

A little calculus and the fact
\partial_{y}\mathcal{F}=\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle
shows that for fixed
m
, the value of
y
which achieves the supremum is precisely one such that
\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{y}=m
.
An important mathematical property of the Legendre transform is that the Hessians
\nabla^{2}_{m}\mathcal{F}^{*}(m)
and
\nabla^{2}_{y}\mathcal{F}(y)
are inverses of each other.
Thus, one might take the inverse of the Hessian of an approximation to
\mathcal{F}^{*}(m)
to derive an approximation of the covariance
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{\beta A,y})
.
This is one of the key insights of algorithmic approaches to the SK model, where the TAP free energy provides an approximation of
\mathcal{F}^{*}(m)
.

The ASL process of El Alaoui, Montanari and Sellke
[
9
]
replaces the term
m_{t}
in (
SL
) with an approximation
\widehat{m}_{t}
computed with AMP (and natural gradient descent to ensure certain regularities), and the computation is designed such that
\widehat{m}_{t}
is close to a stationary point of a custom TAP free energy.
Our perspective, which sidesteps AMP, is to change coordinates and replace the covariance
Q_{t}
in (
HD
) with an approximation obtained from the (inverse of the) Hessian of the classic TAP free energy.
The accuracy of this approximation and the connection to the operator-theoretic notion of a resolvent has been the subject of several recent works
[
2
,
37
,
6
]
.

The classic TAP free energy is given by

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}(m,y)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\beta}{2}\langle m,Am\rangle+\langle y,m\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(m_{i})-\frac{1}{4}n\beta^{2}\left({1-\frac{1}{n}\left\lVert{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2},\qquad\text{where}

(2.2)

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1+x}{2}\log\left({\frac{1+x}{2}}\right)-\frac{1-x}{2}\log\left({\frac{1-x}{2}}\right),

and for fixed
y
one can think of this as an approximation to the Legendre transform of the free energy of the system.
Standard computations and properties of the Legendre transform give that the covariance
Q_{t}=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{y_{t}})
we see in (
HD
) is equivalently the Hessian
\nabla_{y}^{2}\mathcal{F}(y)
(evaluated at
y=y_{t}
) or the inverse of the Hessian
\nabla_{m}^{2}\mathcal{F}^{*}(m)
evaluated at
m=m_{t}=\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{y_{t}}
and
y=y_{t}
.
Then it is natural to define an algorithmic version of Hessian Dynamics (
HD
), which we call Potential Hessian Dynamics (PHD), where one replaces the true covariance
Q_{t}
with the inverse of the Hessian of
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}
.
The use of the word “potential” refers to the role of the binary entropy
h(x)
as a
potential function
that adapts the process to the geometry of the hypercube, as in previous works on Hessian ascent
[
78
,
77
]
.

Here we see the parallel formalism: (
SL
) considers the evolution of the external field
y_{t}
and its algorithmic counterpart is obtained by replacing the “true” drift term
m_{t}
with an approximation that can be computed efficiently given
y_{t}
(note that the noise term is trivial).
Motivated by the coordinate change inherent to the Legendre transform, (
HD
) considers the evolution of the magnetization
m_{t}
and its algorithmic counterpart is obtained by replacing the “true” noise term
Q_{t}
with something that can be computed efficiently given
m_{t}
(note that the drift term is trivial).

2.2.
An unattainable trio

Recall that we have fixed
\beta A
and consider the ideal processes (
SL
) and (
HD
) for the SK measure with external field
y_{t}

\displaystyle??,

\displaystyle??,

\displaystyle m_{t}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{y_{t}},

\displaystyle Q_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{y_{t}}).

Assuming that we have access to the external field, we obtain algorithmic versions of these processes by replacing the terms
m_{t}
and
Q_{t}
—which involve averages over the true Gibbs measure
\mu_{y_{t}}
—with terms that are easier to compute.
The TAP free energy gives us both: for fixed
y
we expect the true magnetization
m
to be close to a stationary point of
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}
(i.e. to satisfy the classic “TAP equations”) and the true covariance
Q
to be close to
(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}})^{-1}
.
The accuracy of the TAP equations for the magnetization at high temperature is covered, e.g., in Talagrand’s monograph
[
109
]
. For the covariance, the TAP approximation has been proved accurate in the case of no external field
[
6
,
2
]
and at high enough temperatures
[
38
]
.
Since the fields introduced by (
SL
) depend on the random interaction matrix
\beta A
, which is not exactly a classic setting for mathematical studies of the SK model, none of these results apply in our setting and we must establish analogues where necessary.

Inspired by the TAP free energy, given the external field
\widehat{y}_{t}
we want
\widehat{m}_{t}
to be a stationary point of
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}
, and we can approximate the covariance with
\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})
where
\widehat{Q}:[-1,1]^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the operator-valued function

(Q
TAP
)

\widehat{Q}(m)=\left(-\beta A+\diag\left({\frac{1}{1-m^{2}}}\right)+\beta^{2}\left(1-\frac{\left\lVert{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{n}\right)I_{n}-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}.

That is, we want the following system of equations:

(ASL)

\displaystyle d\widehat{y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{t}dt+dB_{t},

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

(PHD)

\displaystyle d\widehat{m}_{t}

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t},

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

(TAP)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\nabla_{m}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}_{t},\widehat{y}_{t}).

See (
3.2
) for an explicit expression for
\nabla_{m}\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}
.
The intuition behind the first two equations is clear from the ideal processes, and (
TAP
) plays the role of the Gibbs average relating
m_{t}
and
y_{t}
for the algorithmic analogues
\widehat{m}_{t}
and
\widehat{y}_{t}
.
For use later, we define
\widehat{m}(y)
to be the function that, given an external field
y
, returns an magnetization
\widehat{m}
such that
0=\nabla_{m}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m},y)
; and we define
\widehat{y}(m^{\prime})
similarly to be the function that, given a magnetization
m^{\prime}
, returns a tilt
\widehat{y}
such that
0=\nabla_{m}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TAP}}(m^{\prime},\widehat{y})
. These functions are used as the algorithmic analogue of computing
\left\langle\sigma_{y}\right\rangle
and solving for the field
y
such that
m^{\prime}=\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{y}
respectively.

The trio of equations above form an overdetermined system, hence we can choose to satisfy a pair of the equations and derive the consequences for the third.
The simplest option is to just take the ASL and PHD processes as written:

(ASL-PHD)

\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} d\widehat{y}_{t}&=\widehat{m}_{t}dt+dB_{t}\\
d\widehat{m}_{t}&=\widehat{Q}_{t}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}\end{aligned}\end{cases}

This, however, does not enforce any kind of consistency between
\widehat{y}_{t}
and
\widehat{m}_{t}
that parallels the ideal situation
m_{t}=\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{y_{t}}
.
It turns out to be more useful to enforce that
(\widehat{m}_{t},\widehat{y}_{t})
satisfy (
TAP
), choose one of ASL and PHD, and use Itô’s lemma to derive a version of the other. For ASL, from the calculations in
§
3
, the resulting system is

\displaystyle??

where with
D(m)=\diag\left({\frac{1}{1-m^{2}}}\right)
the function

(2.3)

\displaystyle f(m)

\displaystyle=\left(E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}\right]D(m)^{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\left(\mathsf{Tr}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)\right]I_{n}+2\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)\right)\right)m

represents a modified magnetization term. We show that at high temperatures, the difference between the ideal process given by (
SL
), (
HD
) and the algorithmic process (
ASL-TAP
) is small.
For the analysis, we first prove that the true covariance
Q_{t}
and the approximation
\widehat{Q}(m_{t})
are
O(1)
close on average under the ideal process; integration (via Grönwall’s inequality) then shows the same for the magnetization
m_{t}
and
\widehat{m}(y_{t})
.

However,
O(1)
closeness is not enough to obtain negligible (
o_{n}(1)
) total variation distance. One approach, taken by
[
71
]
for the spherical
p
-spin model, is to add carefully-chosen higher order correction terms to the TAP free energy. We instead take an alternate route: correct the output of ASL-TAP via rejection sampling with importance weights derived from Jarzynski’s equality.

2.3.
Rejection sampling with importance weights from Jarzynski’s equality

Rejection sampling is a method of sampling a distribution
p(x)
by choosing an easier-to-sample proposal distribution
q(x)
such that
p(x)\leqslant Cq(x)
, drawing a sample from
q(x)
, and then accepting it with probability
\frac{p(x)}{Cq(x)}
. This works if the bound is satisfied pointwise; if it is sampled with high probability over
x\sim q
, then we incur some additional error.

However, we do not have access to the probability density of the sample from (
ASL
) to correct it to (
SL
) by rejection sampling. Instead, we will instead first target a certain distribution
\rho_{T}
whose density is explicit and hence acts as a scaffold; Jarzynski’s equality gives us the importance weights to sample from this. We then
\rho_{T}
as a proposal distribution, to sample from the SL density.
Technically, since we only obtain weighted samples from
\rho_{T}
, we incorporate those weights into the acceptance ratio.

Jarzynski’s equality is a method of designing a stochastic differential equation to compute an unbiased estimator for a partition function given an annealing sequence of distributions.
It can also be used as an asymptotically correct sampler for a measure
\rho_{t}\propto e^{-U_{t}}
for some weights
U_{t}
, or as an estimate of an expectation under
\rho_{t}
.
We take the statement below from Albergo and Vanden-Eijnden
[
11
, Proposition 3]
.

Theorem 2.1
(Jarzynski’s equality
[
114
,
116
,
11
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\rho_{t}\propto e^{-U_{t}}
be a sequence of probability densities where
U_{t}
is twice differentiable with respect to
(x,t)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
b_{t}
be continuously differentiable, and suppose that uniformly for
t\in[0,T]
,
\nabla U_{t}
and
b_{t}
are Lipschitz in
x
. Consider the coupled SDE-ODE system

\displaystyle dx_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[{-\frac{1}{2}\nabla U_{t}(x_{t})+b_{t}(x_{t})}\right]dt+dB_{t},

\displaystyle x_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\rho_{0}

\displaystyle dw_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[{\nabla\cdot b_{t}(x_{t})-\left\langle\nabla U_{t}(x_{t}),b_{t}(x_{t})\right\rangle-\partial_{t}U_{t}(x_{t})}\right]dt,

\displaystyle w_{0}

\displaystyle=0.

Then
\frac{Z_{t}}{Z_{0}}=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-U_{t}(x)}dx}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-U_{0}(x)}dx}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{w_{t}}]

and for any bounded measurable function
h
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h(x)\rho_{t}(x)dx=\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{w_{t}}h(x_{t})]}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{w_{t}}]}.

We can use Jarzynski’s equality with either (
ASL-TAP
) or (
PHD-TAP
) to maintain importance weights
\widehat{w}_{t}
designed to correct the distribution of
\widehat{y}_{t}
to follow a distribution
\rho_{t}
that satisfies

(2.4)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}(y)

\displaystyle\propto e^{\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|^{2}}{2t}},

where we recall that
\widehat{m}(y)
is the magnetization
\widehat{m}
such that
0=\nabla_{m}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m},y)
.
Recall that the function
f
defined in (
2.3
) gives the “modified” magnetization one gets from Itô’s lemma in the derivation of ASL-TAP and PHD-TAP.
Augmenting ASL-TAP with importance weights, we obtain

\displaystyle??

PHD-TAP-JE can be similarly derived; see
§
3.1
.
Our algorithm computes a standard (Euler-Maruyama) discretization of the system (
ASL-TAP-JE
) up to some constant time
T=T(\beta)
and uses the importance weights to “correct” the resulting density to be
\rho_{T}
.
Now that we have a known density, we can further correct towards a distribution on tilts arising from the ideal SL process with rejection sampling.
This lets us sample a tilt
\widetilde{y}_{T}
and round it to via cube via
\sigma_{0}=\sign(\widetilde{y}_{T})\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
.
We then run the polarized walk restricted to a Hamming ball of radius
k=\varepsilon(\beta)n
around
\sigma_{0}
and output the resulting point on the hypercube.

It remains to show that (1) we can estimate the density
p_{T}
of the random tilt
y_{T}
obtained by running SL for time
T
, and (2) we can sample from the localized distribution
\mu_{y_{T}}
, both with good probability over
y_{T}\sim p_{T}
.
The standard reduction from partition function estimation to sampling via simulated annealing means it suffices to prove (2).
Technically, to avoid erroneously accepting tilt samples with low probability under
p_{T}
, we show that with high probability over
A
that we can obtain an
underestimate
of
p_{T}(y)
for all
y
, since we can always sample
\mu_{y_{T}}
restricted to a small neighborhood (wedge) around the rounded
y_{T}
, and it is only with good probability over
y_{T}\sim p_{T}
(rather than
\rho_{T}
) that the neighborhood contains most of the mass.

Efficient sampling from the
T
-localized distribution is shown in
§
7
via proving entropy contraction for the polarized walk.

2.4.
Algorithm

We display the full algorithm in
Algorithm
2
.
First, we run ASL-TAP-JE
as derived in
§
2.2
–
2.3
; we separate this out into
Algorithm
3
.
We conduct rejection sampling as described in
§
2.3
to turn our our weighted sample for
\rho_{T}
into an approximate sample for
\mu_{T}
. Finally, we sample from the localized distribution
\mu_{y_{T}}
using the polarized walk restricted to a neighborhood.

Algorithm 2

SK Sampler

1:

Input:
matrix
A
, inverse temperature
\beta
, total time
T
which is a multiple of step size
\eta
, total steps of polarized walk
T_{\mathrm{PW}}
, wedge size
k
.

2:

Output:
Approximate sample from Gibbs measure
\mu_{A}
.

Part 1:
Approximately sample from
\mu_{T}
.

4:

Run

4
(Approximate Rejection Sampler) with subroutine for sampling and density ratio estimation given by
Algorithm

(ASL-TAP-JE with density ratio estimation) to obtain
\widetilde{y}_{T}
.

Approximately sample from localized distribution
\mu_{\beta A,\widetilde{y}_{T}}
.

Initialize
\sigma_{0}=\sign(\widetilde{y}_{T})
.

7:

Run the polarized walk (
Definition
7.9
) restricted to
\left\{{\sigma}:{d_{H}(\sigma_{0},\sigma)\leqslant k}\right\}
for
T_{\mathrm{PW}}
steps and output sample.

Algorithm 3

(ASL-TAP-JE) with density ratio estimation

1:

matrix

, inverse temperature

, total time

, step size

, number of steps

,

,

, and

.

for

do

.

\widetilde{y}_{t+\eta}=\widetilde{y}_{t}+\eta\widetilde{m}_{t}+\sqrt{\eta}\xi_{t}
.
