2.5.
Desiderata

To analyze the algorithm sketched above, it suffices to establish the following desiderata for
\beta<1/2
. We will prove (d1) in
§
4
and then (d0) and (d2)–(d5) in
§
6
, and then show how they will give us guarantees for our sampling via the stochastic processes described in
§
3
. We will prove (d6) in
§
7
to show sampling from the localized distribution.

Below, constants are allowed to depend on
\beta
but not
n
, and
T
is a constant which depends on
\beta
. Recall that
\widehat{Q}(m)
is defined as in (
QTAP
) and define
Q(m)
such that
Q(m)
is the covariance matrix
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{\beta A,y})
for the unique external field
y
such that
\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{\beta A,y}=m
.
Recall also that
D(m)=\diag\left(\frac{1}{1-m^{2}}\right)
.
Then both
\widehat{Q}
and
Q
depend implicitly on
\beta A
. We would like the following to hold with high probability over
A
. (The expectations below are with respect to the various random processes, for fixed
A
.)

(d0)

(SDE existence, uniqueness, and non-escape,
Corollary
6.14
)

The algorithmic SDEs (
PHD
) and (
ASL-TAP-JE
) have unique strong solutions such that
\widehat{m}_{t}\in(-1,1)^{n}
for all
t<\infty
almost surely.

(d1)

(Error of covariance estimate,
Theorem
4.10
)
For all
t\in[0,T]
, for
m_{t}
drawn from HD:

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\widehat{Q}(m_{t})^{-1}Q(m_{t})-I_{n}}\right\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon(t)^{2},

where
\varepsilon(t)
is a constant depending only on
\beta
and
t
and bounded on
[0,T]
.

(d2)

(Convexity and second-order regularity of
\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}
,
Corollary
6.14
)
There are constants
\lambda,\Lambda>0
such that for all
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

\lambda D(m)^{-1}\preceq\widehat{Q}(m)\preceq\Lambda D(m)^{-1}.

(d3)

(Drift error in PHD-TAP,
Corollary
6.14
)

For all
t\in[0,T]
, for
m_{t}
drawn from HD,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{f(m_{t})-m_{t}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon_{\mathrm{drift}}(t)^{2},

where
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{drift}}(t)
is a constant depending only on
\beta
and
t
and bounded on
[0,T]
.

(d4)

(Lipschitzness of diffusion and drift terms,
Corollary
6.14
)

(a)

There exists a constant
L
such that for all
m,w\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

\left\|{\widehat{Q}(m)-\widehat{Q}(w)}\right\|_{F}\leqslant L\left\|{m-w}\right\|_{2}.

(b)

There exists a constant
L_{f}
such that for all
m,w\in(-1,1)^{n}
, with
f
defined as in (
2.3
),

\left\|{f(m)-f(w)}\right\|_{2}\leqslant L_{f}\left\|{m-w}\right\|_{2}.

(d5)

(Bounded mgf of JE weights,
Corollary
6.14
)

Let
(\widehat{y}_{t},\widehat{m}_{t},\widehat{w}_{t})
evolve as in (
ASL-TAP-JE
).
Then for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, there is some finite
C_{\mathrm{JE},T,\lambda}>0
so that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\widehat{w}_{T}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\lambda\widehat{w}_{T}]\right)\right]\leqslant C_{\mathrm{JE},T,\lambda}.

(d6)

(Entropy contraction for localized distribution,
Theorem
7.1
)
With high probability over
A
, with
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
probability over
y_{T}
from the ASL process, the localized distribution
\mu_{A,y_{T}}
is concentrated on a wedge
B_{\varepsilon n}(\sign(y_{T}))
, and
\mu_{A,y_{T}}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(\sign(y_{T}))}
satisfies a entropy contraction with constant
\geqslant\frac{\rho_{\beta}}{n}
.

These desiderata encapsulate the main technical challenges. The main sampling result then follows from these via general arguments.
In particular,
(d0)
,
(d1)
, and
(d4)
(a) are sufficient to conclude
O(1)
Wasserstein-2 error up to a constant time
T
, by a comparison of PHD with HD using Grönwall’s inequality (
Corollary
3.5
).
Adding
(d2)
,
(d3)
, and
(d4)
(b) improves this to a
O(1)
error in KL divergence up to time
T
, by comparison of ASL-TAP with SL via PHD (
Corollary
3.7
) and mirror descent to solve the TAP equation (
§
D.    Mirror descent for solving the TAP equation
).
Finally, adding
(d5)
and
(d6)
enables sampling
\mu_{0}
with
o(1)
total variation error via rejection sampling and the polarized walk (
§
8
).

Remark 2.2
.

(1)

By “with high probability over
A
”, we mean that for
\delta>0
arbitrarily small, with probability
1-\delta
over the draw of
A
, the desiderata hold with constants depending on
\delta
.
Hence, for the desiderata involving expectations,
(d1)
and
(d3)
, by Markov’s inequality it suffices to prove an analogous bound on the expectation over
A
as well, restricted to a “good” set. For instance, for
(d1)
, it suffices to show for some
A
-measurable good set
G
such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}(A\in G)\geqslant 1-o(1)
that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{A,m_{t}}\mathds{1}_{G}\left\|{\widehat{Q}(A,m_{t})^{-1}Q(A,m_{t})-I_{n}}\right\|_{F}^{2}=O(1)

(where
m_{t}
is drawn from HD with interaction matrix
\beta A
).

(2)

Note
(d1)
and
(d3)
are with respect to an ideal process (HD), while
(d5)
is with respect to the algorithmic process (ASL-TAP-JE).
Conditions with respect to the ideal process are used as input to standard SDE error bounds, e.g. Girsanov’s theorem.

(3)

Two high-probability events on
A
suffice to establish
(d0)
and
(d2)
through
(d5)
for ASL-TAP-JE: one simple bound on
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
and one capturing control of the diagonal entries of
\widehat{Q}^{2}
through free probability arguments. These events are described in
§
6.1
.

(4)

Note
(d2)
and
(d4)
will not hold for all
\beta<1
, because there is a
\beta<1
for which the Hessian
\widehat{Q}(m)
of the TAP free energy fails to be PSD at all points
m\in(-1,1)^{n}

[
65
]
.

Corollary 2.3
(Expected operator norm bound on covariance of HD-tilted measure)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

With high probability over
A
, for each
t\in[0,T]
, there is a constant
K(t)>0
such that when
\mu_{t}=\mu_{\beta A,y_{t}}
is the SK measure tilted by running (SL) or (HD) for time
t
,
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\lVert\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{t})\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}}\right]\leqslant K(t)^{2}
.

Proof 1
.

Note that
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{t})=Q(m_{t})
.
We have by desiderata
(d1)
and
(d2)
,

\Lambda^{-1}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert Q(m_{t})\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\lVert\widehat{Q}^{-1}(m_{t})Q(m_{t})\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}}\right]\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left({1+\left\|{\widehat{Q}^{-1}(m_{t})Q(m_{t})-I_{n}}\right\|_{F}}\right)^{2}}\right]\leqslant 2+2\varepsilon(t)^{2}.

2.6.
Proof overview

In this subsection, we overview the design and structure of the proofs of the desiderata (and their interdependencies).

SDE error bounds and rejection sampling with JE (
§
3
)

In
§
3.1
–
3.2
, we derive the equations (
ASL-TAP-JE
) and (
PHD-TAP-JE
), where the weights are such that we obtain an importance weighted sample from the scaffolding distribution
\rho_{T}(y)\propto e^{\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|^{2}}{2T}}
. This is a natural choice of scaffolding distribution because it would equal the probability density of
y_{T}
if
e^{\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)}
were equal to
Z(\beta A,y)
.

Wasserstein bounds and integrating to get magnetization error (
§
3.3
).
A Grönwall argument gives Wasserstein-2 error between the laws of 2 SDE’s given Lipschitzness of the approximate process and closeness of the mean and diffusion terms under the ideal process (
Lemma
3.4
). Applying this to (
PHD-TAP
), we obtain a Wasserstein bound and that under (
SL
),
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\|{\widehat{m}(y_{t})-m_{t}}\right\|^{2}}\right]=O(1)
(
Lemma
3.6
). This allows obtaining
O(1)
KL divergence between (
SL
) and (
ASL
) up to time
T
.

Rejection sampling with JE (
§
3.4
).
We adapt rejection sampling to the case when only the unnormalized ratio between the target distribution
q
and the proposal distribution
p
is (approximately) known and has upper tails bounded, by first drawing a few samples and taking an appropriate quantile of the samples as normalization (
Lemma
3.8
). In our setting, our weights are the product of
e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}
from Jarzynski’s equality and an estimate of the ratio
\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}
, where
p_{T}=\dist(y_{T})
. The density
p_{T}
is a partition function that can be approximated from efficient sampling of the tilted distribution
\mu_{\beta A,y_{T}}
, with high probability under
y_{T}
(shown in
§
7
). This is not necessarily true for
y_{T}
from the proposal distribution, but because we always have mixing on a wedge, this can only result in an underestimate, which is tolerable.
Lemma
3.9
is the main result which tabulates all sources of error: drift error, tails of the Jarzynski weights
\widehat{w}_{T}
, discretization error for the samples and weights (which are furnished in
§
3.5
), and ratio error. The main theorem is proved in
§
8
by plugging in all these sources of error, bounded through the desiderata. We additionally consider the error from approximating the solution to the TAP equation in
§
D.    Mirror descent for solving the TAP equation
: the theory of mirror descent gives linear convergence since
\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}
is relatively smooth and strongly convex in
m
with respect to
D(m)
.

Estimate of the covariance (
§
4
and
§
5
)

The objective of these two sections is to justify the TAP prediction that, for the tilted Gibbs law
G_{A,y_{t},\beta}
, the covariance matrix

P:=P(A,y_{t}):=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(G_{A,y_{t},\beta})

is well approximated by the inverse Hessian of the TAP free energy at the Gibbs magnetization
m_{t}:=\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle
(note that
P
is referred to as
Q_{t}
in other sections). Equivalently, if

\widehat{Q}^{-1}:=\beta^{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{\|m_{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{n}\Bigr)I_{n}-\beta A+D(m_{t})-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}m_{t}m_{t}^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad D(m_{t}):=\diag\Bigl(\frac{1}{1-m_{t,1}^{2}},\dots,\frac{1}{1-m_{t,n}^{2}}\Bigr),

then the main claim proved in this section (
Theorem
4.10
) is

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{A,B_{t}}\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{-1}P-I_{n}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=O_{\beta,t}(1).

The proof is carried out under the planted representation
x_{0}\sim G_{A,\beta}
,
y_{t}=tx_{0}+B_{t}
, and contiguity
[
9
, §4]
transfers the estimate back to the original tilted model.

The argument has two parts: a decomposition of the Frobenius norm error
\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{-1}P-I_{n}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
into functions of overlaps using Gaussian integration by parts and various algebraic simplifications using replica identities, followed by a local cavity interpolation computation identifying the exact values of the non-negligible contributions from certain subsets of those overlap functions.

Cavity interpolation for coefficients of overlaps (
§
5
)

A cavity interpolation isolates one spin, say
\sigma_{n}=\varepsilon
, writes
\sigma=(\rho,\varepsilon)
with
\rho\in\{\pm 1\}^{n-1}
, and interpolates the cavity field seen by
\varepsilon
(that is, the effective local field in the conditional law of
\varepsilon
given the other spins) between the true field and an auxiliary Gaussian field with matched mean and variance (see (
5.15
) in
§
5.4
; cf.
Definition
4.9
). In the planted model, the cavity field contains both an SK contribution and a Curie–Weiss drift, so both pieces must be interpolated simultaneously. At the decoupled endpoint
s=0
, the last spin factorizes from the bulk and sees the one-dimensional replica-symmetric field (
Lemma
5.5
)

Y^{*}:=y_{t,n}+\beta^{2}m^{*}+\beta\sqrt{q^{*}}\,Z,

where the
y_{t}
is the SL tilt and
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is a standard Gaussian. The extra drift term
\beta^{2}m^{*}
comes from the Curie–Weiss component and is absent in the standard SK cavity method (see
Lemma
5.6
).
This field goes into the constants in (
5.28
) that occur later:

\rho_{2}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sech^{2}(Y^{*})],\qquad\rho_{4}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sech^{4}(Y^{*})].

Following Talagrand’s rectangular-sum method from
[
109
, § 1.8]
, the cavity computation is organized around the antisymmetric rectangular sums

f:=R_{1,3}-R_{1,4}-R_{2,3}+R_{2,4},\qquad\widehat{f}:=\widehat{R}_{1,3}-\widehat{R}_{1,4}-\widehat{R}_{2,3}+\widehat{R}_{2,4},

where

\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{\ell}:=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{\ell}-\langle\sigma_{i}\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\langle\sigma_{i}\rangle^{2}}},\qquad\widehat{R}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{\ell}\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{\ell^{\prime}}.

The point of this choice is that
f
and
\widehat{f}
change sign under the swap
3\leftrightarrow 4
, while replica-symmetric bulk quantities remain invariant; this kills non-replica fluctuations and leaves a single stability denominator
1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4}
(
Lemma
5.4
). Writing
\nu(\cdot):=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle
, the key second moments are

U_{n}:=\nu(f^{2}),\qquad V_{n}:=\nu(f\widehat{f}),\qquad W_{n}^{(2)}:=\nu(f\,f^{(2)}),

where
f^{(2)}
is the
D^{2}
-weighted rectangular sum that later enters
\mathsf{Tr}(PD^{2}P)
. The cavity recursion gives

(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})U_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{4}}{n}+O(n^{-2}),\qquad(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})V_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{2}}{n}+O(n^{-2}),

and

W_{n}^{(2)}=\frac{4}{n}+\beta^{2}U_{n}+O(n^{-2})=\frac{4}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2}).

These formulas are proved in
Proposition
5.23
and
Proposition
5.24
and supply one set of the exact coefficients needed in the final trace expansion. The other critical exact estimate in the cavity interpolation comes in the form of the proof that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\mathsf{Tr}[PDP]]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c^{2}\mathsf{Tr}[P^{2}]]+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\mathsf{Tr}[P]]+O(1)\,,

where
c=\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\mathsf{Tr}[P]
(
Lemma
5.30
). This expansion provides the second set of exact coefficients needed in the final trace expansion, and the fact that this form should exist is “guessed” from the behavior of the algorithmic counterpart
\widehat{Q}
to
P
4
4

4

As
\widehat{Q}
is a resolvent evaluated at
c
, one can use analytic subordination to evaluate its derivatives at
c
and take their projections onto the diagonal sub-algebra
\mathcal{D}_{n}
. Doing this reveals that
\widehat{Q}
satisfies a similar identity, and leads to the natural guess that
P
should as well (if they are good proxies for each other).
. Armed with the exact estimates for
W^{(2)}_{n}
and
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\mathsf{Tr}[PDP]]
, one can arrange for exact cancellations between certain terms in
P
that “behave like” the diagonal
D
in
\widehat{Q}
and another set that comes from applying Gaussian integration-by-parts to “mixed” terms that interact with the
\mathsf{GOE}(n)
matrix
A
.

After (identifying and) arranging for the cancellation of the heavy terms, the task of tremendous complexity that remains is to show every other term is of
O(1)
(cf.
Lemma
4.15
). Doing this is an involved and technical exercise, requiring very subtle book-keeping which simultaneously uses the linear-algebraic properties of
P
in conjunction with the cancellations in bulk terms (such as
f
) afforded by good concentration and cavity estimates.

The decisive estimates that control a large set of the remainder terms come from the observation that linear and cubic cavity terms are one order smaller than a naive Hölder bound would suggest. If
G^{-}
is a quadratic bulk observable in the
(n-1)
-spin cavity system, then uniformly along the cavity path (
Lemma
5.8
&
Proposition
5.21
),

\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})=O(n^{-2}),\qquad\nu_{s}(\widehat{f}^{-}G^{-})=O(n^{-2}).

The Hölder estimate would only give
O(n^{-3/2})
(cf.
Lemma
5.19
) because
f^{-}
is
O(n^{-1/2})
and
G^{-}
is
O(n^{-1})
. That weaker bound would be fatal: these terms appear with an
n^{2}
prefactor in the final trace expansion, so
O(n^{-3/2})
would leave an uncontrolled
O(n^{1/2})
remainder. The extra factor
n^{-1/2}
comes from a second-order Taylor expansion along the cavity path and the exact antisymmetry of the rectangular sum in
Lemma
5.22
, while the denominator
1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4}
being bounded away from zero ensures that the resulting self-consistent estimate closes without losing this cancellation. A similar use of the exact control of the Gibbs derivative available during the cavity interpolation (
Lemma
5.6
) sets up a self-consistent linear system to prove that
\nu_{s}(R_{ab}-q^{*})
and
\nu_{s}(M_{a}-m^{*})
are also
O(1/n)
(cf.
Lemma
5.8
) and not just
O(n^{-1/2})
as suggested by naive concentration estimates.

Just as the requirement that
\beta^{2}\rho_{4}<1
is critical in the cubic estimates, so is the requirement that
\rho(\beta^{2}\mathbf{M})<1
for the linear fluctuation estimate (where
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
is an operator that governs the “stability” of a certain linear system). For
\beta<1/2
, both these conditions are true (cf.
Lemma
5.31
).

Covariance error estimate

With cavity asymptotics in hand and concentration for bulk quantities sourced from
[
7
, §3]
, the covariance theorem reduces to an exact trace expansion. One decomposes

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{-1}P-I_{n}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\mathcal{E}_{D}+\mathcal{E}_{A}+\mathcal{E}_{R},

where
\mathcal{E}_{D}
collects the diagonal/TAP terms handled in
Lemma
4.11
,
\mathcal{E}_{A}
collects the terms involving the disorder matrix
A
handled in
Lemma
4.13
, and
\mathcal{E}_{R}
collects terms involving the rank-
1
plant which are handled in
Lemma
4.16
. The trace-to-replica identities in
Lemma
A.3
and
Lemma
4.11
, which include

\mathsf{Tr}[P^{2}]=\frac{n^{2}}{4}\left\langle f^{2}\right\rangle,\qquad\mathsf{Tr}[PDP]=\frac{n^{2}}{4}\left\langle f\widehat{f}\right\rangle,\qquad\mathsf{Tr}[PD^{2}P]=\frac{n^{2}}{4}\left\langle f\,f^{(2)}\right\rangle,

convert
\mathcal{E}_{D}
into a linear combination of
U_{n}
,
V_{n}
,
W_{n}^{(2)}
,
\mathsf{Tr}(P)
, and the rank-one correction coming from
\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}m_{t}m_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

The term
\mathcal{E}_{A}
is treated by Gaussian integration by parts in the GOE entries in (
4.7
) and (
4.14
), giving
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[A_{ij}F]=\frac{1}{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\partial_{ij}F]
for any well-behaved function
F
of
A
, where
\partial_{ij}
is the partial derivative with respect to
A_{ij}
. The basic derivative identity is

\partial_{ij}\left\langle F\right\rangle=\beta\Bigl(\left\langle F\,\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\right\rangle-\left\langle F\right\rangle\left\langle\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\right\rangle\Bigr).

Applying this to
P_{ab}
and expanding the centered four-spin term yields the exact decomposition in
Lemma
A.2
:

\partial_{ij}P_{ab}=\beta\bigl(P_{ai}P_{bj}+P_{aj}P_{bi}\bigr)+\beta\bigl(m_{t,i}\,T_{abj}+m_{t,j}\,T_{abi}+\Gamma_{abij}\bigr),

where
T_{abj}
is a centered three-point response term and
\Gamma_{abij}
is a centered four-point cumulant. The first bracket produces the leading replica traces; every other term carries an extra centered fluctuation and is, therefore, lower order once the
O(n^{-2})
cubic cavity bounds from
Proposition
5.21
and the one-site moment bound
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[D_{jj}^{p}]\leqslant C_{p,\beta,t}
of
Lemma
5.3
are utilized. Some of the other terms carry “repeated indices” along with factors of
D
that requires slightly more delicate control, and this often requires the use of a “diagonally-weighted” cubic lemma, shown in in
Lemma
5.27
– this lemma is also proved via a second-order Taylor expansion, with initial terms controlled via exact decoupling lemmata (cf.
Lemma
5.5
,
Lemma
5.13
&
Lemma
5.14
) in conjunction with
Lemma
5.3
, and second-derivative terms controlled via symmetry and Hölder arguments.

After substituting the explicit asymptotics of
U_{n}
,
V_{n}
, and
W_{n}^{(2)}
into
\mathcal{E}_{D}
and
\mathcal{E}_{A}
, the order-
n
contributions cancel exactly. Once the cancellations involving
(U_{n},V_{n},W^{(2)}_{n})
,
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c^{2}\mathsf{Tr}[P^{2}]]
, and
\beta^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\mathsf{Tr}[P^{2}]]
are made, the remaining contributions are all
O(1)
: the one-site terms
\sigma_{i}
weighted by the entries of
D
are controlled by the uniform moments of
D_{jj}
, the rank-one magnetization terms are controlled by the overlap and magnetization moment bounds, and the
T_{abj}
and
\Gamma_{abij}
terms in
Lemma
A.2
are controlled because their
n^{2}
prefactors are exactly balanced by the
O(n^{-2})
cubic cavity estimates. Consequently,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{A,B_{t}}\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{-1}P-I_{n}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=O_{\beta,t}(1).

Properties of the algorithmic process (
§
6
)

The analysis in
§
6
reduces to one random-matrix input from
§
B.    Resolvent analysis: Regularity and control of the diagonal
: a uniform law for the diagonal of a real-axis squared resolvent. Once that input is available, the rest of the argument is deterministic resolvent calculus. Throughout,
\gamma:=(1-2\beta)/2>0
is fixed, but to keep the roadmap readable we display only the powers of
n
; all suppressed constants depend polynomially on
\beta
and
\gamma^{-1}
.

Note that in keeping with free probability conventions, in these two sections we use the non-commutative
L^{2}
norm
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
on linear operators, which differs from the Frobenius norm
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{F}
by a factor of
1/\sqrt{n}
when the linear operator is a matrix. We also use the normalized trace
\operatorname{tr}_{n}
.

Deterministic resolvent control from a spectral gap

The deterministic part of the argument is developed in
§
6.2
. For
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
, the relevant coefficients are

D(m)=(I_{n}-\diag(m)^{2})^{-1},\qquad a(m)=\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D(m)^{-1}),\qquad\overline{Q}(m)=\bigl(a(m)I_{n}-\beta A+D(m)\bigr)^{-1},

together with the rank-one-corrected resolvent
\widehat{Q}(m)
appearing in ASL–TAP. The basic high-probability event is

\Omega_{\gamma}^{(A)}:=\{\beta\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 1-\gamma\}.

On this event, every matrix
aI_{n}-\beta A+D
with
a\in[0,1]
and
D\succeq I_{n}
is uniformly invertible, so
Lemma
6.1
and
Lemma
6.2
give

\left\lVert\widehat{Q}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}=O(1),\qquad\left\lVert D(m)\widehat{Q}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}=O(1),\qquad\left\lVert D(m)\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}D(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}=O(1),

together with Lipschitz bounds (as measured with
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
)

\operatorname{Lip}(\widehat{Q})=O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\operatorname{Lip}(\widehat{Q}^{2})=O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\operatorname{Lip}(D\widehat{Q}^{2}D)=O(n^{-1/2}).

Thus, after
\Omega_{\gamma}^{(A)}
is imposed, the only nontrivial quantity left is the diagonal of
\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}
, encoded by

\delta_{\diag}(m)=E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\!\left[\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}\right]D(m)^{2}-\Bigl(1+\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\widehat{Q}(m)^{2})\Bigr)I_{n}

from
(
6.5
)
. This is the term that enters the Itô correction in ASL–TAP and controls its drift mismatch with PHD (observe that
\delta_{\diag}(m)
differs from
m-f(m)
by a small
\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)m
).

Free-probability prediction for the diagonal

The target for the diagonal law is explained in
§
B.4
.
Essentially, free probability provides a way to characterize sums and products with GOE matrices by a limiting
semicircular operator

S
as
n\to\infty
.

The operator
S
is an infinite-dimensional object serving as the limiting case of
A
, and (following Biane
[
31
]
) analytic subordination in
Lemma
B.18
produces an analytic map
F
such that

E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\!\left[(zI_{n}-\beta S+D)^{-1}\right]=(F(z)I_{n}+D)^{-1}.

Differentiating this identity at a real point
a
predicts

E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\!\left[D(aI_{n}-\beta S+D)^{-2}D\right]=F^{\prime}(a)\,D(F(a)I_{n}+D)^{-2}D.

At the value used in the algorithm
a=\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-1})
,
Lemma
B.18
gives

F(a)=0,\qquad F^{\prime}(a)=\frac{1}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})},

so the free model collapses to the scalar matrix

E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\!\left[D(aI_{n}-\beta S+D)^{-2}D\right]=\frac{I_{n}}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}.

This is the only consequence of free probability later used in
§
6
: after this specialization, the main task is simply to show that the finite-
n
diagonal is uniformly close to this scalar matrix.

Finite-
n
comparison with the freely independent limit

The main finite-
n
statement is
Corollary
B.35
. Writing

Y(a,D):=E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\!\left[D(aI_{n}-\beta A+D)^{-2}D\right],

that result states that with high probability,

(2.5)

\sup_{a\in[0,1]}\sup_{D\succeq I_{n}}\left\lVert{Y(a,D)-F^{\prime}(a)D(F(a)I_{n}+D)^{-2}D}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant O(n^{-1/2}).

Specializing to
a=\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-1})
produces the event
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
used in
§
6.4
. The proof combines the concentration result in
Theorem
B.9
with the expectation comparison of
Proposition
B.23
.

The expectation comparison follows the same overall template as the modified-Hessian analysis in
[
78
, Section 4]
and
[
77
, Section 4]
, but the observable is different. Here one needs the real-axis squared

D(aI_{n}-\beta A+D)^{-2}D

rather than a regularized imaginary-part resolvent.
We approach the squared resolvent through the limit

-\frac{1}{b}\,\Im\bigl[D(aI_{n}+ibI_{n}-\beta A+D)^{-1}D\bigr]\;\to\;D(aI_{n}-\beta A+D)^{-2}D\qquad(b\downarrow 0),

To control the diagonal of this matrix, we perform Gaussian interpolation on the trace inner product between
DR_{k}(a+bi,t)D
and an arbitrary diagonal matrix
T
, where

W_{k}(t)=\sqrt{1-t}\,(A\otimes I_{k})+\sqrt{t}\,B_{k},\qquad R_{k}(z,t)=\bigl(zI_{nk}-\beta W_{k}(t)+D\otimes I_{k}\bigr)^{-1}.

We take
k\to\infty
to approach the freely independent limit at
t=1
, whereas at
t=0
it stays equal to the quantity of interest
\langle T,\,Y(a,D)\rangle_{\operatorname{tr}_{n}}
.
As in
[
78
,
77
]
, we bound the
t
-derivative of this interpolated observable by applying Gaussian integration by parts, after which we see explicit single-trace terms of order
n^{-1}
and also a covariance term that is shown to be of order
n^{-3/2}
by a Poincaré inequality. Thus the expectation-vs.-free-model comparison is strictly smaller than the final fluctuation scale:

\left\langle T,\;\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{A}[Y(a,D)]-F^{\prime}(a)D(F(a)I_{n}+D)^{-2}D\right\rangle_{\operatorname{tr}_{n}}\;\leqslant\;O(n^{-1})\left\lVert{T}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+O(n^{-3/2})\left\lVert{T}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Unlike in
[
78
,
77
]
, this interpolation encounters a singularity if
aI_{n}-\beta A+D\not\succ 0
.
We therefore multiply the interpolated observable by a smooth cutoff function
\chi_{k}(A,B_{k})
to make it identically 0 in the rare event when
\left\lVert W_{k}(t)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
can be large enough to potentially cause a singularity.
This introduces an exponentially small error, though it requires a free probability characterization of the edges of the spectrum of
W_{k}(t)
in
Corollary
B.22
.

Chaining bound over all
D

The preceding argument controlled
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{A}\langle Y(a,D),T\rangle
for diagonal test matrices
T
, but to obtain
(
2.5
)
, we must obtain a high-probability uniform bound over all choices of
D
and
T
.
Writing
D=W^{-1}
, we study the scalar process

\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)=\langle T,\,Y(a,D)\rangle_{\operatorname{tr}_{n}},\qquad\left\lVert{T}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}=1.

A direct computation, proved in
Lemma
B.8
, gives

\nabla_{A}\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)=\beta\Bigl(X^{-2}DTD\,X^{-1}+X^{-1}DTD\,X^{-2}\Bigr),\qquad X=aI_{n}-\beta A+D.

This shows that
\Phi_{a,W,T}
is Lipschitz in
A
and also Lipschitz in the parameter triple
(a,W,T)
, with constants independent of
n
. The parameter family has effective dimension
2n+1
: one scalar parameter
a
,
n
diagonal degrees of freedom in
W
, and
n-1
in the diagonal test matrix
T
. Pointwise GOE concentration is already of order
n^{-1}
, but a naive
\varepsilon
-net over this family (as used in
[
78
]
) would have size
\exp(\Theta(n\log n))
, introducing an extra
\sqrt{\log n}
and yielding only a bound of
O(\sqrt{\log n}/\sqrt{n})
. Generic chaining (in the form of Dudley-style entropy bounds) removes this logarithmic loss and upgrades the pointwise
n^{-1}
concentration to the sharp uniform
n^{-1/2}
bound in
Theorem
B.9
.

The TAP resolvent and consequences for ASL–TAP

The argument so far applies to
\overline{Q}(m)
, which omits the rank-1 term in
\widehat{Q}(m)
. The transfer to the actual TAP resolvent is isolated in
§
6.3
:
Lemma
6.4
shows that

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\!\left[\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}-\overline{Q}(m)^{2}\right]D(m)^{2}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant O(n^{-1/2}).

Hence the same diagonal law holds for
\widehat{Q}(m)
at the same scale. Combined with
Lemma
6.5
and
Lemma
6.6
, this yields

\sup_{m\in(-1,1)^{n}}\left\lVert{\delta_{\diag}(m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\operatorname{Lip}(\delta_{\diag})\leqslant O(n^{-1/2}).

The drift comparison in
§
6.5
is then deterministic. If
f(m)
denotes the Itô correction map from that section,
Lemma
6.7(b)
and
Lemma
6.7(e)
imply

\left\lVert{m-f(m)}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(1),\qquad\operatorname{Lip}\bigl(m\mapsto\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m))\bigr)\leqslant O(1).

Together with the
O(1)
operator bounds and
O(n^{-1/2})
Lipschitz bounds on
\widehat{Q}
, this yields existence and uniqueness of the SDE, Wasserstein-2 robustness to path perturbations, nonescape from
(-1,1)^{n}
in
Lemma
6.9
, and sub-Gaussian concentration of Lipschitz path functionals (including the Jarzynski weights) in
Theorem
6.13
.

Entropy contraction for Ising models on wedges (
§
7
)

After running (
ASL-TAP-JE
) up to large constant time
T
, we wish to sample from the localized distribution
\mu_{T}=\mu_{\beta A,y_{T}}
using the polarized walk, which is enabled by the main theorem of this section (
Theorem
7.1
):

(1)

For the stochastic localization process
y_{T}=T\sigma^{*}+B_{T}
, for large enough constant
T
, the posterior
\mu_{\beta A,y_{T}}
is exponentially concentrated on a small Hamming ball (wedge)
B_{\varepsilon n}(\sigma^{*})
or
B_{\varepsilon n}(\sign(y_{T}))
.

(2)

If
\Omega=B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
, then with high probability over
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
, the restricted measure
\mu_{\beta A,h}\big|_{\Omega}

satisfies entropy contraction with constant
\geqslant\frac{\rho_{\beta}}{n}
,
with

\rho_{\beta}
depending only on
\beta
.

The intuition is that for large
T
,
y_{T}=T\sigma^{*}+B_{T}

has a large signal to noise ratio, so that the mass of the posterior
\mu_{T}
is concentrated around
\sigma^{*}
. We know that for any Ising model
\mu_{\beta A}
on the hypercube, a log-Sobolev inequality is satisfied when
\lambda_{\max}(\beta A)-\lambda_{\min}(\beta A)<1

[
13
]
. One way to think of this is
\lambda_{\max}(\beta A)-\lambda_{\min}(\beta A)<\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\diam(\{\pm 1\}^{n})
. One might expect that if we restrict to a small
\varepsilon n
-wedge, which has smaller diameter, that this allows sampling when
A
has larger spectral width. The condition we will use to ensure this is that
A_{S\times S}
is small for sets of coordinates of size
O(\varepsilon n)
, which is satisfied whp when we sample
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
as for the SK model.

Huang, Montanari and Pham
[
71
]
prove a functional inequality for the localized distribution for the spherical
p
-spin model by showing log-concavity in a chart; since our distribution is on the hypercube we have to work harder to tame the geometry.

Two-stage decomposition.
We make a two-stage decomposition into product measures restricted to the wedge
\Omega
, and then make a key estimate of the covariance of these measures. The two stages are as follows.

(1)

The needle decomposition of
[
55
]
(
Theorem
7.24
), given a test function
\varphi
, decomposes an Ising model
\mu_{A}
(where
A
is PSD) into rank-1 Ising models
\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}
with
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
, which preserve the expectation under
\varphi
. Therefore, it suffices to prove functional inequalities for the rank-1 models which arise.

Since this requires a PSD interaction matrix, we replace
\beta A
by
\beta A+\gamma I
. This does not change the Gibbs measure on the hypercube, because
\left\langle\sigma,I_{n}\sigma\right\rangle=n
is constant.

(2)

The Hubbard–Stratonovich transform (
Theorem
7.25
) writes each such rank-1 model as a 1-dimensional mixture of product measures,

\mu_{\lambda xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}\big|_{\Omega}=\int p_{\lambda}(u)\,\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}\,x+v}\big|_{\Omega}\,du.

By the localization framework
[
46
]
applied to the rank-1 models, it suffices to control the covariance of
\mu_{\lambda xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}\big|_{\Omega}
.
By variance decomposition, since the variance of
\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}\,x+v}\big|_{\Omega}
is easily controlled, it suffices to control the variance of the 1-dimensional distribution
p_{\lambda}
. We summarize the two-stage decomposition in
Lemma
7.28
.

This would follow if
p_{\lambda}
were log-concave, so we calculate

-(\log p_{\lambda})^{\prime\prime}(u)=1-\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}\mid_{\Omega}}\bigl(\left\langle\sqrt{\lambda}x,\sigma\right\rangle\bigr).

Entropy contraction for
\beta<\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}
(
§
7.2
).
We need to bound the covariance of a restricted product measure. For convenience, we bound the covariance when restricted to
\Omega
, a slice or wedge of size
k
in
\{0,1\}^{n}
. The basic bound,
Lemma
7.21
, is that it can be bounded in terms of its diagonal entries,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{h}|_{\Omega})

\displaystyle\preceq 2\diag\bigl(p_{i}(1-p_{i})\bigr),\qquad p_{i}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}\sigma_{i},

proved using negative dependence and a Gershgorin argument.
This gives the key inequality (
7.3
) for
\Omega=B_{k}(x_{0})
,
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
,

\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}(\langle x,\sigma\rangle)\leqslant 2\max_{|S|=4k}\left\lVert A_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}.

The gain from restricting to a wedge is that only
O(\varepsilon n)
coordinates can fluctuate. Accordingly, the relevant interaction strength is not the full norm of
A
, but the norms of small principal submatrices. A union bound together with the GOE operator-norm tail estimate shows that with high probability,

\sup_{|S|\leqslant 4\varepsilon n}\left\lVert A_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{h(4\varepsilon)},

where
h
is the binary entropy; see
Lemma
7.23
.

Now recall that we actually replace
A
by
\beta A+\gamma I
to make it PSD; we must take
\gamma>2\beta
. The contribution of
\beta A
to the operator norm of small submatrices is small, but there is an unavoidable contribution of
\gamma>2\beta
. In the worst case, the covariance of rank-1 Ising models can be bounded only when the norm is at most 1 (consider the Curie–Weiss model), so this places a limit of
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
. Essentially, considering small submatrices can squash the positive side of the spectrum of
A
, enabling an improvement of a factor of 2 from
[
55
]
.

However, the covariance bound (
2.6
) is a factor of 2 loose from what it would be for an unrestricted product distribution, so this only yields entropy contraction for
\beta<\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}
(
Lemma
7.20
). This is exactly the obstruction to attaining
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
.

Attaining
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
using a refined covariance bound (
§
7.3
).
Although (
2.6
) is not true without the factor 2, we note that the covariance is indeed smaller when the total uncertainty
m=\sum_{i}p_{i}(1-p_{i})
between the coordinates is large:
Corollary
7.36
gives that 2 can be replaced by
1+\delta
when
m\geqslant m(\delta)
. This is a corollary of the main result (
Lemma
7.35
) that as
m\to\infty
,
the off-diagonal covariances are asymptotically rank-one:

\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})=-\frac{p_{i}(1-p_{i})p_{j}(1-p_{j})}{m}(1+o(1))

for
\Omega
a slice of
\{0,1\}^{n}
, and the same formula holds for wedges up to an additional factor
c\in[0,1]
.

The proof proceeds through an asymptotic cross-ratio formula for slice probabilities (
Lemma
7.33
) to obtain the asymptotics for the slice. The computation for the wedge is done through conditioning on slices and summing, using a local limit theorem (
Theorem
C.1
) to control slice probabilities and carefully restricting to windows of size
\omega(\sqrt{m})
.

One still has to handle the complementary regime where
m
is small. In this case, only
O(1)
coordinates can have marginals bounded away from
0
and
1
. The structural
Lemma
7.39
shows that if the variance of
\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle
is still too large, then there must exist coordinates
i,j
with
x_{i}<0<x_{j}
whose marginals are both non-extremal. Under the one-dimensional tilt
h\mapsto h+ux
, however, such a pair can remain simultaneously non-extremal only for
u
in an interval of length
O(1)
; see
Lemma
7.40
. Consequently, the HS density
p_{\lambda}
is strongly log-concave except on a set of
u
’s of bounded measure, while everywhere it still has a crude lower curvature bound. The 1-dimensional
Lemma
7.41
then implies that
p_{\lambda}
has bounded variance, which is exactly what the two-stage decomposition needs.

Concentration of localized measure (
§
7.4
).
The remaining ingredient is the concentration of the stochastic-localization posterior. If
y_{t}=tx_{0}+B_{t}
, then each coordinate of
\sign(y_{t})
differs from the corresponding coordinate of
x_{0}
with probability
\Phi(-\sqrt{t})
, independently across coordinates.

Therefore, using a Chernoff bound, taking

T=O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
, and interpreting the localized measure as a posterior, we obtain concentration on a wedge of radius
\varepsilon n
(
Lemma
7.43
), and choosing
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\beta)
small enough to satisfy the deterministic wedge condition completes the proof of
Theorem
7.1
.

Finally, once the restricted measure on the wedge has entropy contraction, efficient sampling and partition function estimation follow by standard arguments (
§
7.5
).

3.
Combining ASL, PHD, and JE: A quantitative analysis

We first introduce the many SDEs that we consider (
§
3.1
) and Jarzynski’s equality (
§
3.2
). Then we introduce the Wasserstein error bound using Grönwall (
§
3.3
), which we apply to bounding the error in the estimated magnetization in ASL. Finally, we give the guarantees of Jarzynski’s equality (
§
3.4
). This will give us sampling guarantees under our desiderata, provided we can also sample from localized distributions after some constant time.

Because we often apply Itô’s lemma to functions that are only defined on
(-1,1)^{n}
, we use the following local version of Itô’s lemma along with the almost surely infinite escape time of ASL-TAP (
Lemma
6.9
).

Lemma 3.1
(Local Itô’s lemma, see e.g.
[
83
, Theorem 3.4.3]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\mathbb{D}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set and let
t_{0}<t_{1}
be real numbers.
Suppose the Itô process
X_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies
X_{t_{0}}\in\mathbb{D}
and also satisfies the SDE

dX_{t}=\mu_{t}dt+\sigma_{t}dB_{t}

with
\mu_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
when
t\in(t_{0},t_{1})
and
X_{t}\in\mathbb{D}
.
Suppose that
f_{t}(x)
is a vector-valued function that is continuously differentiable in
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
and continuously twice-differentiable in
x\in\mathbb{D}
.
Let
T:=\inf\{t\in(t_{0},t_{1}):X_{t}\not\in\mathbb{D}\}
.
Then for
t\in(t_{0},T)
,
f_{t}(X_{t})
is an Itô process satisfying the SDE

df_{t}(X_{t})=[\partial_{t}f_{t}](X_{t})dt+\nabla_{x}f_{t}(X_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}\mu_{t}dt+\nabla_{x}f_{t}(X_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}\sigma_{t}dB_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{x}^{2}f_{t}(X_{t})\boldsymbol{:}\sigma_{t}\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}dt,

where
\boldsymbol{:}
is the tensor contraction
(A\boldsymbol{:}M)_{k}:=\sum_{i,j}A_{i,j,k}M_{i,j}
and the first two coordinates of

Lemma 3.2.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Defining stochastic localization by (

Proof 2
.

.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\beta Am+y+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\log\left({\frac{1+m}{1-m}}\right)}_{\tanh^{-1}(m)}+\beta^{2}\left({1-\frac{1}{n}\left\|{m}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\right)m,

We also note, for the purposes of solving (

:

(3.3)

\displaystyle\nabla_{m}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(m,y)

\displaystyle=-\beta A+D(m)+\beta^{2}\left({1-\frac{\left\|{m}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{n}}\right)I_{n}-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}}=\widehat{Q}(m)^{-1}

By
(d2)

\nabla_{m}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(m,y)=\widehat{Q}(m)^{-1}\succeq\Lambda^{-1}D(m)\succeq\Lambda^{-1}I_{n}
is strongly convex for

. Thus, if (

). Noting

, we conclude the minimum must be attained in

and equal the unique solution to (
TAP
). In
§
D.    Mirror descent for solving the TAP equation
, we show how the solution can be efficiently found using the fixed point iteration of mirror descent.

Remark 3.3
.

Standard results in stochastic analysis imply that the Lipschitz characterization of the dual SDE coefficients in

and

also suffices to estimate

by an Euler-Maruyama discretization of (

We have opted for mirror descent due to the convenience of geometric convergence.

Derivation of ASL/PHA-TAP

Differentiating (
TAP
) with Itô’s formula gives, using (

),

(3.4)

\displaystyle dy

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}(m)dm+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{Tr}\left[{\left({\nabla_{m}^{2}\left({\tanh^{-1}(m_{i})+\beta^{2}\left({1-\frac{\left\|{m}\right\|^{2}}{n}}\right)m_{i}}\right)}\right)\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}}\right]e_{i}dt.

Now

\displaystyle\nabla_{m}^{2}\left({\tanh^{-1}(m_{i})+\beta^{2}\left({1-\frac{\left\|{m}\right\|^{2}}{n}}\right)m_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2m_{i}}{(1-m_{i}^{2})^{2}}e_{i}^{\otimes 2}-\frac{2\beta^{2}m_{i}}{n}I_{n}-\frac{4\beta^{2}m_{i}}{n}e_{i}^{\otimes 2}-\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{2\beta^{2}m_{j}}{n}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}+e_{j}\otimes e_{i})

so the Itô term is

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{m_{i}}{(1-m_{i}^{2})^{2}}(\widehat{Q}(m)^{2})_{ii}e_{i}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|{\widehat{Q}_{j}(m)}\right\|^{2}m-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}m.

This is exactly
f(m)
. Hence enforcing (
TAP
) during the time evolution gives

\displaystyle d\widehat{y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})^{-1}d\widehat{m}_{t}+f(\widehat{m}_{t})dt.

Assuming (

). Assuming (

\widehat{m}_{t}dt+dB_{t}=\widehat{Q}^{-1}(\widehat{m}_{t})d\widehat{m}_{t}+f(\widehat{m}_{t})dt,

and solving for

gives (

).

3.2.
Jarzynski’s equality

Recall that we use Jarzynski’s equality with either (

) or (
PHD-TAP
) to maintain importance weights so that the distribution of
y_{t}
follows
\rho_{t}
satisfying

\displaystyle??.

This is a natural choice of scaffolding distribution because letting
p_{t}=\dist(y_{t})
where
y_{t}
is as in (
SL
), noting that
y_{t}=t\sigma+\sqrt{t}\xi
for
\sigma\sim\mu_{\beta A}
, we expect

to be an approximation of
Z(\beta A,y)
so that

\displaystyle p_{t}(y)

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle\sigma,\beta A\sigma\rangle}e^{-\frac{\left\|{y-t\sigma}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2t}}=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle\sigma,\beta A\sigma\rangle+\left\langle y,\sigma\right\rangle}e^{-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2t}}

\displaystyle=Z(\beta A,y)e^{-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2t}}\approx e^{\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|^{2}}{2t}}\propto\rho_{t}(y).

By
Theorem
2.1
, for any
\rho_{t}
-integrable function
h
we will have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}h(x)\rho_{t}(x)dx=\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{w_{t}}h(y_{t})]}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{w_{t}}]}

in both cases.
5
5

5

Note that
\rho_{0}
is singular so
Theorem
2.1
does not directly apply. See the end of this subsection for the formal limiting argument.
For ASL, we claimed the resulting system is

(ASL-TAP-JE)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} d\widehat{y}_{t}&=\widehat{m}_{t}dt+dB_{t}\\
d\widehat{m}_{t}&=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\left({\widehat{m}_{t}-f(\widehat{m}_{t})}\right)dt+\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}\\
d\widehat{w}_{t}&=\frac{1}{2}\left[{\mathsf{Tr}(\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t}))+\left\|{\widehat{m}_{t}}\right\|^{2}}\right]dt.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

We will let
\omega(m)=\frac{1}{2}\left[{\mathsf{Tr}(\widehat{Q}(m))+\left\|{m}\right\|^{2}}\right]
, so we can write the last equation as
d\widehat{w}_{t}=\omega(\widehat{m}_{t})dt
.
For PHD, write
f_{\text{err}}(m)=f(m)-m
; the resulting system is

(PHD-TAP-JE)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} d\widehat{m}_{t}&=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}\\
d\widehat{y}_{t}&=f(\widehat{m}_{t})dt+dB_{t}\\
d\widehat{w}_{t}&=\left\{{\frac{1}{2}\left[{\mathsf{Tr}(\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t}))+\left\|{\widehat{m}_{t}}\right\|^{2}}\right]+\nabla_{y}\cdot f_{\text{err}}(\widehat{m}(y_{t}))+\left\langle\widehat{m}_{t}-\frac{\widehat{y}_{t}}{t},f_{\text{err}}(\widehat{m}_{t})\right\rangle}\right\}dt.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

We derive these systems of equations below.

Derivation of ASL/PHD-TAP-JE

Note

\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)=\inf_{m}\left\langle y,m\right\rangle-g(m)=:g_{*}(y)\quad\text{where}\quad g(m)=\frac{\beta}{2}\left\langle m,Am\right\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(m_{i})+\frac{\beta^{2}n}{4}\left({1-\frac{1}{n}\left\|{m}\right\|^{2}}\right)^{2},

where we denote
\widehat{m}(y)
as the argmin in the optimization problem, i.e., that solves
y=\nabla g(m)
, so
\widehat{m}(y)=(\nabla g)^{-1}(y)
.
Then because
\widehat{m}(y)=(\nabla g)^{-1}(y)
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}(\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{y}g_{*}(y)=\nabla_{y}(\left\langle y,\widehat{m}(y)\right\rangle-g(\widehat{m}(y)))

(3.5)

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}(y)+D_{y}\widehat{m}(y)\cdot(y-\nabla g(\widehat{m}(y)))=\widehat{m}(y)

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{y}^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y))

\displaystyle=D_{y}((\nabla_{y}g)^{-1}(y))=(D_{y}(\nabla_{y}g)(\widehat{m}(y)))^{-1}=\nabla_{y}^{2}g(\widehat{m}(y))^{-1}=D_{y}\widehat{m}(y)=\widehat{Q}(y)

\displaystyle\Delta_{y}(\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y))

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\widehat{m}(y)=\mathsf{Tr}[\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}(y))].

We now compute the
dw_{t}
equation in

Theorem
2.1
for
\rho_{t}\propto e^{-U_{t}}
where

-U_{t}(y)=\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|^{2}}{2t}
.
We calculate

\displaystyle-\nabla U_{t}(y)

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}(y)-\frac{y}{t}

For (
ASL-TAP
), we take
b_{t}(y)=\frac{1}{2}\left({\widehat{m}(y)+\frac{y}{t}}\right)
and compute

(3.6)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot b_{t}(y)-\left\langle\nabla U_{t}(y),b_{t}(y)\right\rangle-\partial_{t}U_{t}(y)

\displaystyle=\left[{\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Tr}(\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}(y)))+\frac{n}{2t}}\right]+\left\langle\widehat{m}(y)-\frac{y}{t},\frac{1}{2}\left({\widehat{m}(y)+\frac{y}{t}}\right)\right\rangle+\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|^{2}}{2t^{2}}.

Thus (dropping the part of the expression that doesn’t depend on
y
) we obtain that we can take

dw_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\left[{\mathsf{Tr}(\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t}))+\left\|{\widehat{m}_{t}}\right\|^{2}}\right]dt,

giving (
ASL-TAP-JE
).

For (
PHD-TAP-JE
), we note that to match
Theorem
2.1
, we take

b_{t}(y)=\frac{1}{2}\left({\widehat{m}(y)+\frac{y}{t}}\right)+\left({f(\widehat{m}(y))-\widehat{m}(y)}\right).

The last term will give rise to an extra term in
\nabla\cdot b_{t}(y)
and
\left\langle\nabla U_{t}(y),b_{t}(y)\right\rangle
, showing (
PHD-TAP-JE
).

Limiting argument

Although we have existence and uniqueness of (
ASL-TAP-JE
) for all
t\geqslant 0
, formally, we can only apply
Theorem
2.1
on
[s,t]
for
s>0
.
Let
h
be bounded and measurable.
If we initialize (
ASL-TAP-JE
) with
\widehat{x}_{s}\sim\rho_{s}
,
\widehat{w}_{s}=0
, then
Theorem
2.1
gives us

(3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{s}\sim\rho_{s}}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}h(\widehat{x}_{t})}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{s}\sim\rho_{s}}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\rho_{t}}h(x).

Note that
\omega(m)
is bounded; hence
|\widehat{w}_{s}|\leqslant s\max|\omega|\to 0
as
s\to 0
.
Starting at
x_{0}=0
, we have as
s\to 0
that

(3.8)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{0}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}h(\widehat{x}_{t})}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{0}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{0}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}-\widehat{w}_{s}}h(\widehat{x}_{t})}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{0}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}-\widehat{w}_{s}}}=\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{s}\sim\widehat{p}_{s}}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}-\widehat{w}_{s}}h(\widehat{x}_{t})}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{s}\sim\widehat{p}_{s}}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}-\widehat{w}_{s}}}=\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{s}\sim\widehat{p}_{s},\widehat{w}_{s}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}h(\widehat{x}_{t})}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{s}\sim\widehat{p}_{s},\widehat{w}_{s}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}}

where
\widehat{p}_{s}
be the distribution of
\widehat{x}_{s}
started at
x_{0}=0
.
It remains to note that, because the drift term
\widehat{m}_{t}
of the diffusion is uniformly bounded,

\displaystyle\operatorname{TV}(\widehat{p}_{s},\rho_{s})\leqslant\operatorname{TV}(\widehat{p}_{s},\mathcal{N}(0,s))+\operatorname{TV}(\mathcal{N}(0,s),\rho_{s})\to 0

as
s\to 0
, so the difference between the numerators and denominators of (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) goes to 0 as
s\to 0
, as hence we obtain
\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{0}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}h(\widehat{x}_{t})}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widehat{x}_{0}=0}e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\rho_{t}}h(x)
, as needed.

3.3.
Wasserstein bound, with application to TAP error

We first give a Wasserstein bound for general SDE’s with different drift and diffusion terms.
Consider 2 SDE’s

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}dX_{t}&=f_{t}(X_{t})dt+\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})dB_{t}\\
d\widehat{X}_{t}&=\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})dt+\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})dB_{t}\end{split}

where
f,\widehat{f}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\Sigma,\widehat{\Sigma}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are continuous in
t
and
x
. Suppose that
X_{0}=\widehat{X}_{0}
.
We will think of
X_{t}
as an ideal process and
\widehat{X}_{t}
as an approximate process.

Lemma 3.4
(Grönwall bound for Wasserstein error of SDE’s)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that in (
3.9
),
f,\widehat{f},\Sigma,\widehat{\Sigma}
are Lipschitz in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, uniformly over
[0,T]
. Suppose also that for
t\in[0,T]
,

(1)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{f_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{f}_{t}(X_{t})}\right\|^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon_{f}(t)^{2}
and

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(X_{t})}\right\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon_{\Sigma}(t)^{2}
, where
X_{t}
evolves according to the SDE (
3.9
).

(2)

\widehat{f}_{t}
is
L_{f}
-Lipschitz and

\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
is
L_{\Sigma}
-Lipschitz (with respect to the
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{2}
and
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{F}
norms).

Then for
t\in[0,T]
,
with
X_{t}
and
\widehat{X}_{t}
driven by the same Brownian motion in (
3.9
) (i.e. synchronous coupling),

W_{2}(\mathcal{L}(X_{t}),\mathcal{L}(\widehat{X}_{t}))^{2}\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\|{X_{t}-\widehat{X}_{t}}\right\|^{2}}\right]\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}\left({\frac{\sqrt{2}}{L_{f}}\varepsilon_{f}(s)^{2}+2\varepsilon_{\Sigma}(s)^{2}}\right)e^{(2\sqrt{2}L_{f}+2L_{\Sigma}^{2})(t-s)}ds.

Note that (1) the expectations are with respect to the distribution of the ideal process, and (2) the Lipschitz condition is on the approximations
\widehat{f}_{t}
and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}
, not on
f_{t}
and
\Sigma_{t}
.

Proof 3
.

Let
\Delta_{t}:=X_{t}-\widehat{X}_{t}
.
Note

d\Delta_{t}=(f_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t}))dt+(\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t}))dB_{t}.

By Itô’s formula,

\displaystyle d\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\left\langle\Delta_{t},(f_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t}))dt+(\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t}))dB_{t}\right\rangle+\left\|{\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})}\right\|_{F}^{2}dt

\displaystyle\implies\frac{d}{dt}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\Delta_{t},f_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})\right\rangle+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})}\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant c\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{c}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{f_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})}\right\|^{2}+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{X}_{t})}\right\|_{F}^{2}

for any
c>0
.
Now

\displaystyle\left\|{\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{x})-f_{t}(x)}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\left\|{\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{x})-\widehat{f}_{t}(x)}\right\|^{2}+2\left\|{\widehat{f}_{t}(x)-f_{t}(x)}\right\|^{2}\leqslant 2L_{f}^{2}\left\|{\widehat{x}-x}\right\|^{2}+2\left\|{\widehat{f}_{t}(x)-f_{t}(x)}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\left\|{\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{x})-\Sigma_{t}(x)}\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\left\|{\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(\widehat{x})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(x)}\right\|_{F}^{2}+2\left\|{\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(x)-\Sigma_{t}(x)}\right\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant 2L_{\Sigma}^{2}\left\|{\widehat{x}-x}\right\|^{2}+2\left\|{\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(x)-\Sigma_{t}(x)}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

Plugging in gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left({c+\frac{2L_{f}^{2}}{c}+2L_{\Sigma}^{2}}\right)\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{c}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\widehat{f}_{t}(X_{t})-f_{t}(X_{t})}\right\|^{2}+2\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\widehat{\Sigma}_{t}(X_{t})-\Sigma_{t}(X_{t})}\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left({c+\frac{2L_{f}^{2}}{c}+2L_{\Sigma}^{2}}\right)\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{c}\varepsilon_{f}(t)^{2}+2\varepsilon_{\Sigma}(t)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left({2\sqrt{2}L_{f}+2L_{\Sigma}^{2}}\right)\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{L_{f}}\varepsilon_{f}(t)^{2}+2\varepsilon_{\Sigma}(t)^{2}\text{ by taking $c=\sqrt{2}L_{f}$.}

A Grönwall inequality argument then gives

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\Delta_{t}}\right\|^{2}\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}\left({\frac{\sqrt{2}}{L_{f}}\varepsilon_{f}(s)^{2}+2\varepsilon_{\Sigma}(s)^{2}}\right)e^{(2\sqrt{2}L_{f}+2L_{\Sigma}^{2})(t-s)}ds.

We apply this to the ideal coupled SL-HD process and approximate coupled ASL-PHD process,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}dy_{t}=m_{t}dt+dB_{t}&\\
dm_{t}=Q(m_{t})dB_{t}&\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\widehat{y}_{t}=\widehat{m}_{t}dt+dB_{t}&\\
d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t},&\end{cases}

which can be written as

(3.11)

\displaystyle d\begin{pmatrix}{y_{t}}\\
{m_{t}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{m_{t}}\\
{0}\end{pmatrix}dt+\begin{pmatrix}{I}\\
{Q(m_{t})}\end{pmatrix}dB_{t}

\displaystyle d\begin{pmatrix}{\widehat{y}_{t}}\\
{\widehat{m}_{t}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\widehat{m}_{t}}\\
{0}\end{pmatrix}dt+\begin{pmatrix}{I}\\
{\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})}\end{pmatrix}dB_{t}.

This allows us to obtain a Wasserstein bound for ASL-PHD, using just
(d0)
,
(d1)
, and
(d4)
(a).

Corollary 3.5
(
O(1)
Wasserstein bound for ASL-PHD)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that in (
3.10
),
y_{0}=\widehat{y}_{0}
and
m_{0}=\widehat{m}_{0}
and the following hold:

(1)

The solution to (
3.11
) exists and satisfies
\widehat{m}_{t}\in(-1,1)^{n}
for all
t
almost surely.

(2)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{Q(m_{t})-\widehat{Q}(m_{t})}\right\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon(t)^{2}
.

(3)

\widehat{Q}
is
L
-Lipschitz in
(-1,1)^{n}
with respect to
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{F}
.

Then

W_{2}(\mathcal{L}(y_{t}),\mathcal{L}(\widehat{y}_{t}))^{2}\leqslant W_{2}(\mathcal{L}(y_{t},m_{t}),\mathcal{L}(\widehat{y}_{t},\widehat{m}_{t}))^{2}\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}2\varepsilon(s)^{2}e^{(2\sqrt{2}+2L_{\Sigma}^{2})(t-s)}ds.

Proof 4
.

Apply
Lemma
3.4
to (
3.11
) with
L_{f}=1
,
L_{\Sigma}=L
,
\varepsilon_{f}(t)=0
, and
\varepsilon_{\Sigma}(t)=\varepsilon(t)
.

Note, however, that this does not allow us to obtain KL divergence bounds via Girsanov’s Theorem, because in ASL-PHD,
\widehat{m}_{t}
is not a function of
\widehat{y}_{t}
. For KL divergence bounds, we will need
L^{2}
error for ASL-TAP as compared to the ideal process.

Integrating PHD to get ASL/score error

Lemma 3.6
(Error in estimated magnetization/score)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
(d0)
,
(d1)
,
(d2)
,
(d3)
, and
(d4)
hold to time
T
.
Let
y_{t}
denote the SL process, and
m_{t}=\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{y_{t}}
be the true magnetization for tilt
y_{t}
. Let
\widehat{m}(y)
denote the magnetization obtained by solving the TAP equation. Then for each
t\in[0,T]
there is a constant
C(t)
such that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\|{\widehat{m}(y_{t})-m_{t}}\right\|^{2}}\right]\leqslant C(t).

Proof 5
.

Consider the synchronously coupled SDEs

\displaystyle(\textup{PHD-TAP})\quad

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}\\
d\widehat{y}_{t}=f(\widehat{m}_{t})dt+dB_{t}\end{cases}

\displaystyle(\textup{Ideal})\quad

\displaystyle\begin{cases}dm_{t}=Q(m_{t})dB_{t}=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{y_{t}})dB_{t}\\
dy_{t}=m_{t}dt+dB_{t}.\end{cases}

From the desiderata, we have that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|{\widehat{Q}(m_{t})-Q(m_{t})}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=O(1)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|{f(m_{t})-m_{t}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=O(1)

where
(
3.12
) follows from
(d1)
and (
3.13
) follows from
(d3)
.
By
(d4)
, we also see

f(m)
and
\widehat{Q}(m)
are
O(1)
-Lipschitz in
(-1,1)^{n}
.

Thus, with
(d0)
ensuring non-escape from
(-1,1)^{n}
, we can invoke
Lemma
3.4
to get for some constant
C_{1}(t)
that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\left\lVert{(\widehat{m}_{t},\widehat{y}_{t})-(m_{t},y_{t})}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}]\leqslant e^{Ct}-1=:C_{1}(t).

Note
\widehat{m}(y)
has Lipschitz constant
O(1)
as its Jacobian is
\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}(y))
, which is bounded in operator norm by
(d2)
.
Therefore (note
\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{m}(\widehat{y}_{t})
),

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\|{\widehat{m}(y_{t})-m_{t}}\right\|^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\left\|{m_{t}-\widehat{m}_{t}}\right\|^{2}]+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\left\|{\widehat{m}(\widehat{y}_{t})-\widehat{m}(y_{t})}\right\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\left\|{m_{t}-\widehat{m}_{t}}\right\|^{2}]+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\left\|{\widehat{y}_{t}-y_{t}}\right\|^{2}]\lesssim C_{1}(t).

A standard Girsanov argument (see e.g.
[
47
, §4.4]
) then bounds the KL divergence between the SL and ASL-TAP.

Corollary 3.7
(
O(1)
KL error for ASL-TAP)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
(d0)
,
(d1)
,
(d2)
,
(d3)
, and
(d4)
hold to time
T
. Let
y_{t}
and
\widehat{y}_{t}
follow (
SL
) and (
ASL-TAP
), respectively.
Then

\operatorname{KL}(\dist(y_{T})\|\dist(\widehat{y}_{T}))=O(1).

Proof 6
.

Let
P_{T}
and
\widehat{P}_{T}
denote the path measures of
(y_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
and
(\widehat{y}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
.
Since
m,\widehat{m}
are bounded, Novikov’s condition holds:
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\exp\left({\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|{\widehat{m}(y_{t})-m(y_{t})}\right\|^{2}dt}\right)<\infty
.
By the data processing inequality and Girsanov’s Theorem,

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\dist(y_{T})\|\dist(\widehat{y}_{T}))

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{KL}(P_{T}\|\widehat{P}_{T})\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{P_{T}}\log\frac{dP_{T}}{d\widehat{P}_{T}}

\displaystyle=-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\langle m(y_{t})-\widehat{m}(y_{t}),dB_{t}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|{m(y_{t})-\widehat{m}(y_{t})}\right\|^{2}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|{m(y_{t})-\widehat{m}(y_{t})}\right\|^{2}=O(1),

where the last equality follows from
Lemma
3.6
.

3.4.
Error analysis for Jarzynski’s equality

First we state a standard guarantee for rejection sampling, but allowing for error in sampling from the proposal distribution, and in computing the ratio. We also allow unbounded ratio of densities. Note that we do not assume the normalizing constant is known, so we first draw a set of samples, take an appropriate quantile, and then use it to rescale our draws for rejection sampling.
Compared to
[
59
, Lemma 2]
, we use a quantile, rather than a single sample as reference; this is necessary to avoid needing a two-sided bound for the density ratio.

Algorithm 4

Approximate rejection sampler with unknown normalization

1:

Input:
Oracle for sampling from
\widetilde{Q}\approx Q
, (possibly randomized) function
\widetilde{R}\approx R
where
\frac{dP}{dQ}\propto R
, cutoff parameters
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, failure probability
\delta^{\prime}

2:

Draw
N
samples from
\widetilde{Q}
, where
N=\Omega(C_{1}^{2}\log(1/\delta^{\prime}))
for appropriate constant

3:

Let
R_{0}
be the
p
th quantile of the samples, where
p=1-\frac{1}{6C_{1}}
.

4:

repeat

5:

Draw
X\sim\widetilde{Q}
.

6:

Draw
U\sim\mathsf{Uniform}([0,1])
.

7:

until

U\leqslant\frac{\widetilde{R}(X)}{C_{3}R_{0}}
, where
C_{3}=8C_{2}
.

8:

Output:
X
.

Lemma 3.8
(Approximate rejection sampling with unknown normalization)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
Algorithm
4
is run with
\widetilde{Q}
,
\widetilde{R}
, and parameters
C_{1},C_{2}
satisfying the following.

(1)

(Sampling error)
\widetilde{Q}
is a distribution such that
\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{Q},Q)\leqslant\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}
.

(2)

(Tails of ratio)
Let

C(\varepsilon)=\inf_{C}\left\{{C}:{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left({\frac{dP}{dQ}\wedge C}\right)\geqslant 1-\varepsilon}\right\},

and suppose
C_{1}\geqslant C(1/2)\vee 1
,
C_{2}\geqslant C(\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}})
.

(3)

(Ratio error)
Let
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
be events such that

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{{\frac{\widetilde{R}}{R}\in[0,e^{\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}}]}\right\}

\displaystyle G:

\displaystyle=G_{1}\cap G_{2}\subseteq\left\{{\frac{\widetilde{R}}{R}\in[e^{-\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}},e^{\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}}]}\right\}

and
\widetilde{Q}(G_{1}^{c})\leqslant\delta_{1}
,
P(G_{2}^{c})\leqslant\delta_{2}
. (Note these probabilities can involve randomness in the algorithm.)

Then letting
\widehat{P}
be the output distribution, with probability
\geqslant 1-\delta^{\prime}
over the
N
initial samples,

\operatorname{TV}(\widehat{P},P)\leqslant\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}+\delta_{2}+384C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})

and if

\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}+\delta_{2}+96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, the acceptance probability is at least
\frac{1}{192C_{1}C_{2}}
.

Note that in assumption
3
, we consider two different bad events separately. We exclude the estimated ratio being too large under the sampling distribution
\widetilde{Q}
, while we only need to exclude the estimated ratio being too small under
P
. This will be more convenient for us than excluding it under
\widetilde{Q}
, and this is sufficient because it is okay to underestimate the ratio in places where the target distribution
P
has small mass anyways.

Proof 7
.

We proceed in two steps.

Step 1.
We show that with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
that
R_{0}\in\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]\cdot\left[{\frac{1}{8},12C_{1}}\right]
.
For
0\leqslant p\leqslant 1
, let
q(p)
denote the
p
th quantile of the distribution of
\frac{dP}{dQ}
over
Q
.
Suppose
C_{1}
is such that
C_{1}\geqslant 4[C(1/2)\vee 1]
.
Let
L_{1}=q\left({1-\frac{1}{4C_{1}}}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[{\frac{dP}{dQ}\wedge C_{1}}\right]\leqslant L_{1}\left({1-\frac{1}{4C_{1}}}\right)+C_{1}\frac{1}{4C_{1}}

\displaystyle\implies L_{1}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1/4}{1-\frac{1}{4C_{1}}}\geqslant\frac{1}{8}.

Let
L_{2}=q\left({1-\frac{1}{12C_{1}}}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle 1=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[{\frac{dP}{dQ}}\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{12C_{1}}L_{2}\quad\implies\quad L_{2}\leqslant 12C_{1}.

Now if
N=\Omega\left({C_{1}^{2}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}\right)
with appropriate constant, then taking the
1-\frac{1}{6C_{1}}
quantile,
noting moreover that

\widetilde{Q}\left({\frac{\widetilde{R}}{R}\nin[e^{-\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}},e^{\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}}]}\right)\leqslant\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}\leqslant\frac{1}{24C_{1}},

we obtain the result by Hoeffding’s inequality applied to the random variables
\mathds{1}_{X_{i}<q\left({1-\frac{1}{4C_{1}}}\right)}
and
\mathds{1}_{X_{i}>q\left({1-\frac{1}{12C_{1}}}\right)}
for
X_{i}
drawn iid from
\widetilde{Q}
.

Step 2.
We now analyze the rejection sampling. First, we consider the true ratio under the true proposal distribution:
For
C_{3}=8C_{2}
,
C_{3}R_{0}\in\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]\cdot[C_{2},96C_{1}C_{2}]
. Note that
\frac{R}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]}=\frac{dP}{dQ}
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[{\frac{R}{C_{3}R_{0}}\wedge 1}\right]

(3.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{3}R_{0}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}\left[{\frac{dP}{dQ}\wedge\frac{C_{3}R_{0}}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]}}\right]

(3.15)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{3}R_{0}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R](1-\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}})\geqslant\frac{1}{96C_{1}C_{2}}(1-\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}),

where we use
\frac{C_{3}R_{0}}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}R}\geqslant C_{2}\geqslant C(\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}})
in (
3.14
).
Therefore, for any measurable
A\subseteq\Omega
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}]\geqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R\mathds{1}_{A}]-\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R].

Next, we consider the approximate ratio under the approximate proposal distribution.
Note that

(3.17)

\displaystyle\widehat{P}(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}[(\widetilde{R}\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}]}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}[\widetilde{R}\wedge C_{3}R_{0}]}

(3.18)

\displaystyle P(A)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R\mathds{1}_{A}]}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]}.

Note also that

(3.19)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{1}_{G_{2}^{c}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R\mathds{1}_{G_{2}^{c}}]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]P(G_{2}^{c})\leqslant\delta_{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]

Using
\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{Q},Q)\leqslant\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}
, we have that the numerator in (
3.17
) is

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}[(\widetilde{R}\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}]

\displaystyle\in_{\eqref{i:ert}}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}(Re^{-\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}}\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{1}_{G},\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}(Re^{\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}}\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{1}_{G_{1}}+C_{3}R_{0}\delta_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{1}_{G}-C_{3}R_{0}\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}},\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{1}_{G_{1}}+C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}})}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq_{\eqref{i:ert}}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{1}_{G_{2}}-C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}+C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}})}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq_{\eqref{i:esm}}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{1}_{G_{2}}-C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}),\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}+C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq_{\eqref{e:ars-de2}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}(R\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}+\left[{-\delta_{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]-C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}),C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq_{\eqref{e:S>C}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}R\mathds{1}_{A}+\left[{-(\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}+\delta_{2})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]-C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}),C_{3}R_{0}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}\right]

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}R\mathds{1}_{A}+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]\cdot\left[{-\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}-\delta_{2}-96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}),96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}\right].

Hence

\displaystyle\left|{\widehat{P}(A)-P(A)}\right|=\left|{\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}[(\widetilde{R}\wedge C_{3}R_{0})\mathds{1}_{A}]}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}[\widetilde{R}\wedge C_{3}R_{0}]}-\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R\mathds{1}_{A}]}{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]}}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\max\left\{{P(A)-\frac{P(A)-\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}-\delta_{2}-96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}{1+96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})},\frac{P(A)+96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}{1-96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}-P(A),1}\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}+\delta_{2}+384C(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}}),

where the last inequality follows using
\frac{p+\varepsilon_{1}}{1-\varepsilon_{2}}\wedge 1\leqslant p+2\varepsilon_{1}+2\varepsilon_{2}
if
p\leqslant 1
(split into cases based on whether
\varepsilon_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
).
Taking a maximum over measurable
A
gives the TV bound. Finally, the acceptance probability equals

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}\left[{\frac{\widetilde{R}}{C_{3}R_{0}}\wedge 1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{3}R_{0}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{Q}}[\widetilde{R}\wedge C_{3}R_{0}]\geqslant\frac{1}{C_{3}R_{0}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[R]\cdot\left({1-\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}-\delta_{2}-96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{96C_{1}C_{2}}\left({1-\varepsilon_{\textup{weight}}-\delta_{2}-96C_{1}C_{2}(\delta_{1}+\varepsilon_{\textup{ratio}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{sample}})}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{192C_{1}C_{2}}.

where we use (
3.15
) in the last line.

For Jarzynski’s equality, we give TV sampling guarantees with a number of draws that is exponential in the total drift error. In addition to a bound on the drift error, we need an exponential upper tail and a lower tail for the log-weights. Note that these are over the algorithmic process.

There are 3 distributions in our analysis: the target measure
p_{t}
of the ideal process, the measure
\widehat{p}_{t}
of the approximate process, and the scaffolding measure
\rho_{t}
with JE targets. Samples from
\widehat{p}_{T}
are reweighted by
e^{w_{T}}
to approximate the measure
\rho_{T}
, because we have an explicit expression for
\rho_{T}
but not
\widehat{p}_{T}
. Then we can conduct rejection sampling with ratio
\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}
(or an approximation of this ratio) to obtain (approximate) samples from
p_{T}
.

Lemma 3.9
(Jarzynski’s equality with rejection sampling)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
f_{t}
,
\widehat{f}_{t}
are Lipschitz in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
uniformly over
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.20)

\displaystyle dx_{t}=f_{t}(x_{t})dt+dB_{t},

\displaystyle x_{0}\sim p_{0}

(3.21)

\displaystyle d\widehat{x}_{t}=\widehat{f}_{t}(\widehat{x}_{t})dt+dB_{t}

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{0}\sim p_{0}

and define
\widehat{w}_{t}((\widehat{x}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]})
for
t\in[0,T]
by

(3.22)

\displaystyle d\widehat{w}_{t}=\omega_{t}(\widehat{x}_{t})dt,

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{0}=0

where (
3.21
) and (
3.22
)
is a SDE-ODE system arising from Jarzynski’s equality applied to
\rho_{t}
, where
\rho_{0}=p_{0}
.
Let
p_{t}
,
\widehat{p}_{t}
be the distribution of
x_{t}
,
\widehat{x}_{t}
, respectively.
Below,
\widehat{w}_{t}=\widehat{w}_{t}((\widehat{x}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]})
unless specified otherwise.
Let
Z_{t}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}]
.
Suppose

(1)

(Drift error)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\left\|{\widehat{f}_{t}(x_{t})-f_{t}(x_{t})}\right\|^{2}}\right]\leqslant\varepsilon(t)^{2}
.

(2)

(Exponential upper tail for log-weights)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}\mathds{1}_{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}/Z_{T}\geqslant C_{1}}}\right]\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}Z_{T}
, for
\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{c_{3}\varepsilon}{6\exp\left({\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left({\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon(t)^{2}dt+1}\right)}\right)}
.

(3)

(Lower tail for log-weights)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\leqslant c_{3}}\right]\leqslant\varepsilon_{3}
, for
\varepsilon_{3}=\frac{\varepsilon}{6\exp\left({\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left({\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon(t)^{2}dt+1}\right)}\right)}
.

Then for
L=\exp\left({\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left({\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon(t)^{2}dt+1}\right)}\right)
,
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\wedge\frac{2C_{1}L}{c_{3}}}\right]\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
, and given query access to
\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}
, we can sample from
p_{T}
with TV error
2\varepsilon
by
O\left({\frac{C_{1}^{2}L^{2}\log\left({\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)}{c_{3}^{2}}}\right)
simulations of (
3.21
), by choosing the parameters
C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{2C_{1}L}{c_{3}}
and using the estimate

e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})

in
Algorithm
4
.
Suppose moreover that
(\widetilde{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a process and
\widetilde{w}_{T}((x_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})
are weights such that

(4)

(Path error)

\operatorname{TV}((\widehat{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},(\widetilde{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})\leqslant\varepsilon_{4}
, where
\varepsilon_{4}=\frac{\varepsilon}{384C_{1}^{\prime 2}}
.

(5)

(Weight error)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({|\widetilde{w}_{T}((\widetilde{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})-\widehat{w}_{T}((\widetilde{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})|>\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{2}}\right)\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{2}
.

(6)

(Ratio error)

\widetilde{R}_{2}(x)
is a (randomized) estimate such that there is
R_{2}(x)
with
\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}\propto R_{2}
with

\displaystyle(a)

\displaystyle\forall x,\quad\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({\frac{\widetilde{R}_{2}(x_{T})}{R_{2}(x_{T})}\nin[0,e^{\varepsilon_{4}/2}]\Big|x_{T}=x}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{2}

\displaystyle(b)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({\frac{\widetilde{R}_{2}(x_{T})}{R_{2}(x_{T})}\nin[e^{-\varepsilon_{4}/2},e^{\varepsilon_{4}/2}]}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon

Then we can sample from
p_{T}
with TV error
6\varepsilon
by
O\left({\frac{C_{1}^{2}L^{2}\log\left({\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)}{c_{3}^{2}}}\right)
simulations of (
3.21
) using the estimate
e^{\widetilde{w}_{T}((\widetilde{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})}\widetilde{R}_{2}(\widetilde{x}_{T})

in
Algorithm
4
.

Note for the ratio estimate, in addition for having a 2-sided bound with high probability over the target distribution,
we need that for any point
x
, it is with high probability not much of an overestimate.

Proof 8
.

Note first that from Jarzynski’s equality,

\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{\widehat{w}_{t}}|\widehat{x}_{t}]}{Z_{t}}=\frac{d\rho_{t}}{d\widehat{p}_{t}}(\widehat{x}_{t}).

Define the good sets (1) the ratio between the ideal and approximate distribution is bounded, (2) the ratio between the scaffolding and approximate distribution is not too small, and (3) the weights are bounded,

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\frac{dp_{T}}{d\widehat{p}_{T}}\leqslant L}\right\}

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}|\widehat{x}_{T}]}{Z_{T}}=\frac{d\rho_{T}}{d\widehat{p}_{T}}\geqslant\frac{c_{3}}{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle G_{3}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\leqslant C_{1}}\right\}

and let their complements be
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}
, respectively.
Note
G_{1},G_{2}
are
x_{T}
-measureable and
G_{3}
is
\widehat{w}_{T}
-measurable.
Now

(3.23)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{B_{1}\cup B_{2}\cup B_{3}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}}\right]+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{t}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{G_{1}\cap B_{2}}}\right]+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{t}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{G_{1}\cap G_{2}\cap B_{3}}}\right].

We bound each term separately.
Note that if
\mu\ll\nu
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\mu\|\nu)

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left({\log\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}}\right)\mathds{1}_{\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}\geqslant 1}+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left({\log\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}}\right)\mathds{1}_{\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}<1}\geqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left({\log\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}}\right)\mathds{1}_{\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}\geqslant 1}-1\geqslant\mu\left({\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}\geqslant L}\right)(\log L)-1

(3.24)

\displaystyle\implies\mu\left({\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}\geqslant L}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\operatorname{KL}(\mu\|\nu)+1}{\log L}.

For the first term,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}\frac{dp_{t}}{d\rho_{t}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}}{Z_{T}}|\widehat{x}_{T}}\right]\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{d\rho_{T}}{d\widehat{p}_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}}\right]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\frac{dp_{T}}{d\widehat{p}_{T}}(\widehat{x}_{T})\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathds{1}_{B_{1}}(x_{T}^{*})}\right]\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left[{\frac{dp_{T}}{d\widehat{p}_{T}}(x_{T})>L}\right]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left[{\log\frac{dp_{T}}{d\widehat{p}_{T}}(x_{T})>\log L}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\eqref{e:kl-markov}}\frac{\operatorname{KL}(p_{T}\|\widehat{p}_{T})+1}{\log L}\leqslant\frac{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon(t)^{2}dt+1}{\log L}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{3}

by assumption 1 and Girsanov’s theorem and choosing
L=\exp\left({\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left({\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon(t)^{2}dt+1}\right)}\right)
.
Next, note that by assumption 3, since
\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}|\widehat{x}_{T}]}{Z_{T}}\leqslant\frac{c_{3}}{2}
implies

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left[{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}\geqslant c_{3}Z_{T}|\widehat{x}_{T}}\right]\leqslant\frac{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}|\widehat{x}_{T}]}{c_{3}Z_{T}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
and hence

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left[{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}\leqslant c_{3}Z_{T}|\widehat{x}_{T}}\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{3}\geqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left[{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}\leqslant c_{3}Z_{T}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left[{e^{\widehat{w}_{T}}\leqslant c_{3}Z_{T}|\widehat{x}_{T}}\right]}\right]
