and define for
s\in[0,1]

u_{p}^{(a,b)}(s):=\nu_{s}\big(|\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}|^{p}\big).

Note that the replica indices are
always
exchangeable, and so it suffices to
bound a single pair
u_{p}(s):=\nu_{s}(|\overline{R}^{-}_{12}|^{p})
. Applying
Lemma
5.6
with
k=2
to the function
F:=|\overline{R}^{-}_{12}|^{p}
gives

\displaystyle u_{p}^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=\beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}\!\left(F\,\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{12}\right)-2\beta^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{2}\nu_{s}\!\left(F\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{3}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{\ell,3}\right)+3\beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}\!\left(F\,\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{3,4}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta^{2}\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{2}\nu_{s}\!\left(F\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{\ell}\right)-2\nu_{s}\!\left(F\,\varepsilon^{3}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{3}\right)\right].

Taking absolute values term-by-term and using (
5.20
) is sufficient to get the Lipschitz bound on the derivative. For the first two overlap terms, note that

\displaystyle\Big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{12})\Big|

\displaystyle=\nu_{s}\big(|\overline{R}^{-}_{12}|^{p+1}\big)\leqslant 2\,\nu_{s}\big(|\overline{R}^{-}_{12}|^{p}\big)=2u_{p}(s)\,,

\displaystyle\Big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{3}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{\ell,3})\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\overline{R}^{-}_{\ell,3}\|_{\infty}\,\nu_{s}(F)\leqslant 2u_{p}(s)\,,

when
\ell\in\{1,2\}
. Since there are two such terms and each has coefficient
2\beta^{2}
, these contribute at most
8\beta^{2}u_{p}(s)
.
The final overlap term satisfies the bound,

\Big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{3,4})\Big|\leqslant 2u_{p}(s)\,,

so the contribution is at most
6\beta^{2}u_{p}(s)
. Similarly, the magnetization terms can be bounded as

\Big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{\ell})\Big|\leqslant 2u_{p}(s),\qquad\Big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{3}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{3})\Big|\leqslant 2u_{p}(s),

so the magnetization bracket contributes at most

2\beta^{2}\big(2u_{p}(s)+2u_{p}(s)\big)=8\beta^{2}u_{p}(s)
.

Putting all the bounds together yields that,

|u_{p}^{\prime}(s)|\leqslant(2+8+6+8)\beta^{2}\,u_{p}(s)=24\beta^{2}\,u_{p}(s).

By Grönwall’s inequality,

u_{p}(s)\leqslant e^{24\beta^{2}(1-s)}u_{p}(1)\leqslant e^{24\beta^{2}}u_{p}(1)\leqslant_{\text{\eqref{eq:stab-assume-endpoint}}}C^{(R)}(\beta,p)O(n^{-p/2})\,.

Stability of the magnetization along the interpolation.
Fix
a
and set
v_{p}(s):=\nu_{s}(|\overline{M}^{-}_{1}|^{p})
(using replica symmetry again). Applying
Lemma
5.6
(again) with
k=1
and
F:=|\overline{M}^{-}_{1}|a^{p}
gives

v_{p}^{\prime}(s)=-\beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{1,2})+\beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{2,3})+\beta^{2}\Big(\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{1}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{1})-\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{2})\Big).

Using (
5.20
) gives the following bounds for the overlap terms,

\big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{1,2})\big|\leqslant 2v_{p}(s),\qquad\big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}\,\overline{R}^{-}_{2,3})\big|\leqslant 2v_{p}(s),

and the following bounds for the magnetization terms

\big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{1}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{1})\big|=\nu_{s}(|\overline{M}^{-}_{1}|^{p+1})\leqslant 2v_{p}(s),\qquad\big|\nu_{s}(F\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\overline{M}^{-}_{2})\big|\leqslant 2v_{p}(s).

Therefore,

|v_{p}^{\prime}(s)|\leqslant(2+2+2+2)\beta^{2}v_{p}(s)=8\beta^{2}v_{p}(s),

and Grönwall yields
v_{p}(s)\leqslant e^{8\beta^{2}}v_{p}(1)\leqslant_{\text{\eqref{eq:stab-assume-endpoint}}}C^{(M)}(\beta,p)O(n^{-p/2})
. ∎

We now show that, akin to the statement of
Proposition
5.21
showing that odd cubic moments of
f
and
\widehat{f}
are
O(4)
which defeats naive concentration by
O(1)
, a similar thing happens for the magnetization and overlap fluctuations at
s=1
. This is critical in the evaluation of the “self-similar” terms in the proof of
Proposition
5.23
.

Lemma 5.8
(Overlap and magnetization biases are
O(n^{-1})
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Fix
t\in[0,T]
and
0<\beta<1/2
. Let

\delta q_{n}:=\nu(R_{12})-q^{*},\qquad\delta m_{n}:=\nu(M_{1})-m^{*}.

Assume the hypotheses
(H1)–(H3)
, and let
Y^{*}
be the cavity field and set

\mu_{r}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\tanh^{r}(Y^{*})],\qquad r\in\{1,2,3,4\}\,,

and

\mathbf{M}:=\begin{pmatrix}1-4\mu_{2}+3\mu_{4}&2(\mu_{1}-\mu_{3})\\
\mu_{3}-\mu_{1}&1-\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Then, the following hold:

(1)

The deviations satisfy the stability equation

(5.21)

\begin{pmatrix}\delta q_{n}\\
\delta m_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\beta^{2}\,\mathbf{M}\begin{pmatrix}\delta q_{n}\\
\delta m_{n}\end{pmatrix}\;+\;O(n^{-1}),

where the
O(n^{-1})
is uniform in
n
.

(2)

Furthermore, if
\rho(\beta^{2}\mathbf{M})<1
, then

\delta q_{n}=O(n^{-1}),\qquad\delta m_{n}=O(n^{-1}).

Proof.

We will use exchangebility and last-site decoupling to set up a “self-consistent” linear system described by a specific operator
M
. Provided that
\beta^{2}\rho(M)<1
, it will be the case that the fluctuations of the mean and overlap are
O(n^{-1/2})
stronger than predicted by naive concentration. Invoking
Lemma
5.10
(via Grönwall) will then immediately imply this is true at any
s\in[0,1]
.

Reduce
\nu(R_{12})
and
\nu(M_{1})
to last-spin observables via exchangeability.
By site-exchangeability of the disorder and the Gibbs measure,

\nu(R_{12})=\nu(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad\nu(M_{1})=\nu(\varepsilon^{1}).

This yields that

\delta q_{n}=\nu(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})-q^{*},\qquad\delta m_{n}=\nu(\varepsilon^{1})-m^{*}.

Two-term Taylor expansions around
s=0
.
Applying the exact two-term Taylor identity for
F\in\{\varepsilon^{1},\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\}
yields that

\nu(F)=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu^{\prime}_{0}(F)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\,\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,ds.

Second-order remainder bounds of
O(n^{-1})
.
Fix
F\in\{\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2},\varepsilon^{1}\}
and note
|F|\leqslant 1
. Differentiating twice using
Lemma
5.6
yields a finite linear combination of terms where every summand in
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)
is of the form

\nu_{s}\left(F\;\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)\;\Xi_{1}\Xi_{2}\right),\qquad\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}\in\{\,R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*},\,M^{-}_{a}-m^{*}\,\},

where
\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)
is a bounded polynomial in finitely many last spins (hence
|\mathcal{P}|\leqslant C
).
Thus, by Cauchy–Schwarz and boundedness of
F\mathcal{P}
,

\Big|\nu_{s}\!\big(F\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)\Xi_{1}\Xi_{2}\big)\Big|\leqslant C\;\|\Xi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\;\|\Xi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}.

By
(H1)–(H2)
and
Lemma
5.7
, we have
\|\Xi_{i}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2})
.
Therefore, we obtain that

\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\,\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,ds=O(n^{-1})\,,

for
F=\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}
and
F=\varepsilon^{1}
.

Evaluating
\nu_{0}(F)
for
\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}
and
\varepsilon^{1}
.
At
s=0
,
Lemma
5.5
implies that the last-spin replicas are conditionally i.i.d. given the cavity field
Y^{*}
. This immediately yields,

\displaystyle\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1})=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\tanh(Y^{*})]=\mu_{1}=m^{*}\,,

and

\displaystyle\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\tanh^{2}(Y^{*})]=\mu_{2}=q^{*}\,.

Evaluating
\nu^{0}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})
via exact symmetries.
Applying
Lemma
5.6
with
k=2
and
F=\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}
gives the following linear combination of terms, evaluated at
s=0
by the factorization implied by
Lemma
5.5
.
With
k=2
, the “SK overlap” part is given by

(SK)

\displaystyle=\ \beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}\left(F\,\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\,(R^{-}_{12}-q^{*})\right)-2\beta^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{2}\nu_{s}\left(F\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{3}\,(R^{-}_{\ell,3}-q^{*})\right)+3\beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}\left(F\,\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4}\,(R^{-}_{34}-q^{*})\right).

With
F=\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}
, the last-spin products simplify:

F\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}=1
,

F\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{3}=\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}
,

F\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}=\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{3}
,

F\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4}=\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4}
.
Using these explicit simplifications for the last spin terms along with
Lemma
5.5
at
s=0
, gives

\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon^{i_{r}}X)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\tanh^{r}(Y^{*})]\;\nu_{0}(X)=\mu_{r}\,\nu_{0}(X).

Hence, using replica symmetry for the bulk overlaps under
\nu_{0}
,

\nu_{0}(R^{-}_{12}-q^{*})=\nu_{0}(R^{-}_{13}-q^{*})=\nu_{0}(R^{-}_{34}-q^{*})=:\delta q_{n}^{-},

we obtain

\text{(SK)}\big|_{s=0}=\beta^{2}\Big(1-4\mu_{2}+3\mu_{4}\Big)\,\delta q_{n}^{-}.

A similar calculation for the “CW drift” part gives that, with
k=2
,

\text{(CW)}\ =\ \beta^{2}\Big(\nu_{s}(F\varepsilon^{1}(M^{-}_{1}-m^{*}))+\nu_{s}(F\varepsilon^{2}(M^{-}_{2}-m^{*}))-2\nu_{s}(F\varepsilon^{3}(M^{-}_{3}-m^{*}))\Big).

With
F=\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}
, we have

F\varepsilon^{1}=\varepsilon^{2}
,
F\varepsilon^{2}=\varepsilon^{1}
,
F\varepsilon^{3}=\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}
.
At
s=0
, factorization yields

\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{2}(M^{-}_{1}-m^{*}))=\mu_{1}\,\nu_{0}(M^{-}_{1}-m^{*}),\quad\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}(M^{-}_{2}-m^{*}))=\mu_{1}\,\nu_{0}(M^{-}_{2}-m^{*}),

\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}(M^{-}_{3}-m^{*}))=\mu_{3}\,\nu_{0}(M^{-}_{3}-m^{*}).

By replica symmetry of the bulk magnetizations under
\nu_{0}
, one obtains

\delta m_{n}^{-}:=\nu_{0}(M^{-}_{1}-m^{*})=\nu_{0}(M^{-}_{2}-m^{*})=\nu_{0}(M^{-}_{3}-m^{*})\,,

which gives the simplifications at
s=0
as

\text{(CW)}\big|_{s=0}=2\beta^{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{3})\,\delta m_{n}^{-}.

Combining these two gives the final bound

(5.22)

\nu^{0}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})=\beta^{2}\Big(1-4\mu_{2}+3\mu_{4}\Big)\,\delta q_{n}^{-}\;+\;2\beta^{2}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{3})\,\delta m_{n}^{-}.

Evaluating
\nu^{0}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1})
via exact symmetries.
Similar to the overlap case, applying
Lemma
5.6
with
k=1
and
F=\varepsilon^{1}
yields

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}_{s}(\varepsilon^{1})

\displaystyle=-\beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}\left(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}(R^{-}_{12}-q^{*})\right)+\beta^{2}\,\nu_{s}\left(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}(R^{-}_{23}-q^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta^{2}\Big(\nu_{s}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{1}(M^{-}_{1}-m^{*}))-\nu_{s}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}(M^{-}_{2}-m^{*}))\Big).

Simplify
\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}=\varepsilon^{2}
and
\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{1}=1
.
At
s=0
, factorization via
Lemma
5.5
yields

\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{2}(R^{-}_{12}-q^{*}))=\mu_{1}\,\delta q_{n}^{-},\qquad\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{3}(R^{-}_{23}-q^{*}))=\mu_{3}\,\delta q_{n}^{-},

\nu_{0}(M^{-}_{1}-m^{*})=\delta m_{n}^{-},\qquad\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}(M^{-}_{2}-m^{*}))=\mu_{2}\,\delta m_{n}^{-}.

Thus

(5.23)

\nu^{\prime}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1})=\beta^{2}(\mu_{3}-\mu_{1})\,\delta q_{n}^{-}\;+\;\beta^{2}(1-\mu_{2})\,\delta m_{n}^{-}.

Proving
\delta q_{n}^{-},\delta m_{n}^{-}
equivalent to
\delta q_{n},\delta m_{n}
up to
O(n^{-1})
.
The goal is to show that

(5.24)

\delta q_{n}^{-}=\delta q_{n}+O(n^{-1}),\qquad\delta m_{n}^{-}=\delta m_{n}+O(n^{-1}).

First, note that

R_{12}=R^{-}_{12}+\frac{1}{n}\,\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2},\qquad M_{1}=M^{-}_{1}+\frac{1}{n}\,\varepsilon^{1}.

Taking
\nu
and using
|\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}|\leqslant 1
gives

\nu(R^{-}_{12})=\nu(R_{12})+O(n^{-1})=\nu(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})+O(n^{-1}),\qquad\nu(M^{-}_{1})=\nu(M_{1})+O(n^{-1})=\nu(\varepsilon^{1})+O(n^{-1}).

It remains to compare
\nu_{0}(\cdot)
with
\nu(\cdot)
on centered bulk observables by showing stability along the cavity interpolation. Let
H\in\{R^{-}_{12}-q^{*},M^{-}_{1}-m^{*}\}
and write

\nu(H)-\nu_{0}(H)=\int_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime}_{s}(H)\,ds.

Applying
Lemma
5.6
to
H
shows that every term in
\nu^{\prime}_{s}(H)
contains at least one additional
centered bulk deviation factor
\Xi\in\{R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*},M^{-}_{a}-m^{*}\}
. An application of
(2,2)
-Hölder’s then yields

\nu_{s}(H\Xi)\leqslant\sqrt{\nu_{s}(H^{2})}\sqrt{\nu_{s}(\Xi^{2})}\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:stab-dev-along-s]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:stab-dev-along-s}}}}O(n^{-1})\,.

This implies that

\ \delta q_{n}=\nu(R_{12})-q^{*}=\nu(R^{-}_{12}-q^{*})+O(n^{-1})=\nu_{0}(R^{-}_{12}-q^{*})+O(n^{-1})=\delta q^{-}_{n}+O(n^{-1})\,,

and similarly that

\delta m_{n}=\nu(M_{1})-m^{*}=\delta m^{-}_{n}+O(n^{-1})\,.

yielding (
5.24
).

Finalizing the self-consistent linear-system.
Combining the second-order Taylor expansion with the time
s=0
estimates yields

\nu(R_{12})=\nu(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})=\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})+\nu^{0}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})+O(n^{-1})=\mu_{2}+\nu^{0}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})+O(n^{-1}),

\nu(M_{1})=\nu(\varepsilon^{1})=\nu_{0}(\varepsilon^{1})+\nu^{0}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1})+O(n^{-1})=\mu_{1}+\nu^{0}_{0}(\varepsilon^{1})+O(n^{-1}).

Insert (
5.22
) and (
5.23
) into the above, and use (
5.24
) to replace
\delta q_{n}^{-},\delta m_{n}^{-}

by
\delta q_{n},\delta m_{n}
up to
O(n^{-1})
factors. Since
\mu_{2}=q^{*}
and
\mu_{1}=m^{*}
, we obtain the following self-consistent equations

\begin{pmatrix}\delta q_{n}\\
\delta m_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\beta^{2}\begin{pmatrix}1-4\mu_{2}+3\mu_{4}&2(\mu_{1}-\mu_{3})\\
\mu_{3}-\mu_{1}&1-\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta q_{n}\\
\delta m_{n}\end{pmatrix}+O(n^{-1})\,,

which prove (
5.21
). Furthermore, assuming
\rho(\beta^{2}\mathbf{M})<1
, multiply by
(I-\beta^{2}\mathbf{M})^{-1}
to conclude

\begin{pmatrix}\delta q_{n}\\
\delta m_{n}\end{pmatrix}=O(n^{-1})\,.\qed

Remark 5.9
.

Note that the linear deviation bound for the magnetization and overlaps above relies on the fact that
\rho(\beta^{2}\mathbf{M})<1
to allow for the invertibility of the underlying linear system. For
\beta<1/2
, the bound is proved in
Lemma
5.31
. Therefore, for the remainder of the section (and paper), the bound in
Lemma
5.8
is used without further qualification.

Below we prove a stability result for bounded functions
F
of finite numbers of replicas along the cavity interpolation. The results below essentially use Grönwall’s lemma for propagating stability across the interpolation, invoking
Lemma
5.7
to use uniform concentration for the bulk deviation parameters.

Lemma 5.10
(Stability of bounded continuous functions along the cavity interpolation)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Fix the number of replicas
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
F:\Sigma^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded function of

(\rho^{1},\varepsilon^{1}),\dots,(\rho^{k},\varepsilon^{k})
. Assume that

(5.25)

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\ \max_{a<b\leqslant k+2}\ \big\|R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*}\big\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}\;+\;\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\ \max_{a\leqslant k+1}\ \big\|M^{-}_{a}-m^{*}\big\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}\;\leqslant\;C_{p,k}\,n^{-1/2}.

Then, the following two stability statements hold for
F
along the cavity interpolation,

(1)

Small perturbation for bulk functions
: For bulk functions the following holds

(5.26)

\big|\nu_{s}(F)-\nu_{1}(F)\big|\;\leqslant\;C_{\beta,k}\,n^{-1/2}\int_{s}^{1}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{u})}\,du\;\leqslant\;C_{\beta,k}\,n^{-1/2}\,\sup_{u\in[s,1]}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{u})}.

In particular, if
\sup_{u\in[0,1]}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{u})}=O(n^{-\alpha})
, then

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\big|\nu_{s}(F)-\nu_{1}(F)\big|=O\big(n^{-(\alpha+1/2)}\big).

(2)

Grönwall stability
: Via Grönwall’s inequality,

(5.27)

\big|\nu_{t}(F)-\nu_{1}(F)\big|\;\leqslant\;\Big(e^{4\beta^{2}r(r+1)(1-t)}-1\Big)\,\nu_{1}(F).

Proof.

We prove both statements in chronological order.

Small perturbation for bulk functions.
Applying
Lemma
5.6
to
F
and noting that
|\varepsilon^{\ell}|=1
, implies that each term in
\nu_{s}^{\prime}(F)
can be bounded (up to absolute constants) by

\varepsilon
factors in absolute value. This gives, via a
(2,2)
-Hölder’s inequality,

\big|\nu_{s}\big(F\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})\big)\big|\leqslant\nu_{s}\big(|F|\,|R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*}|\big)\leqslant\|F\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\,\|R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\,.

The same bound holds for the terms involving
M^{-}_{a}-m^{*}
. Since the ultimate bound on the derivative is a finite linear combination of these bulk deviation terms,

|\nu_{s}^{\prime}(F)|\leqslant\beta^{2}\,C_{k}\,\|F\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\Big(\max_{a<b\leqslant k+2}\|R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}+\max_{a\leqslant k+1}\|M^{-}_{a}-m^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\Big)\leqslant C(\beta,k)n^{-1/2}\,.

(
5.26
) then immediately follows from FTOC, yielding that
\nu_{s}(F)-\nu_{1}(F)=\int_{s}^{1}\nu_{u}^{\prime}(F)\,du
.

Gronwall stability.
The proof here is the same as above, except the use of
(2,2)
-Hölder’s inequality, which is substituted by the fact that
|\Xi\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}|\leqslant C
. Therefore, summing over the terms after the application of
Lemma
5.6
gives that

|\nu^{\prime}_{s}(F)|\leqslant C(\beta,k)\nu_{s}(F)\,.

An application of Grönwall’s inequality immediately gives
(
5.27
)
.
∎

A corollary of this is that the deviation of the mean and the overlap is
O(n^{-1})
for any
s\in[0,1]
.

Corollary 5.11
(Mean and overlap deviation along the cavity interpolation)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

The following deviation scales hold at any
s\in[0,1]
,

\nu_{s}(R_{12}-q^{*})=O(n^{-1})\,,

and

\nu_{s}(M_{1}-m^{*})=O(n^{-1})\,.

Proof 20
.

The proof follows by combining
Lemma
5.8
with
Lemma
5.10
, specifically (
5.27
).

Analogues to the “rectangular sum” lemma in
[
109
, §1.8]

At
s=0
, there is an exact factorization
(
Lemma
5.5
) with the RS field

Y^{*}:=y_{t,n}+\beta^{2}m^{*}+\beta\sqrt{q^{*}}\,Z,

and the following constants show up in the scaling

(5.28)

\rho_{2}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sech^{2}(Y^{*})],\qquad\rho_{4}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sech^{4}(Y^{*})].

Finally, let
\nu_{1}=\nu
(the true model), and define the following “centered” second moments:

U_{n}:=\nu(f^{2}),\qquad V_{n}:=\nu(f\widehat{f}),\qquad W_{n}:=\nu(\widehat{f}^{2})\,.

A trivial consequence of
(H1)
and
(H2)
for the planted SK model under the SL external field is:

(5.29)

\nu\big(|R_{1,2}-q^{*}|^{2p}\big)\leqslant C_{p}\,n^{-p},\qquad\nu\big(|M-m^{*}|^{2p}\big)\leqslant C_{p}\,n^{-p},\qquad M:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}.

Define the bulk rectangular sum

f^{-}:=R^{-}_{1,3}-R^{-}_{1,4}-R^{-}_{2,3}+R^{-}_{2,4}.

Lastly, the derivative of the (expected) Gibbs average at time
s=0
is given as

\nu^{0}_{0}(f):=\frac{d}{ds}\nu_{s}(f)|_{s=0}=\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\frac{\nu_{s}(f)-\nu_{0}(f)}{s}\,.

Lemma 5.12
(Unscaled rectangular cancellation
[
109
, Extension of Lemma 1.8.4]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
F^{-}
be any bounded function of the bulk spins in replicas
1,2,3,4
and independent of

(\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{4})
. Then

\nu_{0}^{0}\Big((\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,F^{-}\Big)=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\;\nu_{0}(F^{-}\,f^{-}).

Proof.

Apply
Lemma
5.6
at
s=0
with
k=4
and

F=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})F^{-}
.

SK part.
Exactly as in the proof of
[
109
, Lemma 1.8.4]
, the only surviving replica pairs are
(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4)
,
and the last-spin factor produces the rectangular combination
f^{-}
.
The coefficient is

\nu_{0}\Big((\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{3}\Big)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\left\langle(1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})(1-\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4})\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}\big]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sech^{4}(Y^{*})]=\rho_{4},

where we use
Lemma
5.5
to factorize at
s=0
and conditional independence given
Y^{*}
.

CW drift part.
Each drift term in (
5.17
) contains a factor

\nu_{0}\big((\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\varepsilon^{\ell}\cdot(\text{bulk})\big)
.
By
Lemma
5.5
this factors into a bulk expectation times

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\varepsilon^{\ell}\right\rangle_{Y*}\right]
. This term is
0

for every
\ell
, since for
any
choice of
\ell\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
, by the independence of the replicas under the Gibbs (conditioned on
Y^{*}
) at least one of the centered terms factorizes.
Therefore, both terms in (CW drift) from
Lemma
5.6
contribute identically
0
at
s=0
. ∎

Lemma 5.13
(Scaled rectangular cancellation
[
109
, Recentered and rescaled version of Lemma 1.8.4]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
F^{-}
be as in
Lemma
5.12
. Define the recentered and rescaled last-spin variables at
s=0
as

\widehat{\varepsilon}^{\ell}:=\frac{\varepsilon^{\ell}-\tanh(Y^{*})}{\sech(Y^{*})}\,.

Then

\nu_{0}^{0}\Big((\widehat{\varepsilon}^{1}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2})(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{4})\,F^{-}\Big)=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\;\nu_{0}(F^{-}\,f^{-}).

Proof.

At
s=0
,
\widehat{\varepsilon}^{1}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2}=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})/\sech(Y^{*})
,
hence

(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{1}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2})(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{4})=\sech^{-2}(Y^{*})\,(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4}).

Apply
Lemma
5.6
at
s=0
with

F=\sech^{-2}(Y^{*})\,(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})F^{-}
.
The same cancellation as before yields
f^{-}
on the bulk. The last-spin coefficient becomes

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\sech^{-2}(Y^{*})\left\langle(1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})(1-\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4})\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}\big]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sech^{2}(Y^{*})]=\rho_{2}.

The CW drift part vanishes for the same reason as in the proof of
Lemma
5.12
.
∎

We will also need the following “twice-weighted” scaled cancellation lemma to eventually simplify the
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\mathsf{Tr}[PD^{2}P]]
term.

Lemma 5.14
(Doubly–scaled rectangular cancellation)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
F^{-}
be as in
Lemma
5.12
. Define

\kappa(Y^{*}):=\sech^{-4}(Y^{*}),\qquad a_{n}:=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4}).

Then

\nu_{0}^{0}\Big(\kappa(Y^{*})\,a_{n}\,F^{-}\Big)=\beta^{2}\;\nu_{0}(F^{-}\,f^{-}).

Proof.

Apply
Lemma
5.6
at
s=0
with
k=4
and

F=\kappa(Y^{*})\,(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,F^{-}.

SK overlap part.
Exactly as in
Lemma
5.12
, the only surviving replica pairs
in the SK part are
(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4)
, and the resulting bulk overlap combination is
f^{-}
.
It remains to compute the last–spin coefficient; taking the pair
(1,3)
as representative, this coefficient equals

\displaystyle\nu_{0}\Big(\kappa(Y^{*})\,(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Big[\kappa(Y^{*})\;\big\langle(1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})(1-\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4})\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}\Big].

By
Lemma
5.5
, conditionally on
Y^{*}
the last spins are i.i.d.
\pm 1
with mean
\tanh(Y^{*})
,
so
\langle\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\rangle_{Y^{*}}=\tanh^{2}(Y^{*})
and hence

\big\langle 1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2}\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}=1-\tanh^{2}(Y^{*})=\sech^{2}(Y^{*}),

and similarly for
(3,4)
. Therefore

\big\langle(1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})(1-\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4})\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}=\sech^{4}(Y^{*}).

Multiplying by
\kappa(Y^{*})=\sech^{-4}(Y^{*})
gives coefficient
1
, which gives the final contribution

\beta^{2}\nu_{0}(F^{-}\,f^{-}).

CW drift part.
Each drift term in (
5.17
) contains a factor of the form

\nu_{0}\Big(\kappa(Y^{*})\,(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\cdot(\text{bulk})\Big).

By
Lemma
5.5
this factors into a bulk expectation times

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle\kappa(Y^{*})\,(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\varepsilon^{\ell}\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}\Big]=0,

for every
\ell
because, conditional on
Y^{*}
, the replicas are independent and
at least one centered difference factor has mean
0
.
∎

Invariant symmetries and concentration of the “rectangular sum”

At this point, it is critical to observe that
\nu(f(\dots)G)
is left invariant (or only flips signs) under symmetries that permute the indices of replicas provided that the function
G
is invariant itself.

Lemma 5.15
(Replica antisymmetry “kills” many terms)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
G
be any bounded function of replicas
1,2,3,4
, invariant under swapping indices
3\leftrightarrow 4
.
Then

\nu(f\,G)=0,\qquad\nu(f^{-}\,G)=0,\qquad\nu(a_{n}^{3}\,G)=0,\qquad\nu(a_{n}^{2}\,f\,G)=0,

and the same holds with
f,\ f^{-},\ a_{n}
replaced by
\widehat{f},\ \widehat{f}^{-},\ \widehat{a}_{n}
.

Proof.

Under
\nu_{s}
the joint law is invariant under permutations of replica labels by exchangeability, since
\sigma^{1},\sigma^{2},\sigma^{3},\sigma^{4}\sim G^{\otimes 4}
. Under the index swap
3\leftrightarrow 4
,

f\mapsto-f,\qquad f^{-}\mapsto-f^{-},

while
G
is invariant by assumption and
a_{n}^{2}
is invariant since it is jointly even in
\varepsilon^{3}
and
\varepsilon^{4}
.
Therefore,
\nu_{s}(fG)=-\nu_{s}(fG)=0
and
\nu(a^{2}_{n}fG)=-\nu(a^{2}_{n}fG)=0
. By the fact that
a_{n}^{3}
is odd, one immediately also obtain
\nu(a_{n}^{3}G)=0
.

The same argument applies to the situation where the spins are recentered and rescaled, as the rescaling and recentering is via constants with respect to the Gibbs measure.
∎

In fact, the marvelous symmetry of the “rectangular sum” is revealed in the the fact it is
implicitly
centered around the concentrating value as

f=R_{1,3}-R_{1,4}-R_{2,3}+R_{2,4}=\left(R_{1,3}-q^{*}\right)-\left(R_{1,4}-q^{*}\right)-\left(R_{2,3}-q^{*}\right)+\left(R_{2,4}-q^{*}\right)\,.

This allows (after some effort using
Lemma
5.6
and the “exact cancellations” at
s=0
) us to recover an extra factor of
O(n^{-1/2})
in the cubical moments for various bulk statistics. Doing so requires obtaining concentration bounds for
f
and
\widehat{f}
, which is accomplished in the lemmata below.

Lemma 5.16
(Even moments of “rectangular sum” via concentration)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Fix
s\in[0,1]
. Assume the concentration implied by (
5.29
).
Then for every integer
k\geqslant 1
,

(5.30)

\nu_{s}\!\left(|f|^{2k}\right)\leqslant 4^{2k}\,\frac{k!}{\lambda^{k}}\,C_{\lambda}\,n^{-k}.

Equivalently,

\nu_{s}(|f|^{2k})=O(2k).

Proof 21
.

Since the coefficients sum to zero, we have the exact identity

f=(R_{1,3}-q^{*})-(R_{1,4}-q^{*})-(R_{2,3}-q^{*})+(R_{2,4}-q^{*}).

Furthermore, for any
p\geqslant 1
and any real numbers
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
,

(5.31)

\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}\Big|^{p}\leqslant m^{p-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x_{j}|^{p}\,,

which follows by a triangle and a
(1-1/p,p)
-Hölder inequality.
Apply (
5.31
) with
m=4
,
p=2k
, and

x_{1}=R_{1,3}-q^{*},\quad x_{2}=-(R_{1,4}-q^{*}),\quad x_{3}=-(R_{2,3}-q^{*}),\quad x_{4}=R_{2,4}-q^{*}.

Then

|f|^{2k}\leqslant 4^{2k-1}\Big(|R_{1,3}-q^{*}|^{2k}+|R_{1,4}-q^{*}|^{2k}+|R_{2,3}-q^{*}|^{2k}+|R_{2,4}-q^{*}|^{2k}\Big).

Taking
\nu_{s}
and using replica exchangeability (each pair overlap has the same law),

\nu_{s}(|f|^{2k})\leqslant 4^{2k}\,\nu_{s}\big(|R_{1,2}-q^{*}|^{2k}\big).

Using (
5.29
) on the above implies (
5.30
).

A similar lemma can be shown to hold for the
recentered
and
rescaled
rectangular sums
\widehat{f}
. This will be critical in the proof of
Proposition
5.21
which will, after invocations of Hölder’s inequality, rely on
L^{2p}
bounds on
\widehat{f}
under
\nu_{s}(\cdot)
. The proof for this lemma, however, is far more involved and requires using the cavity interpolation itself to isolate the diagonal weighting in the scaled overlaps, and then using decoupling lemmata along with stability arguments to get the desired concentration.

Lemma 5.17
(Even moments of the singly–scaled rectangular sum)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Assume that for every
p\geqslant 1
,

(5.32)

\sup_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\ \nu_{s}\!\big(D_{ii}^{\,p}\big)\leqslant C(k,\beta,t)<\infty\,.

Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
there exists
C_{k}<\infty
, such that

(5.33)

\nu_{s}\left(|\widehat{f}|^{2k}\right)\leqslant\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\nu_{t}\big(|\widehat{f}|^{2k}\big)\ \leqslant\ C_{k}\,n^{-k}.

Proof.

This proof is by induction and relies on the cavity interpolation. To this end, note that the base case is considered as
k=0
and the bound that
\nu_{1}(|\widehat{f}|^{2\cdot 0})=\nu_{1}(1)=1=O(n^{-0})=O(1)
trivially holds true. Therefore, the entire effort of the proof focuses on establishing the inductive step via a combination of Hölder-type bounds along with
Lemma
5.6
and
Lemma
5.13
to handle the time
0
terms in the Taylor expansion, and some uses of Young’s inequality to handle the second-derivative term (and a few others).

Assume that, for every even integer less than
r<2k
,
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\nu_{s}(\widehat{f}^{r})\leqslant O(n^{-r/2})
. Note the following simplifications using equality of distribution under index exchangeability and last-spin decoupling,

\displaystyle\nu_{1}(\widehat{f}^{2k})

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}(\widehat{f}^{2k-1}\widehat{f})=\nu_{1}\left(\widehat{f}^{2k-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}D_{ii}a_{i}\right)=\nu_{1}\left(\widehat{f}^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}\left(\left(\widehat{f}^{-}+\frac{1}{n}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)}_{:=\mathrm{A}}+\underbrace{\frac{2k-1}{n}\nu_{1}\left(D^{2}_{nn}a^{2}_{n}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-2}\right)}_{:=\mathrm{B}}+\underbrace{\sum_{r=2}^{2k-1}\binom{2k-1}{r}\frac{1}{n^{r}}\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1-r}(D_{nn}a_{n})^{r+1}\right)}_{:=\mathrm{C}}\,.

We now bound all three terms separately.

Bounding
\mathrm{C}
via inductive hypothesis and Hölder’s inequality.
Applying
(p,q)
-Hölder’s inequality to every term in the summand in
\mathrm{C}
and using the fact that
|a_{n}|\leqslant 4
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{r=2}^{k}\frac{1}{n^{r}}\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1-r}(D_{nn}a_{n})^{r+1}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{r=2}^{k}\frac{1}{n^{r}}\nu_{1}((\widehat{f}^{-})^{(2k-1-r)q})^{1/q}\nu_{1}(D^{p(r+1)}_{nn})^{1/p}\sup|a^{p(r+1)}_{n}|

\displaystyle\leqslant_{q=\frac{2k-2}{2k-r-1},\,p=q/(q-1)}\sum_{r=2}^{k}\frac{1}{n^{r}}\nu_{1}(\widehat{f}^{2k-2})^{\frac{2k-1-r}{2k-2}}\nu_{1}\left(D_{nn}^{q(r+1)/(q-1)}\right)^{(q-1)/q}C(k)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:D-moments]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:D-moments}}\,+\,I.H.}}C^{\prime}(k,\beta,t)\sum_{r=2}^{k}\frac{1}{n^{r}}O\left(\frac{1}{n^{(k-1)\frac{(2k-1-r)}{2k-2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}(k,\beta,t)\sum_{r=2}^{k}O\left(\frac{1}{n^{(2k-1+r)/2}}\right)\leqslant_{r\geqslant 1}\frac{C^{\prime}(k,\beta,t)}{n^{k}}\,.

Bounding
\mathrm{B}
via Hölder’s and Young’s inequality.
Applying a
(p,q)
-Hölder’s inequality again with
p=k
and
q=k/(k-1)
, we obtain the following bound using
Lemma
5.3
and Young’s inequality (with
\eta>0
fixed later)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\nu_{1}\left(D^{2}_{nn}a^{2}_{n}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup|a^{2}_{n}|\frac{1}{n}\nu_{1}(D^{2k}_{nn})^{1/k}\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2(k-1)\frac{k}{k-1}}\right)^{(k-1)/k}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:D-moments]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:D-moments}}}\,+\,|a^{2}_{n}|\leqslant 16}\frac{C(k,\beta,t)}{n}\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k}\right)^{(k-1)/k}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{ab\leqslant a^{p}/p+b^{q}/q}\frac{C^{k}(k,\beta,t)}{\eta^{k-1}n^{k}}+\eta\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)\nu_{1}((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k})\leqslant\eta\nu_{1}(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k})+O_{\eta}(n^{-k})\,,

where Young’s inequality is applied with
a=\frac{1}{\eta^{(k-1)/k}}\frac{C(k,\beta,t)}{n}
and
b=\eta^{(k-1)/k}\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k}\right)^{(k-1)/k}
and
p=k
along with
q=k/(k-1)
.

Bounding
\mathrm{A}
via Gibbs-derivative stability along the cavity interpolation.
We take the second-order Taylor expansion along the cavity interpolation, and invoke
Lemma
5.6
and
Lemma
5.13
to evaluate the first two terms, and Hölder’s inequality to evaluate the second derivative.

\displaystyle\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)+\nu^{0}_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)+\int_{0}^{s}(1-t)\nu^{\prime\prime}_{t}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)\,dt\,.

Using
Lemma
5.5
with
F^{-}=(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}
on the first term gives

\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)=\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}\right)\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle D_{nn}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}\left\langle(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}\right]=0\,.

For the second term, we apply
Lemma
5.13
with
F^{-}=(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}
to obtain

\nu^{0}_{0}\left(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}f^{-}\right)\,.

At this point, we note that

-\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu_{0}\left(|(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}f^{-}|\right)\leqslant\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}f^{-}\right)\leqslant\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu_{0}\left(|(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}f^{-}|\right)\,,

and upper bound
\nu_{0}\left(|(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}f^{-}|\right)
via an application of Young’s inequality on
|(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}f^{-}|
with
p=2k/(2k-1)
and
q=2k
, along with linearity of expectation, as

\displaystyle\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu_{0}\left(|(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}f^{-}|\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\eta_{1}\left(1-\frac{1}{2k}\right)\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k}\right)+\frac{\beta^{2}\rho_{2}}{2k\eta^{2k-1}}\nu_{0}\left((f^{-})^{2k}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:replicon-moments-from-mgf]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:replicon-moments-from-mgf}}}}\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\eta_{1}\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k}\right)+O_{\eta_{1}}(n^{-k})

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:stability-s]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:stability-s}}}}\beta^{2}\rho_{2}e^{24\beta^{2}k}\eta_{1}\nu_{1}\left(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k}\right)+O(n^{-k})\,.

For the final term, we invoke
Lemma
5.6
twice and note that every term is a finite linear combination of terms of the form

\nu_{t}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}B(\varepsilon^{\ell})\Xi_{1}\Xi_{2}\right)\,,

where
B(\varepsilon^{\ell})
is an absolutely bounded function of the last spin in a finite number of replicas, and
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}\in\{R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*},M^{-}_{a}-m^{*}\}
. Overlap and magnetization concentration are stable along the cavity interpolation, and so by a
(2k/(2k-1),6k,6k,6k)
-Hölder’s inequality, we obtain the following

\displaystyle\nu_{t}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}B(\varepsilon^{\ell})\Xi_{1}\Xi_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\nu_{t}\left(D^{6k}_{nn}B^{6}(\varepsilon_{\ell})\right)^{1/6k}\nu_{t}\left(\Xi_{1}^{6k}\right)^{1/6k}\nu_{t}\left(\Xi_{2}^{6k}\right)^{1/6k}\nu_{t}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k}\right)^{(2k-1)/2k}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:D-moments]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:D-moments}}}\,+\,B(\varepsilon^{\ell})<\infty}\frac{C(k,\beta,t)}{n}\nu_{t}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k}\right)^{(2k-1)/2k}

At this point, the exact same invocation of a Young’s inequality (with
\eta_{2}>0
fixed later) gives that

\displaystyle\nu_{t}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}B(\varepsilon^{\ell})\Xi_{1}\Xi_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C^{k}(k,\beta,t)}{\eta^{k-1}_{2}n^{k}}+\eta_{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)\nu_{t}\left(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k}\right)

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:stability-s]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:stability-s}}}}O_{\eta_{2}}(n^{-k})+\eta_{2}\nu_{1}e^{24\beta^{2}k}\left(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k}\right)\,.

This immediately gives the final bound

\int_{0}^{s}(1-t)\nu^{\prime\prime}_{t}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)\,dt\leqslant O_{\eta_{2}}(n^{-k})+C^{\prime}\eta_{2}\nu_{1}\left(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k}\right)\,,

for some absolute constant
C^{\prime}>1
. Putting it together gives

\nu_{s}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n}\right)\leqslant\left(\beta^{2}\rho_{2}+C^{\prime}\eta_{2}\right)\nu_{1}\left(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k}\right)+O_{\eta_{2}}(n^{-k})\,.

Combining the bounds.
At this point, it merely remains to combine all the bounds and do a little bit of algebra
9
9

9

Note that
\nu_{1}(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k})=\nu_{1}(|\widehat{f}|^{2k})+O(k)
. This is easy to see via a last-spin decoupling
\widehat{f}=\widehat{f}^{-}+\frac{1}{n}D_{nn}a_{n}
, followed by the binomial identity expansion. All higher-order terms are
O(n^{-k})
via an argument exactly analogous to that used to bound
\mathrm{C}
, and the remaining terms are
\nu_{1}(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k})
and
\frac{1}{n}\nu_{1}(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k-1}D_{nn}a_{n})=\pm\frac{1}{n}\left(\beta^{2}\rho_{2}+C^{\prime}\eta_{2}\right)\nu_{1}\left(|\widehat{f}^{-}|^{2k}\right)+O_{\eta_{2}}(n^{-k})
by the argument to bound
\mathrm{A}
.
. Note by all the bounds above (and the footnote) that

\displaystyle\nu_{1}\left(\widehat{f}^{2k}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\eta_{1}C_{1}(k,\beta)+C_{2}(k,\beta)\eta_{2}+\eta\right)\nu_{1}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{2k}\right)+O(n^{-k})

\displaystyle=\left(\eta_{1}C_{1}(k,\beta)+C_{2}(k,\beta)\eta_{2}+\eta\right)\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)\nu_{1}\left(\widehat{f}^{2k}\right)+O(n^{-k})\,,

which immediately gives

\nu_{1}\left(|\widehat{f}|^{2k}\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\eta_{1}C_{1}(k,\beta)+C_{2}(k,\beta)\eta_{2}+\eta-O(1/n)}O_{\eta,\eta_{2}}(n^{-k})\,,

which is well-defined with a choice of
\eta_{2}=\eta=\eta_{1}=1/(10C(k,\beta))
since
\beta^{2}\rho_{2}<1
for
\beta<1
. ∎

Remark 5.18
.

Note that one can transfer the bound
\nu_{1}(\widehat{f}^{2k})=O(k)
to any point
s\in[0,1)
using
Lemma
5.10
.

O(4)
and
O(5)
bounds for bulk observables via Hölder estimates

In
Lemma
5.22
, we prove that
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)|=O(4)
whenever
F
is a (scaled or unscaled) “rectangular sum”. Before that, it will be critical to use various applications of Hölder’s inequality to reason about the fluctuations of certain bulk observables.

Lemma 5.19
(Hölder bounds for fluctuation monomials)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Fix a finite set of replica indices.
For
s\in[0,1]
, let

\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}:=R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*},\qquad\overline{M}^{-}_{a}:=M^{-}_{a}-m^{*},\qquad f^{-}:=\overline{R}^{-}_{13}-\overline{R}^{-}_{14}-\overline{R}^{-}_{23}+\overline{R}^{-}_{24},

and let
\widehat{f}^{-}
be the corresponding standardized rectangular sum.

Assume that for every
p\geqslant 1
there exists
C_{p}<\infty
such that uniformly in
s\in[0,1]
,

(5.34)

\|\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}+\|\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}\leqslant C_{p}\,n^{-1/2},

and that

(5.35)

\|f^{-}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}\leqslant C_{p}\,n^{-1/2},\qquad\|\widehat{f}^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\leqslant C\,n^{-1/2}.

Let
\mathcal{Q}^{-}
denote the linear span of quadratic bulk observables

\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}\overline{R}^{-}_{cd},\qquad\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}\overline{M}^{-}_{c},\qquad\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\overline{M}^{-}_{b}.

Then:

(i)

For every
p\geqslant 1
and every
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}
,

(5.36)

\|G^{-}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}\leqslant C_{p}\,n^{-1}\,.

(ii)

Let
B
be any bounded random variable
with
\|B\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{B}
.
Then for every
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}
and every centered bulk fluctuation

\Xi,\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\}
,

(5.37)

\displaystyle\big|\nu_{s}(B\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,n^{-2}=O(4),

(5.38)

\displaystyle\big|\nu_{s}(B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,n^{-2}=O(4),

(5.39)

\displaystyle\big|\nu_{s}(B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,n^{-5/2}=O(5),

(5.40)

\displaystyle\big|\nu_{s}(B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,n^{-2}=O(4),

(5.41)

\displaystyle\big|\nu_{s}(B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C\,n^{-5/2}=O(5).

Proof.

All of the above bounds follow by applying Hölder’s inequality with the appropriate set of Hölder conjugates.

Hölder bound for (
5.36
).
It is enough to prove the bound for a monomial
G^{-}=XY
with
X,Y\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\}
,
since
\mathcal{Q}^{-}
is finite-dimensional and linear combinations scale the same via a triangle inequality.
For any
p\geqslant 1
, Hölder gives

\|XY\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}\leqslant\|X\|_{L^{2p}(\nu_{s})}\|Y\|_{L^{2p}(\nu_{s})}.

Using (
5.34
),

\|X\|_{L^{2p}(\nu_{s})}\|Y\|_{L^{2p}(\nu_{s})}\leqslant C_{2p}^{2}\,n^{-1}.

This proves (
5.36
) for monomials, and hence for every
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}
.

Hölder bound for (
5.37
).
As
B
is uniformly bounded,

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|\leqslant\|B\|_{\infty}\,\nu_{s}(|G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}|).

Apply Hölder with exponents
(2,4,4)
:

\nu_{s}(|G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}|)\leqslant\|G^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi_{2}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}.

By (
5.34
) and (
5.36
),

\|G^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1}),\qquad\|\Xi_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\|\Xi_{2}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),

and so,

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|\leqslant C\,n^{-1}\,n^{-1/2}\,n^{-1/2}=C\,n^{-2}\,.

Hölder bound for (
5.38
).
Again using boundedness of
B
,

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)\big|\leqslant\|B\|_{\infty}\,\nu_{s}(|f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi|).

Applying Hölder with conjugates
(4,2,4)
:

\nu_{s}(|f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi|)\leqslant\|f^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\|G^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}.

By (
5.34
), (
5.35
) and (
5.36
),

\|f^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\|G^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1}),\qquad\|\Xi\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),

so

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)\big|\leqslant C\,n^{-1/2}n^{-1}n^{-1/2}=C\,n^{-2}.

Hölder bound for (
5.39
).
Similarly,

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|\leqslant\|B\|_{\infty}\,\nu_{s}(|f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}|).

Apply Hölder with exponents
(4,4,4,4)
:

\nu_{s}(|f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}|)\leqslant\|f^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\|G^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi_{2}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}.

Using the same bounds as before,

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|\leqslant C\,n^{-1/2}\,n^{-1}\,n^{-1/2}\,n^{-1/2}=C\,n^{-5/2}.

Hölder bound for (
5.40
).
Now we use only the
L^{2}
control on
\widehat{f}^{-}
.
Again,

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)\big|\leqslant\|B\|_{\infty}\,\nu_{s}(|\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi|).

Apply Hölder with conjugates
(2,4,4)
:

\nu_{s}(|\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi|)\leqslant\|\widehat{f}^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\|G^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}.

By (
5.35
), (
5.36
), and (
5.34
),

\|\widehat{f}^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\|G^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1}),\qquad\|\Xi\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),

which immediately yields

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)\big|\leqslant C\,n^{-1/2}n^{-1}n^{-1/2}=C\,n^{-2}.

Hölder bound for (
5.41
).
Finally, note that

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|\leqslant\|B\|_{\infty}\,\nu_{s}(|\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}|).

Apply Hölder with conjugates
(2,6,6,6)
:

\nu_{s}(|\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}|)\leqslant\|\widehat{f}^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\|G^{-}\|_{L^{6}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi_{1}\|_{L^{6}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi_{2}\|_{L^{6}(\nu_{s})}.

Using (
5.35
), (
5.36
), and (
5.34
),

\|\widehat{f}^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\|G^{-}\|_{L^{6}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1}),\qquad\|\Xi_{j}\|_{L^{6}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1/2}),

which gives the final inequality,

\big|\nu_{s}(B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})\big|\leqslant C\,n^{-1/2}\,n^{-1}\,n^{-1/2}\,n^{-1/2}=C\,n^{-5/2}.\qed

Cubic moments are
O(4)

This is the central input akin to the one proved in
[
109
, §1.10]
. Namely, odd moments of the “rectangular sums” are one order smaller than naive concentration predicts, because the cavity equation forces them to be nearly multiplied by

\beta^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sech^{4}(Y^{*})]<1
(which is a memory of the so-called dAT line).

For any bounded observable
F
depending on finitely many replicas, the fundamental theorem of calculus implies

\nu(F)-\nu_{0}(F)-\nu_{0}^{0}(F)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\,\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,ds,\qquad\nu_{0}^{0}(F):=\left.\frac{d}{ds}\nu_{s}(F)\right|_{s=0+}.

Therefore it suffices to prove

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)|\leqslant C\,n^{-2}\,.

Unfortunately,
Lemma
5.19
does
not
imply

\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})=O(4)\quad\text{or}\quad\nu_{s}(\widehat{f}^{-}G^{-})=O(4),

because Hölder’s inequality alone only gives
O(3)
for those cubic expressions. The stronger
O(4)
estimate for these
replicon-weighted cubic terms
is exactly the content of
Proposition
5.21
. We first prove a slightly easier lemma that has the same structure (and similar content) as the proof of
Proposition
5.21
. The proof for
Proposition
5.21
is then transparent in structure, and follows from this proof with exactly the same structure and similar invocations of Hölder’s inequality.

Proposition 5.20
(Cubic moments of “rectangular sums” are
O(4)
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

The “rectangular sums” satisfy the following scaling of (mixed and pure) third moments:

\nu(f^{3})=O(4),\qquad\nu(f^{2}\widehat{f})=O(4),\qquad\nu(f\widehat{f}^{2})=O(4),\qquad\nu(\widehat{f}^{3})=O(4).

Proof.

We prove
\nu(f^{3})=O(4)
; the other three bounds follow by the same argument, using invocations of
Lemma
5.3
,
Lemma
5.13
,
Lemma
5.14
and
Lemma
5.17
every time
\widehat{f}
appears.

Reducing to an off-diagonal term.
By exchangeability
[
109
, §1.8, (1.240)]
,

\nu(f^{3})=\nu\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}f^{2}\right)=\nu(a_{n}f^{2}).

Write
f=f^{-}+\frac{1}{n}a_{n}
, hence

f^{2}=(f^{-})^{2}+\frac{2}{n}a_{n}f^{-}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}a_{n}^{2}.

Thus

(5.42)

\nu(f^{3})=\nu\big(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2}\big)+\frac{2}{n}\nu(a_{n}^{2}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\nu(a_{n}^{3}).

By
Lemma
5.15
,
\nu(a_{n}^{2}f^{-})=0
and
\nu(a_{n}^{3})=0
.
So,

(5.43)

\nu(f^{3})=\nu\big(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2}\big).

Applying the second-order Taylor expansion to
\nu(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2})
.
Set

F:=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,(f^{-})^{2},\quad\text{so that}\quad\nu(F)=\nu(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2}).

The main claim is that

(5.44)

\nu(F)=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu_{0}^{0}(F)+O(4).

Bounding
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)
uniformly.
We prove that
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)|=O(4)
.
Differentiating
\nu(s)
twice and applying
Lemma
5.6
twice gives the following

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(f^{3})

\displaystyle=\beta^{4}\Bigg[\sum_{1\leqslant\ell_{3}<\ell_{4}<6}\nu_{s}\left(H_{6}\varepsilon^{\ell_{3}}\varepsilon^{\ell_{4}}(R^{-}_{\ell_{3},\ell_{4}}-q^{*})\right)-6\sum_{\ell_{3}=1}^{6}\nu_{s}\left(H_{6}\varepsilon^{\ell_{3}}\varepsilon^{7}(R^{-}_{\ell_{3},7}-q^{*})\right)+21\nu_{s}\left(H_{6}\varepsilon^{7}\varepsilon^{8}(R^{-}_{7,8}-q^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\sum_{\ell_{3}=1}^{6}\nu_{s}\left(H_{6}\varepsilon^{\ell_{3}}(M^{-}_{\ell_{3}}-m^{*})\right)-6\sum_{\ell_{3}=1}^{6}\nu_{s}\left(H_{6}\varepsilon^{7}(M^{-}_{7}-m^{*})\right)\Bigg]\,,

where
H_{6}
is such that
\nu^{\prime}_{s}(f^{3})=\nu_{s}\left(H_{6}\right)
(inferred from (
5.6
)) as

\displaystyle H_{6}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sum_{1\leqslant\ell<\ell^{\prime}\leqslant 4}f^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})+4\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}f^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{5}(R_{\ell,5}^{-}-q^{*})+10f^{3}\varepsilon^{5}\varepsilon^{6}(R^{-}_{5,6}-q^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}f^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})-4\left(f^{3}\varepsilon^{5}(M^{-}_{5}-m^{*})\right)\Bigg)\,.

Each term in
\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(F)
is a finite linear combination
of expectations of
f^{3}
multiplied by a product of two deviations, each deviation being either

(R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*})
or
(M^{-}_{a}-m^{*})
and (up to)
4
replicas. So, note that

\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(f^{3})=\sum_{k=1}^{A}C_{k}\nu_{s}\left(f^{3}p_{k}\left(\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{8}\right)a\,b\right)\,,

where
A,C_{k}>0
,
a,b\in\{(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*}),(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})\}_{(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\in[8]^{2}}
and
p_{k}(\cdot)
are finite-degree polynomials in no more than
8
variables. Since
\|p_{k}\|\leqslant C
(for some
C>0
), applying a
(2,4,4)
-Hölder inequality yields

\left|\nu_{s}\left(f^{3}p\left(\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{8}\right)a\,b\right)\right|\leqslant C\left|\nu_{s}\left(f^{3}\,a\,b\right)\right|\leqslant C\left(\nu_{s}(f^{6})\right)^{1/2}\left(\nu_{s}(a^{4})\right)^{1/4}\left(\nu_{s}(b^{4})\right)^{1/4}\leqslant_{\text{\eqref{eq:conc-assumptions}}}Cn^{-1}\left(\nu_{s}(f^{6})\right)^{1/2}\,.

Invoking
Lemma
5.16
implies that
\nu_{s}(f^{6})^{1/2}=O(n^{-3/2})
, and combining the bounds proves (
5.44
).

Using exact factorization to show
\nu_{0}(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2})=0
.
Note that
\nu_{0}(F)=0
by
Lemma
5.5
because, conditional on
Y^{*}
,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\mid Y^{*}]=0
. Therefore,
\nu_{0}(f^{3})=\nu_{0}(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2})=0
.

Using exact factorization to bound
\nu^{0}_{0}(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2})=O(4)
.
For the derivative term, apply
Lemma
5.12
with
F^{-}=(f^{-})^{2}
:

\nu_{0}^{0}(F)=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\;\nu_{0}\big((f^{-})^{3}\big).

Comparing
\nu_{0}\left((f^{-})^{3}\right)
and
\nu_{1}\left(fs\right)
.
We now show that

\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{3})-\nu(f^{3})=\left(\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{3})-\nu_{1}((f^{-})^{3})\right)+\left(\nu_{1}((f^{-})^{3})-\nu_{1}(f^{3})\right)=O(4)\,.

Bounding the second term relies on simple algebra and applications of
(H1)
and
Lemma
5.15
. Specifically,

\displaystyle\nu_{1}\left((f)^{3}-(f^{-})^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}\left(\left(f^{-}+\frac{1}{n}a_{n}\right)^{3}-(f^{-})^{3}\right)=\frac{1}{n^{3}}\nu_{1}\left(a_{n}^{3}\right)+\frac{3}{n}\nu_{1}\left(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2}\right)+\frac{3}{n^{2}}\nu_{1}\left(a^{2}_{n}f^{-}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{|a_{n}|\leqslant 4,~\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:antisym]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:antisym}}}}\frac{C}{n^{3}}+\frac{C^{\prime}}{n}\nu_{1}\left((f^{-})^{2}\right)+0

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{{(H1)}}}\frac{C}{n^{3}}+\frac{C^{\prime}}{n^{2}}=O(4)\,.

For the first term one applies the fundamental theorem of calculus along with
Lemma
5.6
and invokes Cauchy-Schwarz on every summand. Expanding,

\displaystyle\nu_{1}\left((f^{-})^{3}\right)-\nu_{0}\left((f^{-})^{3}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{ds}\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\right)ds\leqslant\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\nu^{\prime}_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:deriv]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:deriv}}}}\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\beta^{2}\Bigg(\sum_{1\leqslant\ell<\ell^{\prime}\leqslant 4}\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})\right)+4\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{5}(R_{\ell,5}^{-}-q^{*})\right)+10\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{5}\varepsilon^{6}(R^{-}_{5,6}-q^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})\right)-4\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{5}(M^{-}_{5}-m^{*})\right)\Bigg)\,.

Using
(H1)
and
(H2)
in conjunction with the fact that
\varepsilon^{\ell}\in\{-1,1\}
for every
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, invocations of Cauchy-Schwarz give

\displaystyle|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})\right)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})\right)|\leqslant\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{6}\right)}\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})^{2}\right)}\leqslant Cn^{-3/2}n^{-1/2}=O(4),

\displaystyle|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{5}(R_{\ell,5}^{-}-q^{*})\right)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}(R_{\ell,5}^{-}-q^{*})\right)|\leqslant\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{6}\right)}\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left(R^{-}_{\ell,5}-q^{*})^{2}\right)}\leqslant Cn^{-3/2}n^{-1/2}=O(4),

\displaystyle|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{5}\varepsilon^{6}(R^{-}_{5,6}-q^{*})\right)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}(R^{-}_{5,6}-q^{*})\right)|\leqslant\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{6}\right)}\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left(R^{-}_{5,6}-q^{*})^{2}\right)}\leqslant Cn^{-3/2}n^{-1/2}=O(4),

\displaystyle|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{\ell}(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})\right)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})\right)|\leqslant\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{6}\right)}\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})^{2}\right)}\leqslant Cn^{-3/2}n^{-1/2}=O(4),

\displaystyle|\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{3}\varepsilon^{5}(M^{-}_{5}-m^{*})\right)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|\nu\left((f^{-})^{3}(M^{-}_{5}-m^{*})\right)|\leqslant\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((f^{-})^{6}\right)}\sqrt{\nu_{s}\left((M^{-}_{5}-m^{*})^{2}\right)}\leqslant Cn^{-3/2}n^{-1/2}=O(4).

Adding the bounds on the first and second term gives

\nu_{1}(f^{3})=\nu_{0}\left((f^{-})^{3}\right)+O(4)\,.

Final bound on
\nu_{1}(f^{3})
.
Putting all the bounds together yields

\nu_{1}(f^{3})=\nu_{1}(a_{n}(f^{-})^{2})=\nu_{0}(f^{3})+\nu^{0}_{0}(f^{3})+\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu(f^{3})+O(4).

Since
\beta^{2}\rho_{4}<1
and
\nu_{0}(f^{3})=0
, we conclude

\displaystyle\nu_{1}(f^{3})

\displaystyle=0+\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\nu_{1}(f^{3})+C\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(f^{3})|+O(4)

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4}}O(4)\,,

which is well-defined below the dAT line (
\beta^{2}\rho_{4}<1
).

Mixed cubic moments.
The proofs of
\nu(f^{2}\widehat{f})=O(4)
and
\nu(f\widehat{f}^{2})=O(4)
and
\nu(\widehat{f}^{3})=O(4)
are identical,
replacing one or more factors
f
by
\widehat{f}
and using
Lemma
5.13
(scaled cancellation)
whenever the last-spin factor is standardized. The antisymmetry cancellations provided by
Lemma
5.15

continue to remove all “diagonal” contributions, and the Taylor remainder is
O(4)
because the bulk factor
is always at least quadratic in rectangular sums. Concretely, note that

\nu(f^{2}\widehat{f})=\nu(D_{nn}a_{n}(f^{-})^{2}),\quad\nu(f\widehat{f}^{2})=\nu(D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-}),\quad\nu(\widehat{f}^{3})=\nu(D_{nn}a_{n}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2})\,,

where one uses exchangeability and the facts that
\nu(D_{nn}a^{2}_{n}f^{-})=0=\nu(D_{nn}a^{2}_{n}\widehat{f})
and
\nu(D_{nn}a_{n}^{3})=0
.
Let
F
denote the symmetrized function. Two applications of
Lemma
5.6
will yield, for any of the above terms, that
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)
is a linear combination of terms of the form

\nu_{s}(B_{s}D_{nn}G^{-}\Xi_{1}\Xi_{2})\,,

where
B_{s}
is an absolutely bounded function of the last spins,
G^{-}\in\{(f^{-})^{2},(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2},f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-}\}
and
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}
are centered bulk deviation terms. By applying a
(2,8,8,8,8)
-Hölder’s inequality in conjunction with
Lemma
5.3
,
Lemma
5.16
,
Lemma
5.17
and overlap/magnetization concentration, one obtains that
|\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)|=O(4)
. At
s=0
,
\nu_{0}(F)=0
since
\left\langle a_{n}\mid Y^{*}\right\rangle_{0}=\left\langle(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\mid Y^{*}\right\rangle=0
, for any
F\in G^{-}
.
Note from the proof for
\nu(f^{3})=O(4)
that

\nu(f^{3})=\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{3})+O(4)\,.

The final step is to invoke
Lemma
5.13
and observe the following “towering” chain of implications using the equality above for every
F\in G^{-}
,

\displaystyle\nu^{0}_{0}(f^{2}\widehat{f})

\displaystyle=\nu^{0}_{0}(D_{nn}a_{n}(f^{-})^{2})=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:rect-scaled]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:rect-scaled}}}}\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{3})=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu((f^{-})^{3})+O(4)=O(4)\,,

\displaystyle\nu^{0}_{0}(f\widehat{f}^{2})

\displaystyle=\nu^{0}_{0}(D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-})=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:rect-scaled]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:rect-scaled}}}}\beta^{2}\rho\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}(f^{-})^{2})=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu(f^{2}\widehat{f})+O(4)=O(4)\,,

\displaystyle\nu^{0}_{0}(\widehat{f}^{3})

\displaystyle=\nu^{0}_{0}(D_{nn}a_{n}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2})=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:rect-scaled]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:rect-scaled}}}}\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu_{0}(f^{-}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2})=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\nu(f\widehat{f}^{2})+O(4)=O(4)\,,

where the last equalities in the second and third equations follow by inserting the
O(4)
bound implied by the second-order Taylor expansion for the previous
F
term. The second-to-last equalities in the last two equations follow by expressing

\displaystyle\nu(f^{2}\widehat{f})-\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{2}\widehat{f}^{-})

\displaystyle=\left(\nu(f^{2}\widehat{f})-\nu((f^{-})^{2}\widehat{f}^{-})\right)+\underbrace{(\nu((f^{-})^{2}\widehat{f}^{-})-\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{2}\widehat{f}^{-}))}_{:=\int_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime}_{s}(F)ds}\,,

\displaystyle\nu(f\widehat{f}^{2})-\nu_{0}(f^{-}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\nu(f\widehat{f}^{2})-\nu(f^{-}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2})\right)+\underbrace{\nu(f^{-}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2})-\nu_{0}(f^{-}(\widehat{f}^{-})^{2})}_{:=\int_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime}_{s}(F)ds}\,,

where the first terms in the equalities above can be shown to be small by decoupling the last spin and using symmetry under replica-index exchange to show certain terms are exactly
0
while the remaining terms are the first derivatives, which can be controlled by applying
Lemma
5.6
and Hölder’s inequality with
Lemma
5.3
,
Lemma
5.16
and
Lemma
5.17
to control the remainder term to be
O(4)
.
∎

We now “boost” the lemma above to deal with the situation where we have a
single
copy of the rectangular sum weighted by quadratic powers of linear combinations of bulk deviation terms such as
(R_{ab}-q^{*})
or
(M_{ab}-m^{*})
. This lemma will be crucial to bound remainders in the
exact
limiting estimate developed for cavity terms like
W^{(2)}_{n}
. The structure of the proof will be
exactly
the same as that of the proof of
Proposition
5.20
.

Proposition 5.21
(Cubic moments of “rectangular sums” with quadratic weights are
O(4)
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Fix a finite number of replicas and let
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}
be any quadratic bulk observable, where

\mathcal{Q}:=\mathsf{span}\left\{(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})^{2},(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})^{2},(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*})\right\}\,.

Then, for every
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\nu_{s}\big(f^{-}\,G^{-}\big)

\displaystyle=O(4)\,,

\displaystyle\nu_{s}\left(\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=O(4)\,.

The same statement holds with
f^{-}
replaced by
\widehat{f}^{-}
(and with
G^{-}
allowed to include standardized overlaps).

Proof.

We prove the claim for
s=1
; the same argument holds for any
s\in[0,1]

with constants uniform in
s
, since
Lemma
5.6
and the
s=0
factorization/cancellation (
Lemma
5.5
) are unchanged.

Fix
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}
. Since each centered bulk deviation has size
O(n^{-1/2})
in all
L^{p}
by (
5.29
),
we have

(5.45)

\|G^{-}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1})\,.

Exchangeability converts
\nu(f^{-}G^{-})
into a cavity-site expectation.
Note that
f
is permutation-invariant in the site index.
Also
G^{-}
is a function of bulk overlaps/magnetizations, hence, also permutation-invariant in the site index – this is because its definition takes an average of all terms
i\in[n-1]
, which is invariant under permutations of
\{1,\dots,n-1\}
,
and the difference to using
\{1,\dots,n\}
is
O(n^{-1})
.

Therefore, by site exchangeability,

(5.46)

\nu(f\,G^{-})=\nu\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\,G^{-}\right)=\nu(a_{n}\,G^{-}).

Moreover,
f=f^{-}+\frac{1}{n}a_{n}
, hence using (
5.45
) and the fact that
|a_{n}|\leqslant 4
and an application of Hölder’s inequality,

(5.47)

\nu(f\,G^{-})=\nu(f^{-}\,G^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu(a_{n}G^{-})=\nu(f^{-}\,G^{-})+O(4).

Combining (
5.46
) and (
5.47
) gives

(5.48)

\nu(f^{-}\,G^{-})=\nu(a_{n}\,G^{-})+O(4)\,.

Two-term Taylor expansion for
\nu(a_{n}G^{-})
with
O(4)
remainder.
Set

F:=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,G^{-}=a_{n}G^{-}.

Write the exact Taylor identity

(5.49)

\nu(F)=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu_{0}^{0}(F)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)\,\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,ds.

The goal is to prove that the remainder is

(5.50)

\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\,\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,du=O(4).

First, obtain
\nu_{u}^{\prime}(F)
via
Lemma
5.6
. Every term in
\nu_{u}^{\prime}(F)
is a finite linear combination of expectations of the form

\nu_{s}\big(G^{-}\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)\Xi\big),\qquad\Xi\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\},

with
\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)
a bounded polynomial in finitely many replicas of last spins, containing
(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})
.
Differentiating once more, every term in
\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(F)
is a finite linear combination of expectations of the form

(5.51)

\nu_{s}\big(G^{-}\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)\Xi_{1}\Xi_{2}\big),\qquad\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\}.

Since all last-spin factors are bounded by an absolute constant
O(1)
in absolute value, we bound the magnitude of each term in
(
5.51
) by

C\,\nu_{u}(|G^{-}|\,|\Xi_{1}|\,|\Xi_{2}|).

Using (
5.45
) and
\|\Xi_{j}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{u})}=O(n^{-1/2})
from (
5.29
), an application of
(2,4,4)
-Hölder yields

\nu_{u}(|G^{-}|\,|\Xi_{1}|\,|\Xi_{2}|)\leqslant\|G^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{u})}\|\Xi_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{u})}\|\Xi_{2}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{u})}=O(n^{-1})\cdot O(n^{-1/2})\cdot O(n^{-1/2})=O(n^{-2})=O(4)\,.

Since
\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(F)
is a finite linear combination of such terms,
\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(F)=O(4)
,
and (
5.50
) follows.

Evaluate
\nu_{0}(F)
and
\nu_{0}^{0}(F)
exactly via factorization.

Lemma
5.5
at
s=0
gives, conditionally on
Y^{*}
, the last spins are i.i.d. with mean
\tanh(Y^{*})
. So
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2}\mid Y^{*}]=0
and, therefore,

\nu_{0}(F)=0.

For
\nu_{0}^{0}(F)
, applying the unscaled rectangular cancellation lemma in
Lemma
5.12
with bulk factor
F^{-}=G^{-}
gives

\nu_{0}^{0}(F)=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu_{0}(G^{-}\,f^{-})\,.

Thus (
5.49
) and (
5.50
) give

(5.52)

\nu(a_{n}G^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu_{0}(G^{-}f^{-})+O(4).

Replacing
\nu_{0}(G^{-}f^{-})
by
\nu(G^{-}f^{-})
up to
O(4)
fluctuations.
We show

(5.53)

\nu(G^{-}f^{-})-\nu_{0}(G^{-}f^{-})=O(4).

Indeed,

\nu(G^{-}f^{-})-\nu_{0}(G^{-}f^{-})=\int_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime}_{s}(G^{-}f^{-})\,ds.

Applying
Lemma
5.6
to
H:=G^{-}f^{-}
yields that every term in
\nu^{\prime}_{s}(H)
is a finite linear combination of expectations of the form

\nu_{s}\big(H\,\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)\,\Xi\big),\qquad\Xi\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\}.

Now
H=G^{-}f^{-}
is
cubic
in centered deviations.
Hence, using
(2,4,4)
-Hölder along with (
5.45
) and
Lemma
5.16
,

\big|\nu_{s}(H\,\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)\,\Xi)\big|\leqslant C\,\|G^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\|f^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\|\Xi\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1})O(n^{-1/2})O(n^{-1/2})=O(4)\,.

Integrating over
s
yields (
5.53
).

Therefore, (
5.52
) becomes

(5.54)

\nu(a_{n}G^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu(G^{-}f^{-})+O(4).

Final stability equation.
Combining (
5.48
) with (
5.54
) gives

\nu(f^{-}G^{-})=\nu(a_{n}G^{-})+O(4)=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu(G^{-}f^{-})+O(4)=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu(f^{-}G^{-})+O(4).

Thus,

(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})\,\nu(f^{-}G^{-})=O(4).

Since
0<\beta<1/2
and
\rho_{4}\leqslant 1
, we have
1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4}>0
and, so,

\nu(f^{-}G^{-})=O(4)\,,

yielding the final result.

Uniformity in
s\in[0,1]

For a general endpoint
s\in[0,1]
, repeat the same argument with
\nu
replaced by
\nu_{s}
throughout;
all estimates remain uniform because:
(i) the derivative identity has the same form,
(ii)
s=0
factorization and the cancellation lemma are unchanged,
(iii) the moment bounds (
5.29
) are assumed uniform along the interpolation.
∎

Lemma 5.22
(
O(4)
Taylor remainder for “rectangular sums”)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle:=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,f^{-},

\displaystyle F_{2}

\displaystyle:=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\widehat{f}^{-},

\displaystyle F_{3}

\displaystyle:=(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{1}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2})(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{4})\,\widehat{f}^{-},\qquad\widehat{\varepsilon}^{\ell}:=\frac{\varepsilon^{\ell}-\tanh(Y^{*})}{\sech(Y^{*})}\quad(s=0).

Then

\nu(F_{i})=\nu_{0}(F_{i})+\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{i})+O(4)\qquad\text{for }i\in\{1,2,3\}.

Proof.

We prove the three cases separately, using
Lemma
5.19
and
Proposition
5.21
. The goal will be to, as is the approach in all prior lemmata, evaluate the Gibbs measure (and its derivative) at time
s=0
and apply uniform bounds on the second derivative, concluding the bound via the Taylor expansion. So, for every type of rectangular sum
F
, we have

\nu(F_{1})-\nu_{0}(F_{1})-\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{1})=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\,\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F_{1})\,ds\,,

and it suffices to show

\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F_{1})=O(4)\,.

Case 1:
F_{1}=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,f^{-}

We apply
Lemma
5.6
twice to
\nu_{s}(F_{1})=\nu_{s}(a_{n}f^{-})
and begin by looking at the structure of the terms in the linear combination.
Each application of
Lemma
5.6
inserts one centered bulk fluctuation

\Xi\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\}
together with a bounded polynomial
\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{k})
in the last-spin variables of the replicas.
Hence, every term in
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F_{1})
is a finite linear combination of quantities of the form

(5.55)

T_{s}(B,G^{-}):=\nu_{s}\big(B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\big),

where

•

B
is a bounded random variable depending only on finitely many last spins, with
\|B\|_{\infty}\leqslant C
;

•

G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}
is quadratic in centered bulk fluctuations.

To conclude
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F_{1})=O(4)
it, therefore, suffices to show that every
T_{s}(B,G^{-})
in (
5.55
) is
O(4)
.

Taylor-expanding the
T_{s}(B,G^{-})
terms.
Fix
B,G^{-}
as above and denote
H:=B\,f^{-}\,G^{-}
. Then,
T_{s}(B,G^{-})=\nu_{s}(H)
. Taylor expanding around time
0
gives,

(5.56)

\nu_{s}(H)=\nu_{0}(H)+\nu_{0}^{0}(H)+\int_{0}^{s}(s-u)\,\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(H)\,du.

Bounding the
s=0
term
\nu_{0}(H)
as
O(4)
.
By exact factorization at
s=0
via
Lemma
5.5
,

\nu_{0}(H)=\underbrace{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle B\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}\Big]}_{=:c_{0}(B)}\,\nu_{0}(f^{-}\,G^{-})\,.

Boundedness of
B
implies that
|c_{0}(B)|\leqslant C
and
Proposition
5.21
implies
\nu_{0}(f^{-}\,G^{-})=O(4)
. Therefore,

(5.57)

\nu_{0}(H)=O(4).

Bounding the first derivative term
\nu_{0}^{0}(H)
as
O(4)
.
Applying
Lemma
5.6
at
s=0
to the observable
H=Bf^{-}G^{-}
yields a linear combination of terms of the form

\nu_{0}\big(B^{\prime}\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi\big),\qquad\Xi\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\},

where
B^{\prime}
is again a bounded polynomial in finitely many last-spin variables.
Again, by
Lemma
5.5
at
s=0
,

\nu_{0}\big(B^{\prime}\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi\big)=\underbrace{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\langle B^{\prime}\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}\Big]}_{=:c_{1}(B^{\prime})}\,\nu_{0}\big(f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi\big)\,.

Using
Lemma
5.19
, specifically (
5.38
) with
B\equiv 1
, gives

\nu_{0}\big(f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi\big)=O(4).

Since
\nu_{0}^{0}(H)
is a finite linear combination of
O(4)
terms,

(5.58)

\nu_{0}^{0}(H)=O(4)\,.

The Taylor remainder is
O(5)
.
Differentiate
\nu_{s}(H)
once again via
Lemma
5.6
for the observable
Bf^{-}G^{-}\Xi
.
Each term in
\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(H)
is then a finite linear combination of expectations of the form

\nu_{u}\big(B^{\prime\prime}\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}\big),\qquad\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\},

with
B^{\prime\prime}
bounded. Invoking
Lemma
5.19
, specifically (
5.39
),

\nu_{u}\big(B^{\prime\prime}\,f^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}\big)=O(5)\,.

Therefore,

(5.59)

\int_{0}^{s}(s-u)\,\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(H)\,du=O(5)\,.

Concluding the final bound for
\nu(F_{1})
.
From (
5.56
), (
5.57
), (
5.58
), and
(
5.59
), we get

T_{s}(B,G^{-})=\nu_{s}(H)=O(4)\,.

Since
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F_{1})
is a finite linear combination of terms
T_{s}(B,G^{-})
, we have
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F_{1})=O(4)
.
Therefore,

\nu(F_{1})=\nu_{0}(F_{1})+\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{1})+O(4).

Case 2:
F_{2}=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\widehat{f}^{-}

The proof is (basically) identical, with every occurrence of
f^{-}
replaced by
\widehat{f}^{-}
.
Indeed, after differentiating twice using
Lemma
5.6
, every term in
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F_{2})
is a finite linear combination of

\widetilde{T}_{s}(B,G^{-}):=\nu_{s}\big(B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\big),

where
B
is bounded and
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}
is quadratic in bulk deviation terms. Taylor expanding
\widetilde{T}_{s}(B,G^{-})
around time
0
gives

\widetilde{T}_{s}(B,G^{-})=\nu_{0}(\widetilde{H})+\nu_{0}^{0}(\widetilde{H})+\int_{0}^{s}(s-u)\,\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{H})\,du,\qquad\widetilde{H}:=B\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}.

At
s=0
, factorization gives

\nu_{0}(\widetilde{H})=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\langle B\rangle_{Y^{*}}]\,\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}),

and
Proposition
5.21
, with
f
replaced by
\widehat{f}
and
\mathcal{Q}
including the rescaled-and-centered bulk deviation terms, yields

\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-})=O(4),

which immediately implies that
\nu_{0}(\widetilde{H})=O(4)
. For the derivative term, every contribution to
\nu_{0}^{0}(\widetilde{H})
is a bounded last-spin coefficient
times
\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)
, and
Lemma
5.19
, specifically (
5.40
), gives

\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)=O(4)\,,

which implies that
\nu_{0}^{0}(\widetilde{H})=O(4)
and it remains to uniformly bound the second derivative. For the second-derivative remainder, every term is of the form

\nu_{u}(B^{\prime\prime}\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}),

with
B^{\prime\prime}
bounded, and
Lemma
5.19
(via (
5.41
)) gives

\nu_{u}(B^{\prime\prime}\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2})=O(5).

Putting this altogether gives that

\nu(F_{2})=\nu_{0}(F_{2})+\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{2})+O(4).

Case 3:
F_{3}=(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{1}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2})(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{4})\,\widehat{f}^{-}

For this case, the terms that depends on the last-spin is
not
bounded, but it has moments of all orders.
More specifically, at
s=0
,

\widehat{\varepsilon}^{\ell}=\frac{\varepsilon^{\ell}-\tanh(Y^{*})}{\sech(Y^{*})},

which implies that

|\widehat{\varepsilon}^{\ell}|\leqslant\frac{|\varepsilon^{\ell}|+|\tanh(Y^{*})|}{\sech(Y^{*})}\leqslant 2\cosh(Y^{*}).

Therefore,

|\widehat{a}_{n}|=\Big|(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{1}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2})(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{4})\Big|\leqslant C\,\cosh^{4}(Y^{*}),

and since
Y^{*}\sim\mathcal{N}(\beta^{2}m^{*}+t,\beta^{2}q^{*}+t)
, the term
\cosh^{4}(Y^{*})
has finite moments of all orders, i.e.,
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\cosh(Y^{*})^{4k}]\leqslant C(\beta,t,k)<\infty
for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Thus, every last-spin factor appearing in derivatives of
F_{3}
satisfies a bound of
O(1)
with an adequate application of Hölder’s inequality.

Differentiating
\nu_{s}(F_{3})
twice using
Lemma
5.6
yields that every term in
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F_{3})
is a finite linear combination of

\widehat{T}_{s}(B_{s},G^{-}):=\nu_{s}(\widehat{a}_{n}B_{s}\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}),

where
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}
is quadratic and
B_{s}
is a bounded function in a finite number of replicas of the last spin. Taylor expanding
\widehat{T}_{s}(B_{s},G^{-})
around
s=0
gives

\widehat{T}_{s}(B_{s},G^{-})=\nu_{0}(\widehat{H})+\nu_{0}^{0}(\widehat{H})+\int_{0}^{s}(s-u)\,\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(\widehat{H})\,du,\qquad\widehat{H}:=B_{s}\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}.

At
s=0
,
Lemma
5.5
, the boundedness of
B_{s}
, and the fact that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}[|\widehat{a}_{n}|]\leqslant C\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}[\cosh^{4}(Y^{*})]\leqslant C(\beta,t)
gives

\displaystyle\nu_{0}\left(B_{s}\widehat{a}_{n}\widehat{f}^{-}G^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}G^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[\left\langle\widehat{a}_{n}B_{s}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[prop:D15]{Proposition~\ref*{prop:D15}}}}O(4)\sup|B_{s}|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}[\cosh^{4}(Y^{*})]\leqslant C(\beta,t)O(4)=O(4)\,.

For the derivative term, applying
Lemma
5.6
implies that
\nu_{0}^{0}(\widehat{H})
is a finite linear combination of finite coefficients times terms of the form

\nu_{0}((B_{s}\widehat{a}_{n})\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi)
. Then, by a
(2,6,6,6)
-Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\nu_{0}((B_{s}\widehat{a}_{n})\widehat{f}^{-}G^{-}\Xi)

\displaystyle\leqslant\nu_{0}(|\widehat{a}_{n}|^{2}B^{2}_{s})\nu_{0}\left((\widehat{f}^{-})^{6}\right)^{1/6}\nu_{0}\left((G^{-})^{6}\right)^{1/6}\nu_{0}\left(\Xi^{6}\right)^{1/6}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:hatf-moments]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:hatf-moments}},~\hyperref@@ii[lem:D-moments]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:D-moments}},~\hyperref@@ii[lem:holder-bookkeeping]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:holder-bookkeeping}}}}\sup|B^{2}_{s}|\nu_{0}(\widehat{a}^{2}_{n})^{1/2}O(n^{-1/2})O(n^{-1})O(n^{-1/2})

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\nu_{0}(|\widehat{a}_{n}|^{2})\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}(\cosh^{8}(Y^{*}))}C(\beta,t)O(n^{-2})=O(4)\,.

This implies that
\nu_{0}^{0}(\widehat{H})=O(4)
. For the second-derivative remainder, two applications of
Lemma
5.6
implies that every term is of the form

\nu_{u}(\widehat{a}_{n}\,B_{u}\,\widehat{f}^{-}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}),

where
B_{u}^{\prime}
is absolutely bounded. An application of
(2,8,8,8,8)
-Hölder’s inequality with invocations of
Lemma
5.3
,
Lemma
5.16
,
Lemma
5.17
and
Lemma
5.19
yields that each such term is
O(4)
. Therefore,

\nu(F_{3})=\nu_{0}(F_{3})+\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{3})+O(4).\qed

Proposition 5.23
(CLT bounds for the
(U_{n},V_{n},W_{n})
system
[
109
, Mild refinement of Theorem 1.10.1]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Define

U_{n}:=\nu(f^{2}),\qquad V_{n}:=\nu(f\widehat{f}),\qquad W_{n}:=\nu(\widehat{f}^{2}).

Then,

\displaystyle(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})\,U_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\rho_{4}}{n}+O(4),

\displaystyle(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})\,V_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\rho_{2}}{n}+O(4),

\displaystyle W_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{n}+\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\,V_{n}+O(4).

Equivalently,

U_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{4}}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2}),\qquad V_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{2}}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2}),\qquad W_{n}=\frac{4}{n}\Big(1+\frac{\beta^{2}\rho_{2}^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4}}\Big)+O(n^{-2}).

Proof.

This proof uses site-exchangeability to along with a decoupling of the last spin to obtain
exact
estimates for the time
0
terms. The remainder term is shown to be
O(4)
using
Lemma
5.22
.

Exchangeability and diagonal/off-diagonal decompositions.
Define

a_{i}:=(\sigma_{i}^{1}-\sigma_{i}^{2})(\sigma_{i}^{3}-\sigma_{i}^{4}),\qquad\widehat{a}_{i}:=(\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{1}-\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{2})(\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{3}-\widehat{\sigma}_{i}^{4}),

so that
f=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}a_{i}
and
\widehat{f}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\widehat{a}_{i}
.
By exchangeability of sites,

U_{n}=\nu(f^{2})=\nu\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}a_{i}f\Big)=\nu(a_{n}f),\qquad V_{n}=\nu(f\widehat{f})=\nu(a_{n}\widehat{f}),\qquad W_{n}=\nu(\widehat{f}^{2})=\nu(\widehat{a}_{n}\widehat{f}).

Write
f=f^{-}+\frac{1}{n}a_{n}
and
\widehat{f}=\widehat{f}^{-}+\frac{1}{n}\widehat{a}_{n}
where
f^{-},\widehat{f}^{-}
depend only on the bulk spins.
Then

(5.60)

\displaystyle U_{n}

\displaystyle=\nu(a_{n}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu(a_{n}^{2}),

(5.61)

\displaystyle V_{n}

\displaystyle=\nu(a_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu(a_{n}\widehat{a}_{n}),

(5.62)

\displaystyle W_{n}

\displaystyle=\nu(\widehat{a}_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu(\widehat{a}_{n}^{2}).

Computing the self-similar terms via exact factorization.
We compute the self-similar terms in the expansion above at time
s=0
via application of
Lemma
5.5
in conjunction with the observation that
\left\langle\sigma_{n}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}=\tanh(Y^{*})
to conclude that

\nu_{0}(a_{n}^{2})=4\rho_{4},\qquad\nu_{0}(a_{n}\widehat{a}_{n})=4\rho_{2},\qquad\nu_{0}(\widehat{a}_{n}^{2})=4\,.

We now invoke
Lemma
5.6
to write
\nu^{0}_{0}(F)
exactly as a term controlled via
Lemma
5.8
and (
5.27
), and invoke
Lemma
5.6
twice to control
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)
via
Lemma
5.7
and concentration, for
F\in\{a^{2}_{n},a_{n}\widehat{a}_{n},\widehat{a}^{2}_{n}\}
. Specifically,

\frac{1}{n}\nu(F)=\frac{1}{n}\nu_{0}(F)+\frac{1}{n}\nu^{0}_{0}(F)+\frac{1}{n}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)ds\,.

\displaystyle\nu^{0}_{0}(F)

\displaystyle=\beta^{2}\sum_{1\leqslant\ell<\ell^{\prime}\leqslant 4}\nu_{0}(F\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}(R^{-}_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*}))-4\beta^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}\nu_{0}(F\varepsilon^{\ell}(R^{-}_{\ell,5}-q^{*}))+10\beta^{2}\nu_{0}(F\varepsilon^{5}\varepsilon^{6}(R^{-}_{5,6}-q^{*}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\beta^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{4}\nu_{0}(F\varepsilon^{\ell}(M^{-}_{\ell}-m^{*}))-4\beta^{2}\nu_{0}(F\varepsilon^{5}(M^{-}_{5}-q^{*}))\,.

Now, note that, for any choice of
F\in\{a^{2}_{n},a_{n}\widehat{a}_{n},\widehat{a}^{2}_{n}\}
, the terms
F\varepsilon^{\ell}
or
F\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}
are, conditioned on
Y^{*}
, functions that are independent of the first
(n-1)
spins at
s=0
. Then, by
Lemma
5.5
,

\nu_{0}(F\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}\Xi^{-})=\nu_{0}(\Xi^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[F\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}\right]\,,

and

\nu_{0}(F\varepsilon^{\ell}\Xi^{-})=\nu_{0}(\Xi^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[F\varepsilon^{\ell}\right]\,,

where
\Xi^{-}\in\{R_{ab}-q^{*},M^{-}_{a}-m^{*}\}
. Now, in the event that
F=a^{2}_{nn}
, one has that
|F\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}|\leqslant C
, and
|F\varepsilon^{\ell}|\leqslant C^{\prime}
. In the event that
F
has a power of
\widehat{a}_{n}=D_{nn}a_{n}
, then one can invoke a
(2,2)
-Hölder’s inequality in conjunction with
Lemma
5.3
to conclude that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[D^{r}_{nn}a^{r}_{n}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}\right]\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[D^{2r}_{nn}]^{1/2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[a^{2r}_{nn}]^{1/2}\leqslant C(\beta,r,t)<\infty
for
r\in\{1,2\}
. Then, using
Lemma
5.8
in conjunction with (
5.27
) immediately yields that

\nu_{0}(\mathcal{P}\Xi^{-})=\nu_{0}(\Xi^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}[\mathcal{P}]\leqslant C(\beta,r,t)e^{C^{\prime}(\beta)}O(1/n)=O(1/n)\,,

where
\mathcal{P}\in\{F\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}},F\varepsilon^{\ell}\}
. To conclude, note that two applications of
Lemma
5.6
yield that
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)
is a finite linear sum of terms of the form

\nu_{s}(F\,B\,\Xi^{-}_{1}\,\Xi^{2}_{-})\leqslant_{\text{(2,4,4)-H\"{o}lder's}}\nu_{s}((F\,B)^{2})^{1/2}\left\lVert{\Xi^{-}_{1}}\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\left\lVert{\Xi^{-}_{2}}\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\nu_{s})}\leqslant O(n^{-1})\,,

where we use
Lemma
5.7
and
Lemma
5.3
in conjunction with the fact that
B
is an absolutely bounded function of the last spin of a finite number of replicas. Putting the bounds on the first derivative and second derivative above together with the explicit evaluations of
\nu_{0}(F)
immediately yields

\frac{1}{n}\nu(a_{n}^{2})=\frac{4\rho_{4}}{n}+O(n^{-2}),\qquad\frac{1}{n}\nu(a_{n}\widehat{a}_{n})=\frac{4\rho_{2}}{n}+O(n^{-2}),\qquad\frac{1}{n}\nu(\widehat{a}_{n}^{2})=\frac{4}{n}+O(n^{-2}).

Bounding non-similar terms in
U_{n}
via
Lemma
5.22
.
Note that

\nu(a_{n}f^{-})=\nu\big((\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,f^{-}\big)=\nu(F_{1})\,,

and so
Lemma
5.22
immediately implies that

\nu(F_{1})=\nu_{0}(F_{1})+\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{1})+O(4).

By
Lemma
5.5
,
\nu_{0}(F_{1})=\nu_{0}(f^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[\left\langle a_{n}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}\right]=0
, and
Lemma
5.13
implies that

\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{1})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu_{0}\big((f^{-})^{2}\big).

At this point, we let
B
be an absolutely bounded function in the last spin of
4
replicas, and observe that
