\displaystyle\nu(f^{2})-\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{2})=\left(\nu(f^{2})-\nu((f^{-})^{2})\right)+\left(\nu((f^{-})^{2})-\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2}{n}\nu(a_{n}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\nu(a^{2}_{n})\right)+\int_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime}_{s}((f^{-})^{2})\,ds

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:deriv]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:deriv}},\,}|a_{n}|\leqslant 4,\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:D16-corrected]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:D16-corrected}}}}\frac{2\beta^{2}\rho_{4}}{n}\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{2})+\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathsf{span}\{\nu_{s}(Bf^{-}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}))\}\right)\,ds+O(4)

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:replicon-moments-from-mgf]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:replicon-moments-from-mgf}},~\eqref{eq:stability-diff}}}O(4)+O\left(\nu_{s}(B\left(f^{-}\Xi_{1}^{-}\Xi^{-}_{2}\right))\right)\,,

where we used the fact that
f^{-}
is a sum of 4 centered overlap terms, and the
\Xi^{-}_{a}
represented centered bulk deviation terms. Now, if
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left(\nu_{s}(B(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2}))-\nu_{s}(B)\nu_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right)=O(4)
, then by
Proposition
5.21
we can conclude that
\nu_{s}(Bf^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})=O(4)
and so
\nu_{s}(f^{2})=\nu_{s}((f^{-})^{2})+O(4)
. So, to obtain the bound note for
s=0
by
Lemma
5.5
,

|\nu_{0}(Bf^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})|=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\left\langle B\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}]\nu_{0}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})=_{|B|\leqslant C,\,\text{\hyperref@@ii[prop:D15]{Proposition~\ref*{prop:D15}}}}O(4)\,.

It now suffices to bound
|\left(\nu_{s}(Bf^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})-\nu_{s}(B)\nu_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right)^{\prime}|=O(4)
and apply FTOC. Note that, by
Lemma
5.6
,
\nu^{\prime}_{s}(Bf^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})
is a finite linear combination of terms of the form

|\nu_{s}(B^{\prime}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2}\Xi)|\leqslant_{(2,8,8,8,8)\text{-H\"{o}lder's}}\nu_{s}(B^{\prime 2})(\nu_{s}(\Xi^{8}))^{4/8}\leqslant O(4)\,.

Similarly, applying
Lemma
5.6
gives that
\nu^{\prime}_{s}(B)
is a finite linear combination of terms
\nu_{s}(B\Xi)
bounded by
O(n^{-1/2})
, and that
\nu^{\prime}_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{1}_{i}\Xi^{2}_{i})
is a finite linear combination of terms
\nu_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2}\Xi)
bounded by
O(n^{-2})
. By the product rule, this implies that

\displaystyle\left(\nu_{s}(Bf^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})-\nu_{s}(B)\nu_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu^{\prime}_{s}(Bf^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})-\nu^{\prime}_{s}(B)\nu_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})-\nu_{s}(B)\nu^{\prime}_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{1}_{i}\Xi^{2}_{i})

\displaystyle=O(4)+O(4)+O(4)=O(4)\,,

which yields the desired bound. Putting it altogether gives that

\nu(a_{n}f^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,U_{n}+O(4)\,

and inserting into (
5.60
) along with the diagonal term immediately gives

\,U_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{4}}{(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})n}+O(4).

Bounding non-similar terms in
V_{n}
via
Lemma
5.22
.
Similarly,

\nu(a_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})=\nu\big((\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\widehat{f}^{-}\big)=\nu(F_{2}).

By
Lemma
5.22
,

\nu(F_{2})=\nu_{0}(F_{2})+\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{2})+O(4).

Again,
Lemma
5.5
gives that
\nu_{0}(F_{2})=\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}[\left\langle a_{n}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}]=0
by factorization at
s=0
. Furthermore, using
Lemma
5.12
gives

\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{2})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}\,f^{-}).

And
\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}\,f^{-})=V_{n}+O(4)
. This follows by a strategy similar to the one used to show that
\nu_{0}(f^{2})=\nu_{1}(f^{2})+O(4)
, and we give a terse outline below.

\displaystyle\nu(f\widehat{f})-\nu_{0}(f\widehat{f})=\left(\nu(f\widehat{f})-\nu(f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-})\right)+\left(\nu(f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-})-\nu_{0}(f\widehat{f}^{-})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{n}\nu(\widehat{a}_{n}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu(a_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\nu_{0}(D_{nn}a^{2}_{n})\right)+\int_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime}_{s}(f\widehat{f}^{-})\,ds

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:deriv]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:deriv}},\,\hyperref@@ii[lem:D16-corrected]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:D16-corrected}},\,\hyperref@@ii[lem:D-moments]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:D-moments}}}}\frac{1}{n}\nu_{0}(\widehat{a}_{n}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu^{0}_{0}(\widehat{a}_{n}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu_{0}(a_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})O(4)+\frac{1}{n}\nu^{0}_{0}(a_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{span}\{\nu_{s}(B\widehat{f}^{-}G^{-})\}\,ds

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:fact-s0]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:fact-s0}},\,\hyperref@@ii[lem:rect-scaled]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:rect-scaled}}}}0+\frac{1}{n}\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{2})+\frac{1}{n}\nu_{0}(f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-})+O(4)=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:replicon-moments-from-mgf]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:replicon-moments-from-mgf}},~\hyperref@@ii[lem:hatf-moments]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:hatf-moments}}}}O(4)\,,

where one can prove
\nu_{s}(B\widehat{f}G)=O(4)
by bounding
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left(\nu_{s}(B\widehat{f}G)-\nu_{s}(B)\nu_{s}(\widehat{f}G)\right)^{\prime}=O(4)
by a strategy similar to the one invoking the absolute boundedness of
B
and the moment bounds for
\widehat{f}
proved in
Lemma
5.17
.
This finally gives,

\nu(a_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,V_{n}+O(4)\,,

and inserting into (
5.61
) allows us to obtain

V_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{2}}{(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})n}+O(4).

Bounding non-similar terms in
W_{n}
.
Write

\nu(\widehat{a}_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})=\nu\big((\widehat{\varepsilon}^{1}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{2})(\widehat{\varepsilon}^{3}-\widehat{\varepsilon}^{4})\,\widehat{f}^{-}\big)=\nu(F_{3}).

By
Lemma
5.22
,

\nu(F_{3})=\nu_{0}(F_{3})+\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{3})+O(4).

As before, by
Lemma
5.5
,
\nu_{0}(F_{3})=\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\mid Y^{*}\right]=0
. Furthermore, by
Lemma
5.13
,

\nu_{0}^{0}(F_{3})=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\,\nu_{0}(\widehat{f}^{-}\,f^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\,V_{n}+O(4)\,,

where we invoke the fact that
\nu(f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-})=\nu_{0}(f^{-}\widehat{f}^{-})+O(4)
as shown previously in the proof. This gives

\nu(\widehat{a}_{n}\widehat{f}^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\,V_{n}+O(4).

Inserting the above equation into (
5.62
) along with the self-similarity term gives

W_{n}=\frac{4}{n}+\beta^{2}\rho_{2}\,V_{n}+O(4).

Solving the first two relations yields

U_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{4}}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2}),\qquad V_{n}=\frac{4\rho_{2}}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2}),

and substituting it into the third finally gives

W_{n}=\frac{4}{n}\Big(1+\frac{\beta^{2}\rho_{2}^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4}}\Big)+O(n^{-2}).\qed

We use the following slight modification of
Proposition
5.23
to deal with “doubly scaled” rectangular sum limits, which appear in the replica-identity based simplification of
\mathsf{Tr}[PD^{2}P]
.

Proposition 5.24
(Extension of
Proposition
5.23
to
\nu(f\,f^{(2)})
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Recall
m_{i}=\langle\sigma_{i}\rangle
and
D_{ii}=(1-m_{i}^{2})^{-1}
.
Define the
D^{2}
–weighted rectangular sum

a_{i}^{(2)}:=D_{ii}^{2}\,a_{i},\qquad f^{(2)}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{(2)},\qquad f^{(2),-}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leqslant n-1}a_{i}^{(2)}.

Then

(5.63)

W_{n}^{(2)}:=\nu\big(f\,f^{(2)}\big)=\frac{4}{n}+\beta^{2}\,U_{n}+O(4)\,.

Consequently, using
Proposition
5.23
,

(5.64)

W_{n}^{(2)}=\frac{4}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2})\,.

Proof.

The proof is completely similar in structure to that of
Proposition
5.23
with the only differences being in the evaluation of
\nu_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}a_{n})
, the explicit evaluation of
\nu^{0}_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}f^{-})
via
Lemma
5.14
, and an invocation of the fact that terms of the form
\nu_{s}(D^{2}_{nn}a_{n}B_{s}f\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})
are
O(4)
(which follows directly by observing that the powers of
D_{nn}
do not change the proof strategy of
Lemma
5.22
, case-3).

Note that,

\displaystyle\nu(ff^{(2})=\nu(a^{(2}_{n}f)=\nu(a^{(2}_{n}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu(a^{(2)}_{n}a_{n})

\displaystyle=\left(\nu_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}f^{-})+\nu^{0}_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}f^{-})+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(a^{(2)}_{n}f^{-})\,ds\right)+\frac{1}{n}\left(\nu_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}a_{n})+\nu^{0}_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}a_{n})+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(a^{(2)}_{n}a_{n})\,ds\right)\,.

Since
a_{n}^{(2)}a_{n}=D_{nn}^{2}a_{n}^{2}
, we compute it at
s=0
. Under
\nu_{0}
,
m_{n}=\langle\varepsilon\rangle_{0}=\tanh(Y^{*})
, and so

D^{2}_{nn}=\left(\frac{1}{1-m_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}=\sech^{-4}(Y^{*}).

Moreover,

a_{n}^{2}=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})^{2}(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})^{2}=4(1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})(1-\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4}),

so conditioning on
Y^{*}
and using independence of replicas at
s=0
,

\big\langle a_{n}^{2}\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}=4\sech^{4}(Y^{*}).

Therefore

(5.65)

\nu_{0}(D_{nn}^{2}a_{n}^{2})=4.

Applying
Lemma
5.6
followed by
Lemma
5.5
immediately yields that
\nu^{0}_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}a_{n})=\mathsf{span}\left\{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}[D^{2}_{n}na^{2}_{n}B]\nu_{0}(\Xi^{-})\right\}
which is immediately
O(1/n)
by a combination of
Lemma
5.3
and
Lemma
5.8
, along with the absolute boundedness of
B
.

Similarly, an application of
Lemma
5.6
twice yields that
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(D^{2}_{nn}a^{2}_{n})=\mathsf{span}\left\{\nu_{s}(D^{2}_{nn}a^{2}_{n}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right\}
, which are
O(1/n)
terms by a combination of
Lemma
5.3
,
(H1)
and
(H2)
in conjunction with a
(2,4,4)
-Hölder’s inequality.

For the terms in the first bracket, observe that, by
Lemma
5.5
,

\nu_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}f^{-})=\nu_{0}(f^{-})\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[D^{2}_{nn}a_{nn}\right]=0\,,

and by
Lemma
5.14
,

\nu^{0}_{0}(a^{(2)}_{n}f^{-})=\beta^{2}\nu_{0}((f^{-})^{2})=\beta^{2}\nu(f^{2})+O(4)\,,

where the last equality by the same strategy in the proof of
Proposition
5.23
. The last remaining thing to observe is that
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(a^{(2)}_{n}f^{-})=\mathsf{span}\left\{\nu_{s}(D^{2}_{nn}a_{n}B_{s}f\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right\}
, and each such term is
O(4)
by exactly the same strategy as in the proof of
Lemma
5.22
, case-3.

Putting the above together immediately gives,

W^{(2)}_{n}=\nu(ff^{(2)})=\beta^{2}\nu(f^{2})+\frac{4}{n}+O(4)\,,

and substituting
U_{n}=\nu(f^{2})
closes the proof of the main claim. Observe that substituting the expression for
U_{n}
in
Proposition
5.23
further gives

W_{n}^{(2)}=\frac{4}{n}+\beta^{2}\frac{4\rho_{4}}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2})=\frac{4}{n(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})}+O(n^{-2})\,.\qed

Bounds on “diagonally-weighted” tracial terms

Controlling certain remainder terms in
\mathcal{E}_{D}
and
\mathcal{E}_{A}
will requiring bounding terms where there are “diagonally-weighted” (by
D_{ii}
or
D^{2}_{ii}
) Gibbs averages (in expectation over the disorder) of a rectangular sum
f
multiplied by some bounded polynomial in a spin and some power of a bulk deviation (such as centered overlaps and magnetizations).

This is accomplished by extending the cavity estimates of the type in
Proposition
5.21
and
Proposition
5.28
with diagonal tilts, by explicitly evaluating the Gibbs derivative at
s=0
by using the fact that
D_{nn}
can be exactly estimated in the cavity field, and using Hölder-type estimates on the “bulk term” to bound its second derivative.

Lemma 5.25
(The
a_{n}
–term is
O(n^{-2})
for linear bulk deviations)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Assume
(H1)
–
(H3)
and
Lemma
5.3
.
Let
\Xi
be any centered
linear
bulk deviation of the form

\Xi\in\mathsf{span}\{\,M_{a}-m^{*},\;R_{ab}-q^{*}\,\}\,,

where
a,b
are among a fixed finite set of replica indices.
Define

B:=D_{nn}\,(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,a_{n}=D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})^{2}\,.

Then

(5.66)

\frac{1}{n}\,\nu(B\,\Xi)=O(n^{-2})\,.

Proof.

The proof will follow by the standard Taylor expansion, and control of the second derivative. We will isolate the “cavity” by removing the last spin, using exchangeability, and the exact factorization at
s=0
given by
Lemma
5.5
.

Split
\Xi
into a bulk part plus an explicit
O(1/n)
last-spin correction.
If
\Xi=M_{a}-m^{*}
then

M_{a}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i<n}\rho_{i}^{a}+\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{a}=:M_{a}^{-}+\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{a}\,,

which yields
\Xi=(M_{a}^{-}-m^{*})+\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{a}
.
Similarly, for
\Xi=R_{ab}-q^{*}
,

R_{ab}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i<n}\rho_{i}^{a}\rho_{i}^{b}+\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{a}\varepsilon^{b}=:R^{-}_{ab}+\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{a}\varepsilon^{b}\,,

which gives
\Xi=(R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*})+\frac{1}{n}\varepsilon^{a}\varepsilon^{b}
.
In either case we can write

\Xi=\Xi^{-}+\frac{1}{n}\,\Pi(\varepsilon),

where
\Xi^{-}
depends only on the bulk spins
\rho
and

\Pi(\varepsilon)
is a bounded polynomial in finitely many last spins with
|\Pi|\leqslant 1
.

Therefore

\nu(B\Xi)=\nu(B\Xi^{-})+\frac{1}{n}\nu(B\,\Pi(\varepsilon)).

Since
|(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})a_{n}|\leqslant 8
,
|\Pi|\leqslant 1
and
Lemma
5.3
implies bounded moments for
D_{nn}
,

|\nu(B\,\Pi)|\leqslant 8\,|\nu(D_{nn})|\leqslant 8\,\|D_{nn}\|_{L^{1}(\nu)}=O(1)

Hence

\frac{1}{n}\nu(B\,\Pi)=O(n^{-1}).

Thus it remains to prove

(5.67)

\nu(B\,\Xi^{-})=O(n^{-1}).

Apply the Taylor expansion to
\nu(B\Xi^{-})
around
s=0
.
Let
F:=B\,\Xi^{-}
, viewed as a function of
k
replicas. Using the two-term Taylor formula along the interpolation
(\nu_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
,

\nu(F)=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu_{0}^{0}(F)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\,\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,ds.

Prove
\nu_{0}(F)=0
.
At
s=0
, by
Lemma
5.5
, the last spins
(\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{k})
are conditionally i.i.d. given
Y^{*}
.
Moreover,
D_{nn}
is a function of
Y^{*}
only at
s=0
, since
m_{n}=\tanh(Y^{*})
and
D_{nn}=\sech^{-2}(Y^{*})
. At the same time,
\Xi^{-}
depends only on the bulk spins
\rho^{j}
, which makes it independent of the last spins given
Y^{*}
.
Therefore,

\nu_{0}(F)=\nu_{0}(\Xi^{-})\,\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Big[D_{nn}\,\big\langle(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})a_{n}\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}\Big].

But
a_{n}
contains the factor
(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})
, and conditional on
Y^{*}
,

\big\langle\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2}\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}=\tanh(Y^{*})-\tanh(Y^{*})=0,

hence
\langle(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})a_{n}\rangle_{Y^{*}}=0
and thus
\nu_{0}(F)=0
.

Bound
\nu_{0}^{0}(F)
by
O(n^{-1})
using
(2,2,4)
-Hölder.
Apply
Lemma
5.6
at
s=0
to the observable
F
.
Every term in
\nu_{0}^{0}(F)
is a finite linear combination of expectations of the form

\beta^{2}\,\nu_{0}\left(B^{\prime}\,\Xi^{-}\,\Delta\right),

where:

•

B^{\prime}
is a bounded polynomial in finitely many last spins multiplied by
D_{nn}
, hence

\|B^{\prime}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{0})}=O(1)
for all fixed
p
by
Lemma
5.3
, which comes from reasoning similar to that used to bound
\nu(B\Xi)
above.

•

\Delta
is a centered bulk fluctuation of the form

\Delta\in\{R^{-}_{uv}-q^{*},\;M^{-}_{u}-m^{*}\}
for some replica indices
u,v
.

By Hölder’s inequality with exponents
(2,4,4)
,

\big|\nu_{0}(B^{\prime}\,\Xi^{-}\,\Delta)\big|\leqslant\|B^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{0})}\,\|\Xi^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{0})}\,\|\Delta\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{0})}.

Using the concentration assumptions (
5.29
) (and replica exchangeability),

\|\Xi^{-}\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{0})}=O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\|\Delta\|_{L^{4}(\nu_{0})}=O(n^{-1/2}),

and
\|B^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{0})}=O(1)
, hence each summand is
O(n^{-1})
.
Since there are only finitely many summands,

\nu_{0}^{0}(F)=O(n^{-1}).

Bounding
|\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)|
in the Taylor remainder.
Differentiating the terms in
\nu^{\prime}_{s}(F)
once more by applying
Lemma
5.6
yields that each term in
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)
is a finite linear combination of terms of the form

\nu_{s}\left(B_{s}\,\Xi^{-}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\right)\,,

where
B_{s}
is again a bounded last-spin polynomial times
D_{nn}
, and
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
are centered bulk fluctuations.
Applying Hölder with exponents
(4,4,4,4)
and invoking
Lemma
5.3
gives

\big|\nu_{s}(B_{s}\,\Xi^{-}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2})\big|\leqslant\|B_{s}\|_{L^{4}}\,\|\Xi^{-}\|_{L^{4}}\,\|\Delta_{1}\|_{L^{4}}\,\|\Delta_{2}\|_{L^{4}}=O(1)\cdot O(n^{-1/2})\cdot O(n^{-1/2})\cdot O(n^{-1/2})=O(n^{-3/2}),

uniformly in
s\in[0,1]
. Integrating against
(1-s)
preserves the order, so the remainder is
O(n^{-3/2})
.

Combining the previous bounds immediately yields

\nu(B\Xi^{-})=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu_{0}^{0}(F)+O(n^{-3/2})=0+O(n^{-1})+O(n^{-3/2})=O(n^{-1}),

which is (
5.67
). This immediately yields
\nu(B\Xi)=O(n^{-1})
, hence

\frac{1}{n}\,\nu(B\Xi)=O(n^{-2})\,.\qed

Remark 5.26
.

Note the following important property about the proof of
Lemma
5.25
– The
specific
power of
D_{nn}
and the choice of
(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})
is not necessary for the proof to work. The proof would go through for any
B
of the form

B:=D^{k}_{nn}p_{d}(\varepsilon^{3},\varepsilon^{4},\dots,\varepsilon^{\ell})a_{n}\,,

for
p_{d}
being a degree-
d
polynomial in replicas not including
1
and
2
, and
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. This is so because
\nu_{0}(B\Xi^{-})=0
for such a choice (as the
(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})
provides the “centering” term in
a_{n}
, conditional on
Y^{*}
) and the scaling of the remaining bounds on
\nu^{0}_{0}(B\Xi^{-})
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(B\Xi^{-})
are also unchanged because of the application of Hölder’s inequality. This also applies to the proof of
Lemma
5.27
.

Lemma 5.27
(Diagonal
D
–weighted one-leg term)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Assume
(H1)
–
(H3)
and
Proposition
5.21
.
Let
f_{1234}
be the rectangular sum and let
\Xi
be any centered bulk deviation
depending on finitely many replicas (e.g.
\Xi=M_{a}-m^{*}
or
\Xi=R_{ab}-q^{*}
).
Then

(5.68)

\frac{n^{2}}{4}\,\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{n}D_{jj}\,\Big\langle(\sigma_{j}^{3}-\sigma_{j}^{4})\,\Xi\,f_{1234}\Big\rangle\Bigg]\Bigg|\;=\;O(n).

More precisely,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{n}D_{jj}\,\Big\langle(\sigma_{j}^{3}-\sigma_{j}^{4})\,\Xi\,f_{1234}\Big\rangle\Bigg]\;=\;O(n^{-1}).

Proof.

The proof proceeds by “decoupling” the final site after invoking exchangeability, writing down the second-order Taylor expansion, applying exact estimates at
s=0
, and bounding the derivative uniformly thereafter by appealing to
Lemma
5.5
.

Reducing the sum over
j
to a single cavity via exchangeability.
By exchangeability of sites under
(A,y_{t})
,

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{n}D_{jj}\,\big\langle(\sigma_{j}^{3}-\sigma_{j}^{4})\,\Xi\,f_{1234}\big\rangle\Bigg]=n\;\nu\Big(D_{nn}\,(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\Xi\,f_{1234}\Big),

where
\sigma=(\rho,\varepsilon)
with
\varepsilon=\sigma_{n}
and
\nu(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}\langle\cdot\rangle
.

Thus, it suffices to show that

(5.69)

\nu\Big(D_{nn}\,(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\Xi\,f_{1234}\Big)=O(n^{-2}).

Separating the bulk from
f_{1234}
.
Write

f_{1234}=f^{-}_{1234}+\frac{1}{n}a_{n},

where
f^{-}_{1234}
depends only on the bulk spins
\rho
.
Then

\nu\Big(D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\Xi f_{1234}\Big)=\nu\Big(D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\Xi f^{-}_{1234}\Big)+\frac{1}{n}\,\nu\Big(D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\Xi a_{n}\Big).

The second term is
O(n^{-2})
by
Lemma
5.25
.
So, the goal is to now show that

(5.70)

\nu\Big(D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\Xi f^{-}_{1234}\Big)=O(n^{-2}).

Apply the Taylor expansion to
\nu(D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\Xi f^{-}_{1234})
.
Let

F:=D_{nn}(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,\Xi\,f^{-}_{1234}.

Apply the two-term Taylor formula along the interpolation
(\nu_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
to obtain

\nu(F)=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu_{0}^{0}(F)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,ds.

We prove that each term is
O(n^{-2})
.

Prove that
\nu_{0}(F)=0
.
At
s=0
, by
Lemma
5.5
, conditional on
Y^{*}
the last spins

\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{4}
are i.i.d. with mean
\tanh(Y^{*})
.
Hence
\langle\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4}\rangle_{Y^{*}}=0
, and since
D_{nn}
has finite moments by
Lemma
5.3
,

\nu_{0}(F)=\nu_{0}\Big(D_{nn}\,\Xi\,f^{-}_{1234}\,\big\langle\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4}\big\rangle_{Y^{*}}\Big)=0.

Bound the first derivative term
\nu_{0}^{0}(F)
as
O(n^{-2})
.
Apply
Lemma
5.6
at
s=0
to the observable
F
with
k=4
.
Because the last-spin polynomial contains
(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})
, the only nonzero contributions
in the SK overlap part are those where the pair
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
involves replica
3
or
4
.
Similarly, in the CW drift part, only terms with
\ell=3,4
survive.
Consequently one obtains an identity of the form

(5.71)

\nu_{0}^{0}(F)=\beta^{2}\sum_{u}c_{u}\;\nu_{0}\Big(f^{-}_{1234}\,\Xi\,\Delta_{u}\Big),

where

•

each coefficient
c_{u}
is a last-spin expectation at
s=0
of a bounded polynomial in
(\varepsilon^{1},\dots,\varepsilon^{6})

multiplied by
D_{nn}
; hence
|c_{u}|\leqslant C
by the fact
c_{u}=\nu_{0}(B_{u}D_{nn})\leqslant\left(\sup B_{s}\right)|\nu_{0}(D_{nn})|
and
Lemma
5.3
, and

•

each
\Delta_{u}
is a
centered linear bulk fluctuation
, namely one of

R^{-}_{3,a}-R^{-}_{4,a},\qquad M^{-}_{3}-M^{-}_{4},\qquad\text{for some }a\in\{1,2,5\}.

In particular,
\Xi\Delta_{u}
is a quadratic bulk observable in centered deviations.
Therefore each term in (
5.71
) is exactly of the type covered by
Proposition
5.21
, which immeidiately implies that

\nu_{0}\Big(f^{-}_{1234}\,\Xi\,\Delta_{u}\Big)=O(n^{-2}).

Adding the finitely many terms of this form gives

\nu_{0}^{0}(F)=O(n^{-2}).

Apply Hölder to the Taylor remainder.
We now apply
Lemma
5.6
once more to
\nu_{s}^{\prime}(F)
to obtain that each term in
\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)
is a finite linear combination of expectations of the form

\nu_{s}\Big(B_{s}\;D_{nn}\;f^{-}_{1234}\;\Xi\;\Xi_{1}\;\Xi_{2}\Big),

where
B_{s}
is a bounded polynomial in finitely many last spins and
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}
are centered bulk fluctuations
of the form
(R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*})
or
(M^{-}_{a}-m^{*})
.
By applying
(2,8,8,8,8)
-Hölder’s, and using
Lemma
5.3
to control the
D_{nn}
moments, along with (
5.29
) together with
Lemma
5.16
to control
f^{-}_{1234}
and the centered deviations, one obtains the following bound via a
(2,8,8,8,8)
-Hölder”s inequality

\big|\nu_{s}^{\prime\prime}(F)\big|\;\leqslant\;C\;\|D_{nn}\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{s})}\;\|f^{-}_{1234}\|_{L^{8}(\nu_{s})}\;\|\Xi\|_{L^{8}(\nu_{s})}\;\|\Xi_{1}\|_{L^{8}(\nu_{s})}\;\|\Xi_{2}\|_{L^{8}(\nu_{s})}\;=\;O(n^{-2})\,,

and so

\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\nu^{\prime\prime}(s)ds\leqslant|\nu^{\prime\prime}(s)|=O(n^{-2})\,.

Combining the bounds above gives (
5.70
). This yields the final result of (
5.69
) as

\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{n}D_{jj}\,\big\langle(\sigma_{j}^{3}-\sigma_{j}^{4})\,\Xi\,f_{1234}\big\rangle\Bigg]=n\cdot O(n^{-2})=O(n^{-1})\,.\qed

The final lemma we need to assert that the remainder terms in
\mathcal{E}_{A}
are
O(1)
is a mild generalization of
Proposition
5.21
to handle cubic moments across
6
replicas (as opposed to
4
).

Proposition 5.28
(Replicon-weighted cubic terms with
general
quadratic weights)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Fix a finite set of replica indices and
s\in[0,1]
.
Let

\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}:=R^{-}_{ab}-q^{*},\qquad\overline{M}^{-}_{a}:=M^{-}_{a}-m^{*},\qquad f^{-}:=\overline{R}^{-}_{13}-\overline{R}^{-}_{14}-\overline{R}^{-}_{23}+\overline{R}^{-}_{24}.

Let
\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{full}}
be the linear span of
all
quadratic bulk monomials built from these centered
deviations:

\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{full}}:=\mathrm{span}\Big\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}\overline{R}^{-}_{cd},\ \overline{R}^{-}_{ab}\overline{M}^{-}_{c},\ \overline{M}^{-}_{a}\overline{M}^{-}_{b}\Big\},

where
a,b,c,d
range over the fixed finite replica set. Then, for every
G^{-}\in\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{full}}
,

\displaystyle\nu_{s}\big(f^{-}\,G^{-}\big)

\displaystyle=O(4)

\displaystyle\nu_{s}\left(\widehat{f}^{-}\,\widehat{G}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=O(4)\,.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
Proposition
5.21
, and indicate the only point where the enlarged class

\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{\mathrm{full}}
matters.

Bounding
G^{-}
in
L^{p}
.
Each
\overline{R}^{-}_{ab}
and
\overline{M}^{-}_{a}
is
O(n^{-1/2})
in every
L^{p}(\nu_{s})
uniformly in
s
by the moment
bounds implied by
(H1)
–
(H3)
and
Lemma
5.10
.
This means that any quadratic monomial in these variables is
O(n^{-1})
in every
L^{p}
,
and so for every fixed
p
,

\|G^{-}\|_{L^{p}(\nu_{s})}=O(n^{-1})\qquad\text{uniformly in }s\in[0,1].

Reducing
\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})
to a last-spin observable via exchangeability.
Using exchangeability, write
f=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}
with

a_{i}=(\sigma_{i}^{1}-\sigma_{i}^{2})(\sigma_{i}^{3}-\sigma_{i}^{4})
and
f=f^{-}+\frac{1}{n}a_{n}
.
Then site exchangeability gives

\nu_{s}(f\,G^{-})=\nu_{s}(a_{n}\,G^{-}),

and since
\|G^{-}\|_{L^{1}}=O(n^{-1})
and
|a_{n}|\leqslant 4
,

\nu_{s}(f\,G^{-})-\nu_{s}(f^{-}\,G^{-})=\frac{1}{n}\nu_{s}(a_{n}G^{-})=O(n^{-2})=O(4).

Thus

\nu_{s}(f^{-}\,G^{-})=\nu_{s}(a_{n}G^{-})+O(4).

Taylor expanding
\nu_{s}(a_{n}G^{-})
around
s=0
.
Set
F:=(\varepsilon^{1}-\varepsilon^{2})(\varepsilon^{3}-\varepsilon^{4})\,G^{-}
.
Then
\nu_{s}(a_{n}G^{-})=\nu_{s}(F)
and

\nu_{s}(F)=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu_{0}^{0}(F)+\int_{0}^{s}(s-u)\,\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(F)\,du.

At
s=0
, factorization (
Lemma
5.5
) gives
\nu_{0}(F)=0
.
By the unscaled rectangular cancellation lemma (
Lemma
5.12
),

\nu_{0}^{0}(F)=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu_{0}(f^{-}G^{-}).

O(4)
bound for the Taylor remainder.
Differentiate twice using the derivative identity (
Lemma
5.6
).
Exactly as in the proof of
Proposition
5.21
,
every term in
\nu_{u}^{\prime\prime}(F)
is a finite linear combination of expectations of the form

\nu_{u}\Big(B_{u}\,G^{-}\,\Xi_{1}\,\Xi_{2}\Big),

where
B_{u}
is a bounded polynomial in finitely many last spins, and

\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}\in\{\overline{R}^{-}_{ab},\overline{M}^{-}_{a}\}
.
By Hölder and the
L^{p}
size bounds (
\|G^{-}\|_{L^{p}}=O(n^{-1})
and
\|\Xi_{j}\|_{L^{p}}=O(n^{-1/2})
),
each such term is
O(n^{-2})=O(4)
.
Thus, the second-order Taylor remainder is
O(4)
.

Using linear stability at
\beta<1
.
We have shown

\nu_{s}(a_{n}G^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu_{0}(f^{-}G^{-})+O(4).

Replacing
\nu_{0}(f^{-}G^{-})
by
\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})
costs only
O(4)
by the same argument in
Proposition
5.21
.
Therefore

\nu_{s}(a_{n}G^{-})=\beta^{2}\rho_{4}\,\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})+O(4).

Combining the above with
\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})=\nu_{s}(a_{n}G^{-})+O(4)
immediately gives

(1-\beta^{2}\rho_{4})\,\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})=O(4),

and since
\beta^{2}\rho_{4}<1
for
\beta<1/2
, we conclude
\nu_{s}(f^{-}G^{-})=O(4)
.
∎

Main cavity estimate via single-diagonal tilts

The final estimate that is key to obtaining the bound
\mathcal{E}_{A}+\mathcal{E}_{D}=O(1)
is the observation that, up to
O(1)
factors,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\mathsf{Tr}[PDP]]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\mathsf{Tr}[P]]+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c^{2}\mathsf{Tr}[P^{2}]]\,.

This requires using the limits for
U_{n}
and
V_{n}
established in
Proposition
5.23
in conjunction with a slight modification of the limit for
W^{(2)}_{n}
that happens when there is conjugation by the elements of the diagonal matrix
D
.

Lemma 5.29
(Singly scaled cancellation at
s=0
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let the last-spin variable
\varepsilon:=\sigma_{n}
and bulk spins

\sigma^{-}:=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n-1})
.
Let
D_{nn}=(1-m_{n}^{2})^{-1}
and
P_{nn}=1-m_{n}^{2}
computed under
\nu_{s}
.
Fix any bounded bulk observable
F^{-}=F^{-}(\sigma^{1,-},\dots,\sigma^{4,-})
depending on finitely
many replicas and only on the first
n-1
coordinates.
Then,

(5.72)

\nu_{0}^{0}\!\Big(D_{nn}\,a_{n}\,f^{-}\,F^{-}\Big)=\beta^{2}\,\nu_{0}\!\Big(P_{nn}\,(f^{-})^{2}\,F^{-}\Big)\,.

Proof.

We use the same differentiation formula for
\nu_{s}^{\prime}(\cdot)
as the one used throughout the cavity CLT proofs, and evaluate it at
s=0
. Note that

\nu^{0}_{0}(D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}F^{-})=\left(D^{\prime}_{nn}\nu_{0}(a_{n}f^{-}F^{-})\right)+\left(D_{nn}\nu^{\prime}_{0}(a_{n}f^{-}F^{-})\right)=\nu^{\prime}_{0}(D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}F^{-})\,.

At
s=0
, apply
Lemma
5.6
with the function of evaluation
D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}F^{-}
. Using
Lemma
5.5
, since
\left\langle a_{n}\varepsilon^{\ell}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}=0
for any
\ell\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
by the centering of
a_{n}
, all the (CW) terms in the derivative vanish. Similarly, since
\left\langle\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{5}a_{n}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}=0
, all but the first set of the (SK) terms are also
0
. So, we evaluate the remaining list of terms, that is

\nu^{0}_{0}\left(D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}F^{-}\right)=\beta^{2}\sum_{1\leqslant\ell<\ell^{\prime}\leqslant 4}\nu_{0}\left(f^{-}F^{-}(R_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}-q^{*})\right)\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[D_{nn}(Y^{*})\left\langle a_{n}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{Y^{*}}\right]\,,

and independence implies the only contributing pairs are
(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4)
. It’s easy to see that the following simplifications ensue

a_{n}\varepsilon^{\ell}\varepsilon^{\ell^{\prime}}=(-1)^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}}(1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})(1-\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4})\,,

for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in\{3,4\}
. Now,

(-1)^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[D_{nn}(1-\varepsilon^{1}\varepsilon^{2})(1-\varepsilon^{3}\varepsilon^{4})\right]=(-1)^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}\left[P^{-1}_{nn}P^{2}_{nn}\right]=(-1)^{\ell+\ell^{\prime}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y^{*}}[P_{nn}]\,.

Putting the above together with the definition of
f^{-}=(R^{-}_{1,3}-q^{*})+(R^{-}_{2,4}-q^{*})-(R^{-}_{2,3}-q^{*})-(R^{-}_{1,4}-q^{*})
gives that

\nu^{0}_{0}(D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}F^{-})=\beta^{2}\nu_{0}\left(P_{nn}(f^{-})^{2}F^{-}\right)\,.\qed

Lemma 5.30
(Cavity identity for the diagonally-titled trace of the squared-covariance)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Assume
(H1)–(H3)
and
Proposition
5.21
.
Then

(5.73)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[c\,\mathsf{Tr}(PDP)\big]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[c\,\mathsf{Tr}(P)\big]+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[c^{2}\,\mathsf{Tr}(P^{2})\big]+O(1).

Proof.

By
Lemma
A.3
and the independence of replicas,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\,\mathsf{Tr}(PDP)]=\frac{n^{2}}{4}\,\nu\left(c\,f\,\widehat{f}\right),\qquad\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c^{2}\,\mathsf{Tr}(P^{2})]=\frac{n^{2}}{4}\,\nu\big(c^{2}\,f^{2}\big).

So, it suffices to prove

(5.74)

\nu\big(c\,f\,\widehat{f}\big)=\frac{4}{n^{2}}\,\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\,\mathsf{Tr}(P)]+\nu\big(c^{2}\,f^{2}\big)+O(4)\,.

Exchangeability reduction to the last site using the mean of
\widehat{f}
.
Write
\widehat{f}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}
with
\widehat{a}_{i}:=D_{ii}a_{i}
.
Then, by site exchangeability under
\nu
,

\nu(cf\widehat{f})=\nu\Big(cf\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}\Big)=\nu\big(cf\widehat{a}_{n}\big)=\nu\big(c\,D_{nn}\,a_{n}\,f\big).

Decomposing
f=f^{-}+\frac{1}{n}a_{n}
gives

(5.75)

\nu(cf\widehat{f})=\nu\big(c\,D_{nn}\,a_{n}\,f^{-}\big)+\frac{1}{n}\,\nu\big(c\,D_{nn}\,a_{n}^{2}\big).

Identifying the diagonal term as
exactly

\frac{4}{n^{2}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\mathsf{Tr}(P)]
.
As in the proof of
Lemma
5.29
, because replicas are i.i.d. under the Gibbs measure, the following holds for any fixed instance of the disorder,

\langle D_{nn}a_{n}^{2}\rangle=4P_{nn}.

Therefore, using the simplification above and exchangeability once more yields

(5.76)

\frac{1}{n}\,\nu(cD_{nn}a_{n}^{2})=\frac{4}{n}\,\nu(cP_{nn})=\frac{4}{n^{2}}\left(n\nu(cP_{nn})\right)=\frac{4}{n^{2}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}cP_{ii}\right]=\frac{4}{n^{2}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[c\mathsf{Tr}[P]\right].

Cavity expansion for the off-diagonal term.
Setting
F:=c\,D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}
and applying the two-term Taylor formula in the cavity interpolation gives

\nu(F)=\nu_{0}(F)+\nu_{0}^{0}(F)+\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)\,ds.

At
s=0
, conditional on the last-spin field
Y^{*}
one has
\langle a_{n}\mid Y^{*}\rangle=0
, while
cD_{nn}
depends only on
(Y^{*},\sigma^{-})
but not on the replica labels,
and so
\nu_{0}(F)=0
. Furthermore, by
Lemma
5.29
with
F^{-}=c
10
10

10

Note that
c=c^{-}+\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\varepsilon^{5}\right\rangle^{2}
, and one can observe that
\nu^{0}_{0}(\left\langle\varepsilon^{5}\right\rangle^{2}D_{nn}a_{n}f^{-})=\beta^{2}\nu_{0}\left(\left\langle\varepsilon^{5}\right\rangle^{2}P_{nn}(f^{-})^{2}\right)
by the same calculations as those in the proof of
Lemma
5.29
with the observation that
\left\langle\varepsilon^{5}\right\rangle^{2}
is a constant with respect to the symmetries that result in cancellations after the application of
Lemma
5.5
.
,

(5.77)

\nu^{0}_{0}(cD_{nn}a_{n}f^{-})=\beta^{2}\,\nu_{0}\left(c\,P_{nn}\,(f^{-})^{2}\right).

To bound the second derivative, note that two applications of
Lemma
5.6
imply that
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)
is given by
\mathsf{span}\left\{\nu_{s}(Bf^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right\}
with
B_{s}=D_{nn}a_{n}\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon)
. Then, by the same strategy used to bound the deviation
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left(\nu_{s}(B_{s}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})-\nu_{s}(B)\nu_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right)=O(4)
in the proof of
Proposition
5.23
, we obtain the fact that
\nu^{\prime\prime}_{s}(F)=O(4)
.

Replacing
\nu_{0}(cP_{nn}(f^{-})^{2})
by
\nu(cP_{nn}f^{2})
up to
O(4)
.
Simplifying as in the proof of
Proposition
5.23
gives

\displaystyle\nu(cP_{nn}f^{2})-\nu_{0}(cP_{nn}(f^{-})^{2})=\left(\nu(cP_{nn}f^{2})-\nu(cP_{nn}(f^{-})^{2})\right)+\left(\nu(cP_{nn}(f^{-})^{2})-\nu_{0}(cP_{nn}(f^{-})^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2}{n}\nu(cP_{nn}a_{n}f^{-})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\nu(cP_{nn}a^{2}_{n})\right)+\int_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime}_{s}(cP_{nn}(f^{-})^{2})\,ds

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:deriv]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:deriv}},}\,|cP_{nn}a^{2})n|\leqslant C}\frac{2}{n}\left(\nu_{0}(cP_{nn}a_{n}f^{-})+\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{span}\left\{\nu_{s}(cP_{nn}a_{n}f^{-}\Xi^{-})\right\}\,ds\right)+\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{span}\left\{\nu_{s}\left(cP_{nn}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2}\right)\right\}\,ds+O(4)

\displaystyle=_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[lem:fact-s0]{Lemma~\ref*{lem:fact-s0}}\,+\,}(2,2)-\text{H\"{o}lder's}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathsf{span}\left\{\nu_{s}\left(cP_{nn}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2}\right)\right\}\,ds+O(4)\,.

Now, observe the following fact using a re-centering argument in conjunction with
(4,4,4,4)
-Hölder’s and a final two-term Taylor expansion,

\displaystyle\nu_{s}\left(cP_{nn}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=_{c=\beta^{2}\left\langle(1-R_{56})\right\rangle}\beta^{2}(1+q^{*})\nu_{s}(P_{nn}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})-\beta^{2}\nu_{s}\left(P_{nn}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2}(R_{56}-q^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{|P_{nn}|\leqslant 1,\text{\hyperref@@ii[prop:D17]{Proposition~\ref*{prop:D17}}}}\beta^{2}(1+q^{*})\nu_{s}(P_{nn}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})+O(4)

\displaystyle=O(4)\,,

where the last equality uses the fact that
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left(\nu_{S}(P_{nn}f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})-\nu_{s}(P_{nn})\nu_{s}(f^{-}\Xi^{-}_{1}\Xi^{-}_{2})\right)=O(4)
, which follows via uses of Hölder’s inequality and
Proposition
5.21
to control the derivative in a similar way to the way it was used in the proof of
Proposition
5.23
.
Inserting this into (
5.77
) yields

(5.78)

\nu(cD_{nn}a_{n}f^{-})=\beta^{2}\,\nu\big(c\,P_{nn}\,f^{2}\big)+O(4).

Invoking exact exchangeability via
\beta^{2}\nu(cP_{nn}f^{2})=\nu(c^{2}f^{2})
.
Recall
c=\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\mathsf{Tr}(P)
, hence

\beta^{2}\,\nu(cP_{nn}f^{2})=\frac{\beta^{4}}{n}\,\nu\big(\mathsf{Tr}(P)\,P_{nn}\,f^{2}\big).

We now expand
\mathsf{Tr}(P)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}P_{ii}
and invoke exchangeability of the joint law of
\{P_{ii}\}_{i\leqslant n}
together with the site-symmetry of
f^{2}
to obtain

\nu\big(\mathsf{Tr}(P)\,P_{nn}\,f^{2}\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nu(P_{ii}P_{nn}f^{2})=\nu(P_{nn}^{2}f^{2})+(n-1)\nu(P_{11}P_{22}f^{2}).

On the other hand,

\frac{1}{n}\,\nu\big(\mathsf{Tr}(P)^{2}f^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j}\nu(P_{ii}P_{jj}f^{2})=\nu(P_{nn}^{2}f^{2})+(n-1)\nu(P_{11}P_{22}f^{2}),

so

\nu\big(\mathsf{Tr}(P)\,P_{nn}\,f^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{n}\,\nu\big(\mathsf{Tr}(P)^{2}f^{2}\big).

Therefore

\beta^{2}\,\nu(cP_{nn}f^{2})=\frac{\beta^{4}}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\,\nu(\mathsf{Tr}(P)^{2}f^{2})=\nu\Big(\frac{\beta^{4}}{n^{2}}\mathsf{Tr}(P)^{2}\,f^{2}\Big)=\nu(c^{2}f^{2}).

Combining with (
5.78
) gives

(5.79)

\nu(cD_{nn}a_{n}f^{-})=\nu(c^{2}f^{2})+O(4).

Inserting (
5.76
) and (
5.79
) into (
5.75
) gives

\nu(cf\widehat{f})=\nu(c^{2}f^{2})+\frac{4}{n^{2}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[c\mathsf{Tr}(P)]+O(4).

This is exactly (
5.74
). Multiplying by
n^{2}/4
and using
Lemma
A.3
yields (
5.73
).
∎

5.5.
Uniform spectral bound for
\mathbf{M}

In
Lemma
5.8
it was critical to argue that
\beta^{2}\rho(M)<1
for every
\beta<1/2
to allow for the inversion of the operator
I-\beta^{2}M
to not cause a divergence when obtaining
O(1)
fluctuations for the centered bulk observables. In the claim below, we provide a small lemma proving that the spectral radius of this operator arising from the cavity interpolation is indeed bounded as desired at high-temperature.

Lemma 5.31
(Spectral-radius bound for
\beta^{2}\mathbf{M}
when
\beta<1/2
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
Y^{*}
be a random variable that is almost-surely finite. Set
T:=\tanh(Y^{*})\in[-1,1]
. Define

\mu_{r}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[T^{r}],\qquad r\in\{1,2,3,4\},

and

\mathbf{M}:=\begin{pmatrix}1-4\mu_{2}+3\mu_{4}&2(\mu_{1}-\mu_{3})\\
\mu_{3}-\mu_{1}&1-\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Then for every
\beta\in(0,1/2)
,

\rho(\beta^{2}\mathbf{M})\;<\;1.

Proof.

Factorization yields

1-4T^{2}+3T^{4}=(1-T^{2})(1-3T^{2}),\qquad T-T^{3}=T(1-T^{2})\,,

for
T\in[-1,1]
. Hence, with
S:=1-T^{2}=\sech^{2}(Y^{*})\in[0,1]
, we can rewrite the entries of
\mathbf{M}
as

1-4\mu_{2}+3\mu_{4}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[(1-4T^{2}+3T^{4})]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[S(1-3T^{2})],

\mu_{1}-\mu_{3}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[T-T^{3}]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[T(1-T^{2})]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[TS],

1-\mu_{2}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[1-T^{2}]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[S],\qquad\mu_{3}-\mu_{1}=-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[TS].

This allows
\mathbf{M}
to be written as the expectation of a random matrix depending only on
T
as

(5.80)

\mathbf{M}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{X}(T)\right]:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}(1-T^{2})\begin{pmatrix}1-3T^{2}&2T\\
-T&1\end{pmatrix}.

For any square matrix
\mathbf{M}
, the spectral radius is bounded by the operator norm as

\rho(\mathbf{M})\leqslant\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}.

By the convexity of the operator norm,

\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}=\left\lVert\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\mathbf{X}(T)]\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant_{\text{Jensen's}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\left\lVert\mathbf{X}(T)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\big].

Upper bounding the operator norm by the Frobenius norm further gives

\rho(\mathbf{M})\leqslant\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\|\mathbf{X}(T)\|_{F}\big]\leqslant\sup_{|T|\leqslant 1}\|\mathbf{X}(T)\|_{F}.

To bound the supremum over
T
, note that for any
T\in[-1,1]
and
u:=T^{2}
, a direct computation yields

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{X}(T)\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-T^{2})^{2}\Big((1-3T^{2})^{2}+5T^{2}+1\Big)

\displaystyle=(1-u)^{2}\Big((1-3u)^{2}+5u+1\Big)

\displaystyle=(1-u)^{2}(2-u+9u^{2})

\displaystyle=9u^{4}-19u^{3}+13u^{2}-5u+2\,.

Differentiating
\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}(T)}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}
with respect to
u
gives

\frac{d}{du}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}(T)}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}=(u-1)\,(36u^{2}-21u+5).

The equation
36u^{2}-21u+5
has discriminant
(-21)^{2}-4\cdot 36\cdot 5=-279<0
, so it is strictly positive for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, using the fact that
(u-1)<0
implies that
\frac{d}{du}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}(T)}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}<0
, and
\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}(T)}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}
is strictly decreasing on
[0,1]
. This implies that

\sup_{T\in[-1,1]}\|\mathbf{X}(T)\|_{F}=\sqrt{2}.

This immediately implies that

\rho(\beta^{2}\mathbf{M})\leqslant\beta^{2}\|\mathbf{M}\|_{2}\leqslant_{\left\lVert{M}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{2}}\beta^{2}\sqrt{2}<_{\beta<1/2}<1.\qed

5.6.
The Nishimori line for the planted SK model

A critical fact used to “convert” overlap-concentration in the planted SK model to magnetization concentration is the fact that the external fields introduced by the SL process force
m^{*}=q^{*}
. This is a specific consequence of the fact that the SL process induces Gaussians with the same mean and variance.

Lemma 5.32
(Nishimori condition for
\beta<1/\sqrt{3}
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Assume
0<\beta<1/\sqrt{3}
. Let
(m^{*},q^{*})
solve the fixed-point equations

\displaystyle m^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\Big[\tanh\big(t+\beta^{2}m^{*}+\sqrt{\beta^{2}q^{*}+t}\,G\big)\Big],

\displaystyle q^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\Big[\tanh^{2}\big(t+\beta^{2}m^{*}+\sqrt{\beta^{2}q^{*}+t}\,G\big)\Big].

Then
m^{*}=q^{*}
.

Proof.

Set

\alpha:=t+\beta^{2}q^{*}\geqslant 0,\qquad\delta:=\beta^{2}(m^{*}-q^{*}),

so that
t+\beta^{2}m^{*}=\alpha+\delta
and
\sqrt{\beta^{2}q^{*}+t}=\sqrt{\alpha}
.
Define

h(x):=\tanh(x)-\tanh^{2}(x).

Then, the fixed-point equations can be rewritten as

m^{*}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh(\alpha+\delta+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big],\qquad q^{*}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh^{2}(\alpha+\delta+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big],

and so

(5.81)

m^{*}-q^{*}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Big[h\big(\alpha+\delta+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G\big)\Big].

An identity at
\delta=0
.
We claim that for every
\alpha\geqslant 0
,

(5.82)

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[h(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big]=0,\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh^{2}(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big].

If
\alpha=0
, the identity is trivial. Assume
\alpha>0
.

Let
\varphi_{m,\alpha}
denote the density of
\mathcal{N}(m,\alpha)
:

\varphi_{m,\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\alpha}}\exp\!\Big(-\frac{(x-m)^{2}}{2\alpha}\Big).

Then

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh(x)\,\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)\,dx,\qquad\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh^{2}(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh^{2}(x)\,\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)\,dx.

Let
\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}
be the density of
\mathcal{N}(-\alpha,\alpha)
. Note
\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x)=\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(-x)
, so
the sum
\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}+\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}
is even, while
\tanh
is odd; hence

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh(x)\big(\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)+\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x)\big)\,dx=0,

and therefore

(5.83)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh(x)\,\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh(x)\big(\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)-\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x)\big)\,dx.

Similarly,
\tanh^{2}
is even and
\int\tanh^{2}\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}=\int\tanh^{2}\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}
(change
x\mapsto-x
), so

(5.84)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh^{2}(x)\,\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh^{2}(x)\big(\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)+\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x)\big)\,dx.

A direct computation gives the likelihood ratio

\frac{\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)}{\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x)}=\exp(2x),\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}(x)=e^{2x}\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x).

Using (
5.83
) and (
5.84
) and rewriting everything in terms of
\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}
yields

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh(x)\,(e^{2x}-1)\,\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x)\,dx,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[\tanh^{2}(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}\,G)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tanh^{2}(x)\,(e^{2x}+1)\,\varphi_{-\alpha,\alpha}(x)\,dx.

But the integrands coincide pointwise because
\tanh(x)=(e^{2x}-1)/(e^{2x}+1)
, implying

\tanh(x)(e^{2x}-1)=\tanh^{2}(x)(e^{2x}+1).

Hence the two expectations are equal, proving (
5.82
).

Lipschitz bound forces
\delta=0
for
\beta<\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
.
Differentiate

h^{\prime}(x)=\sech^{2}(x)\bigl(1-2\tanh(x)\bigr),

so
|h^{\prime}(x)|\leqslant 1\cdot 3=3
for all
x
, and thus
h
is globally
3
-Lipschitz:

|h(u)-h(v)|\leqslant 3|u-v|\quad\forall u,v\in\mathbb{R}.

Using (
5.81
) and (
5.82
),

\displaystyle|m^{*}-q^{*}|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[h(\alpha+\delta+\sqrt{\alpha}G)\big]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\big[h(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}G)\big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\Big|h(\alpha+\delta+\sqrt{\alpha}G)-h(\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha}G)\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant 3|\delta|=3\beta^{2}|m^{*}-q^{*}|.

Since
0<\beta<\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
implies
3\beta^{2}<1
, the inequality forces
|m^{*}-q^{*}|=0
, i.e.
m^{*}=q^{*}
.
∎

6.
Properties of the algorithmic stochastic process

Recall the algorithmic SDE

\displaystyle??

simulated by the main sampler (
Algorithm
2
).
Its coefficients are given by the functions

(6.1)

\displaystyle D(m)

\displaystyle:=(I_{n}-\diag(m)^{2})^{-1},

\displaystyle a(m)

\displaystyle:=\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D(m)^{-1}),

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}(m)

\displaystyle:=\left(a(m)I_{n}-\beta A+D(m)-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}.

These coefficients of the SDE for
\widehat{m}_{t}
(and consequently the SDE itself) turn out to be remarkably well-behaved under certain high-probability events on the GOE matrix
A
.
This is mainly due to the boundedness and regularity of the resolvent
(z,M)\to(z-M)^{-1}
, which
(
ASL-TAP
)
inherits from its similarity with
(
PHD
)
, the resolvent-based SDE whose diffusion coefficient is simply the Hessian of the TAP free energy
d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}
.

Because ASL-TAP is closely related to the Gibbs measure and PHD is closely related to the TAP free energy, the closeness of these two processes is central to the functioning of
Algorithm
2
.

We introduce the high-probability events in
§
6.1
, including one simple spectral norm bound and another more sophisticated “control of the diagonal” event from free probability theory.
In
§
6.2
, we collect boundedness and Lipschitzness properties for the resolvent
\widehat{Q}(m)
implied by the simple spectral norm event.
The event controlling the diagonal is stated for the resolvent
\widehat{Q}(m)
without the rank-1 term, so
§
6.3
shows that the addition of the rank-1 term doesn’t change the diagonal too much.

§
6.4
then gives statements for control of the diagonal that directly control quantities appearing in
(
ASL-TAP
)
.

§
6.5
shows that the SDEs
(
ASL-TAP
)
and
(
PHD
)
are close to each other.
Some consequences for the
(
ASL-TAP
)
trajectories are detailed in
§
6.6
, including that the trajectory almost surely stays within the open solid hypercube
(-1,1)^{n}
and that Lipschitz functionals of the trajectories obey sub-Gaussian concentration.
Finally,
§
6.7
details how these properties of
(
ASL-TAP
)
contribute to the success of the sampling algorithm
Algorithm
2
.

6.1.
High-probability events

There are two high-probability events on the GOE matrix
A
that underpin most of the well-behaved properties of the algorithmic processes and their SDE coefficients.

The first is a simple bound on the spectral norm of
A
. Let
\gamma:=(1-2\beta)/2
so that
\beta=1/2-\gamma
, and define the event

(6.2)

\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}=\left\{\vphantom{\big|}\beta\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 1-\gamma\right\}.

By
[
19
, Proposition 1]
, when
\beta<1/2
, we have
\beta\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\beta(2+\frac{1-2\beta}{2\beta})=1-\gamma
with probability at least
1-e^{-C_{\beta}n}
for some constant
C_{\beta}
.

The second event asserts that the sum
\beta A+D
for diagonal
D
hews very closely to the (uniformly) freely independent case, which includes having every diagonal entry of the squared resolvents
(z-\beta A+D)^{-2}
essentially determined by
z
and
D
.
For some constant
C_{\mathrm{free}}
,

(6.3)

\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}=\left\{\forall D\in\mathcal{D}_{n}([1,\infty))\mathrel{.}\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\left(\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-1})-\beta A+D\right)^{-2}\right]D^{2}-\frac{I_{n}}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{free}}\beta}{\gamma^{4}\sqrt{n}}\right\}.

As shown in
Corollary
B.35
, this occurs with probability at least
1-e^{-O((\log n)^{\xi})}
with some
\xi>1
.

6.2.
Basic resolvent and Lipschitzness bounds

We start with bounds implied by the simple spectral norm condition
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 6.1
(Basic resolvent bounds assuming
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
holds.
Let
D(m),a(m),\widehat{Q}(m)
be as in
(
6.1
)
.
Let also

\overline{Q}(m):=\left(a(m)I_{n}-\beta A+D(m)\right)^{-1}

be the version of
\widehat{Q}
without the rank-1 term.
Then for all
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
, we have the following bounds:

(a)

\left\lVert\overline{Q}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\gamma^{-1},\qquad\left\lVert\widehat{Q}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\gamma^{-1}.

(b)

\left\lVert D(m)\overline{Q}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2\gamma^{-1},\qquad\left\lVert D(m)\widehat{Q}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2\gamma^{-1}.

(c)

\left\lVert D(m)\overline{Q}^{2}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2\gamma^{-2},\qquad\left\lVert D(m)\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2\gamma^{-2}.

(d)

\left\lVert D(m)\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)D(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 4\gamma^{-2}.

Proof.

The
\overline{Q}
parts of
Lemma
6.1(a)
,
Lemma
6.1(b)
, and
Lemma
6.1(c)
follow directly from
Lemma
B.1
with

W\;\leftarrow\;I_{n}-\diag(m)^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad M\;\leftarrow\;a(m)I_{n}-\beta A,

so that

\min\Spec(|X|)\;\geqslant\;a(m)-(1-\gamma)+1\;\geqslant\;\gamma\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left\lVert{M}\right\rVert\;\leqslant\;2-\gamma.\qquad

For the
\widehat{Q}
parts and for
Lemma
6.1(d)
, first note that
mm^{\mathsf{T}}/n\preceq\diag(m)^{2}
, which follows by taking the inequality
\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\mathsf{T}}/n\preceq I_{n}
and multiplying by
\diag(m)
on both the left and right.
Also we have
D(m)\succeq I_{n}+\diag(m)^{2}
by convexity of
x\mapsto(1-x)^{-1}
.
We can therefore apply
Lemma
B.1
with

W\;\leftarrow\;I_{n}-\diag(m)^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad M\;\leftarrow\;a(m)I_{n}-\beta A-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}},

so that

X\;\;\leftarrow\;\;a(m)I_{n}-\beta A+D(m)-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}}\;\;\succeq\;\;0-(1-\gamma)I_{n}+(I_{n}+\diag(m)^{2})-2\beta^{2}\diag(m)^{2}\;\;\succeq\;\;\gamma I_{n}.

So
\min\Spec(|X|)\geqslant\gamma
and
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2-\gamma
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
(Basic resolvent Lipschitzness assuming
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

In the same setting as
Lemma
6.1
, over the domain
m\in(-1,1)^{m}
, we have the following Lipschitz constants with respect to
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
:

(a)

\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant\frac{\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(2+8\beta^{2}+\frac{16\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

(b)

\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant\frac{\gamma^{-3}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(4+16\beta^{2}+\frac{32\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

(c)

\left\lVert{D\widehat{Q}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant\frac{\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(4+16\beta^{2}+\frac{32\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

(d)

\left\lVert{D\widehat{Q}^{2}D}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant\frac{\gamma^{-3}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(16+64\beta^{2}+\frac{128\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

Proof.

We apply
Lemma
B.4
with

W\;\leftarrow\;I_{n}-\diag(m)^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad M\;\leftarrow\;aI_{n}-\beta A-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}},

following the proof of
Lemma
6.1
.
Then
da=-2\beta^{2}m^{\mathsf{T}}dmI_{n}/n
, so

dW\,=\,-2\diag(m\odot dm)\qquad\text{and}\qquad dM\,=\,-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\left(m^{\mathsf{T}}dmI_{n}+(dm)m^{\mathsf{T}}+m(dm)^{\mathsf{T}}\right),

and we have

\left\lVert{dW}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}\;\leqslant\;\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\left\lVert{dm}\right\rVert\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left\lVert{dM}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}\;\leqslant\;\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\left(\sqrt{n}+2\right)\left\lVert{dm}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}.

Then by
Lemma
B.4(a)
,
Lemma
B.4(b)
,
Lemma
B.4(c)
, and
Lemma
B.4(e)
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{d\widehat{Q}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\left(\frac{2\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{8\beta^{2}\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\left\lVert{dm}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}},

\displaystyle\left\lVert{d\widehat{Q}^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\left(\frac{4\gamma^{-3}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{16\beta^{2}\gamma^{-3}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\left\lVert{dm}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}},

\displaystyle\left\lVert{d(D\widehat{Q})}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\left(\frac{4\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{16\beta^{2}\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\left\lVert{dm}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}},

\displaystyle\left\lVert{d(D\widehat{Q}^{2}D)}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}

\displaystyle{}\leqslant\left(\frac{16\gamma^{-3}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{16(4\gamma^{-1}-2)\beta^{2}\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)\left\lVert{dm}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}.

We conclude Lipschitzness by
Fact
B.3
since
\left\lVert{dm}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Fr\acute{e}}{}}=1
when
m
is the independent variable.
∎

Finally, we record here the strict convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TAP}}
, equivalent to the positive-definiteness of
\widehat{Q}
—which is a simple spectral bound assuming
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
—although it will not be used until
Corollary
6.14 (d2)
.

Lemma 6.3
(Strict convexity of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{TAP}}
assuming
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
holds.
Let
D(m),a(m),\widehat{Q}(m)
be as in
(
6.1
)
.
Then

\widehat{Q}(m)\succ\frac{1}{4}D(m)^{-1}.

Proof.

Since
D(m)\succ 0
, the lemma statement is equivalent to

Y:=D(m)^{1/2}\widehat{Q}(m)D(m)^{1/2}\succ\frac{1}{4}I_{n}.

The final line of
Lemma
6.1
implies
\widehat{Q}(m)\succ 0
since
X=\widehat{Q}(m)^{-1}
.
Therefore,
Y
is positive-definite and

Y\succeq\left\lVert Y^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}^{-1}I_{n},

so it is sufficient to show that
\left\lVert Y^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}<4
. We expand

Y^{-1}=a(m)D(m)^{-1}-\beta D(m)^{-1/2}AD(m)^{-1/2}+I_{n}-\frac{2\beta}{n}D(m)^{-1/2}mm^{\mathsf{T}}D(m)^{-1/2}.

By triangle inequality and the fact that
\left\lVert D(m)^{-1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 1
,

\left\lVert Y^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant a(m)+\left\lVert\beta A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}+1+\frac{2\beta}{n}\left\lVert{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant 1+(1-\gamma)+1+2\beta<4.\qed

6.3.
Diagonal error from rank-1 term of TAP Hessian

The event
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
in
(
6.3
)
captures the control over the diagonal that we get from the free probability theory argument in
§
B.6
.
But this control is on the inverse
\overline{Q}
of the TAP Hessian
without the rank-1 term
, whereas the rank-1 term is present throughout the actual algorithmic process.

We need just one fact to capture the discrepancy between these two resolvents.

Lemma 6.4
(Small diagonal error from rank-1 terms)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

In the same setting as
Lemma
6.1
, for all
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
,
the diagonal error between
\widehat{Q}^{2}
and
\overline{Q}^{2}
satisfies

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)-\overline{Q}^{2}(m)\right]D^{2}(m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{16\beta^{2}\gamma^{-3}}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

We factor with the second-order resolvent identity
X^{-2}-Y^{-2}\equiv X^{-2}(Y-X)Y^{-1}+X^{-1}(Y-X)Y^{-2}
for all invertible matrices
X,Y
.
Since
\overline{Q}{}^{-1}-\widehat{Q}^{-1}=2\beta^{2}mm^{\mathsf{T}}/n
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}^{2}-\overline{Q}^{2}{}

\displaystyle\;=\;\widehat{Q}^{2}(\overline{Q}{}^{-1}-\widehat{Q}^{-1})\overline{Q}+\widehat{Q}(\overline{Q}{}^{-1}-\widehat{Q}^{-1})\overline{Q}{}^{2}

\displaystyle\;=\;\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}mm^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{Q}+\widehat{Q}mm^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{Q}{}^{2}\right].

Then, applying the identity
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[uv^{\mathsf{T}}]\equiv\diag(u)\diag(v)
,

(6.4)

E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}-\overline{Q}{}^{2}\right]D^{2}=\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\left[\diag(D\widehat{Q}^{2}m)\diag(D\overline{Q}m)+\diag(D\widehat{Q}m)\diag(D\overline{Q}{}^{2}m)\right].

We now give a quick inequality for generic
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
By AM-GM and the
\ell_{4}
-to-
\ell_{2}
norm inequality
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{4}\leqslant\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{2}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\diag(x)\diag(y)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}y_{i})^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{j}(x_{i}^{4}+y_{i}^{4})=\frac{1}{2n}\left(\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{4}^{4}+\left\lVert{y}\right\rVert_{4}^{4}\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{2n}\left(\left\lVert{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}+\left\lVert{y}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\right)\,.

Applying this inequality to the two terms in
(
6.4
)
and then using the basic resolvent bounds
Lemma
6.1(b)
and
Lemma
6.1(c)
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}-\overline{Q}{}^{2}\right]D^{2}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4\beta^{4}}{n^{2}}\left\lVert{\diag(D\widehat{Q}^{2}m)\diag(D\overline{Q}m)+\diag(D\widehat{Q}m)\diag(D\overline{Q}{}^{2}m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4\beta^{4}}{n^{3}}\left(\left\lVert{\gamma^{1/2}D\widehat{Q}^{2}m}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}+\left\lVert{\gamma^{-1/2}D\overline{Q}m}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}+\left\lVert{\gamma^{-1/2}D\widehat{Q}m}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}+\left\lVert{\gamma^{1/2}D\overline{Q}{}^{2}m}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{4\beta^{4}}{n^{3}}\left(16\gamma^{-6}+16\gamma^{-6}+16\gamma^{-6}+16\gamma^{-6}\right)\left\lVert{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}.\qed

6.4.
Control of the diagonal

The diagonal entries of the instantaneous covariance matrix
(dm_{t})(dm_{t})^{\mathsf{T}}=\widehat{Q}^{2}dt
hold a special position in the analysis because they determine the instantaneous variance of each coordinate:
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[dm_{t,i}^{2}]=(\widehat{Q}^{2})_{i,i}dt
.

In particular, the condition
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
implies that the diagonal entries of
\widehat{Q}^{2}
are close to a scalar multiple of the diagonal entries of
D^{-2}
, a fact that will be succinctly captured by bounds on the following quantity:

(6.5)

\delta_{\diag}(m):=E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}]\,D(m)^{2}-(1+\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}))I_{n}

First, we record operator norm and Lipschitzness bounds on
\delta_{\diag}
that hold even under
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}\setminus\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
.

Lemma 6.5
(Diagonal properties assuming only
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
holds.
Let
D(m),a(m),\widehat{Q}(m)
be as in
(
6.1
)
and let
\delta_{\diag}(m)
be as in
(
6.5
)
. Then with
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

(a)

\left\lVert\delta_{\diag}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 4\gamma^{-2}.

(b)

\left\lVert{\delta_{\diag}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant\frac{\gamma^{-3}}{\sqrt{n}}(4+\beta^{2})\left(4+16\beta^{2}+\frac{32\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

where the Lipschitz constant is with respect to
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
.

Proof.

Part
6.5(a)
.

E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[\cdot]
and
\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\cdot)I_{n}
are both contractions for
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
, and
\delta_{\diag}
is a difference of two PSD matrices, so its operator norm is bounded by
\max(\left\lVert D\widehat{Q}^{2}D\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}},\beta^{2}\left\lVert\widehat{Q}^{2}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}})
, which is given by
Lemma
6.1(d)
and
Lemma
6.1(a)
.

Part
6.5(b)
.
Again
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}[\cdot]
and
\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\cdot)I_{n}
are both contractions for
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
, so it suffices by triangle inequality to add the basic Lipschitz bounds
\left\lVert{D\widehat{Q}^{2}D}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}
and
\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}
established in
Lemma
6.2(d)
and
Lemma
6.2(b)
.
∎

Then a strong uniform bound on the 2-norm of
\diag_{\delta}
follows straightforwardly from
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
and the rank-1 diagonal error bound in
§
6.3
.

Lemma 6.6
(Diagonal control assuming
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

In the same setting as
Lemma
6.5
, suppose additionally that
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
holds.
Then there is a constant
C_{\diag}
depending on
C_{\mathrm{free}}
so that for all
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

\left\lVert{\delta_{\diag}(m)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\diag}}{\gamma^{4}\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

The definition
(
6.3
)
of
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
tells us

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\overline{Q}{}^{2}\right]D^{2}-\frac{I_{n}}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{free}}\beta}{\gamma^{4}\sqrt{n}}.

Then triangle inequality with the smallness of the diagonal error from the rank-1 term of
\widehat{Q}
(
Lemma
6.4
) allows us to replace the
\overline{Q}
with
\widehat{Q}
to get

(6.6)

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}{}^{2}\right]D^{2}-\frac{I_{n}}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{free}}\beta+16\beta^{2}\gamma}{\gamma^{4}\sqrt{n}}.

Contractivity of
D^{-1}
finds

\left\lVert{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}\right]-\frac{D^{-2}}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{free}}\beta+16\beta^{2}\gamma}{\gamma^{4}\sqrt{n}}.

Taking the trace of the above and applying
\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\cdot)\leqslant\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{1}}\leqslant\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
yields the inequality in

\left|\left(1+\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\widehat{Q}^{2})\right)-\left(\frac{1}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}\right)\right|=\left|\left(1+\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\widehat{Q}^{2})\right)-\left(1+\frac{\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}{1-\beta^{2}\operatorname{tr}_{n}(D^{-2})}\right)\right|\leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{free}}\beta^{3}+16\beta^{4}\gamma}{\gamma^{4}\sqrt{n}}.

Then triangle inequality with
(
6.6
)
obtains the bound.
∎

6.5.
Error between ASL-TAP and PHD is small

When we compare the two SDEs

d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}

d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\left({\widehat{m}_{t}-f(\widehat{m}_{t})}\right)dt+\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}

from
(
PHD
)
and
(
ASL-TAP
)
respectively, we see that they only differ by the ASL-TAP drift term

\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\left({\widehat{m}_{t}-f(\widehat{m}_{t})}\right)dt.

With the basic resolvent bounds and the control over the diagonal in hand, we are ready to show that this error between the two SDEs is in fact small and regular.
This closeness between PHD and ASL-TAP then underpins the functioning of the sampling algorithm, as it enjoys both PHD’s regularity and connection to the TAP free energy (as leveraged in
§
3.3
) and ASL’s direct connection to the Gibbs measure.

Lemma 6.7
(Closeness of ASL-TAP and PHD)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
and
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
hold.
Let
D(m),a(m),\widehat{Q}(m)
be as in
(
6.1
)
and let
\delta_{\diag}(n)
be as in
(
6.5
)
.
Let
f(m):=(\delta_{\diag}(m)+I_{n}+2\beta^{2}\widehat{Q}^{2}/n)m
as in
(
2.3
)
.
Let
C_{\diag}
be the constant in
Lemma
6.6
.
Then with
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

(a)

\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\;\leqslant\;34\gamma^{-4}+4\gamma^{-2}+1+O\left(n^{-1/2}\right).

(b)

\left\lVert{m-f(m)}\right\rVert_{2}\;\leqslant\;C_{\diag}\gamma^{-4}+O(n^{-1/2}).

(c)

The drift coefficient of
(
ASL-TAP
)
is bounded by

\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m))}\right\rVert_{2}\;\leqslant\;C_{\diag}\gamma^{-5}+O(n^{-1/2}).

(d)

The first dual drift coefficient of
(
d-ASL-TAP
)
is bounded by

\left\lVert{D(m)\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m))}\right\rVert_{2}\;\leqslant\;2C_{\diag}\gamma^{-5}+O(n^{-1/2}).

(e)

The drift coefficient of
(
ASL-TAP
)
is Lipschitz:

\left\lVert{m\mapsto\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m))}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\;\leqslant\;4C_{\diag}\gamma^{-6}+34\gamma^{-5}+4\gamma^{-3}+\gamma^{-1}+1+O(n^{-1/2}).

Proof.

Part
6.7(a)
.
We have by product rule that

\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant\left\lVert{\delta_{\diag}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\sqrt{n}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}\to\infty}+C_{\delta}+1+\frac{2}{n}C_{\widehat{Q}}^{2},

where
C_{\delta}
is a uniform bound on
\left\lVert\delta_{\diag}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
and
C_{\widehat{Q}}
is a uniform bound on
\left\lVert\widehat{Q}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
and
\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}\to\infty}
is the Lipschitz constant with respect to the operator norm.
Note that
\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}\to\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{n}\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}
, so by the Lipschitzness of
\delta_{\diag}
in
Lemma
6.5(a)
, the simple resolvent Lipschitzness bound
Lemma
6.2(b)
for
\widehat{Q}^{2}
, control of the diagonal from
\Omega^{\mathrm{free}}
in
Lemma
6.5(b)
for
C_{\delta}
, and the simple resolvent operator norm bound
Lemma
6.1(a)
for
C_{\widehat{Q}}
,

\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant\gamma^{-4}(4+\beta^{2})(4+16\beta^{2})+4\gamma^{-2}+1+O(1/\sqrt{n}).

Part
6.7(b)
.
We notice that

m-f(m)=-\delta_{\diag}(m)m-2\beta^{2}\widehat{Q}^{2}m/n.

So after applying triangle inequality, the bound for the first term follows by

\left\lVert{\delta_{\diag}(m)m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\diag}(m)_{i}^{2}m_{i}^{2}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\diag}(m)_{i}^{2}\leqslant n\left\lVert{\delta_{\diag}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

and control of the diagonal from
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
(
Lemma
6.6
).
For the second term,
\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}^{2}m}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\left\lVert\widehat{Q}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}\left\lVert{m}\right\rVert_{2}
and then the simple resolvent operator norm bound
Lemma
6.1(a)
concludes.

Parts
6.7(c)
and
6.7(d)
.
We combine the just-established bound on
\left\lVert{m-f(m)}\right\rVert_{2}(
Lemma
6.7(b)
) with the simple resolvent operator norm bounds
Lemma
6.1(a)
and
Lemma
6.1(b)
respectively.

Part
6.7(e)
.
By the product rule,

\left\lVert{m\mapsto\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m))}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant C_{\widehat{Q}}\left(1+\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\right)+C_{mf}\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}\to\infty},

where
C_{\widehat{Q}}
is a uniform bound on
\left\lVert\widehat{Q}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
and
C_{mf}
is a uniform bound on
\left\lVert{m-f(m)}\right\rVert_{2}
and
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}\to\infty}
is the Lipschitz constant with respect to the operator norm.
From
Lemma
6.1(a)
, we can take
C_{\widehat{Q}}=\gamma^{-1}
and from
Lemma
6.7(b)
, we can take
C_{mf}\leqslant C_{\diag}\gamma^{-4}+O(n^{-1/2})
.
By
Lemma
6.7(a)
, we also have
\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant 34\gamma^{-4}+4\gamma^{-2}+1+O\left(n^{-1/2}\right)
and from
Lemma
6.2(a)
we have
\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}\to\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{n}\left\lVert{\widehat{Q}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant 4\gamma^{-2}+O(1/\sqrt{n})
.
∎

6.6.
Properties of the trajectory

We now concern ourselves with the
(
ASL-TAP
)
and the
(
PHD
)
evolutions for
\widehat{m}_{t}
, specifically the initial condition
\widehat{m}_{0}=0
and the SDEs

(6.7)

d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\big(\widehat{m}_{t}-f(\widehat{m}_{t})\big)\,dt+\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\,dB_{t}.

(6.8)

d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\,dB_{t}.

The boundedness and Lipschitzness properties established so far will be enough to show the basic fact that
\widehat{m}_{t}
remains inside
(-1,1)^{n}
at all times (
Lemma
6.9
).
Moreover, they will imply transportation inequalities that yield a strong sub-Gaussian concentration statement for Lipschitz functionals of
\{\widehat{m}_{t}\}_{t}
(
Theorem
6.13
).

For the proof of existence, uniqueness, and non-escape of the SDE solutions, it is convenient to make a change of coordinates
\tanh^{-1}:(-1,1)^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the dual space of the magnetization.

We apply this change of coordinates to (
PHD
), given by
\widehat{m}_{0}=0
and
d\widehat{m}_{t}=\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}
.
Let

\widehat{x}_{t}=\tanh^{-1}(\widehat{m}_{t})
.
Applying Itô’s formula to the change of variables
\tanh^{-1}:(-1,1)^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
gives

\displaystyle d\widehat{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left({\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})^{2}}\right)\left({\frac{\widehat{m}_{t}}{(1-\widehat{m}_{t}^{2})^{2}}}\right)dt+\diag\left({\frac{1}{1-\widehat{m}_{t}^{2}}}\right)\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}

(d-PHD)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left({\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})^{2}}\right)D(\widehat{m}_{t})^{2}\widehat{m}_{t}}_{\widehat{f}^{*}(\widehat{x}_{t})}dt+\underbrace{D(\widehat{m}_{t})\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})}_{\widehat{\Sigma}(\widehat{x}_{t})}dB_{t},

where operations on vectors are interpreted component-wise. Define
\widehat{f}^{*}(\widehat{x}_{t})
and
\widehat{\Sigma}(\widehat{x}_{t})
as above, noting that
\widehat{m}_{t}=\tanh(\widehat{x}_{t})
, and that here,
\widehat{f}^{*}
is not denoting a convex conjugation.

We can similarly apply this change of coordinates to (
ASL-TAP
) to obtain

(d-ASL-TAP)

d\widehat{x}_{t}=\left[{D(\widehat{m}_{t})\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})(\widehat{m}_{t}-f(\widehat{m}_{t}))+E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left({\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})^{2}}\right)D(\widehat{m}_{t})^{2}\widehat{m}_{t}}\right]dt+D(\widehat{m}_{t})\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})dB_{t}.

We show that the coefficients of (
d-PHD
) and (
d-ASL-TAP
) are Lipschitz.

Lemma 6.8
(Lipschitzness of dual SDE coefficients)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
and
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
hold.
Then over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(1)

\widehat{\Sigma}
is Lipschitz (with respect to
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
) with constant
\frac{\gamma^{-2}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(4+16\beta^{2}+\frac{32\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
.

(2)

\widehat{f}^{*}
is Lipschitz with constant
\gamma^{-3}\left(16+64\beta^{2}+\frac{128\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+4\gamma^{-2}
.

(3)

With
m:=\tanh(x)
, the function
x\mapsto D(m)\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m))
is Lipschitz with constant
8C_{\diag}\gamma^{-6}+68\gamma^{-5}+8\gamma^{-3}+2\gamma^{-1}+2+O(n^{-1/2})
.

Proof.

Lipschitzness of
\widehat{\Sigma}
.
This follows by combining
Lemma
6.2(c)
with the Lipschitzness of
\tanh
and the multiplicativity of Lipschitz constants under composition of functions.

Lipschitzness of
\widehat{f}^{*}
.
Since
\tanh
is Lipschitz, it suffices to bound the Lipschitz constant of
\widehat{f}^{*}(x)
as a function of
m:=\tanh(x)
.
By product rule, this Lipschitz constant is bounded by

\left\lVert{m\mapsto E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left({\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}}\right)D(m)^{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\sqrt{n}+\sup_{m\in(-1,1)}\left\lVert E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left({\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}}\right)D(m)^{2}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}.

The first term is bounded by
Lemma
6.2(d)
after noting the contractivity of
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
.
The second term is bounded by
Lemma
6.1(d)
.

Lipschitzness of
x\mapsto D(m)\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m))
.
This follows by the same product-rule argument as
Lemma
6.7(e)
, but replacing
Lemma
6.1(a)
and
Lemma
6.2(a)
with
Lemma
6.1(b)
and
Lemma
6.2(c)

∎

Lemma 6.9
(ASL-TAP and PHD stay in the cube)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Assume that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
and
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
hold. Then (
d-PHD
), (
d-ASL-TAP
), (
PHD
), and (
ASL-TAP
) all have unique global strong solutions, and in all of them,

\widehat{m}_{t}\in(-1,1)^{n}
for all
t\geqslant 0
almost surely.

Proof 22
.

By
Lemma
6.8
, the coefficients of
dt
and
dB_{t}
for
d\widehat{x}_{t}
are globally Lipschitz as functions of
\widehat{x}_{t}
in both (
d-PHD
) and (
d-ASL-TAP
). This implies that the coefficients are also
locally bounded. Therefore, the standard Lipschitz theorem for SDEs yields a
unique global adapted solution for
\widehat{x}_{t}
driven by the Brownian motion
B_{t}
, and the solution is strong with respect to the Brownian
filtration
[
102
, Chapter IX, Theorem 2.1]
.

Since
\tanh:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to(-1,1)^{n}
coordinate-wise is
C^{\infty}
, we can apply Itô’s formula
again to
\widehat{m}_{t}:=\tanh(\widehat{x}_{t})
, showing that this reverses the dual transformation and recovers solutions to (
PHD
) and (
ASL-TAP
) respectively. In particular, these solutions for
\widehat{m}
are
continuous semimartingale satisfying
\widehat{m}_{t}\in(-1,1)^{n}
for every
t\geqslant 0
.

It remains to prove pathwise uniqueness for (
PHD
) and (
ASL-TAP
).
Let
\widetilde{m}
be any other continuous primal solution, driven by the same
Brownian motion and started from the same initial condition, and define its exit
time

\widetilde{\tau}:=\inf\{t\geqslant 0:\widetilde{m}_{t}\notin(-1,1)^{n}\}.

For
t<\widetilde{\tau}
, set

\widetilde{x}_{t}:=\tanh^{-1}(\widetilde{m}_{t}).

Again, since
\tanh^{-1}
is
C^{\infty}
on
(-1,1)^{n}
, Itô’s
formula shows that
\widetilde{x}
satisfies the dual SDE on
[0,\widetilde{\tau})
.

Fix
T>0
. On the event
\{T<\widetilde{\tau}\}
, both
\widetilde{x}
and
\widehat{x}
solve
the same dual SDE on
[0,T]
with the same Brownian motion and the same initial
condition. By pathwise uniqueness for the dual SDE, they coincide on
[0,T]
on
that event. Letting
T
range over the positive rationals and extending to irrational
t
by continuity, it follows that

\widetilde{x}_{t}=\widehat{x}_{t}\qquad\text{for all }t<\widetilde{\tau}\quad\text{a.s.}

Therefore

\widetilde{m}_{t}=\tanh(\widetilde{x}_{t})=\tanh(\widehat{x}_{t})=\widehat{m}_{t}\qquad\text{for all }t<\widetilde{\tau}.

If
\widetilde{\tau}<\infty
, then by continuity of
\widehat{x}
and
\tanh
,

\widetilde{m}_{\widetilde{\tau}}=\lim_{t\uparrow\widetilde{\tau}}\widetilde{m}_{t}=\lim_{t\uparrow\widetilde{\tau}}\tanh(\widehat{x}_{t})=\tanh(\widehat{x}_{\widetilde{\tau}})\in(-1,1)^{n},

which contradicts the definition of
\widetilde{\tau}
.
Hence
\widetilde{\tau}=\infty
a.s., and consequently

\widetilde{m}_{t}=\widehat{m}_{t}\qquad\text{for all }t\geqslant 0\quad\text{a.s.}

Thus the primal SDE is pathwise unique.

To obtain sub-Gaussian concentration for all Lipschitz functionals of the trajectory of
\widehat{m}_{t}
, we first restate a powerful characterization of transportation inequalities in terms of said sub-Gaussian concentration.
Then we adjust and restate a result of Ütsun̈el yielding a transportation inequality for any Itô process that has a Lipschitz drift coefficient and a bounded and Lipschitz diffusion coefficient.

Definition 6.10
.

A probability measure
\mu
on a metric space
(E,d)
satisfies a
T_{p}
transportation inequality with constant
C
if

W_{p}(\mu,\nu)\leqslant\sqrt{2C\,H(\nu\mid\mu)},

where

W_{p}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Sigma(\mu,\nu)}\left(\int(d(x,y))^{p}d\pi(x,y)\right)^{1/p}

is the Wasserstein distance, where
\Sigma(\mu,\nu)
is the subset of probability measures over
E\times E
whose first marginal is
\mu
and whose second marginal is
\nu
, and
H(\nu\mid\mu)
is the relative entropy.

Theorem 6.11
(
T_{1}
inequality & sub-Gaussian concentration
[
52
, Theorem 1.1, Bobkov & Götze]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\mu
be a measure on a metric space
(E,d)
that satisfies a
T_{1}
transport inequality with constant
C>0
. This is equivalent to
\mu
satisfying the following concentration under push-forward by any
K
-Lipschitz function
F:(E,d)\to\mathbb{R}
that is
\mu
-integrable:
first, in moment-generating function form,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\left(F-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[F]\right)\right)\right]\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}CK^{2}}{2}\right)\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},

and in sub-Gaussian tail bound form,

\mu\left(\left|F-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[F]\right|>r\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{r^{2}}{2CK^{2}}\right)\qquad\forall r>0.

Theorem 6.12
((Modified)
T_{2}
transport inequality for regular Itô processes
[
113
, Theorem 1]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
0<C,K<\infty
and
0<T<\infty
.
Consider an Itô process
\{X_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
given by
X_{0}=0
and

dX_{t}=b_{t}(X_{t})dt+\sigma_{t}(X_{t})dB_{t}\,.

for
t\in(0,T)
.
Assume that the diffusion coefficients
\{\sigma_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
are matrix-valued functions satisfying
\left\lVert\sigma_{t}(x)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C
for every
0\leqslant t\leqslant T
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Assume also that both the diffusion coefficients and the drift coefficients
\{b_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
satisfy the following Lipschitz conditions for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
0\leqslant t\leqslant T
:

\left\lVert{b_{t}(x)-b_{t}(y)}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant K\left\lVert{x-y}\right\rVert_{2}\,,

\left\lVert{\sigma_{t}(x)-\sigma_{t}(y)}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{K}{\sqrt{n}}\left\lVert{x-y}\right\rVert_{2}.

Then
\dist(X)
satisfies a
T_{2}
transportation inequality on
C^{0}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})
with constant
3TC^{2}e^{3T(T+4)K^{2}}
, where
X
refers to the entire path
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
,
C^{0}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the set of continous paths in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
on
[0,T]
, and the metric on
C^{0}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})
is given by the uniform norm
d(X,Y):=\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\left\lVert{X_{t}-Y_{t}}\right\rVert_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to Ütsun̈el’s original argument, but with minute changes to accommodate
t\in[0,T]
instead of
t\in[0,1]
and
\left\lVert\sigma_{t}(x)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C
instead of
\left\lVert\sigma_{t}(x)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 1
.
Specifically, after establishing the domination
d_{W}^{2}(\dist(X),\mu)\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\left\lVert{x^{v}_{t}-x_{t}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}]
, we calculate the difference between the SDEs
dx_{t}=\sigma_{t}(x_{t})d\beta_{t}+b_{t}(x_{t})dt
and
dx^{v}_{t}=\sigma_{t}(x^{v}_{t})dz_{t}+b_{t}(x^{v}_{t})dt
with
d\beta_{t}=dz_{t}-dv_{t}
to see

x^{v}_{r}-x_{r}=\int_{0}^{r}(\sigma_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-\sigma_{s}(x_{s}))dz_{s}+\int_{0}^{r}(b_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-b_{s}(x_{s}))ds+\int_{0}^{r}\sigma_{s}(x_{s})dv_{s}.

By
\left\lVert{a+b+c}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 3(\left\lVert{a}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert{b}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert{c}\right\rVert^{2})
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert{x^{v}_{r}-x_{r}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\left\lVert{\int_{0}^{r}(\sigma_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-\sigma_{s}(x_{s}))dz_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+3\left\lVert{\int_{0}^{r}(b_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-b_{s}(x_{s}))ds}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+3\left\lVert{\int_{0}^{r}\sigma_{s}(x_{s})dv_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

By Doob’s
L^{2}
maximal inequality, Itô isometry, and then the Lipschitzness of
\sigma_{s}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\sup_{r\leqslant t}\left\lVert{\int_{0}^{r}(\sigma_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-\sigma_{s}(x_{s}))dz_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\left\lVert{\int_{0}^{t}(\sigma_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-\sigma_{s}(x_{s}))dz_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=4n\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert{\sigma_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-\sigma_{s}(x_{s})}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}ds\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 4K^{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert{x^{v}_{s}-x_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}ds\right].

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the Lipschitzness of
b_{s}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{r\leqslant t}\left\lVert{\int_{0}^{r}(b_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-b_{s}(x_{s}))ds}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant t\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert{b_{s}(x^{v}_{s})-b_{s}(x_{s})}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}ds\leqslant K^{2}t\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert{x^{v}_{s}-x_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}ds.

Then by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the boundedness of
\sigma_{s}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{r\leqslant t}\left\lVert{\int_{0}^{r}\sigma_{s}(x_{s})dv_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant t\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert{\sigma_{s}(x_{s})\dot{v}_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}ds\leqslant C^{2}t\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert{\dot{v}_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}ds.

Combining the last 4 displayed inequalities and letting
u(t):=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\sup_{r\leqslant t}\left\lVert{x^{v}_{t}-x_{t}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right]
,
