u(t)\leqslant 3K^{2}(t+4)\int_{0}^{t}u(s)ds+3C^{2}t\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\left\lVert{\dot{v}_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}ds\right].

The result follows from Grönwall’s inequality and the property of the exponential martingale that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left\lVert{\dot{v}_{s}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}ds\right]=2\,H(\mu\mid\dist(X))
.
∎

Theorem 6.13
(Transport and sub-Gaussian concentration for ASL-TAP)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
from
(
6.2
)
and
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
from
(
6.3
)
both hold.

Let
0<T<\infty
.
Let
\mathcal{W}:=C^{0}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})
, equipped with the uniform norm

\|x\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}\left\lVert{x_{t}}\right\rVert_{2}
, and let

P:=\dist(\widehat{m}_{\cdot})
be the law of the full path of the ASL-TAP SDE
(
6.7
)
on
\mathcal{W}
.
For some constant
C_{K}
, define

c_{T_{2}}:=\frac{3T}{\gamma^{2}}\exp\left(\frac{3T(T+4)C_{K}^{2}}{\gamma^{12}}\right).

Then:

(i)

For every probability
measure
\mu
on
\big(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{W})\big)
, where
\mathcal{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathcal{W}
,

d_{W,2}(P,\mu)^{2}\ \leqslant\ 2c_{T_{2}}\,H(\mu\mid P).

(ii)

For every
P
-integrable
K_{F}
-Lipschitz functional
F:(\mathcal{W},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{P}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\left(F-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{P}[F]\right)\right)\right]\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}c_{T_{2}}K_{F}^{2}}{2}\right),\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

In sub-Gaussian tail bound form,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}_{P}\Big(\big|F-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{P}[F]\big|>r\Big)\ \leqslant\ 2\exp\!\left(-\frac{r^{2}}{2c_{T_{2}}K_{F}^{2}}\right),\qquad r>0.

Proof.

To apply
Theorem
6.12
to the SDE (
6.7
), we need to show that the drift coefficient
\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\big(\widehat{m}_{t}-f(\widehat{m}_{t})\big)
is
K
-Lipschitz and the diffusion coefficient
\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})
is
K/\sqrt{n}
-Lipschitz and bounded everywhere by
C
. By
Lemma
6.9
and the Kirszbraun theorem, we can use a Lipschitz extension of
\widehat{Q}
outside of
(-1,1)^{n}
without affecting the trajectories, so
the basic resolvent operator norm bound
Lemma
6.1(a)
shows that we can take
C\leftarrow\gamma^{-1}
.
The closeness of PHD and ASL-TAP (
Lemma
6.7(e)
) controls the Lipschitz constant of the drift term, which, when combined with a basic resolvent Lipschitzness bound (
Lemma
6.2(a)
) on the diffusion term, shows that we can take
K\leftarrow\max(C_{K}\gamma^{-6},4\gamma^{-2})+O(1/\sqrt{n})
for some constant
C_{K}
.
Hence we find the stated
T_{2}
inequality with constant
c_{T_{2}}=3TC^{2}e^{3T(T+4)K^{2}}
.

Since
d_{W,1}\leqslant d_{W,2}
, this implies a
T_{1}
inequality with constant
c_{T_{2}}
.
Applying
Theorem
6.11
to a
K_{F}
-Lipschitz
F
yields the conclusion.
∎

6.7.
Implications for the sampling algorithm

We can now index various algorithmic desiderata stated in
§
2.5
into the algorithmic process properties we just proved.

Corollary 6.14
(Algorithmic desiderata)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose that
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
from
(
6.2
)
and
\Omega^{(\mathrm{free})}
from
(
6.3
)
both hold. Then

(d0)

The algorithmic SDEs (
PHD
) and (
ASL-TAP-JE
) have unique strong solutions such that
\widehat{m}_{t}\in(-1,1)^{n}
for all
t<\infty
almost-surely.

(d2)

There are constants
\lambda,\Lambda>0
such that
for all
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

\lambda D(m)^{-1}\preceq\widehat{Q}(m)\preceq\Lambda D(m)^{-1}.

(d3)

For all
t\in[0,T]
, for
m_{t}
drawn from
(
HD
)
,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left\|{f(m_{t})-m_{t}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon_{\mathrm{drift}}(t)^{2},

where
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{drift}}(t)
is a constant depending only on
\beta
and
t
and bounded on
[0,T]
.

(d4)

(a)

There exists a constant
L
such that for all
m,w\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

\left\|{\widehat{Q}(m)-\widehat{Q}(w)}\right\|_{F}\leqslant L\left\|{m-w}\right\|_{2}.

(b)

There exists a constant
L_{f}
such that for all
m,w\in(-1,1)^{n}
,

\left\|{f(m)-f(w)}\right\|_{2}\leqslant L_{f}\left\|{m-w}\right\|_{2}.

(d5)

As in
(
ASL-TAP-JE
)
and with
\widehat{m}_{t}
solving
(
6.7
)
, let

\widehat{w}_{T}\ :=\ \frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathsf{Tr}\big(\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}_{t})\big)\;+\;\left\lVert{\widehat{m}_{t}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)dt.

Then for all
T\in[0,\infty)
,
\widehat{w}_{T}
is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy
\lambda^{2}c_{T_{2}}C_{\diag}^{2}T^{2}/\gamma^{8}
, i.e. for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\widehat{w}_{T}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{B}[\lambda\widehat{w}_{T}]\right)\right]\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}c_{T_{2}}C_{\diag}^{2}T^{2}}{2\gamma^{8}}\right),

where
c_{T_{2}}
is the function of
T
and
\gamma
given in
Theorem
6.13
and
C_{\diag}
is the constant (for
\beta<1/2
) in
Lemma
6.6
.

Proof.

We give the proofs in sequential order. First,
Corollary
6.14 (d0)
is given by
Lemma
6.9
.

Part
6.14 (d2)
.
The lower bound is
Lemma
6.3
. The upper bound follows from the operator bound on
D\widehat{Q}
given
\Omega^{(A)}_{\gamma}
(
Lemma
6.1(b)
) with
\Lambda\leftarrow 2\gamma^{-1}
, since
\left\lVert D\widehat{Q}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2\gamma^{-1}
implies
D^{1/2}\widehat{Q}D^{1/2}\preceq 2\gamma^{-1}I_{n}
, and then multiply on the left and right by
D^{-1/2}
.

Part
6.14 (d3)
.
The closeness of PHD and ASL-TAP (
Lemma
6.7(b)
) implies
\left\lVert{f(m)-m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant C_{\diag}\gamma^{-4}+O(n^{-1/2})
uniformly for all
m\in(-1,1)^{n}
for a constant
C_{\diag}
, therefore the inequality is also true in expectation over
m_{t}
drawn from
(
HD
)
.

Part
6.14 (d4)
.
The first item is
Lemma
6.2(a)
, obtaining
L\leftarrow 4\gamma^{-2}+O(1/\sqrt{n})
.
The second item is
Lemma
6.7(a)
, with
L_{f}\leftarrow 34\gamma^{-4}+4\gamma^{-2}+1+O(1/\sqrt{n})
.

Part
6.14 (d5)
.
Let
\omega(m):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{Tr}(\widehat{Q}(m))+\left\lVert{m}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)
.
To show that
\omega
is Lipschitz, we differentiate with respect to
m
using Fréchet derivatives (
Definition
B.2
):

\displaystyle d\omega

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\widehat{Q}\left(-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}m^{\mathsf{T}}dm\cdot I_{n}+2D\diag(m\odot dm)D-\frac{2\beta^{2}}{n}\left(m\,dm^{\mathsf{T}}+(dm)m^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right)\widehat{Q}\right]+m^{\mathsf{T}}dm

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{Tr}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}D^{2}\diag(m)\diag(dm)\right]-\beta^{2}m^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\operatorname{tr}_{n}[\widehat{Q}^{2}]I_{n}+\frac{2}{n}\widehat{Q}^{2}\right)dm+m^{\mathsf{T}}dm

\displaystyle=-m^{\mathsf{T}}E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}D^{2}\right]dm+\beta^{2}m^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\operatorname{tr}_{n}[\widehat{Q}^{2}]I_{n}+\frac{2}{n}\widehat{Q}^{2}\right)dm+m^{\mathsf{T}}dm

\displaystyle=m^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\beta^{2}\left(\operatorname{tr}_{n}(\widehat{Q}^{2})I_{n}+\frac{2}{n}\widehat{Q}^{2}\right)+I_{n}-E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}\right]D^{2}\right)dm

(6.9)

\displaystyle=(m-f(m))^{\mathsf{T}}dm.

Therefore, by bounded Fréchet derivatives implying Lipschitzness (
Fact
B.3
) and the closeness of PHD and ASL-TAP (
Lemma
6.7(b)
),
\omega
is
C_{\diag}\gamma^{-4}
-Lipschitz.
So for
x,y\in C^{0}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\left|\widehat{w}_{T}(x)-\widehat{w}_{T}(y)\right|\leqslant\int_{0}^{T}|\omega(x_{t})-\omega(y_{t})|\,dt\leqslant TC_{\diag}\gamma^{-4}\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\lVert{x_{t}-y_{t}}\right\rVert_{2},

so
\left\lVert{\widehat{w}_{T}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}\leqslant TC_{\diag}\gamma^{-4}
, with
\widehat{w}_{T}
as a functional of the path
\{\widehat{m}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
metricized by the uniform norm.
Then apply sub-Gaussian concentration for Lipschitz path functionals of ASL-TAP (
Theorem
6.13
) with
F\leftarrow\widehat{w}_{T}
and
K_{F}\leftarrow TC_{\diag}\gamma^{-4}
.
∎

7.
Sampling from the time-
T
localized distribution

Our main theorem is the following: the stochastic localization process for SK will concentrate the measure on a small wedge
B_{\varepsilon n}
after large constant time, and when the wedge is small enough, the measure will allow rapid mixing for an appropriate Markov chain. For technical reasons, we prove fast mixing for the polarized walk (see
Definition
7.9
) rather than Glauber dynamics, although we conjecture the same result holds for Glauber dynamics. Note that entropy contraction implies a modified log-Sobolev inequality for the corresponding Markov chain, in this case the polarized walk.

Theorem 7.1.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider the stochastic localization process for the Gibbs distribution of the SK model with inverse temperature
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
.
There are constants
T(\beta)
,
\varepsilon(\beta)
, and
c(\beta)
such that
with probability
\geqslant 1-e^{-c(\beta)n}
, for
T\geqslant T(\beta)
,

(1)

\mu_{\beta A,y_{T}}(B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y_{T})))\geqslant 1-e^{-c(\beta)n}
.

(2)

For any
x_{0}
,
the polarized walk on
\mu_{\beta A,y_{T}}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(x_{0})}
satisfies entropy contraction with constant
\geqslant\frac{\rho_{\beta}}{n}
.

This will follow from
Lemma
7.30
(entropy contraction on the wedge) and
Lemma
7.43
(concentration of Gibbs measure on the wedge). The main intuition for (2) is that a wedge has smaller diameter, which effectively counteracts a large operator norm of
\beta A
. Concretely, this is formalized through the fact that
A_{S\times S}
has smaller operator norm for smaller sets
S
.

Remark 7.2

(Necessity of
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
)
.

We conjecture that the theorem holds for all
\beta>0
. Our proof relies on
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
, because the needle decomposition of
[
55
]
into rank-1 Ising models (
Theorem
7.24
) only works for PSD matrices. Our
A
is not PSD, which we fix by adding a multiple of the identity. However, this breaks the property of
A
having small operator norm on subsets of
O(\varepsilon n)
indices, which we need for the resulting rank-1 Ising models to have bounded covariance. When
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
, we can add
\gamma I
with
\gamma<1
to make it PSD, and then choose a small enough
\varepsilon>0
so the positive part of
A
has arbitrarily small operator norm on subsets of
\varepsilon n
indices, for small enough
\varepsilon
.
Our threshold for
\beta
is 2 times the threshold for
[
55
]
because we only have to ensure the most negative eigenvalue of
A
is bounded by 1, rather than the difference between the extreme eigenvalues.

After giving preliminaries in
§
7.1
, we first prove in
§
7.2
entropy contraction
when
\beta<\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}
. Our proof is significantly simpler in this case, and showcases the main idea, the two-stage decomposition of
Lemma
7.28
. This reduces to bounding the variance of any 1-dimensional marginal arising in the decomposition, and central to this is the bound on the covariance of a product distribution restricted to a wedge by its diagonal (
Lemma
7.21
). Unfortunately, this naive bound is off by a factor of 2 compared to the unrestricted setting, and we have to do some significant work in proving and then applying a tighter version of this bound that holds for most tilts. We do this in
§
7.3
. In
§
7.4
, we show that for large enough
T
, with high probability that most of the mass is concentrated in the wedge. Finally, in
§
7.5
, we show as a corollary that we can efficiently sample and compute the partition function for
\mu_{A,y_{T}}
.

We use the polarized walk because our proof requires a Markov chain that is fast mixing for all product distributions on wedges. This is false for Glauber dynamics, as the mass of such a distribution may be concentrated on the boundary slice of the wedge, where any two points differ in at least 2 coordinates. The polarized walk, defined in terms of a down-up walk, allows such transitions.

7.1.
Preliminaries on Markov kernels, Markov chain mixing, and measures on the hypercube

Throughout, we assume that the state space
\Omega
is finite.
We follow the convention that Markov kernels
P
operate on the left on functions (
Pf
) and on the right for measures (
\nu P
).

Definition 7.3
.

Let
P
be a Markov kernel from
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\prime}
. We say that
P
satisfies
(1-\rho)
-entropy contraction
with respect to
\mu
if for any if for any probability measure
\nu\ll\mu
,

\operatorname{KL}(\nu P\|\mu P)\leqslant(1-\rho)\operatorname{KL}(\nu\|\mu).

We call the largest
\rho
for which this holds the entropy contraction constant, and denote it by
\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu)
when
P
is understood.

For Markov chains, iterating this inequality gives exponential convergence.
We quantify the time it takes the Markov chain to mix by the
mixing time
,

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{mix}}(P,\nu,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{{t}:{\operatorname{TV}(\nu P^{t},\mu)\leqslant\varepsilon}\right\},

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{mix}}(P,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\nu}t_{\mathrm{mix}}(P,\nu,\varepsilon).

Lemma 7.4
(Mixing time)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose
(\Omega,P)
is an ergodic, reversible Markov chain with stationary distribution
\mu
. If
P
satisfies entropy contraction with constant
\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}
, then for all
\nu
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{mix}}(P,\nu,\varepsilon)\leqslant\left\lceil\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}}\left({\log\log\max_{x\in\Omega}\frac{\nu(x)}{\mu(x)}+\log\left({\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)}\right)\right\rceil

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{mix}}(P,\varepsilon)\leqslant\left\lceil\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}}\left({\log\log\max_{x\in\Omega}\frac{1}{\mu(x)}+\log\left({\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)}\right)\right\rceil.

For instance, if
\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}=\Omega\left({\frac{1}{n}}\right)
and
\min_{x\in\Omega}\mu(x)=e^{-\mathsf{poly}(n)}
, then we have optimal mixing,
t_{\mathrm{mix}}(P,\varepsilon)=O(n\log n)
.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\Omega
and
P
be a Markov kernel from
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\prime}
. Define the
adjoint
(or
time-reversal
) of
P
with respect to the distribution
\mu
to be the kernel
P^{*}_{\mu}
from
\Omega^{\prime}
to
\Omega
, where
P^{*}_{\mu}(Y,\cdot)
is the regular conditional distribution of
X|Y
when
X\sim\mu
and
Y\sim P(X,\cdot)
.

The adjoint can be interpreted as a posterior distribution of
X
where
\mu
is the prior and the output
Y
of the channel
P
is observed.

Proposition 7.6
(Equivalent criterion for entropy contraction,
[
14
, Proposition 26–27]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose
\mu
is a probability measure on
\Omega
and
P
is a Markov kernel from
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\prime}
.
If
\nu\ll\mu
, then

\operatorname{KL}(\nu\|\mu)-\operatorname{KL}(\nu P\|\mu P)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim\mu P}\left[{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{X\sim(P_{\mu}^{*})(Y,\cdot)}\left[{\frac{d\nu}{d\mu}(X)}\right]}\right].

Hence,
P
satisfies
(1-\rho)
-entropy contraction with respect to
\mu
iff for all non-negative functions
f
on
\Omega
,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim\mu P}\left[{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{X\sim(P_{\mu}^{*})(Y,\cdot)}[f(X)]}\right]\geqslant\rho\cdot\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu}[f].

Lemma 7.7
(Concavity of entropy decrement,
[
14
, Lemma 29]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
P
be a Markov kernel from
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\prime}
and
f
be a non-negative function on
\Omega
. If
\mu=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mu_{\theta}]
is a mixture distribution, then

\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim\mu P}\left[{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{X\sim(P_{\mu})^{*}(Y,\cdot)}[f(X)]}\right]\geqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\theta\sim\pi}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim\mu_{\theta}P}\left[{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{X\sim(P_{\mu_{\theta}})^{*}(Y,\cdot)}[f(X)]}\right]}\right].

We first introduce the down-up walk for distributions on
\binom{[n]}{k}
.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution on
\binom{[n]}{k}
. Define the
down-up walk
as the Markov chain defined by the composition of the
down
and
up operators

P_{\mu}=D_{k\to k-1}U_{k-1\to k,\mu}
, defined below.

(1)

The down operator
D_{k\to k-1}
is defined by

D_{k\to k-1}(S,T)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{k},&\text{if $T=S\setminus\{i\}$ for some $i\in S$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2)

The up operator
U_{k-1\to k,\mu}
is defined as the adjoint of
D_{k\to k-1}
with respect to
\mu
. Explicitly,

U_{k-1\to k,\mu}(T,S)=\begin{cases}\frac{\mu(S)}{\sum_{j\in[n]\setminus T}\mu(T\cup\{j\})},&\text{if $S=T\cup\{i\}$ for some $i\in[n]\setminus T$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We recall the polarized walk for a distribution on
\{\pm 1\}^{n}
given in
[
14
]
. It can be defined in terms of the down-up walk after homogenizing.

Definition 7.9

(
[
14
, Definition 47, 84]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution on
\mathcal{P}([n])
. Define the polar homogenization kernel
\Pi
from
\mathcal{P}([n])
to
\binom{[n]\sqcup[n]}{n}
, by taking
S_{1}
to
S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}
where
S_{2}\in\binom{[n]}{n-|S_{1}|}
is chosen uniformly at random. Note that its adjoint
\Pi^{*}
takes
S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}
(where
S_{1},S_{2}
are subsets of the disjoint copies of
[n]
) to
S_{1}
.

Define the
polarized walk
for
\mu
as the Markov chain defined as the composition of the down and up operators
P_{\nu}^{\mathrm{pol}}=D^{\mathrm{pol}}U_{\nu}^{\mathrm{pol}}
, defined below. These correspond to the down and up operators on the polar homogenized distribution.

(1)

The down operator
D^{\mathrm{pol}}
is defined as
D^{\mathrm{pol}}=\Pi D_{n\to n-1}\Pi^{*}
. Explicitly,

(7.1)

\displaystyle D^{\mathrm{pol}}(S,T)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if $T=S\setminus\{i\}$ for some $i\in S$,}\\
\frac{n-|S|}{n},&\text{if $T=S$,}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2)

The up operator
U_{\mu}^{\mathrm{pol}}
is defined as
U^{\mathrm{pol}}=\Pi U_{n-1\to n,\mu}\Pi^{*}
. Explicitly,

(7.2)

\displaystyle U_{\nu}^{\mathrm{pol}}(T,S)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{\mu(S)}{\mu D^{\mathrm{pol}}(T)},&\text{if $S=T\cup\{i\}$ for some $i\in[n]\setminus T$}\\
\frac{n-|T|}{n}\cdot\frac{\mu(S)}{\mu D^{\mathrm{pol}}(T)},&\text{if $S=T$,}\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

For a distribution
\mu
on
\{\pm 1\}^{n}
, define the polarized walk centered at
x_{0}
by the above with the bijection
\{\pm 1\}^{n}\to\mathcal{P}([n])
defined by

x\mapsto x_{+}:=\left\{{i}:{x_{i}\neq(x_{0})_{i}}\right\}
.
Let
\rho_{\text{EC}}(\mu)
denote the maximum
\rho
for which
D^{\mathrm{pol}}
satisfies
(1-\rho)
-entropy contraction with respect to
\mu
.
11
11

11

Note that the dependence on
x_{0}
is implicit in the notation. When
\mu
is supported on the wedge
B_{r}(x_{0})
, we take
x_{0}
as the center for the polarized walk.

Note that the down operators do not depend on
\mu
, but the up operators do.
Moreover,
U_{\mu}^{\mathrm{pol}}
is the adjoint of
D^{\mathrm{pol}}
with respect to
\mu
. Also, note that by the data processing inequality,
P_{\mu}^{\mathrm{pol}}
has at least as much entropy contraction as
D^{\mathrm{pol}}
with respect to
\mu
.

We also require properties about measures on
\{0,1\}^{n}
related to the geometry of their generating polynomials. We refer to
[
33
]
and
[
12
]
for background.

Definition 7.10
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution on
\{0,1\}^{n}
. The
generating polynomial
of
\mu
is the polynomial in
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
given by

p_{\mu}(z)=\sum_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\mu(z)z^{\sigma},

where
z^{\sigma}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{\sigma_{i}}
.

We will also write
z^{S}=\prod_{i\in S}z_{i}
for
S\subseteq[n]
. We write
\partial_{i}:=\partial_{z_{i}}
.

Definition 7.11
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{R}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
.

(1)

We say that
p
is
real stable
if
p
has no zero in the upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, where
\mathbb{H}=\left\{{z\in\mathbb{C}}:{\Im z>0}\right\}
.
A distribution
\mu
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
is
strongly Rayleigh
if
p_{\mu}
is real stable.

(2)

We say that
p
is
log-concave
if
\log p
is concave on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
. We consider 0 to be log-concave.

(3)

We say that
p
is
strongly log-concave
if for all
k\geqslant 0
and
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in[n]
,
\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{k}}p
is log-concave.

(4)

We say that
p
is
completely log-concave
if for all
k\geqslant 0
and
V\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}^{n\times k}
,

D_{V}p(z):=\left({\prod_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{ij}\partial_{i}}\right)p(z)

is non-negative and log-concave.

A distribution
\mu
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
is (strongly/completely) log-concave if
p_{\mu}
is (strongly/completely) log-concave.

Proposition 7.12.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

The following implications hold for a polynomial
p\in\mathbb{R}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
:

\displaystyle\textup{Real stable}\implies\textup{Completely log-concave}\implies\textup{Strongly log-concave}\implies\textup{Log-concave}

If
p
is homogeneous,
p
is completely log-concave iff it is strongly log-concave.

Proposition 7.13.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

The following operations preserve real stability and (strong/complete) log-concavity.
12
12

12

(s/c) log-concave is to be read as strongly log-concave or completely log-concave or log-concave, respectively.

(1)

Product: If
p,q
are real stable (respectively, log-concave) then
p\cdot q
is real stable (log-concave).

(2)

Symmetrization: If
p(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is real stable ((s/c) log-concave), then
p(z_{1},z_{1},z_{3},\ldots,z_{n})
is real-stable ((s/c) log-concave).

(3)

Specialization: If
p(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is real stable ((s/c) log-concave), then
p(a,z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is real-stable ((s/c) log-concave) for
a\in\mathbb{R}
(
a\geqslant 0
).

(4)

External field: If
p(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is real stable ((s/c) log-concave), then so is
q(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=p(w_{1}z_{1},\ldots,w_{n}z_{n})
for any
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\geqslant 0
.

(5)

Differentiation: If
p
is real stable or strongly or completely log-concave, then so is
\partial_{i}p
.

(6)

Homogenization: If
p
is real stable, then
p^{\mathrm{hom}}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n+1})=z_{n+1}^{\deg p}p\left({\frac{z_{1}}{z_{n+1}},\cdots,\frac{z_{n}}{z_{n+1}}}\right)
is real stable.

(7)

Polarization (
[
14
, Proposition 43]
,
[
36
]
): Suppose
p(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}c_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}
is homogeneous and strongly log-concave with degree at most
\kappa_{i}
in
z_{i}
. Then so is its polarization with degree
\kappa
,
\operatorname{polar}_{\kappa}(p)
, defined by

\operatorname{polar}_{\kappa}(p)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}c_{\alpha}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\binom{\kappa_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}}e_{\alpha_{i}}(z_{i1},\ldots,z_{i\kappa_{i}})

as a polynomial in
z_{ij}
,
i\in[n]
,
j\in[\kappa_{i}]
, where
e_{k}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=\sum_{S\in\binom{[n]}{k}}y^{S}
is the
k
th elementary symmetric polynomial.

Lemma 7.14.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

The following hold.

(1)

The elementary symmetric polynomial
e_{k}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\sum_{S\in\binom{[n]}{k}}z^{S}
is real stable.

(2)

For any matroid
M
,
p_{M}(z)=\sum_{B\in M}z^{B}
is completely log-concave
[
12
, Theorem 4.2]
, and its homogenization

p_{M}^{\mathrm{hom}}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},y)=\sum_{B\in M}z^{B}y^{n-|B|}

is also completely log-concave
[
15
, Theorem 4.1]
.

This allows us to show the following.

Lemma 7.15.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider a restricted product distribution on
\{0,1\}^{n}
,
\mu=\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}
.

(1)

If
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{k}
, then
\mu
is strongly Rayleigh.

(2)

If
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}
, then
\mu
is completely log-concave.

Proof 23
.

Let
w_{i}=e^{h_{i}}
.

Slice
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{k}
.
The generating polynomial of
\mu
is
p(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\propto\sum_{S\in\binom{[n]}{k}}\prod_{i\in S}w_{i}z_{i}
.
This is an elementary symmetric polynomial under the substitution
z_{i}\mapsfrom w_{i}z_{i}
and rescaling, hence real stable by
Lemma
7.14
and
Proposition
7.13
.

Wedge
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}
.
The generating polynomial of
\mu
is
p(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\propto\sum_{S\in\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}}\prod_{i\in S}w_{i}z_{i}
.
This is a matroid polynomial under the substitution
z_{i}\mapsfrom w_{i}z_{i}
and rescaling, hence completely log-concave by
Lemma
7.14
and
Proposition
7.13
.

Real stability and log-concavity give some useful facts about the corresponding measures.

Lemma 7.16
(Negative correlation for strongly Rayleigh measures,
[
33
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

If
\mu
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
is strongly Rayleigh, then it satisfies negative correlation: for any
i\neq j
,

\mu(\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{j}=1)\leqslant\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu(\sigma_{j}=1).

Lemma 7.17
(Ultra-log-concavity of rank sequence,
[
68
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

If
p(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}x^{\sigma}
,
c_{\sigma}\geqslant 0
is strongly log-concave, then letting
a_{k}=\sum_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k}c_{\sigma}
, the sequence
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n})
is ultra-log-concave, that is,

\left({\frac{a_{k}}{\binom{n}{k}}}\right)^{2}\geqslant\frac{a_{k-1}}{\binom{n}{k-1}}\cdot\frac{a_{k+1}}{\binom{n}{k+1}},

and hence log-concave,
a_{k}^{2}\geqslant a_{k-1}a_{k+1}
.

Lemma 7.18
(Entropy contraction for log-concave distributions,
[
49
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\mu
be a log-concave distribution on
\binom{[n]}{k}
. Then
D_{n\to n-1}
satisfies
(1-\frac{1}{k})
-entropy contraction with respect to
\mu
: If
\nu\ll\mu
, then

\operatorname{KL}(\nu D_{k\to k-1}\|\mu D_{k\to k-1})\leqslant\operatorname{KL}(\nu\|\mu).

We also introduce the functional inequalities for Langevin diffusion on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Given a probability density
\mu(x)\propto e^{-U(x)}
with
U\in C^{1}
, define the generator by
\mathscr{L}f=-\left\langle\nabla f,\nabla U\right\rangle+\Delta f
and the Dirichlet form by

\mathscr{E}(f,g)=\left\langle f,\mathscr{L}g\right\rangle_{\mu}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\langle\nabla f,\nabla g\right\rangle d\mu.

Theorem 7.19
(Bakry–Émery,
[
22
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose
\mu
is
\alpha
-log-concave, that is,
\mu(x)\propto e^{-U(x)}
where
U
is
\alpha
-strongly convex. Then
\mu
satisfies a log-Sobolev and hence Poincaré inequality with constant
\alpha
, that is,

\displaystyle\forall f

\displaystyle:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0},

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu}(f)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2\alpha}\mathscr{E}(f,\log f)=\frac{1}{2\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\left\|{\nabla f}\right\|^{2}}{f}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\forall f

\displaystyle:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu}(f)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}\mathscr{E}(f,f)=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\|{\nabla f}\right\|^{2}\,d\mu.

In particular,
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)
satisfies a log-Sobolev and hence Poincaré inequality with constant
\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}
.

7.2.
Entropy contraction for Ising models on the slice/wedge

We use the needle decomposition into rank-1 Ising models by
[
55
]
, then decompose a rank-1 into product measures using Hubbard–Stratonovich
[
73
]
(see also
[
28
]
,
[
82
]
), and use the localization framework of
[
46
]
to derive the functional inequality.
We follow
[
14
]
which applies the localization framework to the polarized walk.
We draw inspiration from
[
71
,
70
]
who show a weak Poincaré inequality for localized distributions for the spherical
p
-spin model. Whereas they proceed by showing a Poincaré inequality on spherical caps, we show entropy contraction on wedges. Our proof shares some features with
[
90
]
, who also use Hubbard–Stratonovich and Gaussian approximation, and prove a similar bound on the covariance matrix in their setting.

Lemma 7.20
(Entropy contraction for SK on wedges)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
and
0<\beta<\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}
.
There is a constant
c_{1}
such that for small enough
\varepsilon
, with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,

\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{A,h}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}\exp\left({-4\left({1+\frac{1}{1-2\left({\beta+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}+\beta c_{1}\sqrt{h(4\varepsilon)}}\right)^{2}}}\right)}\right).

Here
h(\varepsilon)=-\varepsilon\log\varepsilon-(1-\varepsilon)\log(1-\varepsilon)
is the binary entropy function.
We first understand product distributions restricted to wedges, which will be the basic components in our decomposition.

Lemma 7.21
(Covariance bound for product distributions on the slice/wedge)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider a product distribution on
\{0,1\}^{n}
restricted to the slice or wedge,
\mu=\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}
where
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{k}
or
\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}
.
Let
p_{i}=\left\langle\sigma_{i}\right\rangle_{\mu}
.
Then

\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu)\preceq 2\cdot\diag(p_{i}(1-p_{i})).

The best bound we can obtain on the operator norm of the covariance is
\left\lVert\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu)\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, which by itself is not useful. This lemma gives us the useful fact that the covariance cannot have many large eigenvalues. As a corollary,
\mathsf{Tr}[\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu)]\leqslant 2k
.

Proof 24
.

Slice
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{k}
.
Since
\mu
is strongly Rayleigh by
Lemma
7.15
, we have negative correlation (
Lemma
7.16
): for any
i\neq j
,

\mu(\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{j}=1)\leqslant\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu(\sigma_{j}=1).

Hence, the off-diagonal entries in
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu_{h}}(\sigma)
are all
\leqslant 0
. Let
D=\diag(\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{\mu})\diag(1-\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle_{\mu})
,
C=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu)
, and
M=2D-C
. All entries of
M
are non-negative.
By linearity of expectation,
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu(\sigma_{j}=1)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\sigma_{j}=k
.
Hence for all
i
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq i}M_{ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq i}[\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu(\sigma_{j}=1)-\mu(\sigma_{i}=1,\sigma_{j}=1)]=\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\sum_{j\neq i}[\mu(\sigma_{j}=1)-\mu(\sigma_{j}=1|\sigma_{i}=1)]

\displaystyle=\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)[(k-\mu(\sigma_{i}=1))-(k-1)]=\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)(1-\mu(\sigma_{i}=1))=M_{ii}.

Therefore, by Gerschgorin’s disc theorem, all eigenvalues of
M
are non-negative, so
C\preceq D
.

Wedge
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}
.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq i}M_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\sum_{j\neq i}[\mu(\sigma_{j}=1)-\mu(\sigma_{j}=1|\sigma_{i}=1)]=\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu(\sigma_{i}=0)\sum_{j\neq i}[\mu(\sigma_{j}=1|\sigma_{i}=0)-\mu(\sigma_{j}=1|\sigma_{i}=1)]

\displaystyle=\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu(\sigma_{i}=0)\left[{\sum_{j\neq i}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{j}|\sigma_{i}=0]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{j}|\sigma_{i}=1]}\right].

Let
a_{m}=\mu(\sum_{j\neq i}\sigma_{j}=m|\sigma_{i}=0)
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq i}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{j}|\sigma_{i}=0]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{j}|\sigma_{i}=1]

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k}k}{a_{0}+\cdots+a_{k}}-\frac{a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k-1}(k-1)}{a_{0}+\cdots+a_{k-1}}=\frac{a_{k}(kS-T)}{S(S+a_{k})}

where
S=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}a_{m}
and
T=\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}ma_{m}
. We claim this is
\leqslant 1
. Indeed, this is equivalent to

a_{k}(a_{k-2}+\cdots+(k-1)a_{0})\leqslant(a_{0}+\cdots+a_{k-1})^{2}.

Note
\mu_{h}|_{\Omega\cap\{\sigma_{i}=0\}}
is completely log-concave by
Lemma
7.15
. Hence, the sequence
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{k})
is log-concave (
Lemma
7.17
), so
a_{i}a_{j}\geqslant a_{i-1}a_{j+1}
for any
0<i\leqslant j<k
. Using this repeatedly shows the desired inequality, and we also obtain
\sum_{j\neq i}M_{ij}\leqslant M_{ii}
.

Lemma
7.21
allows us the following calculation. For any
x
such that
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
, for
\Omega=B_{k}(x_{0})
(noting a factor of 4 from rescaling from
\{0,1\}^{n}
to
\{\pm 1\}^{n}
),

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}(\left\langle x,\sigma\right\rangle)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left\langle x,2\diag\left({\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu(\sigma_{i}=-1)}\right)x\right\rangle

(7.3)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}(8\mu(\sigma_{i}=1)\wedge 2)x_{i}^{2}\leqslant 2\max_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{4k}}\sum_{j=1}^{4k}x_{i_{j}}^{2}\leqslant 2\max_{|S|=4k}\left\lVert A_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}.

We would like to rule out the bad example where
A
is concentrated on a subset of coordinates
S
, so there is no reduction in the interaction strength of
A
on
S
.
For this, we show that with high probability, we can bound the operator norms by all submatrices of size
\varepsilon n\times\varepsilon n
by some function of
\varepsilon
that is converging to 0 (which is in fact
O\left({\varepsilon\log\left({\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)}\right)
. The idea that the worst subsystem is only a small factor worse is also used by
[
3
]
for
p
-spin systems
13
13

13

This will not give sharp bounds, but this is fine because we can always run for longer time to concentrate the localized distribution on smaller slices.
.

Theorem 7.22
(
[
117
, Corollary 7.3.2]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
. For some constant
c
,

P(\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\geqslant 2+t)\leqslant 2e^{-cnt^{2}}.

Lemma 7.23
(High-probability bound on operator norm of small submatrices)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

There is a constant
c_{1}
such that the following holds.
Let
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
. Then

P\left({\forall|S|\leqslant\varepsilon n,\left\lVert A_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant c_{1}\sqrt{h(\varepsilon)}}\right)\geqslant 1-2e^{-h(\varepsilon)n}.

Proof 25
.

We can apply
Theorem
7.22
with a union bound over all
e^{(h(\varepsilon)+o(1))n}
sets of
\leqslant\varepsilon n
coordinates of
[n]
:

P\left({\forall|S|\leqslant\varepsilon n,\left\lVert A_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant(2+t)\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\geqslant 1-2e^{(h(\varepsilon)+o(1)-c\varepsilon t^{2})n}.

We can choose
t=\Theta\left({\sqrt{\frac{h(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}}\right)
with appropriate constants to get the result.

The following gives a decomposition of Ising models into rank-1 Ising models (with interaction matrix bounded by the original) which preserve the expectation of a given test function
\varphi
. This means that to prove a functional inequality (Poincaré or log-Sobolev) or the associated contraction of variance or entropy, it suffices to bound a functional inequality for these rank-1 Ising models, since no new variance/entropy is added by the decomposition (i.e., we have conservation of variance/entropy).

Theorem 7.24
(Needle decomposition for Ising models,
[
55
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\Omega
be any finite subset of
\left\{{v}:{\left\|{v}\right\|=R}\right\}
, and consider Ising models on
\Omega
.
Given a test function
\varphi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and an Ising model
\mu_{A,h}
where
A
is PSD, there exists a measure
\nu_{\varphi}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that

\mu_{A,h}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mu_{uu^{\mathsf{T}},v}d\nu_{\varphi}(u,v)

where
\nu_{\varphi}
-a.s.,
uu^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
,
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{A,h}}\varphi=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{uu^{\mathsf{T}},v}}\varphi
.

We note although
[
55
]
only consider
\Omega=\{\pm 1\}^{n}
, the proof does not rely on the particular structure of
\Omega
, other than the fact that the norm is constant on
\Omega
.
While
[
55
]
prove the Poincaré inequality for mixture components through an influence argument, we make a further decomposition into a log-concave mixture of product distributions. This is given by the Hubbard–Stratonovich transform (
[
73
,
82
]
) (see
[
28
]
for an alternative log-concave decomposition).
(The localization process which gives the rank-1 decomposition can be thought of as an annealing step
[
46
, §4]
.)

Theorem 7.25
(Hubbard–Stratonovich transform,
[
73
,
82
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
A=XX^{\mathsf{T}}
be PSD, and let
\Omega
be any finite subset of
\left\{{v}:{\left\|{v}\right\|=R}\right\}
. Then we have the following decomposition of
\mu_{A}
into “product measures” restricted to
\Omega
, where
\lambda_{H}
denotes Lebesgue measure on the hyperplane
H
:

\displaystyle\mu_{A,h}|_{\Omega}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\int_{\im(A)}p(u)\mu_{X^{\mathsf{T}}u+h}|_{\Omega}(\sigma)d\lambda_{\im(A)}(u)

\displaystyle\text{where }p(u)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left({-\frac{1}{2}\left\|{u}\right\|^{2}}\right)\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega}\exp\left({\left\langle Xu+h,\sigma\right\rangle}\right).

Lemma 7.26
(Entropy contraction for polarized walk on product distribution on wedge)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

A product distribution restricted to the wedge
\Omega=B_{r}(x_{0})
,
\mu_{v}|_{\Omega}(x)\propto\exp(\left\langle v,x\right\rangle)
on
\Omega
, satisfies
\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{v}|_{\Omega})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}
.

Proof 26
.

Associating
\mu:=\mu_{v}|_{\Omega}
with a measure on
\mathcal{P}([n])
via the map
x\mapsto x_{+}:=\left\{{i}:{x_{i}\neq(x_{0})_{i}}\right\}

and letting
w_{i}=e^{-2v_{i}(x_{0})_{i}}
, the generating polynomial of
\mu_{v}
is
g(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\propto\sum_{|S|\leqslant r}\prod_{i\in S}w_{i}z_{i}
. This is a matroid polynomial under the substitution
z_{i}\mapsfrom w_{i}z_{i}
.
By
Lemma
7.14
and preservation of complete log-concavity under external fields (
Proposition
7.13
), its homogenization

p^{\mathrm{hom}}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},y)\propto\sum_{|S|\leqslant r}\prod_{i\in S}w_{i}z_{i}\cdot y^{n-|S|}.

is completely (and hence strongly) log-concave.
By
Proposition
7.13
,
the polarization (with degree
(1,\ldots,1,n)
)

\operatorname{polar}p^{\mathrm{hom}}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\propto\sum_{|S|\leqslant r}\prod_{i\in S}w_{i}z_{i}\cdot\frac{1}{\binom{n}{|S|}}e_{n-|S|}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})

is strongly log-concave.
The corresponding measure on
\binom{[n]\sqcup[n]}{n}
is exactly the polar homogenization
\mu\Pi
(
Definition
7.9
).
Therefore, by
Lemma
7.18
,
D_{n\to n-1}
has
(1-\frac{1}{n})
-entropy contraction with respect to
\mu\Pi
, and this implies that
D^{\mathrm{pol}}
satisfies the same contraction with respect to
\mu
by noting that
D^{\mathrm{pol}}
is defined so as to be the “projection” of
D_{n\to n-1}
via
\Pi_{\mu}^{*}
: for any
\nu\ll\mu
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{KL}(\nu D^{\mathrm{pol}}\|\mu D^{\mathrm{pol}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{KL}(\nu\Pi D_{n\to n-1}\|\mu\Pi D_{n\to n-1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\left({1-\frac{1}{n}}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\nu\Pi\|\mu\Pi)=\left({1-\frac{1}{n}}\right)\operatorname{KL}(\nu\|\mu).

For the last equality, we note that the kernel
\Pi
applied to distinct points has disjoint supports.

Theorem 7.27
(Entropy contraction from covariance bound under localization, cf.
[
14
, Theorem 86]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\nu
be a measure on
B_{r}(x_{0})

and
J
be a PSD
n\times n
matrix. For any
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 1
and every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, consider the probability measure defined by

\frac{d\mu_{\lambda,v}}{d\nu}(x)\propto\exp\left({-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left\langle x,Jx\right\rangle+\left\langle v,x\right\rangle}\right).

Suppose that for some
\alpha:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\left\lVert\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{\lambda,v})\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\alpha(\lambda),\quad\forall\lambda\in[0,1],\,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and for some
\varepsilon>0
,

\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{1,v})\geqslant\varepsilon,\quad\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Then

\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\nu)\geqslant\varepsilon\exp\left({-\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\int_{0}^{1}\alpha(\lambda)\,d\lambda}\right).

Note that
[
14
, Theorem 86]
is written for a specific measure, but the proof works for any measure as stated above. This is the analogue of
[
46
, Theorem 49]
, which is for Glauber dynamics.

Proof 27
.

Stochastic localization with driving matrix
C_{t}=J^{1/2}
gives the decomposition of measure

\nu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mu_{1,v}\,d\pi(v)

where
\pi
is the distribution of the resulting
v
in the stochastic localization process. Using the given operator norm bound on the covariance and
[
46
, Lemma 40]
,
\mu_{\lambda,v}
is
\alpha(\lambda)\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
-entropically stable with respect to
\psi(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{J^{1/2}(x-y)}\right\|^{2}
. Hence by
[
46
, Proposition 39]
, for a test function
f
,

(7.4)

\displaystyle\exp\left({-\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\int_{0}^{1}\alpha(\lambda)\,d\lambda}\right)\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\nu}[f]\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{v\sim\pi}[\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu_{1,v}}[f]].

Next, note by the equivalent definition of entropy contraction in
Proposition
7.6
and
Lemma
7.7
,

(7.5)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{v\sim\pi}[\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu_{1,v}}[f]]\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[p:EC-equiv]{Proposition~\ref*{p:EC-equiv}}}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{v\sim\pi}\left[\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim\mu_{1,v}D^{\mathrm{pol}}}\left[\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{U_{\mu_{1,v}}^{\mathrm{pol}}(z,\cdot)}[f]\right]\right]\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[l:concave-entropy-dec]{Lemma~\ref*{l:concave-entropy-dec}}}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim\nu D^{\mathrm{pol}}}\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{U_{\nu}^{\mathrm{pol}}(z,\cdot)}[f].

Then combining (
7.4
) and (
7.5
) gives

\varepsilon\exp\left({-\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\int_{0}^{1}\alpha(\lambda)\,d\lambda}\right)\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\nu}[f]\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim\nu D^{\mathrm{pol}}}\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{U_{\nu}^{\mathrm{pol}}(z,\cdot)}[f]

and the conclusion follows using the equivalent definition of entropy contraction in
Proposition
7.6
.

Lemma 7.28
(Entropy contraction for Ising model on wedges via two-stage decomposition)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\Omega=B_{r}(x_{0})
for some
x_{0}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
.
Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a PSD matrix and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that for all
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
,
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if we define

(7.6)

\displaystyle p(u)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left({-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\right)\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega}\exp\left({\left\langle u\sqrt{\lambda}x+h,\sigma\right\rangle}\right),

then
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}(p)\leqslant V(\lambda)
, for some non-decreasing function
V:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
.
Then

\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{A,h}|_{B_{r}(x_{0})})\geqslant\frac{1}{n}\exp\left({-2\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\left({1+\int_{0}^{1}V(\lambda)\,d\lambda}\right)}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{n}\exp\left({-2\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\left({1+V(1)}\right)}\right).

Proof 28
.

Given a test function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
decompose
\mu_{A,h}|_{\Omega}
using
Theorem
7.24
,

(7.7)

\displaystyle\mu_{A,h}|_{\Omega}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega}\,d\nu_{f}(x,v),

where
\nu_{f}
is supported on
x
’s such that
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
.
Suppose
x
is such that
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
, and consider
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 1
. The Hubbard–Stratonovich transform (
Theorem
7.25
) gives us

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{\lambda}(u)\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}|_{\Omega}(\sigma)du

where
p=p_{\lambda}
is defined by (
7.6
).
By assumption,
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}(p)\leqslant V(\lambda)
.
Note

\frac{d}{du}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}}\sigma=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}}\left({\left\langle\sigma,\sqrt{\lambda}x\right\rangle}\right)\leqslant 2\lambda\left\|{x}\right\|^{2}

by
Lemma
7.21
, so
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}}\sigma
is
2\lambda\left\|{x}\right\|^{2}
-Lipschitz.
Therefore,
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}}\sigma
has covariance at most
2\lambda\left\|{x}\right\|^{2}V(\lambda)
.
By covariance decomposition,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu_{\lambda xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{p}(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}|_{\Omega}}\sigma)+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{p}\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}|_{\Omega}}(\sigma)

(7.9)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left({2\lambda\left\|{x}\right\|^{2}V(\lambda)+2}\right)I_{n}.

Defining
(\cdot)_{\lambda,v^{\prime}}
as in
Theorem
7.27
with
J=xx^{\mathsf{T}}
, we have that for all
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 1
that

\left\lVert\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}((\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega})_{1-\lambda,v^{\prime}})\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2(1+\lambda\left\|{x}\right\|^{2}V(\lambda))\quad\forall\lambda\in[0,1],\,v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

because (
7.9
) applies to
(\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega})_{1-\lambda,v^{\prime}}=\mu_{\lambda xx^{\mathsf{T}},v+v^{\prime}}|_{\Omega}
. Hence by
Theorem
7.27
, noting
\left\|{x}\right\|^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}
,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega})

\displaystyle\geqslant\inf_{v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{v^{\prime}}|_{\Omega})\cdot\exp\left({-2\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\left({1+\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\int_{0}^{1}\lambda V(\lambda)\,d\lambda}\right)}\right)

(7.10)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{n}\exp\left({-2\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\left({1+\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\int_{0}^{1}\lambda V(\lambda)\,d\lambda}\right)}\right),

by
Lemma
7.26
.
Now we proceed as in
Theorem
7.27
, noting that we have preservation of entropy in the decomposition: for
f\geqslant 0
such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{A,h}}f>0
, by
Theorem
7.24
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu_{A,h}}[f]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{A,h}}[f\log f]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{A,h}}[f]\log\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{A,h}}[f]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(x,v)\sim\nu_{f}}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}}[f\log f]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}}[f]\log\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}}[f]}\right]}\right]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(x,v)\sim\nu_{f}}\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}}[f].

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu_{A,h}}[f]

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(x,v)\sim\nu_{f}}\left[{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}}[f]}\right]\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[p:EC-equiv]{Proposition~\ref*{p:EC-equiv}}}}\frac{1}{\inf_{xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega})}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(x,v)\sim\nu_{f}}\left[{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}D^{\mathrm{pol}}}\left[{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{U_{\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}}^{\mathrm{pol}}(z,\cdot)}[f]}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\hyperref@@ii[l:concave-entropy-dec]{Lemma~\ref*{l:concave-entropy-dec}}}}\frac{1}{\inf_{xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega})}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{z\sim\mu_{A,h}}\left[{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Ent}}}_{U_{\mu_{A,h}}^{\mathrm{pol}}(z,\cdot)}[f]}\right].

By
Proposition
7.6
, this shows that
\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{A,h}|_{\Omega})\geqslant\inf_{xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{xx^{\mathsf{T}},v}|_{\Omega})
, which, combined with (
7.10
), gives the desired result.

Lemma 7.29
(Entropy contraction for Ising model on wedges)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Suppose
A
is a PSD matrix such that for all
|S|\leqslant 4\varepsilon n
,
\left\lVert A_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
. Let
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{\leqslant\varepsilon n}
. Then for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{A,h}|_{\Omega})\geqslant\exp\left({-2\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\left({1+\frac{\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}}{1-2C^{2}}}\right)}\right).

Proof.

It suffices to compute an upper bound
V(\lambda)
in
Lemma
7.28
.
Let
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
, and define
p
as in (
7.6
).
We compute that the mixture distribution is log-concave by (
7.3
) (note
\lambda xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq\lambda A
),

\displaystyle-\log p^{\prime\prime}(u)=1-\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{u\sqrt{\lambda}x+v}|_{\Omega}}\left({\left\langle\sqrt{\lambda}x,\sigma\right\rangle}\right)\geqslant 1-2\lambda^{2}C^{2}.

Therefore,
p
has variance at most
V(\lambda):=\frac{1}{1-2\lambda^{2}C^{2}}
, and plugging this into
Lemma
7.28
gives the result.
∎

Proof 29

(Proof of
Lemma
7.20
)
.

For
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
, consider
\beta A+\gamma I
, where
2\beta<\gamma<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
(e.g.
\gamma=\beta+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}
). With probability
\geqslant 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,
\beta A+\gamma I
is PSD and has operator norm bounded by
\sqrt{2}
.
Also with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
, by
Lemma
7.23
, for
|S|\leqslant 4\varepsilon n
,

\left\lVert(\beta A+\gamma I)_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\gamma+\beta c_{1}\sqrt{h(4\varepsilon)}<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

for small enough
\varepsilon
.
The result than follows from
Lemma
7.29
.

7.3.
Attaining
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
using refined covariance bound

Extending entropy contraction to all
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
hinges on
Lemma
7.35
, which gives a better covariance bound when the uncertainty isn’t limited to a small number of coordinates.

Lemma 7.30
(Entropy contraction for SK on wedges, II)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Fix
x_{0}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
.
Let
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
and
0<\beta<\frac{1}{2}
.
There is a constant
\varepsilon_{0}
such that for all
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
(constants depending on
\beta
),
\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{A,h}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})})\geqslant\frac{\rho_{\beta}}{n}
where
\rho_{\beta}>0
is a constant depending only on
\beta
.

We have the following elementary fact about product distributions on slices.

Lemma 7.31
(Monotonicity)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider a product distribution
\mu_{h}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
, and let
\mu^{(k)}=\mu_{h}|_{\binom{[n]}{k}}
.
Let
p_{j}^{k}=\mu^{(k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)
. Then for
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
,
p_{j}^{k^{\prime}}\leqslant p_{j}^{k}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
p_{j}^{k}\leqslant p_{j}^{k+1}
. This is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k-1}e^{h_{j}+\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k-1}e^{h_{j}+\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}+\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k}e^{\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k}e^{h_{j}+\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k}e^{h_{j}+\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}+\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k+1}e^{\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k}e^{\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k-1}e^{h_{j}+\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k+1}e^{\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}{\sum_{\left\|{\sigma_{-j}}\right\|_{1}=k}e^{h_{j}+\left\langle h_{-j},\sigma_{-j}\right\rangle}}

\displaystyle\iff a_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant a_{k-1}a_{k+1}

where
a_{k}=\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)
. This holds by log-concavity of
\mu_{h}
.
∎

We bound the differences between the “effective uncertainty” (sum of covariances of individual coordinates) for the unrestricted distribution (with appropriate tilts) and the distribution restricted to a slice or wedge. We will frequently need to switch between these quantities in the coming proofs.

Lemma 7.32.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider a product distribution
\mu_{h}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
f(p)=p(1-p)
.
For
k\in[0,n]
, let
t(k)
be such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}}
be such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k
,
q_{i}^{(k)}=\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{i}=1)
, and
n^{(k)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(q_{i}^{(k)})
. (When
k=0
or
n
, set
t=-\infty
,
t=\infty
, respectively.) For
k\in\{0,\ldots n\}
, let
p_{i}^{(k)}=\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)
and
m^{(k)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(p_{i}^{(k)})
;
let
p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}=\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)
and
m^{(\leqslant k)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})
.
Then the following hold.

(1)

We have for
k<l
that

\displaystyle|m^{(k)}-m^{(l)}|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(k)})-f(p_{i}^{(l)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({\max_{p\in[p_{i}^{(k)},p_{i}^{(l)}]}f(p)-\min_{p\in[p_{i}^{(k)},p_{i}^{(l)}]}f(p)}\right)\leqslant l-k

\displaystyle|n^{(k)}-n^{(l)}|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(q_{i}^{(k)})-f(q_{i}^{(l)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({\max_{p\in[q_{i}^{(k)},q_{i}^{(l)}]}f(p)-\min_{p\in[q_{i}^{(k)},q_{i}^{(l)}]}f(p)}\right)\leqslant l-k.

(2)

Let
\overline{k}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
.
We have

\displaystyle|m^{(k)}-n^{(k)}|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(k)})-f(q_{i}^{(k)})}\right|

\displaystyle=O(\sqrt{n^{(k)}})=O(\sqrt{m^{(k)}})

\displaystyle|m^{(\leqslant k)}-m^{(k\wedge\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})-f(p_{i}^{(k\wedge\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})}\right|

\displaystyle=O(\sqrt{n^{(k\wedge\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}})=O(\sqrt{m^{(\leqslant k)}})

Proof 30
.

The proof proceeds in two parts, where we split the second part further into two cases.

Part 1.
For (1), note first that
t
exists because
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function tending to 0 and
n
as
t\to-\infty
and
t\to\infty
, respectively. (It is continuous and increasing because
\frac{d}{dt}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})>0
.)
Also note that
p^{(k)}_{i}
is monotonically increasing in
k
by
Lemma
7.31
. Note that
f(p)
is 1-Lipschitz on
[0,1]
. Hence

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(k)})-f(p_{i}^{(l)})}\right|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({\max_{p\in[p_{i}^{(k)},p_{i}^{(l)}]}f(p)-\min_{p\in[p_{i}^{(k)},p_{i}^{(l)}]}f(p)}\right)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{(l)}-p_{i}^{(k)}=l-k.

Similarly, for
k,l\in[0,n]
, we obtain the second inequality in the same way.

Part 2a.
Now consider (2). In the following, all expectations are with respect to
\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}
.
Note that
q_{i}^{(k)}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{i}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}]]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}]
. Then noting
f(y)=f(x)+(1-2x)(y-x)-(y-x)^{2}
, taking
x=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}]
and
y=f(p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})})
and noting the linear term disappears under expectation,

\displaystyle\left|{f(q_{i}^{(k)})-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|=\left|{f(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}])-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{f(p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})})+\left({p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}]}\right)^{2}}\right]-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})})-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left({p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}]}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})})-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left({p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}-p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma^{\prime}}\right\|_{1})}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})})-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|{p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}-p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma^{\prime}}\right\|_{1})}}\right|

where
\sigma^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
\sigma
.
Therefore, by the first part,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(q_{i}^{(k)})-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left[{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})})-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left|{p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}-p_{i}^{(\left\|{\sigma^{\prime}}\right\|_{1})}}\right|}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left|{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}-k}\right|+\frac{1}{2}\left|{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}-\left\|{\sigma^{\prime}}\right\|_{1}}\right|\leqslant 2\left({\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\left({\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}-k}\right)^{2}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=2\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}}(\sigma_{i})}\right)^{1/2}=2\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(q_{i})}\right)^{1/2}=2\sqrt{n^{(k)}}.

This shows that
|m^{(k)}-n^{(k)}|\leqslant 2\sqrt{n^{(k)}}
. Thus,
\sqrt{n^{(k)}}=O(\sqrt{m^{(k)}})
as
m^{(k)}\to\infty
, which shows the first equation in the second part.

Part 2b.
Now consider
p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}
. First suppose
k\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
.
We first consider
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}^{(\leqslant k)}
.
Note by Cauchy-Schwarz

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}}[k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}}[(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})\mathds{1}_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)^{1/2}}\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})^{1/2}=O(1)\cdot\sqrt{n^{(k)}}.

where the bound
\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)=\Omega(1)
holds by Berry-Esseen (or
Theorem
C.1
).
Therefore,

\displaystyle k-O(\sqrt{n^{(k)}})\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}}[\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k]\leqslant k

Now since
k\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
,
t(k)\leqslant 0
. We have that
\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma)\propto\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma)e^{t(k)\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}}
, so
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}
stochastically dominates
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}^{(\leqslant k)}
. Hence

\displaystyle k-O(\sqrt{n^{(k)}})\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}[\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k]\leqslant k.

As in part 1, by 1-Lipschitzness of
f
and noting
p_{i}^{(k)}\geqslant p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}
by monotonicity,

(7.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})-f(p_{i}^{(k)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}(p_{i}^{(k)}-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})=k-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}[\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k]=O(\sqrt{n^{(k)}}).

This shows
|m^{(\leqslant k)}-m^{(k)}|=O(\sqrt{n^{(k)}})
as
n^{(k)}\to\infty
. Combining with part 2a, we get
|m^{(\leqslant k)}-n^{(k)}|=O(\sqrt{n^{(k)}})
, and hence this is also
O(\sqrt{m^{(\leqslant k)}})
as
m^{(\leqslant k)}\to\infty
.

Now suppose
k>\overline{k}
. By monotonicity,
p_{i}^{(\leqslant\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}\leqslant p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}\leqslant q_{i}^{(\overline{k})}
. Hence by 1-Lipschitzness of
f
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})-f(p_{i}^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})-f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})}\right|+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})-f(p_{i}^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}(q_{i}^{(\overline{k})}-p_{i}^{(\leqslant\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})-f(p_{i}^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left({\overline{k}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}[\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant\overline{k}]}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{f(p_{i}^{(\leqslant\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})-f(p_{i}^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)})}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant(\overline{k}-\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)+O(\sqrt{n^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}})+O(\sqrt{n^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}})=O(\sqrt{n^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}})

where both terms are bounded by (
7.11
) applied to
\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor
. Since
|m^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}-n^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}|=O(\sqrt{n^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}})
, this is also
O(\sqrt{m^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}})
, and hence
O(\sqrt{m^{(\leqslant k)}})
.

Lemma 7.33
(Cross-ratio for product distribution)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider a product distribution
\mu_{h}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
p_{i}=\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1)
and
m=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(1-p_{i})
.
Let
a_{k}=\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{i}=k)
.
Then for any
k_{1}\leqslant k_{2}
with
k_{i}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=o(m)
for
i=1,2
, we have

\frac{a_{k_{1}-1}a_{k_{2}+1}}{a_{k_{1}}a_{k_{2}}}=1-\frac{k_{2}-k_{1}+1}{m}(1+o(1)).

In the proof we also show this is
1-\frac{k_{2}-k_{1}+1}{m^{(k)}}(1+o(1))
, where
m^{(k)}
is defined as in (
7.12
).

Proof 31
.

Let
f(p)=p(1-p)
.
Let
\mu_{h}^{(k)}:=\mu_{h}|_{\binom{[n]}{k}}
.
The proof hinges on a combinatorial interpretation.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k_{1}-1}a_{k_{2}+1}}{a_{k_{1}}a_{k_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left({\sum_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k_{1}-1}e^{\left\langle h,\sigma\right\rangle}}\right)\left({\sum_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k_{2}+1}e^{\left\langle h,\sigma\right\rangle}}\right)}{\left({\sum_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k_{1}}e^{\left\langle h,\sigma\right\rangle}}\right)\left({\sum_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k_{2}}e^{\left\langle h,\sigma\right\rangle}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}\frac{\left\{{(\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\sigma_{2}^{\prime})}:{\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\in\binom{[n]}{k_{1}-1},\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\in\binom{[n]}{k_{2}+1},\sigma_{1}^{\prime}+\sigma_{2}^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}}\right\}}{\left\{{(\sigma_{1}^{\prime\prime},\sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime})}:{\sigma_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in\binom{[n]}{k_{1}},\sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\binom{[n]}{k_{2}},\sigma_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}}\right\}}.

For
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
s_{1}=s_{1}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}):=\left|{\left\{{j}:{(\sigma_{1})_{j}=1,\,(\sigma_{2})_{j}=0}\right\}}\right|
and
s=s(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}):=\left|{\left\{{j}:{(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})_{j}=1}\right\}}\right|
.
(If we think of
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
as sets, then
s_{1}=|\sigma_{1}\setminus\sigma_{2}|
and
s=|\sigma_{1}\triangle\sigma_{2}|
.)
Let
r=k_{2}-k_{1}+1
.
Note that for
(\sigma_{1}^{\prime\prime},\sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime})
as in the denominator,
|\sigma_{1}^{\prime\prime}\triangle\sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime}|=|\sigma_{1}\triangle\sigma_{2}|=s=s_{1}+(k_{2}-k_{1}+s_{1})=2s_{1}+r-1
; we must choose
\sigma_{1}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\sigma_{2}^{\prime\prime}
with cardinality
s_{1}
from
\sigma_{1}\triangle\sigma_{2}
. In the numerator we choose it of size
s_{1}-1
. Hence the integrand equals

\binom{2s_{1}+r-1}{s_{1}-1}\big/\binom{2s_{1}+r-1}{s_{1}}=\frac{s_{1}}{s_{1}+r}=\frac{s-r+1}{s+r+1}
.
We can sample from
\mu_{h}^{(k^{\prime})}
as follows. Choose
t(k^{\prime})
such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t(k^{\prime})\mathds{1}}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime}
. Now sample from the product distribution
\mu_{h+t(k^{\prime})\mathds{1}}
and accept a sample only if
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime}
. This formulation allows us to use concentration for sums of independent random variables.
Let
p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}=\mu_{h}^{(k^{\prime})}(\sigma_{j}=1)
and

(7.12)

\displaystyle m^{(k^{\prime})}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})

be the effective uncertainty for
\mu_{h}^{(k^{\prime})}
.
Let
q_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}=\mu_{h+t(k^{\prime})\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{j}=1)
and
n^{(k^{\prime})}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(q_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})
be the effective uncertainty for
\mu_{h+t(k^{\prime})\mathds{1}}
.
By
Theorem
C.1
, with
\gamma
as the pdf of the standard normal,

\mu_{h+t(k^{\prime})\mathds{1}}\left({\left\{{\sigma}:{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime}}\right\}}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{(k^{\prime})}}}\gamma(0)-o\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{(k^{\prime})}}}}\right)=\Omega\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{(k^{\prime})}}}}\right).

Then

R(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}):=\frac{d\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu^{(k_{2})}}{d\mu_{h+t(k_{1})\mathds{1}}\otimes\mu_{h+t(k_{2})\mathds{1}}}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\leqslant C\sqrt{n^{(k_{1})}n^{(k_{2})}}
for some
C
for
n^{(k_{1})},n^{(k_{2})}
large enough.
By
Lemma
7.32
,

\displaystyle|m-n^{(k_{1})}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|{k_{1}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}}\right|=o(m)

\displaystyle|n^{(k_{1})}-n^{(k_{2})}|

\displaystyle\leqslant r-1

\displaystyle|n^{(k_{1})}-m^{(k_{1})}|

\displaystyle=O\left({\sqrt{m^{(k_{1})}}}\right)

\displaystyle\implies\left|{m-m^{(k_{1})}}\right|

\displaystyle=O\left({\sqrt{m^{(k_{1})}}}\right)+O\left({|k_{1}-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}|}\right).

Therefore, when
r=o(m^{(k_{2})})
, we have
n^{(k_{1})},n^{(k_{2})}=m^{(k_{2})}(1+o(1))
.
Let
2m^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}^{(k_{1})}(1-q_{j}^{(k_{2})})+(1-q_{j}^{(k_{1})})q_{j}^{(k_{2})}
.
Note
2m^{\prime}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}s
and

\displaystyle 2m^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[{q_{j}^{(k_{1})}(1-q_{j}^{(k_{1})})+q_{j}^{(k_{1})}(q_{j}^{(k_{2})}-q_{j}^{(k_{1})})}\right]+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[{(1-q_{j}^{(k_{2})})q_{j}^{(k_{2})}+(q_{j}^{(k_{2})}-q_{j}^{(k_{1})})q_{j}^{(k_{2})}}\right]

\displaystyle\in\sum_{j=1}^{n}f(q_{j}^{(k_{1})})+f(q_{j}^{(k_{2})})+\left[{-2|r-1|,2|r-1|}\right]=m^{(k_{1})}+m^{(k_{2})}+\left[{-2|r-1|,2|r-1|}\right]

by
Lemma
7.32
.
We moreover have

\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}s=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f\left({q_{j}^{(k_{1})}(1-q_{j}^{(k_{2})})+(1-q_{j}^{(k_{1})})q_{j}^{(k_{2})}}\right)\leqslant 2m^{\prime}.

Let
I=\{r-1,r+1,\ldots,2\left\lfloor m^{\prime}-\sqrt{m^{\prime}}\log m^{\prime}\right\rfloor-r-3\}
. If
s\nin I
, we have
\frac{2r}{s+r+1}\leqslant\frac{r}{\left\lfloor m^{\prime}-\sqrt{m^{\prime}}\log m^{\prime}\right\rfloor}
.
Now by Bernstein’s inequality as
m^{(k_{2})}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}\left[{\frac{s-r+1}{s+r+1}}\right]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}\left[{1-\frac{2r}{s+r+1}}\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\left({1-\frac{r}{\left\lfloor m^{\prime}-\sqrt{m^{\prime}}\log m^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\right)-4r\sum_{j\in I}\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}(s\leqslant j)\frac{1}{(j+r+1)(j+r+3)}

\displaystyle\geqslant\left({1-\frac{r}{\left\lfloor m^{\prime}-\sqrt{m^{\prime}}\log m^{\prime}\right\rfloor}}\right)-4r\sum_{j\in I}C\sqrt{n^{(k_{1})}n^{(k_{2})}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h+t(k_{1})\mathds{1}}\otimes\mu_{h+t(k_{2})\mathds{1}}}(s\leqslant j)\frac{1}{(j+r+1)(j+r+3)}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\frac{r}{m^{\prime}}(1+o(1))-O(1)r\sum_{j\in I}m^{\prime}\exp\left({-\frac{(j-2m^{\prime})^{2}/2}{|j-2m^{\prime}|/3+2m^{\prime}}}\right)\frac{1}{(j+r+1)(j+r+3)}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\frac{r}{m^{\prime}}(1+o(1))-O(1)r\sum_{j\in I}m^{\prime}\exp\left({-\Omega\left({\frac{(j-2m^{\prime})^{2}}{m^{\prime}}}\right)}\right)\frac{1}{(j+r)^{2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\frac{r}{m^{\prime}}(1+o(1))-O(1)rm^{\prime 2}e^{-\Omega(\log^{2}m^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\frac{r}{m^{\prime}}(1+o(1))

by considering the sum as a Gaussian tail. For the upper bound, for
j>2m^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}\left[{\frac{s-r+1}{s+r+1}}\right]

\displaystyle=1-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}\left[{\frac{2r}{s+r+1}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 1-\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\sim\mu_{h}^{(k_{1})}\otimes\mu_{h}^{(k_{2})}}(s\leqslant j)\frac{2r}{j+r+1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 1-\left({1-4m^{\prime}(1+o(1))\exp\left({-\frac{(j-2m^{\prime})^{2}/2}{(j-2m^{\prime})/3+2m^{\prime}}}\right)}\right)\frac{2r}{j+r+1}.

Choosing
j=2m^{\prime}+\sqrt{m^{\prime}}\log m^{\prime}
, we get an upper bound of
1-\frac{r}{m^{\prime}}(1-o(1))
.
Finally, note
m^{\prime}=m^{(k_{2})}(1+o(1))=m(1+o(1))
.

The following computation is useful.

Lemma 7.34.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\sigma(x)=\frac{e^{x}}{1+e^{x}}
. Let
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
E(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(h_{i}+t)
and
m(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(h_{i}+t)(1-\sigma(h_{i}+t))
. Then

\displaystyle E^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=m(t)

\displaystyle m^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(h_{i}+t)(1-\sigma(h_{i}+t))(1-2\sigma(h_{i}+t))\in[-m(t),m(t)].

Proof 32
.

Immediate.

We can now obtain tight control of the off-diagonal entries of the covariance matrix when the effective uncertainty is large.

Lemma 7.35
(Covariance matrix of product distribution on slice/wedge under high effective uncertainty)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider a product distribution
\mu_{h}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

(1)

Consider the restriction to a slice,
\mu^{(k)}:=\mu_{h}|_{\binom{[n]}{k}}
.
Let
p_{i}^{(k)}=\mu^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)
and define the effective uncertainty as
m^{(k)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{(k)}(1-p_{i}^{(k)})
.
Then as
m^{(k)}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu^{(k)}}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})=-p_{i}^{(k)}(1-p_{i}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}(1-p_{j}^{(k)})\cdot\frac{1}{m^{(k)}}(1+o(1)).

(2)

Consider the restriction to a wedge,
\mu^{(\leqslant k)}:=\mu_{h}|_{\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}}
.
Let
p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}=\mu^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)
and define the effective uncertainty as
m^{(\leqslant k)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})
.
Then as
m^{(\leqslant k)}\to\infty
, there exists
0<c<1
depending on
h
,
k
such that for all
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k)}}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})=-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})\cdot\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}(c+o(1)).

Proof.

Let
f(p)=p(1-p)
.
For a distribution
\mu
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
, let
\mu_{-i}
denote the distribution of all coordinates except the
i
th one (for
\mu_{h}
, denote this by
\mu_{h,-i}
).
We use the notation of
Lemma
7.32
, further using a subscript
-i
to denote the relevant quantities for the distribution
\mu_{h,-i}
.
Define the odds ratio by
\mathrm{odds}(p)=\frac{p}{1-p}
for
0<p<1
.
Let
a_{k^{\prime}}=\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime})
and
a_{k^{\prime},-i}=\mu_{h,-i}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime})
. (Here
\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{[n]\setminus\{i\}}
.)

Part (1).
Let
1\leqslant r<k
. First note that we can approximate the following quotient of odds ratios with
Lemma
7.33
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{odds}(\mu^{(k-r)}(\sigma_{i}=1))}{\mathrm{odds}(\mu^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu^{(k-r)}(\sigma_{i}=1)}{\mu^{(k-r)}(\sigma_{i}=0)}\Bigg/\frac{\mu^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)}{\mu^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=0)}

(7.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{k-r-1,-i}a_{k,-i}}{a_{k-r,-i}a_{k-1,-i}}=1-\frac{r}{m_{-i}^{(k-1)}}(1+o(1))

Let
p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}=\mu^{(k^{\prime})}(\sigma_{j}=1)
and
m^{(k^{\prime})}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}(1-p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})
.
We also have the equation

(7.14)

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=(1-p_{i}^{(k)})p_{j,-i}^{(k)}+p_{i}^{(k)}p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)}.

Hence writing
m^{(k^{\prime})}=p_{i}^{(k^{\prime})}(1-p_{i}^{(k^{\prime})})+\sum_{j\neq i}p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}(1-p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})
,

\sum_{j\neq i}\min_{p\in[p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)},p_{j,-i}^{(k)}]}f(p)\leqslant m^{(k)}\leqslant 1+\sum_{j\neq i}\max_{p\in[p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)},p_{j,-i}^{(k)}]}f(p).

By
Lemma
7.32
(1), this interval has length at most 1 and contains
m_{-i}^{(k-1)}
and
m_{-i}^{(k)}
, so that
m^{(k)}=m_{-i}^{(k-1)}+O(1)=m_{-i}^{(k)}+O(1)
. Hence

(7.15)

\displaystyle\frac{p_{i}^{(k-r)}/(1-p_{i}^{(k-r)})}{p_{i}^{(k)}/(1-p_{i}^{(k)})}=\frac{\mathrm{odds}(\mu^{(k-r)}(\sigma_{i}=1))}{\mathrm{odds}(\mu^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1))}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{r}{m^{(k)}}(1+o(1))

Applying this for
r=1
,
i
replaced by
j
and
\mu
replaced with
\mu|_{-i}
gives that

(7.16)

\displaystyle\frac{p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)}/(1-p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)})}{p_{j,-i}^{(k)}/(1-p_{j,-i}^{(k)})}=1-\frac{1}{m_{-i}^{(k)}}(1+o(1))=1-\frac{1}{m^{(k)}}(1+o(1))

We solve (
7.14
) and (
7.16
). For ease of notation, let
q=p_{j}^{(k)}
,
p=p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)}
,
r=p_{j,-i}^{(k)}
,
a=p_{i}^{(k)}
,
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{m^{(k)}}
. Then (
7.14
) becomes
q=(1-a)r+qp
and (
7.16
) becomes
\frac{p/(1-p)}{r/(1-r)}=1-\varepsilon(1+o(1))
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. This gives

\displaystyle\frac{1-r}{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-p)(1-\varepsilon(1+o(1)))}{p}

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{p+(1-p)(1-\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon))}

\displaystyle\implies q

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-a)p}{p+(1-p)(1-\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon))}+ap

\displaystyle=(1-a)p(1+\varepsilon(1+o(1))(1-p)+o(\varepsilon(1-p)))+ap

\displaystyle=p+(1-a)\varepsilon p(1-p)(1+o(1))

\displaystyle\implies 0

\displaystyle=up^{2}-(1+u)p+q

where
u=(1-a)\varepsilon(1+o(1))
. Then (as
u\to 0
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+u)+(1+u)\sqrt{1-\frac{4uq}{(1+u)^{2}}}}{2q}=\frac{1+u}{q}\left({1-\left({\frac{qu}{(1+u)^{2}}}\right)+O(q^{2}u^{2})}\right)

\displaystyle\implies p

\displaystyle=q(1-u+O(u^{2}))\left({1+\frac{qu}{(1+u)^{2}}+O(u^{2})}\right)=q-q(1-q)u(1+o(1)).

Substituting back in,

\displaystyle p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)}

\displaystyle=p_{j}^{(k)}-(1-p_{i}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}(1-p_{j}^{(k)})\frac{1}{m^{(k)}}(1+o(1))

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu^{(k)}}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})

\displaystyle=p_{i}^{(k)}(p_{j,-i}^{(k-1)}-p_{j}^{(k)})=-p_{i}^{(k)}(1-p_{i}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}(1-p_{j}^{(k)})\frac{1}{m^{(k)}}(1+o(1)).

Part (2).
For the second part, we use the superscripts
{}^{(\leqslant k^{\prime})}
as the obvious analogue of the notations in the first part.
To compute expectations, we will condition on which slice the sample is in. Towards this, we let
a_{k^{\prime}}=\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime})
and
S_{k}=\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k}a_{k^{\prime}}=\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k^{\prime})
. For
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
, note
\mu^{(\leqslant k)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime})=\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k}}
.
The analogue of (
7.14
) is

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}=(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}+p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}.

Then

(7.17)

\displaystyle p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}=(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})(p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}).

The rest of the proof will be devoted to computing
p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}
.
We have

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k}a_{k^{\prime}}p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{S_{k-1}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}a_{k^{\prime}}p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}

(7.18)

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}}\right)

Suppose
\frac{\mathrm{odds}(p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})}{\mathrm{odds}(p_{j}^{(k)})}=R_{k^{\prime}}
. Then

p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}=\frac{R_{k^{\prime}}}{R_{k^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p_{j}^{(k)}}-1}
and

(7.19)

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}=-(1-p_{j}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}\frac{1-R_{k^{\prime}}}{1-p_{j}^{(k)}(1-R_{k^{\prime}})}=-(1-p_{j}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}(1-R_{k^{\prime}})(1+O(1-R_{k^{\prime}}))

as
R_{k^{\prime}}\to 1
.

Computing
p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}
.
Let
p_{i}=\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1)
,
m=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(1-p_{i})
, and
\overline{k}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
. We show that

(7.20)

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{c+o(1)}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})

for some
0\leqslant c\leqslant 1
depending on
h,k,j
, as
m^{(\leqslant k)}\to\infty
, where

(1)

If
k=\overline{k}+\Omega(\sqrt{m}\log m)
, then
c=0
.

(2)

If
k<\overline{k}+\sqrt{m}\log m
, then
c=\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{r=1}^{k-((k\wedge\overline{k})-L(n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}))}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r
, for any function
L(x)=\omega(\sqrt{x})
.

Consider these 2 cases.

(1)

Suppose
k=\overline{k}+\Omega\left({\sqrt{m}\log m}\right)
(for any choice of constant). We note for any
L

(7.21)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}p_{j}^{(k)}=\mu^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{j}=1\text{ and }\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)

\displaystyle=p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}\mu_{-j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k-1)

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}<L}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k}}(1-p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})=\mu^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{j}=0\text{ and }\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<L)

\displaystyle=(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})\mu_{-j}^{(\leqslant k)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<L)

(7.22)

\displaystyle=(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})\frac{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<L)}{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}.

We note by monotonicity (
Lemma
7.31
) that
p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}\leqslant p_{j}^{(k)}
for
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
. Hence,

\displaystyle 0\geqslant_{\eqref{e:diff-k-1-k}}p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle\geqslant_{\eqref{e:diff-k-1-k}}-\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}p_{j}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=_{\eqref{e:asp1}}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}\mu_{-j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k-1)=o\left({\frac{1}{m}}\right)p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}

by noting that
\mu_{-j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k-1)=\frac{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k-1)}{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}=\frac{o(1/m)}{1-o(1)}=o\left({\frac{1}{m}}\right)
by Bernstein’s inequality, noting that the mean and standard deviation for
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h}
differ from
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h,-j}
by at most 1.
We also have

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}}\right)=\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({(1-p_{j}^{(k)})-(1-p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant-\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k-1}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k}}(1-p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=_{\eqref{e:asp2}}-\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})\frac{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle=-o\left({\frac{1}{m}}\right)(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})

by Bernstein’s inequality for
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h}
.
Putting the last two inequalities together,

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}=o\left({\frac{1}{m}}\right)p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})=o\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})

where the last equality holds because by
Lemma
7.32
(2), noting
k>\overline{k}
and
m=n^{(\overline{k})}
,

\displaystyle|m^{(\leqslant k)}-m|

\displaystyle\leqslant|m^{(\leqslant k)}-m^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}|+|m^{(\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}-n^{(\overline{k})}|=O(\sqrt{m^{(\leqslant k)}}).

(2)

Suppose
k<\overline{k}+\sqrt{m}\log m
. We would like to use (
7.18
) with (
7.19
), but because (
7.19
) only holds for
R_{k^{\prime}}\to 1
, we split the sum and first show the tail is negligible.
Let
L^{\prime}=\left\lfloor 2\sqrt{n^{(k)}}\log n^{(k)}\right\rfloor
. We have using (
7.18
) that

\displaystyle p_{j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}
