\displaystyle=_{\eqref{e:pk'-pk}}\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\left({\sum_{k^{\prime}<k-L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}}\right)-\sum_{k^{\prime}=k-L^{\prime}}^{k-1}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}(1-p_{j}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}(1-R_{k^{\prime}})(1+O(1-R_{k^{\prime}}))}\right)

(7.23)

\displaystyle=_{\eqref{e:odds-ratio-r}}\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}<k-L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}}\right)-\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{r=1}^{L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}(1-p_{j}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}\frac{r}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}(1+o(1)),

where we use
R_{k-r}=1-\frac{r}{m^{(k)}}(1+o(1))=1-\frac{r}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}(1+o(1))
when
r=o(m^{(k)})
and as
m^{(\leqslant k)}\to\infty
by (
7.15
) and
Lemma
7.32
(2).
For the first sum (tail term) in (
7.23
), we note by monotonicity that
p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}\leqslant p_{j}^{(k)}
for
k^{\prime}\leqslant k
, so

\displaystyle 0\geqslant\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}<k-L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant-\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}p_{j}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=_{\eqref{e:asp1}}-\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}\mu_{-j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k-1)

\displaystyle\geqslant-\frac{\mu_{h+t(k-1)\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h+t(k-1)\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}\mu_{-j}^{(\leqslant k-1)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k-1)

(7.24)

\displaystyle=_{\textup{\eqref{e:tail-k-L'}}}o\left({\frac{1}{n^{(k)}}}\right)p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}=_{\textup{\hyperref@@ii[l:mm']{Lemma~\ref*{l:mm'}}(2)}}o\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}.

We claim that

(7.25)

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}=o\left({\frac{1}{n^{(k)}}}\right).

We prove this by considering two cases.

(a)

k\geqslant\overline{k}
: As
m=n^{(\overline{k})}
, we have by
Lemma
7.32
that

\displaystyle|m^{(\leqslant k)}-m|

\displaystyle\leqslant|m^{(\leqslant k)}-n^{(k)}|+|n^{(k)}-m|

\displaystyle\leqslant O(\sqrt{m^{(\leqslant k)}})+|k-\overline{k}|=O(\sqrt{m^{(\leqslant k)}})+O(\sqrt{m}\log m)=o(m^{(\leqslant k)})=o(n^{(k)})

as
m^{(\leqslant k)}\to\infty
(equivalently,
m\to\infty
or
n^{(k)}\to\infty
).
Hence
2\sqrt{n^{(k)}}\log n^{(k)}\sim 2\sqrt{m}\log m
. Then
\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)=\Omega(1)
by Berry-Esseen (or
Theorem
C.1
) and
\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-L^{\prime})=o\left({\frac{1}{m}}\right)=o\left({\frac{1}{n^{(k)}}}\right)
by Bernstein’s inequality, so
(
7.25
) holds.

(b)

k<\overline{k}
:
This means
t(k)<0
, so we have, with Bernstein’s inequality for
\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h+t(k)\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}=o\left({\frac{1}{n^{(k)}}}\right).

Now we show (
7.24
) but with
1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}
instead.
Note that

(7.26)

\displaystyle\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k^{\prime})\leqslant\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k^{\prime}+1).

We also have

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}<k-L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}<k-L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({(1-p_{j}^{(k)})-(1-p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant-\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k-1}}\sum_{k^{\prime}<k-L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k}}(1-p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=_{\eqref{e:asp2}}-\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})\frac{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle\geqslant_{\eqref{e:bd-w-wo-j}}-\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})\frac{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-2)}

(7.27)

\displaystyle=o\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})

where the final line uses
\frac{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L^{\prime})}{\mu_{h,-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-2)}=o\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)
,
established in a similar way to before by considering 2 cases, noting that the relevant quantities for
\mu_{h}
and
\mu_{h,-j}
differ by at most 1 (
|m_{-j}-m|\leqslant 1
, etc.).

Combining (
7.24
) and (
7.27
), we obtain the bound on the first term of (
7.23
):

\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{k^{\prime}<k-L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{S_{k-1}}\left({p_{j}^{(k^{\prime})}-p_{j}^{(k)}}\right)=o\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}).

Now we address the second term in (
7.23
).
We have by log-concavity of the sequence
a_{0},\ldots,a_{k}
(
Lemma
7.15
with
Lemma
7.17
) that

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{r=1}^{L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r\leqslant\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r\leqslant\frac{1}{S_{k}S_{k-1}}\sum_{r=1}^{k}(a_{k}a_{k-r}+\cdots+a_{k-r+1}a_{k-1})\leqslant\frac{S_{k}S_{k-1}}{S_{k}S_{k-1}}=1,

so the second term is bounded by
\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}(1-p_{j}^{(k)})p_{j}^{(k)}(1+o(1))
.

Now we show we can further truncate the sum. Note that
\sum_{r=1}^{s}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}}(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})\mathds{1}_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\geqslant k-s}
. In the following, we consider when
n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}\to\infty
; note by
Lemma
7.32
(1) that
m^{(\leqslant k)}\sim m^{(k\wedge\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}\sim n^{(k\wedge\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor)}\sim n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}
. We will show that for any
L=\omega(\sqrt{n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}})
that

\sum_{r=1}^{(0\vee(k-\overline{k}))+L}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r=(1-o(1))\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r

Using this for both
L=L^{\prime}-(0\vee(k-\overline{k}))
and for
L=L(n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})})
establishes that

\sum_{r=1}^{L^{\prime}}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r=(1\pm o(1))\sum_{r=1}^{k-((k\wedge\overline{k})-L(n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}))}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r

and proves the claim about the constant
c
.

(a)

Suppose
k\geqslant\overline{k}-\sqrt{m}
.
By the local limit
Theorem
C.1
, by comparing
\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}
to a truncated Gaussian centered around
\overline{k}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}}(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}}(\overline{k}-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})+(k-\overline{k})=\Omega(\sqrt{m}+|k-\overline{k}|).

Note that by
Lemma
7.32
(1),
n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}\sim n^{(\overline{k})}=m
,
so we have
L=\omega(\sqrt{m})
.
By Cauchy-Schwarz and Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}}(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})\mathds{1}_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<(k\wedge\overline{k})-L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}}(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})\mathds{1}_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<(k\wedge\overline{k})-L}

\displaystyle=O(1)\sqrt{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})+(k-\overline{k})^{2}}\sqrt{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<(k\wedge\overline{k})-L)}

\displaystyle=o(\sqrt{m}+k-\overline{k})=o\left({\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}}(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})}\right)

as
n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}\sim m\to\infty
.
Therefore,
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}}(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})\mathds{1}_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\geqslant(k\wedge\overline{k})-L}=(1-o(1))\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k-1)}}(k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1})
.

(b)

For
k<\overline{k}-\sqrt{m}
, let
t
be such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k
.

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{r=1}^{k}a_{k-r}r}{S_{k-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{s=1}^{k}a_{k-s}^{\prime}}{\sum_{s=1}^{k}a_{k-s}^{\prime}e^{-ts}}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\left({\frac{a_{k-r}^{\prime}}{\sum_{s=1}^{k}a_{k-s}^{\prime}}}\right)e^{-tr}r

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{s=1}^{k}a_{k-s}^{\prime}}{\sum_{s=1}^{k}a_{k-s}^{\prime}e^{-ts}}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}}[e^{-tR}R|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1]

where
R=k-\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
.
Consider two cases. Define
E(t)
,
m(t)
as in
Lemma
7.34
, so we have
E(-t)=k
,
E(0)=\overline{k}
,
m(-t)=n^{(k)}
,
m(0)=m
.

(i)

\frac{n^{(k)}}{m}\in[\frac{1}{e},e]
: Within this case, as
n^{(k)}\to\infty
,
m\to\infty
.
Then
Lemma
7.34
gives

\sqrt{m}=E(0)-E(-t)\leqslant\int_{-t}^{0}m(s)ds\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}m(0)e^{s}ds=m(e^{t}-1)

so
t=\Omega\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}}\right)=\Omega\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)
.

(ii)

\frac{n^{(k)}}{m}\nin[\frac{1}{e},e]
:
Lemma
7.34
gives

m=m(0)\in m(-t)\cdot[e^{-t},e^{t}]=n^{(k)}\cdot[e^{-t},e^{t}]

so
t>1
.

In either case, we have

\min_{|r|\leqslant\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}e^{-tr}r=O(1)\cdot\max_{|r|\geqslant\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}e^{-tr}r
.
When
L\geqslant\sqrt{n^{(k)}}
, by Berry-Esseen,

\displaystyle E_{1}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}}[e^{-tR}R\mathds{1}_{k-L\leqslant\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1}]

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{|r|\leqslant\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}e^{-tr}r\cdot\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}(k-\sqrt{n^{(k)}}\leqslant\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)

\displaystyle=\min_{|r|\leqslant\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}e^{-tr}r\cdot\Omega(1)

\displaystyle E_{2}:=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}}[e^{-tR}R\mathds{1}_{\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{|r|\geqslant L}e^{-tr}r\cdot\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}<k-L)

\displaystyle=\min_{|r|\leqslant\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}e^{-tr}r\cdot o(1).

Thus
E_{2}=o(E_{1})
and dividing by
\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)
, we obtain that

\frac{\sum_{r=1}^{k-L}a_{k-r}r}{S_{k-1}}=(1-o(1))\frac{\sum_{r=1}^{k}a_{k-r}r}{S_{k-1}}.

as
n^{(k)}\to\infty
, as needed.

Comparing with
\mu_{-i}^{(\leqslant k)}
.
We now apply the above analysis to the distribution
\mu_{-i}^{(\leqslant k)}
, showing that we get a constant
c
that is independent of
i
.
Again, we split into 2 cases:

(1)

If
k\geqslant\overline{k}+\sqrt{m}\log m
, then we also have

k-1\geqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h,-i}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}+(m_{-i}(1+o(1))\sqrt{(m_{-i}(1+o(1))}-O(1)
for each
i
, so using (
7.17
) and case 1 above for (
7.20
), we obtain

p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}=(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})\cdot o\left({\frac{1}{m_{-i}^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)})=(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})\cdot o\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})

For the last equality, we reason as follows. We
note by (
7.20
) that
p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}=o\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}}\right)p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)})
, and use the following observation twice:
If
x-y=o(y(1-y))
, then because this is
o(\min\{y,1-y\})
, we have
x(1-x)=y(1-y)(1+o(1))
. This observation gives us

(7.28)

\displaystyle p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)})\sim p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}(1-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)})\sim p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}).

(2)

Suppose
k<\overline{k}+\sqrt{m}\log m
.
We now show for a certain choice
L=\omega(\sqrt{n^{(k\wedge\overline{k})}})
that

(7.29)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k,-i}}{S_{k,-i}}\sum_{r=1}^{k-((k\wedge\overline{k})-L)}\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k-1,-i}}r=\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}\sum_{r=1}^{k-((k\wedge\overline{k})-L)}\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}r(1+o(1)).

We have

(7.30)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k}}=(1-p_{i}^{(k-r)})\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k,-i}}+p_{i}^{(k-r)}\frac{a_{k-r-1,-i}}{S_{k-1,-i}}\in\left[{\min\left\{{\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k,-i}},\frac{a_{k-r-1,-i}}{S_{k-1,-i}}}\right\},\max\left\{{\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k,-i}},\frac{a_{k-r-1,-i}}{S_{k-1,-i}}}\right\}}\right].

We will use this equation for
r=0
to get
\frac{a_{k,-i}}{S_{k,-i}}\approx\frac{a_{k}}{S_{k}}
and for
k\mapsfrom k-1
,
r\mapsfrom r-1
to get
\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k-1,-i}}\approx\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k-1}}
.
For this, we bound the length of the interval in (
7.30
),
\left|{\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k,-i}}-\frac{a_{k-r-1,-i}}{S_{k-1,-i}}}\right|
.
For ease of notation, we consider the full distribution
\mu_{h}
and replace by
\mu_{h,-i}
at the end.

We bound
\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k}}-\frac{a_{k-r-1}}{S_{k-1}}
by considering two cases.

(a)

First, suppose
k\leqslant\overline{k}
.
We would like to use
Theorem
C.1
; however it does not give good bounds in the tails. We can use it to get bounds on ratios if we first tilt the measure so the mean is
k
. Towards this, let
t
be such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k
; note
t\geqslant 0
. Let
a_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}=\mu_{h-t\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k^{\prime})
. Note that
a_{k-r}\propto e^{-tr}a_{k-r}^{\prime}
. Choose
L
such that
\gamma\left({-\frac{L}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)\geqslant\frac{1}{(n^{(k)})^{1/4}}\geqslant\gamma\left({-\frac{L+1}{\sqrt{{n^{(k)}}}}}\right)
. Note by Bernstein’s inequality that

\displaystyle a_{k-L-1}+\cdots+a_{0}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-t(L+1)}(a_{k-L-1}^{\prime}+\cdots+a_{0}^{\prime})=e^{-t(L+1)}(a_{k}^{\prime}+\cdots+a_{k-L}^{\prime})\leqslant(a_{k}+\cdots+a_{k-L})o(1)

\displaystyle\implies a_{k}+\cdots+a_{k-L}

\displaystyle=(1-o(1))(a_{k}+\cdots+a_{0}).

Then by
Theorem
C.1
, for
r\leqslant L

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k}}

\displaystyle=(1-o(1))\frac{a_{k-r}}{a_{k}+\cdots+a_{k-L}}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)+\widetilde{O}\left({\frac{1}{n^{(k)}}}\right)}{\sum_{s=0}^{L}\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}\gamma\left({-\frac{s}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)+\widetilde{O}\left({\frac{1}{n^{(k)}}}\right)}\right)e^{-tr}}=(1+o(1))\frac{\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)e^{-tr}}{\sum_{s=0}^{L}\gamma\left({-\frac{s}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)e^{-ts}}

and similarly

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k-r-1}}{S_{k-1}}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\frac{\gamma\left({-\frac{r+1}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)e^{-t(r+1)}}{\sum_{r=1}^{L+1}\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)e^{-tr}}.

Then

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k}}\Big/\frac{a_{k-r-1}}{S_{k-1}}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\frac{\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)}{\gamma\left({-\frac{r+1}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)}\frac{{\sum_{r=1}^{L+1}\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)e^{-tr}}}{e^{-t}\sum_{r=0}^{L}\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)e^{-tr}}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\frac{{\sum_{r=0}^{L}\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)(1+o(1))e^{-tr}}}{\sum_{r=0}^{L}\gamma\left({-\frac{r}{\sqrt{n^{(k)}}}}\right)e^{-tr}}=1+o(1).

Applying this to
\mu_{h,-i}
, in light of (
7.30
), we obtain
\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k,-i}}=\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k}}(1+o(1))
.

(b)

Next suppose
k>\overline{k}
. Note
\frac{a_{\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor}}{a_{\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor+1}}=1\pm o(1)
as
n^{(\overline{k})}\to\infty
by
Theorem
C.1
.
Because
k<\overline{k}+O(\sqrt{m}\log m)
, we have by
Lemma
7.33
that for
\overline{k}<k^{\prime}\leqslant k
,

\displaystyle\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{a_{k^{\prime}-1}}\frac{a_{\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor}}{a_{\left\lfloor\overline{k}\right\rfloor+1}}=1-o(1)\implies\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{a_{k^{\prime}-1}}=1\pm o(1).

Similarly, or using
Theorem
C.1
, we have for
\overline{k}-L\leqslant k^{\prime}<\overline{k}
that
\frac{a_{k^{\prime}}}{a_{k^{\prime}+1}}=1\pm o(1)
.

We note
S_{k}=\Omega(1)
and
S_{k}-S_{k-1}=a_{k}=o(1)
. Hence we also have
\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k}}\Big/\frac{a_{k-r-1}}{S_{k}}=1+o(1)
. In the same way, we obtain
\frac{a_{k-r,-i}}{S_{k,-i}}=\frac{a_{k-r}}{S_{k}}(1+o(1))
.

Applying the above analysis for
\mu_{h}
replaced by
\mu_{h,-i}
and noting the relevant quantities
\overline{k}
,
m
differ by
O(1)
, we get that the corresponding terms of (
7.29
) differ by a factor of
1\pm o(1)
, establishing that equation.

Hence by (
7.17
), for some
c\leqslant 1
independent of
i
,

\displaystyle p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}

\displaystyle=-(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})(1-p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)})p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k)}\left({\frac{1}{m_{-i}^{(\leqslant k)}}(c+o(1))}\right)

\displaystyle=-(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}\left({\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}(c+o(1))}\right)

using (
7.28
) and

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu^{(\leqslant k)}}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})

\displaystyle=p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}(p_{j,-i}^{(\leqslant k-1)}-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})=-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{i}^{(\leqslant k)})p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)}(1-p_{j}^{(\leqslant k)})\cdot\frac{1}{m^{(\leqslant k)}}(c+o(1)).\qed

Corollary 7.36
(Covariance bound under high total uncertainty)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Consider a product distribution
\mu_{h}
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
.
Let
\Omega=\binom{[n]}{k}
or
\binom{[n]}{\leqslant k}
. Let
p_{i}=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}\sigma_{i}
.
Given
\delta
, there exists
m(\delta)
such that if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(1-p_{i})\geqslant m(\delta)
, then

\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{h}|_{\Omega})\preceq(1+\delta)\diag(p_{i}(1-p_{i})).

This follows from
Lemma
7.35
by noting that it says that the deviation from the covariance being a diagonal matrix of the variances is approximately a negative rank-1 matrix, plus a negligible part.

Proof 33
.

u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
u_{i}=p_{i}(1-p_{i})
and let

D=\diag(u)
.
By
Lemma
7.35
, there is a constant
c\in[0,1]
depending on
h,n,k
such that

\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}(\mu_{h}|_{\Omega})=D+\frac{c}{m}\diag\left({p_{i}^{2}(1-p_{i})^{2}}\right)-\frac{uu^{\mathsf{T}}}{m}c+\frac{uu^{\mathsf{T}}}{m}\circ E\preceq\left({1+\frac{c}{m}}\right)D+\frac{uu^{\mathsf{T}}}{m}\circ E

where all entries of
E
are
o(1)
.
Now, all rows of
\frac{uu^{\mathsf{T}}}{m}\circ E
sum to
o(1)
, so its maximum eigenvalue goes to 0 as
m\to\infty
. Hence, choosing large enough, this is
\preceq(1+\delta)D
.

The following bounds the marginal probability of a coordinate of
\mu_{h}|_{\binom{[n]}{k}}
in terms of
\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}
for appropriate
t
.

Lemma 7.37.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\sigma(x)=\frac{e^{x}}{1+e^{x}}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be given and let
t_{0}
solve

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma(h_{i}+t_{0})=k.

There exist
C_{1},C_{2}
depending on
\varepsilon
such that

\frac{\sigma(h_{i}+t_{0}-C_{1})-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\leqslant\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)\leqslant\frac{\sigma(h_{i}+t_{0}+C_{2})}{1-\varepsilon}.

Proof 34
.

The conclusion follows readily from the following two claims.

(1)

There is
C_{1}
such that the
(1-\varepsilon)
-quantile of
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h+(t_{0}-C_{1})\mathds{1}}
is
\leqslant k
.

(2)

There is
C_{2}
such that the
\varepsilon
-quantile of
\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}
under
\mu_{h+(t_{0}+C_{2})\mathds{1}}
is
\geqslant k
.

Indeed, under these claims, by monotonicity of
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{i}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k]
in
k
(
Lemma
7.31
),

\displaystyle\sigma(h_{i}+t_{0}-C_{1})

\displaystyle=\mu_{h+(t_{0}-C_{1})\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{i}=1)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+(t_{0}-C_{1})\mathds{1}}}[\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{i}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}]]

\displaystyle\leqslant_{(1)}(1-\varepsilon)\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{i}|\left\|{\sigma_{1}}\right\|=k]+\varepsilon=(1-\varepsilon)\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)+\varepsilon

and

\displaystyle\sigma(h_{i}+t_{0}+C_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu_{h+(t_{0}+C_{2})\mathds{1}}(\sigma_{i}=1)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+(t_{0}+C_{2})\mathds{1}}}[\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{i}|\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}]]

\displaystyle\geqslant_{(2)}(1-\varepsilon)\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{i}|\left\|{\sigma_{1}}\right\|=k]=(1-\varepsilon)\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1).

Solving these inequalities gives the result.

Now we prove the claims. Without loss of generality, suppose
t_{0}=0
.
Define
E(t)
and
m(t)
as in
Lemma
7.34
.
First, consider if
m(0)<\varepsilon
. Then by Chebyshev’s inequality,

\max\{\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\geqslant k+1),\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)\}\leqslant m(0)<\varepsilon,

which shows the claims for any
C_{1},C_{2}\geqslant 0
.

Suppose now that
m(0)\geqslant\varepsilon
. We first show the existence of
C_{2}
.
Let
\overline{E}(t)=E(t)-E(0)
. Note
m^{\prime}(t)\leqslant m(t)=\overline{E}^{\prime}(t)
, so integrating gives
m(t)-m(0)\leqslant\overline{E}(t)
. We have
\overline{E}(1)=\int_{0}^{1}m(t)dt\geqslant\int_{0}^{1}e^{-t}m(0)dt\geqslant(1-e^{-1})m(0)
. Hence for
t\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{m(t)}{\overline{E}(t)}=\frac{m(t)}{\overline{E}(t)+m(0)}\frac{\overline{E}(t)+m(0)}{\overline{E}(t)}\leqslant 1\cdot\frac{2-e^{-1}}{1-e^{-1}}.

Consider 2 cases.

(1)

m(t)\geqslant\frac{\overline{E}(t)}{C}
for
1\leqslant t\leqslant T:=1+C\log\left({\frac{2-e^{-1}}{(1-e^{-1})^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)
. Then
\overline{E}^{\prime}(t)\geqslant\frac{\overline{E}(t)}{C}
in this interval so

\overline{E}(T)\geqslant\overline{E}(1)e^{\frac{T-1}{C}}\geqslant(1-e^{-1})m(0)e^{\frac{T-1}{C}}\geqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{2-e^{-1}}{1-e^{-1}}

and by Chebyshev’s inequality,

\mu_{h+T\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)\leqslant\frac{m(T)}{\overline{E}(T)^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon.

(2)

There is
1\leqslant t\leqslant T
such that
m(t)<\frac{\overline{E}(t)}{C}
, where we take
C=\frac{1}{1-e^{-1}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. Then

\mu_{h+T\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)\leqslant\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)\leqslant\frac{m(t)}{\overline{E}(t)^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{C}\frac{1}{\overline{E}(1)}\leqslant\frac{1}{C}\frac{1}{(1-e^{-1})\varepsilon}\leqslant\varepsilon.

For the existence of
C_{1}
, we consider
E_{-}(t):=-E(-t)
and
m_{-}(t)=m(-t)
. Note that we also have
E_{-}^{\prime}(t)=m_{-}(t)
and
m_{-}^{\prime}(t)\in[-m_{-}(t),m_{-}(t)]
. Applying the same argument to
E_{-}
and
m_{-}
hence furnishes the lower bound.

The purpose of the lemma above is in the following corollary.

Corollary 7.38.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Given
0<q<\frac{1}{2}
, if
h_{i}>h_{j}+C_{q}
for a large enough constant
C_{q}
, then
\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)
and
\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)
cannot both be in
[q,1-q]
. The same holds true for
\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}
.

Proof 35
.

First consider
\mu_{h}^{(k)}
. Suppose both
\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)
and
\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)
are in
[q,1-q]
.
By
Lemma
7.37
with
\varepsilon=\frac{q}{2}
, there exist
C_{1},C_{2}
such that letting
t
be such that
\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{h+t\mathds{1}}}\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(h_{i}+t-C_{1})-q/2}{1-q/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)\leqslant 1-q

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leqslant\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)\leqslant\frac{\sigma(h_{j}+t+C_{2})}{1-q/2}.

Hence

\displaystyle\sigma(h_{i}+t-C_{1})

\displaystyle\leqslant(1-q)\left({1-\frac{q}{2}}\right)+\frac{q}{2}=:q_{1}

\displaystyle q_{2}:=q\left({1-\frac{q}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sigma(h_{j}+t+C_{2})

\displaystyle\implies h_{i}-h_{j}-(C_{1}+C_{2})

\displaystyle=(h_{i}+t-C_{1})-(h_{j}+t+C_{2})\leqslant\sigma^{-1}(q_{1})-\sigma^{-1}(q_{2}).

Hence we can take
C_{q}=\sigma^{-1}(q_{1})-\sigma^{-1}(q_{2})+(C_{1}+C_{2})
.

Next consider
\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}
. Suppose both
\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)
and
\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)
are in
[q,1-q]
. We have

\displaystyle\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1\text{ and }\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}=\frac{\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu_{-i}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}

so

\displaystyle\frac{\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)}{\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1)}{\mu_{h}(\sigma_{j}=1)}\frac{\mu_{-i}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}{\mu_{-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}.

Note that

\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)\leqslant\mu_{-i}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)\leqslant\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k).

Consider two cases.

(1)

\mu^{(\leqslant k)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)\geqslant 1-\frac{q}{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\left({1-\frac{q}{2}}\right)\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)\leqslant\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)\leqslant\frac{q}{2}+\left({1-\frac{q}{2}}\right)\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1)

and similarly for
j
. Hence if
\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{i}=1),\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)\in[q,1-q]
, then

\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{i}=1),\mu_{h}^{(k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)\in\left[{\frac{q/2}{1-q/2},\frac{1-q}{1-q/2}}\right]
. The result then follows from the first part with
q
replaced by
\frac{q/2}{1-q/2}
.

(2)

\mu^{(\leqslant k)}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}=k)<1-\frac{q}{2}
. Then
\frac{\mu_{-i}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}{\mu_{-j}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}\in\left[{\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)},\frac{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k)}{\mu_{h}(\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{1}\leqslant k-1)}}\right]\subseteq[\frac{q}{2},\frac{2}{q}]
. Hence if
\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{i}=1),\mu_{h}^{(\leqslant k)}(\sigma_{j}=1)\in[q,1-q]
, then

\frac{\mu_{h}(\sigma_{i}=1)}{\mu_{h}(\sigma_{j}=1)}\in\left[{\frac{q}{1-q}\cdot\frac{q}{2},\frac{1-q}{q}\cdot\frac{2}{q}}\right]
. We can take
C_{q}=\sigma^{-1}\left({\frac{1-q}{q}\cdot\frac{2}{q}}\right)-\sigma^{-1}\left({\frac{q}{1-q}\cdot\frac{q}{2}}\right)
.

In preparation for applying the results to the Ising model, we now consider product distributions on
\{-1,1\}^{n}
rather than on
\{0,1\}^{n}
. The previous results still hold up to scaling.
The following says that in order for the variance of
\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle
on a product distribution on the slice or wedge to be large, there must be a coordinate on which
x
is positive and bounded away from 0, and where
x
is negative and bounded away from 0.

Lemma 7.39.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
x_{0}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
and
\Omega=B_{k}(x_{0})
or
\partial B_{k}(x_{0})
.
Let
B<C
and
\varepsilon,\delta>0
be given.
There exist
q,c,C_{\delta}>0
such that the following hold.
Suppose that
\left\|{x}\right\|\leqslant C
,
\max_{|S|\leqslant 4k\vee\frac{48m(\delta)}{\delta}}\left\|{x_{S}}\right\|\leqslant B
and
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)>(1+\delta)B^{2}
, where
m(\delta)
is as in
Corollary
7.36
.
Then there exists
i,j
with
x_{i}\leqslant-c
and
x_{j}\geqslant c
, with
p_{i}=\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}(\sigma_{i}=1)\in[q,1-q]
and
p_{j}=\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}(\sigma_{j}=1)\in[q,1-q]
.

The idea is that from
Corollary
7.36
, we must have a small block of coordinates with large variance. Then we use the fact that off-diagonal entries of the covariance are negative, which forces us to have both positive and negative entries in that block of
x
.

Proof 36
.

Let
\Sigma=\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}(\sigma)
and
D=4\diag(\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}(\sigma_{i}=1)\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}(\sigma_{i}=-1))
.

First we claim that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(1-p_{i})<m(\delta)
. Indeed, if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(1-p_{i})\geqslant m(\delta)
, then by
Corollary
7.36
, because
4p_{i}(1-p_{i})\leqslant 1
and
4\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(1-p_{i})\leqslant 4k
,

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=x^{\mathsf{T}}\Sigma x\leqslant(1+\delta)x^{\mathsf{T}}Dx=4(1+\delta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(1-p_{i})x_{i}^{2}\leqslant(1+\delta)B^{2},

which is a contradiction.

Thus there are at most
\frac{m(\delta)}{q(1-q)}
coordinates such that
p_{i}\in[q,1-q]
.
Let
S=\left\{{i}:{p_{i}\in[q,1-q]}\right\}
,
S_{+}=\left\{{i}:{i\in S,x_{i}>0}\right\}
,
S_{-}=\left\{{i}:{i\in S,x_{i}<0}\right\}
.
We first bound with a block-diagonal matrix by the inequality
2ab\leqslant\lambda a^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda}b^{2}
, for
\lambda>0
:

\Sigma\preceq(\Sigma+\lambda D)\circ J_{S}+\left({\Sigma+\frac{1}{\lambda}D}\right)\circ J_{S^{c}},

where
J_{S}
denotes the matrix that is all-1 on
S\times S
and 0 elsewhere, and
\circ
denotes Hadamard product.
Choose
\lambda=\frac{\delta}{2}
,
q=\frac{\delta}{16}
.
Let
\left\|{x_{S_{+}}}\right\|=r_{+}
,
\left\|{x_{S_{-}}}\right\|=r_{-}
,
\left\|{x_{S^{c}}}\right\|=r_{0}
. Note that

x_{S_{\pm}}^{\mathsf{T}}\Sigma_{S_{\pm}}x_{S_{\pm}}^{\mathsf{T}}\leqslant x_{S_{\pm}}^{\mathsf{T}}D_{S_{\pm}}x_{S_{\pm}}^{\mathsf{T}}\leqslant r_{\pm}^{2}
because off-diagonal entries only contribute negatively.
Then

\displaystyle x^{\mathsf{T}}\Sigma x

\displaystyle\leqslant x_{S}^{\mathsf{T}}(\Sigma+\lambda D)_{S\times S}x_{S}+x_{S^{c}}^{\mathsf{T}}\left({\Sigma+\frac{1}{\lambda}D}\right)_{S^{c}\times S^{c}}x_{S^{c}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{+}(r_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2})+2\left\lVert\Sigma+\lambda D\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}r_{+}r_{-}+\left({2+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)x_{S^{c}}^{\mathsf{T}}D_{S^{c}\times S^{c}}x_{S^{c}}.

For
i\in S^{c}
,
\left({2+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)\cdot 4p_{i}(1-p_{i})\leqslant\frac{4}{\delta}\cdot 4q=1
. Hence, noting
\sum_{i\in S^{c}}\left({2+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)\cdot 4p_{i}(1-p_{i})\leqslant\sum_{i\in S^{c}}\left({2+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)\cdot 4p_{i}\leqslant\frac{4}{\delta}\cdot 4m(\delta)
,

\displaystyle\left({2+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)x_{S^{c}}^{\mathsf{T}}D_{S^{c}\times S^{c}}x_{S^{c}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S^{c}}\left({2+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)\cdot 4p_{i}(1-p_{i})x_{i}^{2}\leqslant\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\subseteq S^{c}\\
|T|\leqslant\frac{16}{\delta}m(\delta)\end{subarray}}\left\|{\widehat{x}_{T}}\right\|^{2}.

Note
\lambda_{+}(r_{+}^{2}+r_{-}^{2})=\lambda_{+}\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}^{2}
with
|S|\leqslant\frac{m(\delta)}{q(1-q)}
and
\left\lVert\Sigma+\lambda D\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 2+\lambda
, so noting that
\frac{16}{\delta}m(\delta)+\frac{m(\delta)}{q(1-q)}\leqslant\frac{48m(\delta)}{\delta}
,

x^{\mathsf{T}}\Sigma x\leqslant\lambda_{+}\max_{|T|\leqslant\frac{16}{\delta}m(\delta)+\frac{m(\delta)}{q(1-q)}}\left\|{x_{T}}\right\|^{2}+2(2+\lambda)r_{+}r_{-}\leqslant\lambda_{+}B^{2}+2(2+\lambda)r_{+}r_{-}.

Because
\lambda=\frac{\delta}{2}
, in order the inequality
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h}|_{\Omega}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)\geqslant(1+\delta)B^{2}
to hold, we must have
8r_{+}r_{-}\geqslant 2\left({2+\frac{\delta}{2}}\right)r_{+}r_{-}\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}B^{2}
. Because
r_{+},r_{-}\leqslant C
, we must have
r_{+},r_{-}\geqslant\frac{\delta B^{2}}{16C}
. Then there must be entries in
x_{S_{+}}
and
x_{S_{-}}
that are
\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{|S|}}\frac{\delta B^{2}}{16C}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{q(1-q)}{m(\delta)}}\frac{\delta B^{2}}{16C}=:c
.

Lemma 7.40.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\delta>0
be given. Then there is a constant
K(\delta,B,C)
such that for
x
such that
\left\|{x}\right\|\leqslant C
and
\max_{|S|\leqslant 4k\vee\frac{48m(\delta)}{\delta}}\left\|{x_{S}}\right\|\leqslant B
(with
m(\delta)
as in
Corollary
7.36
),

\lambda\left[{\left\{{u}:{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h+ux}|_{\Omega}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)>(1+\delta)B^{2}}\right\}}\right]\leqslant K(\delta,B,C),

where
\lambda
denotes Lebesgue measure over
\mathbb{R}
.

The idea of the proof is that the set of
u
’s for which the condition in
Lemma
7.39
is satisfied for a given
(i,j)
has at most constant measure, because the probabilities
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
will drift apart.
Since there are a constant number of possible pairs
(i,j)
for a given
x
, the set of
u
’s for which the variance is large also has at most constant measure.

Proof 37
.

By
Lemma
7.39
, there exist
q,c>0
such that if
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h+ux}|_{\Omega}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)>(1+\delta)B^{2}
, then there exist
i,j
with
x_{i}\leqslant-c
and
x_{j}\geqslant c
such that
\mu_{h+ux}|_{\Omega}(\sigma_{k}=1)\in[q,1-q]
for
k\in\{i,j\}
:

\displaystyle\left\{{u}:{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h+ux}|_{\Omega}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)>(1+\delta)B^{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:x_{i}\leqslant-c\\
j:x_{i}\geqslant c\end{subarray}}\left\{{u}:{\mu_{h+ux}|_{\Omega}(\sigma_{i}=1),\mu_{h+ux}|_{\Omega}(\sigma_{j}=1)\in[q,1-q]}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:x_{i}\leqslant-c\\
j:x_{i}\geqslant c\end{subarray}}\left\{{u}:{|(h_{i}+ux_{i})-(h_{j}+ux_{j})|\leqslant C_{q}}\right\},

where the second inclusion follows from
Corollary
7.38
for appropriate constant
C_{q}
. For given
x
, there are at most
\left({\frac{C}{c}}\right)^{2}
coordinates
i
with
|x_{i}|\geqslant c
, so there are at most
\left[{\frac{1}{2}\left({\frac{C}{c}}\right)^{2}}\right]^{2}
such pairs of
(i,j)
, and the interval of
u
where each inequality is true has measure at most
\frac{C_{q}}{c}
, so the measure of the set is at most
\left({\frac{C^{4}}{4c^{4}}}\right)\frac{C_{q}}{c}
.

Lemma 7.41
(Variance bound for mostly log-concave distribution)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
L,\alpha>0
.
Consider a probability measure
\mu
with density
p
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
-(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)
such that

(1)

-(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)\geqslant-L
everywhere, and

(2)

-(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)\geqslant\alpha
except on a set of Lebesgue measure
M
.

Fix
A>0
.
Then
\mu
has log-Sobolev constant at least
\frac{\alpha}{2}\exp\left({-\frac{M^{2}(L+\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha}}\right)
.
Thus,
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}(\mu)\leqslant\frac{2}{\alpha}\exp\left({\frac{M^{2}(L+\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha}}\right)
.

Proof 38
.

We will show that
\mu
has bounded ratio with a strongly log-concave measure.
Define an unnormalized probability density
\widetilde{q}
as follows. Let
\widetilde{q}(0)=p(0)
and define
g
(which will be set to be
(\log\widetilde{q})^{\prime}
) by

\displaystyle g(0)

\displaystyle=(\log p)^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle\text{for }u>0,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{+}}{du}g(u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\frac{\alpha}{2},&(\log p)^{\prime}(u)>-\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{ or }g(u)<(\log p)^{\prime}(u)\\
(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u),&(\log p)^{\prime}(u)\leqslant-\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{ and }g(u)=(\log p)^{\prime}(u).\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{for }u<0,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{-}}{du}g(u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\frac{\alpha}{2},&(\log p)^{\prime}(u)>-\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{ or }g(u)<(\log p)^{\prime}(u)\\
(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u),&(\log p)^{\prime}(u)\leqslant-\frac{\alpha}{2}\text{ and }g(u)=(\log p)^{\prime}(u).\end{cases}

Then we let

\log\widetilde{q}(u)=\int_{0}^{u}g(t)dt
. Note that
\widetilde{q}
is
\frac{\alpha}{2}
-strongly log-concave.
We now bound
\log\widetilde{q}-\log p
. This is maximized when the area between the graphs of
g
and
(\log p)^{\prime}
is maximized. Intuitively, this happens when the part when
(\log p)^{\prime}\not\leqslant-\frac{\alpha}{2}
is a single interval and maximally violated,
(\log p)^{\prime}=L
, and then this gap is shrunk as slowly as possible (with rate
\frac{\alpha}{2}
). We now show this formally.
Let
I_{+}=\left\{{u>0}:{(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)>-\alpha}\right\}
and
I_{-}=\left\{{u<0}:{(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)>-\alpha}\right\}
. These is the set where
(\log p)^{\prime}
is increasing, or not decreasing enough. Let
M_{\pm}=\lambda(I_{\pm})
. Note
M_{+}+M_{-}\leqslant M
.
Define also the set where
(\log p)^{\prime}
is decreasing but
g\neq(\log p)^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle D_{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{{u>0}:{(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)\leqslant-\alpha\text{ and }g(u)\neq(\log p)^{\prime}(u)}\right\}.

Note that
I_{+},D_{+}
form a partition of the set
\left\{{u>0}:{g(u)\neq(\log p)^{\prime}(u)}\right\}
.
Let
F_{+}^{I},F_{+}^{D}
be the cumulative distribution functions for
I_{+}
and
D_{+}
, respectively.
Because
(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)-g^{\prime}(u)\leqslant L+\frac{\alpha}{2}
on
I_{+}
, and
(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)-g^{\prime}(u)\leqslant 0
elsewhere,

\displaystyle(\log p)^{\prime}(u)-g(u)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)F_{+}^{I}(u).

Because
\lim_{u\to\infty}(\log p)^{\prime}(u)-g(u)=0
and
(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)-g^{\prime}(u)\leqslant-\alpha-\left({-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)=-\frac{\alpha}{2}
on
D_{+}
,

\displaystyle(\log p)^{\prime}(u)-g(u)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{2}(F_{+}^{D}(\infty)-F_{+}^{D}(u)).

Note the following claim. If
f(x)\geqslant b
and
\int_{A}f(x)dx\leqslant B
for
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, then letting
F(x)=\int_{0}^{x}f(t)\mathds{1}_{A}(t)dt
, using the substitution
t=F^{-1}(u)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}F(t)dt\leqslant\int_{0}^{F(x)}u\frac{dF^{-1}}{du}(u)du\leqslant\int_{0}^{F(x)}\frac{u}{b}du\leqslant\frac{F(x)^{2}}{2b}\leqslant\frac{B^{2}}{2b}.

We apply this to
f(x)=[(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(x_{0}-u)-g^{\prime}(x_{0}-u)]-[(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})-g^{\prime}(x_{0})]
for
u\to\infty
, noting

-\int_{0}^{\infty}(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)-g^{\prime}(u)dF_{-}^{I}(u)=\int_{0}^{\infty}(\log p)^{\prime\prime}(u)-g^{\prime}(u)dF_{+}^{I}(u)\leqslant\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)M_{+}

to conclude

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(\log p)^{\prime}(u)-g(u)dF_{-}^{I}(u)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{\alpha}\left({\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)M_{+}}\right)^{2}

Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(\log p)^{\prime}(u)-g(u)du

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}(\log p)^{\prime}(u)-g(u)dF_{+}^{I}(u)+\int_{0}^{\infty}(\log p)^{\prime}(u)-g(u)dF_{-}^{I}(u)

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)F_{+}^{I}(u)dF_{+}^{I}(u)+\frac{1}{\alpha}\left({\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)M_{+}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}M_{+}^{2}\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{\alpha}\left({\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)M_{+}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant M_{+}^{2}\left({L+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)\left({\frac{L}{\alpha}+1}\right)\leqslant\frac{M_{+}^{2}}{\alpha}(L+\alpha)^{2}.

Similarly, we have
\int_{-\infty}^{0}g(u)-(\log p)^{\prime}(u)du\leqslant\frac{M_{-}^{2}}{\alpha}(L+\alpha)^{2}
. Thus
(\sup-\inf)(\log\widetilde{q}-\log p)\leqslant\frac{M^{2}}{\alpha}(L+\alpha)^{2}
. Taking into account the normalization constant, letting
q(u)\propto\widetilde{q}(u)
be a probability density,
|\log q-\log p|\leqslant\frac{M^{2}}{\alpha}(L+\alpha)^{2}
. By Bakry-Emery, because
q
is
\frac{\alpha}{2}
-strongly log-concave, it has log-Sobolev constant at least
\frac{2}{\alpha}
. By Holley-Stroock,
p
has log-Sobolev constant at least
\frac{\alpha}{2}\exp\left({-\frac{M^{2}(L+\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha}}\right)
. This implies a Poincaré inequality with the same constant and hence the variance bound.

Lemma 7.42
(Entropy contraction for Ising model on wedges, II)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
x_{0}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
.
Given
\delta>0
,
C
,
B<\frac{1}{1+\delta}
, there is
C_{\delta}
such that the following holds. Suppose
A
is a PSD matrix such that
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C^{2}
and for all
|S|\leqslant 4\varepsilon n\wedge\frac{48m(\delta)}{\delta}
(
m(\delta)
as in
Corollary
7.36
),
\left\lVert A_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant B^{2}
. Then for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\cdot\rho_{\mathrm{EC}}(\mu_{A,h}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})})
is bounded below by a positive constant depending only on
\delta
,
C
, and
B
.

Proof.

We use the two-stage decomposition,
Lemma
7.28
.
Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
xx^{\mathsf{T}}\preceq A
, so
\left\|{x}\right\|\leqslant C
.
It suffices to bound
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}(p)
, where
p
is given by (
7.6
), for
\lambda=1
.
We have

\lambda\left[{\left\{{u\in\mathbb{R}}:{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h+ux}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)>(1+\delta)B^{2}}\right\}}\right]\leqslant K

for some constant
K
by
Lemma
7.40
.
Note also that

\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{h+ux}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})}}(\left\langle\sigma,x\right\rangle)\leqslant x^{\mathsf{T}}\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Cov}}}_{\mu_{h+ux}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})}}(\sigma)x\leqslant 2C^{2}

for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
by
Lemma
7.21
.
Then

\displaystyle-\log p^{\prime\prime}(u)=1-\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{\mu_{ux+h}|_{B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0})}}(\left\langle x,\sigma\right\rangle)\geqslant\begin{cases}-(2C^{2}-1),&\text{everywhere}\\
1-(1+\delta)B>0,&\text{except on a set of Lebesgue measure $K$}.\end{cases}

Therefore, by
Lemma
7.41
,
\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{p}(u)
is bounded by a constant.
The result then follows from
Lemma
7.28
.
∎

Lemma
7.30
then follows in the same way as
Lemma
7.20
.

Proof 39

(Proof of
Lemma
7.30
)
.

For
A\sim\operatorname{GOE}(n)
, consider
\beta A+\gamma I
, where
2\beta<\gamma<1
(e.g.
\gamma=\beta+\frac{1}{2}
). With probability
\geqslant 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,
\beta A+\gamma I
is PSD and has operator norm bounded by
2
.
Also with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
, by
Lemma
7.23
, for
|S|\leqslant 4\varepsilon n
,

\left\lVert(\beta A+\gamma I)_{S\times S}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\gamma+\beta c_{1}\sqrt{h(4\varepsilon)}<1

when
\varepsilon
is small enough.
The result than follows from
Lemma
7.42
.

7.4.
Concentration of
\left\langle\sigma,x_{0}\right\rangle

The fact that the localized distribution is concentrated on a wedge is a generic property of the stochastic localization process.

Lemma 7.43.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\mu
be any distribution on the hypercube, and consider the stochastic localization process with
x_{0}\sim\mu
,
y_{t}=tx_{0}+B_{t}
.
Let
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
.

(1)

With probability
\geqslant 1-2e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}
,

\mu_{y_{t}}(B_{\varepsilon n}(\sign(y_{t})))\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}.

In particular, for
t\geqslant 2\log\left({\frac{e^{2}}{\varepsilon}}\right)
, with probability
\geqslant 1-2e^{-\frac{n\varepsilon}{2}}
,

\mu_{y_{t}}(B_{\varepsilon n}(\sign(y_{t})))\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{n\varepsilon}{2}}.

(2)

With probability
\geqslant 1-2e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}
,

\mu_{y_{t}}(B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0}))\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}.

In particular, for
t\geqslant 2\log\left({\frac{2e^{2}}{\varepsilon}}\right)
, with probability
\geqslant 1-2e^{-\frac{n\varepsilon}{4}}
,

\mu_{y_{t}}(B_{\varepsilon n}(x_{0}))\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{n\varepsilon}{4}}.

Proof 40
.

Let
\Phi
denote the cdf of the normal distribution. Note that for each
i
,

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}((x_{0})_{i}\neq\sign((y_{t})_{i}))=\Phi(-\sqrt{t})

and these events are independent.
By a Chernoff bound,

(7.31)

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}(d(x_{0},\sign(y_{t}))\geqslant\varepsilon)\leqslant e^{-nD_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}.

Because

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}(d(x_{0},\sign(y_{t}))>\varepsilon)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}[d(\sign(y_{t}),x_{0})>\varepsilon|y_{t}]]=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\mu_{y_{t}}(B_{n\varepsilon}(\sign(y_{t}))^{c})],

we have by Markov’s inequality that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({\mu_{y_{t}}(B_{n\varepsilon}(\sign(y_{t})))\geqslant 1-2e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}}\right)\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}.

Using
\Phi(u)\leqslant e^{-\frac{1}{2}u^{2}}
for
u\leqslant 0
, we have for
e^{-\frac{1}{2}t}\leqslant\varepsilon
that

\displaystyle D_{\operatorname{KL}}\left({\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t})}\right)\geqslant D_{\operatorname{KL}}\left({\varepsilon\|e^{-\frac{1}{2}t}}\right)\geqslant\varepsilon\log\frac{\varepsilon}{e^{-t/2}}+\left({1-\varepsilon}\right)\log\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-e^{-t/2}}\geqslant\varepsilon\left({\log\varepsilon+\frac{t}{2}-1}\right).

Plugging in
t\geqslant\log\left({\frac{2e^{2}}{\varepsilon}}\right)
gives the simplified bound.

For (2), consider
\widehat{x}_{0}
drawn from the localized distribution
\mu_{y_{t}}
. Note that this is a posterior distribution of
x_{0}
given the observation
y_{t}
(with prior
\mu
), so the distribution of
(x_{0},y_{t})
and
(\widehat{x}_{0},y_{t})
are identical.

Since
(x_{0},y_{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\widehat{x}_{0},y_{t})
, we also have that (
7.31
) holds for
d(\widehat{x}_{0},\sign(y_{t}))
. Hence

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}(d(x_{0},\widehat{x}_{0})\geqslant 2\varepsilon)\leqslant\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}(d(x_{0},y_{t})\geqslant\varepsilon)+\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}(d(\widehat{x}_{0},y_{t})\geqslant\varepsilon)\leqslant 2e^{-nD_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}

Because
\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}(d(x_{0},\widehat{x}_{0})\geqslant 2\varepsilon)=\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}[d(\widehat{x}_{0},x_{0})\geqslant 2\varepsilon|x_{0}]]
, we have by Markov’s inequality that

\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({\mu_{y_{t}}(B_{2\varepsilon n}(x_{0}))\geqslant 1-2e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}}\right)\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{n}{2}D_{\operatorname{KL}}(\varepsilon\|\Phi(-\sqrt{t}))}.

Now replace
\varepsilon
with
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.
Plugging in
t\geqslant 2\log\left({\frac{2e^{2}}{\varepsilon}}\right)
gives the final statement.

Proof 41

(Proof of
Theorem
7.1
)
.

This follows from combining
Lemma
7.43
and
Lemma
7.30
.

7.5.
Sampling and partition function calculation

Corollary 7.44
(Sampling)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
.
There are constants
\varepsilon(\beta),T,\rho_{\beta}
depending on
\beta
such that with probability
\geqslant 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
, for any
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
,

(1)

For any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}
satisfies entropy contraction with constant
\geqslant\frac{1}{n}\rho_{\beta}
, and

t_{\mathrm{mix}}(\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))},\delta_{\sign(y)},\varepsilon)\leqslant\left\lceil\frac{n}{\rho_{\beta}}\log\left({\frac{2n}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)\right\rceil.

(2)

For
y_{T}=Tx_{0}+B_{T}
drawn according to SL, taking the polarized walk for

\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y_{T}}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y_{T}))}
for

\geqslant\left\lceil\frac{n}{\rho_{\beta}}\log\left({\frac{4n}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)\right\rceil
steps starting from
\sign(y_{T})
gives a sample that is
\varepsilon
-close in TV distance to
\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y_{T}}
.

Proof 42
.

For (1), We note that by
Theorem
7.1
, with probability
\geqslant 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,
\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}
satisfies entropy contraction with constant
\frac{1}{n}\rho_{\beta}
.
Let
\osc_{\Omega}f:=\max_{\Omega}f-\min_{\Omega}f
.
As part of the high-probability event, we may assume
\left\lVert\beta A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 1
.
Note that

\frac{d\delta_{\sign(y)}}{d\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}}\leqslant e^{\osc_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}(\beta^{\prime}\left\langle x,Ax\right\rangle)}2^{n}\leqslant e^{2n}2^{n}

The mixing time bound follows from
Lemma
7.4
.

For (2), apply (1) with
\varepsilon
replaced by
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
and
y=y_{T}
, and note further that by
Theorem
7.1
, we can choose
T
large enough so that with high probability,
\operatorname{TV}(\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))},\mu_{\beta^{\prime}A,y})\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.

Algorithm 5

Simulated annealing for partition function estimation

Sampling oracles for
\widetilde{p}_{\ell}
(approximations to
p_{\ell}\propto q_{\ell}
) for
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M
(distributions on
\Omega
), for example, Glauber dynamics or the polarized walk;
Z_{1}=\int_{\Omega}q_{1}\,d\omega
; number of samples
N
; number of trials
R
.

Estimate of
\int_{\Omega}q_{\ell}\,d\omega
for each
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M+1
.

Let
g_{\ell}(x):=\frac{q_{\ell+1}(x)}{q_{\ell}(x)}
.

for

1\leqslant r\leqslant R

do

Let
\widehat{Z}^{r}_{1}=Z_{1}
.

for

1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M

do

Obtain samples
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\sim\widetilde{p}_{\ell}
.

Let
\widehat{Y}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}g_{\ell}(x_{k})
.

Let
\widehat{Z}^{r}_{\ell+1}=\widehat{Z}^{r}_{\ell}\widehat{Y}_{\ell}
.

end

for

end

for

for

2\leqslant\ell\leqslant M+1

do

Let
\widehat{Z}_{\ell}
be the median of
\left\{{\widehat{Z}_{\ell}^{r}}:{1\leqslant r\leqslant R}\right\}
.

end

for

Lemma 7.45
(Partition function estimation with simulated annealing,
[
82
, Lemma C.4]
, cf.
[
54
]
)
.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
. Suppose that
p_{\ell},1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M+1
are distributions on
\Omega
, and that in
Algorithm
5
we are given sampling oracles for
\widetilde{p}_{\ell}
,
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M
such that the following hold for each
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant M
.

(1)

(Variance bound)
\frac{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{P_{\ell}}(g_{\ell}(x))}{(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{P_{\ell}}g_{\ell}(x))^{2}}\leqslant\sigma^{2}
.

(2)

(Bias bound)
\left|{\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{P_{\ell}}g_{\ell}(x)-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{P}_{\ell}}g_{\ell}(x)}\right|\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{4M}
.

Then taking
N\geqslant\frac{320\sigma^{2}M}{\varepsilon^{2}}
and
R\geqslant 32\log\left({\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)
, with probability
1-\delta
, the output
\widehat{Z}
satisfies
\widehat{Z}\in[e^{-\varepsilon},e^{\varepsilon}]\cdot Z
.

A non-adaptive temperature schedule of length
O(n)
is sufficient for partition function estimation. Note that a shorter schedule of length
O(\sqrt{n}\log n\log\log n)
is possible, and can be found in
n\mathsf{poly}\log(n)
total queries to approximate sampling oracles at the different temperatures
[
105
]
, or constructed non-adaptively
[
87
]
, but we use a geometric non-adaptive schedule for simplicity.

Corollary 7.46.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Let
Z_{\beta A,y}|_{\Omega}:=\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega}e^{\frac{1}{2}\beta\left\langle\sigma,A\sigma\right\rangle+\left\langle\sigma,y\right\rangle}
.
Let
\varepsilon=e^{-O(n)}
.
Under the same conditions as
Corollary
7.44
(for fixed
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
), we have that for
Algorithm
5
with
unnormalized distributions

q_{\ell}(\sigma)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\ell}\left\langle\sigma,A\sigma\right\rangle+\left\langle\sigma,y\right\rangle}
,
schedule
\beta_{\ell}=\frac{(\ell-1)\beta}{4n}
,
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant 4n+1
,
N=\Omega\left({\frac{n}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)
, and
R=\Omega\left({\log\left({\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)}\right)
,
with the polarized walk started at
\sign(y)
with
\Omega\left({n\log\left({\frac{n}{\varepsilon}}\right)}\right)
steps, for
\delta=e^{-O(n)}
, with probability
\geqslant 1-\delta
, the following hold.

(1)

For any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\widehat{Z}_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}\in[e^{-\varepsilon},e^{\varepsilon}]\cdot Z_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}\subseteq[0,e^{\varepsilon}]\cdot Z_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}

(2)

For
y_{T}=Tx_{0}+B_{T}
drawn according to SL,

\widehat{Z}_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}\in[e^{-\varepsilon},e^{\varepsilon}]\cdot Z_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}.

Proof 43
.

With probability
\geqslant 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 4
. Then
g_{\ell}(\sigma)=e^{(\beta_{\ell+1}-\beta_{\ell})\left\langle\sigma,A\sigma\right\rangle}\in[e^{-1},e]
, so
\frac{\mathop{{}\bm{\mathrm{Var}}}_{p_{\ell}}(g_{\ell}(\sigma))}{(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{p_{\ell}}g_{\ell}(\sigma))^{2}}\leqslant e^{4}
.
By
Corollary
7.44
,
with probability
\geqslant 1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
,
the polarized walk started at
\sign(y)
mixes to TV distance
\frac{\varepsilon}{16n}
within
O\left({\frac{n}{\rho_{\beta}}\log\left({\frac{n}{\varepsilon}}\right)}\right)
steps. Then by
Lemma
7.45
, with

N=\Omega\left({\frac{n}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)
samples and
R=\Omega\left({\log\left({\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)}\right)
repetitions,
we obtain a
e^{\varepsilon}
factor approximation for (1).
For (2), apply (1) with
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
to get
\widehat{Z}_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}\in[e^{-\varepsilon/2},e^{\varepsilon/2}]\cdot Z_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}
. It remains to note by
Theorem
7.1
(1) that

Z_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}|_{B_{\varepsilon(\beta)n}(\sign(y))}\in[1-e^{-O(n)},1]\cdot Z_{\beta^{\prime}A,y}
.

8.
Proof of main theorem

We prove the following formal version of
Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 8.1.
p_{p_{p_{p}}}

Given
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
and
\delta>0
, with probability
1-\delta
over
A\sim\mathrm{GOE}(n)
, for any
\varepsilon>e^{-cn}
for appropriate constant
c
,
Algorithm
2
outputs a sample from a distribution
\widehat{\mu}
such that
\operatorname{TV}(\widehat{\mu},\mu_{\beta A})\leqslant\varepsilon
in time
\mathsf{poly}(n,\exp\left({\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right))
.
In particular, this gives a polynomial-time algorithm for sampling from the SK model for
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
with TV distance
o_{n}(1)
in polynomial time.

We first prove the theorem assuming that in
Algorithm
3
,
\widetilde{m}_{t}
is an exact solution of the TAP equation (
TAP
) with
\widetilde{y}_{t}
for each
t\in\{0,\ldots,T\}
. In
§
D.    Mirror descent for solving the TAP equation
, we show that solving the TAP equation is efficient, and take into account the error involved.

Proof 44
.

We check the conditions of
Lemma
3.9
where the ideal process
y_{t}
is (
SL
), the approximate process is (
ASL-TAP-JE
) with weights targeting
\rho_{t}
given by (
2.4
). The algorithm computes the Euler-Maruyama discretization with some step size
h
to be determined:

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{t+h}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{y}_{t}+h\widetilde{m}_{t}+\sqrt{h}\xi_{t},\quad\xi_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{0}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{t+h}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{w}_{t}+\frac{h}{2}\left[{\mathsf{Tr}(\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}(\widetilde{y}_{t})))+\left\|{\widehat{m}(\widetilde{y}_{t})}\right\|^{2}}\right].

Part 1 (drift error).
This holds with
\varepsilon(t)=O(1)
by
Lemma
3.6
.

Part 2 & 3 (tails for log-weights).
Choose any
\lambda>1
. By
(d5)
and
Lemma
3.10
we have that the tail bounds hold with
C_{1}=\left({\frac{C_{\mathrm{JE},T,\lambda}}{\varepsilon_{1}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}
and
c_{3}=\frac{C_{\mathrm{JE},T,-1}C_{\mathrm{JE},T,1}}{\varepsilon_{3}}
.

Part 4 (path error).
We use
Lemma
3.11
. For this, we need a bound
\left\lVert D_{y}\widehat{m}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant L
and
\left\|{\widehat{m}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant M
, where here
\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert_{\infty}
is the functional
L^{\infty}
norm. Note

\left\lVert D_{y}\widehat{m}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}=\left\lVert\widehat{Q}(\widehat{m}(y))\right\rVert_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\Lambda

by
(d2)
, so we can take
L=\Lambda=O(1)
. Note that
\left\|{\widehat{m}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{N}
,
so we can take
M=\sqrt{n}
. To obtain
\operatorname{TV}(\dist((\widehat{y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}),\dist((\widetilde{y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}))\leqslant\varepsilon_{4}
it suffices to take
h\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{3/2}\varepsilon_{4}}{\sqrt{T}LM}\wedge\frac{\varepsilon_{4}^{2}}{2nTL^{2}}
, so it suffices for
h=O\left({\frac{\varepsilon_{4}^{2}}{n}}\right)
with an appropriate constant.

Part 5 (weight error).
We use
Lemma
3.12
. For this, we need a bound
\left\|{\nabla\omega}\right\|_{2}\leqslant L_{\omega}
.
First we bound
\left\|{f(m)}\right\|_{2}
where
f
is defined in (
2.3
). Note that
(d2)
gives that
\widehat{Q}(m)^{-2}\preceq\Lambda^{2}D(m)^{-2}
and considering the diagonal elements individually,
E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}\right]D(m)^{2}\preceq\Lambda^{2}I_{n}
.
Hence

\displaystyle-\beta^{2}\Lambda^{2}\left({1+\frac{2}{n}}\right)I_{n}

\displaystyle\preceq\left(E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}\right]D(m)^{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\left(\mathsf{Tr}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)\right]I_{n}+2\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)\right)\right)\preceq\Lambda^{2}I_{n}

\displaystyle\implies\left\|{f(m)}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\left(E_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\left[\widehat{Q}(m)^{2}\right]D(m)^{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\left(\mathsf{Tr}\left[\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)\right]I_{n}+2\widehat{Q}^{2}(m)\right)\right)m}\right\|_{2}\leqslant\Lambda^{2}\sqrt{n}.

By (
6.9
), we have
\nabla_{\widehat{m}}\omega=\widehat{m}-f(\widehat{m})
so

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla_{y}\omega}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|{(D_{y}\widehat{m})(\widehat{m}-f(\widehat{m}))}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lambda\cdot(\Lambda^{2}+1)\sqrt{n}.

Hence we can take
L_{\omega}=\Lambda(\Lambda^{2}+1)\sqrt{n}=O(\sqrt{n})
.
To obtain
\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({|\widetilde{w}_{T}((\widetilde{y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})-\widehat{w}_{T}((\widetilde{y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{2}}\right)\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{2}
, it suffices to take
h\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon_{4}/2}{2L_{\omega}TM}\wedge\frac{(\varepsilon_{4}/2)^{2}}{2L_{\omega}^{2}T^{2}n}\wedge\frac{1}{4L_{\omega}\sqrt{TnC\log\left({\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{4}/2}}\right)}}
for appropriate constant
C
, so it suffices for
h=O\left({\frac{\varepsilon_{4}^{2}}{n^{2}}}\right)
with an appropriate constant.

Part 6 (ratio error).
The joint density of
(x,y_{T})
where
\sigma\sim\mu_{\beta A}
and
y_{T}=Tx_{0}+B_{T}
is given by the SL process, relative to
\lambda_{\mathrm{Unif}}\otimes\lambda_{\mathrm{Leb}}
where
\lambda_{\mathrm{Unif}}
is the uniform measure on
\{\pm 1\}^{n}
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{Leb}}
is Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, is

\mu(\sigma,y)\propto e^{\frac{1}{2}\beta\langle\sigma,A\sigma\rangle}e^{-\frac{\left\|{y-T\sigma}\right\|^{2}}{2T}},

so summing over
\sigma
gives (letting
p_{T}=\dist(y_{T})
)

p_{T}(y)\propto\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}\mu(\sigma,y)\propto\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\beta\langle\sigma,A\sigma\rangle}e^{-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|^{2}}{2T}+\langle y,\sigma\rangle}.

Recalling that
\rho_{T}(y)\propto e^{\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)-\frac{\left\|{y}\right\|^{2}}{2T}}
from (
2.4
),

\displaystyle\frac{dp_{T}}{d\rho_{T}}(y)\propto\frac{Z_{\beta A,y}}{e^{\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)}}=:R_{2}(y)

where
Z_{\beta A,y}=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\beta\left\langle\sigma,A\sigma\right\rangle+\left\langle\sigma,y\right\rangle}
. Hence, with

\widetilde{R}_{2}(y)=\frac{\widehat{Z}_{\beta A,y}}{e^{\mathcal{F}_{\,\mathrm{TAP}}(\widehat{m}(y),y)}}

with
\widehat{Z}_{\beta A,y}
calculated as in
Corollary
7.46
, we have that
\frac{\widetilde{R}_{2}(y)}{R_{2}(y)}=\frac{\widehat{Z}_{\beta A,y}}{Z_{\beta A,y}}
and the guarantees transfer:

\displaystyle\forall y,\quad\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({\frac{\widetilde{R}_{2}(y_{T})}{R_{2}(y_{T})}\nin[0,e^{\varepsilon_{4}/2}]\Big|y_{T}=y}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{2}

\displaystyle\mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left({\frac{\widetilde{R}_{2}(y_{T})}{R_{2}(y_{T})}\nin[e^{-\varepsilon_{4}/2},e^{\varepsilon_{4}/2}]}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon

by taking
\varepsilon\mapsfrom\varepsilon_{4}
.

Putting everything together.
Thus by
Lemma
3.9
, the output of Part 1 of
Algorithm
2
is a sample
\widetilde{y}_{T}\sim\widetilde{p}_{T}
with
\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{p}_{T},p_{T})=O(\varepsilon)
. This takes
O\left({\frac{C_{1}^{2}L^{2}\log\left({\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)}{c_{3}^{2}}}\right)
simulations, each of which takes time polynomial in
\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{4}}
and
n
. Here
L=\exp\left({\frac{3}{\varepsilon}\left({\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon(t)^{2}dt+1}\right)}\right)=\exp(O(1/\varepsilon))
and
\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{4}}=O\left({\frac{L^{2}}{\varepsilon}}\right)=\exp(O(1/\varepsilon))
. By
Corollary
7.44
, with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
over
y_{T}\sim p_{T}
, the polarized walk for
\left\lceil\frac{n}{\rho_{\beta}}\log\left({\frac{4n}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\right)\right\rceil
steps from
\sign(y)
gives a sample
\widehat{\sigma}
that is
\varepsilon
in TV distance from
\mu_{\beta A,y_{T}}
. Letting
\widetilde{\mu}
be the distribution of
\widehat{\sigma}
for tilt
\widetilde{y}_{T}
, we have by the chain rule for TV distance that

\displaystyle\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{\mu},\mu)

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{p}_{T},p_{T})+\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{y_{T}\sim p_{T}}\operatorname{TV}(\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\sigma}|y_{T}),\mathcal{L}(\sigma|y_{T}))=O(\varepsilon)+e^{-\Omega(n)}+\varepsilon=O(\varepsilon)\,,

when
\varepsilon=e^{-O(n)}
. Choosing
\varepsilon=\Theta{\left({\frac{1}{\log n}}\right)}
, we obtain TV distance
\varepsilon=o(1)
in polynomial time.
